# Discernment of the Truth of Astronomical Principles <note>original: "सिद्धांततत्त्वविवेकः" (Siddhāntatattvaviveka). Composed in 1658 by Kamalākara, this is a foundational text of the Indian astronomical tradition that famously integrated elements of Islamic (Zij) astronomy into the classical Sanskrit framework.</note>

->4014<- <note>original: "४०१४"; the accession number corresponding to the library slip.</note>

<margin>
4014
Folio: 99
# 62 - 71 missed <note>A modern archival note indicating that folios 62 through 71 are absent from this specific manuscript copy.</note>
Gupta <note>original: "गुप्त" (Gupta); likely a reference to a specific cataloger or a classification category within the library.</note>
</margin>

<term>Siddhāntatattvaviveka</term>: A "Siddhānta" is a comprehensive treatise on Indian <term>astronomy</term> (Jyotisha) that covers everything from planetary positions to eclipses. "Tattva" refers to reality or truth, and "Viveka" means discernment or critical inquiry.

<term>Kamalākara</term>: The author of this work. He was a prominent astronomer at the court of the Mughal Emperor Shah Jahan and is known for his sophisticated mathematical approach.

<summary>This title page identifies the manuscript as the "Siddhāntatattvaviveka," a 17th-century masterwork of Indian mathematical astronomy. It includes modern library cataloging notes indicating that several pages are missing from this particular physical volume.</summary>

<keywords>Siddhāntatattvaviveka, Kamalākara, Indian astronomy, Jyotiṣa, Sanskrit manuscript, archival notes, Raghunatha Temple Library</keywords>

<meta>This page follows the title folio and archival notes. It serves as a formal internal heading for folio 172 of the manuscript. The text continues the technical exposition of astronomical principles by Kamalākara.</meta>

[Page 172]

->Discernment of Truth, Folio 172<-
<note>original: "तत्त्वविवेक पत्र १७२" (Tattvaviveka patra 172). This acts as a running header to ensure the loose leaves of the manuscript remain in the correct order.</note>

->108<-
<note>The number 108 is highly auspicious in Indian traditions, often representing the wholeness of existence, though here it likely functions as a specific section or sequence marker within the chapter.</note>

<term>Tattvaviveka</term>: A shortened reference to the full title, *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*. It literally means "Discernment of the Truth" or "Inquiry into Reality," reflecting the author's goal to critically examine astronomical observations against mathematical theory.

<term>Patra</term>: The Sanskrit word for "leaf." In the context of manuscripts, it refers to a single folio. Before the widespread use of bound books in India, manuscripts consisted of individual leaves (originally palm leaves, later paper) kept between wooden covers.

<note>The physical condition of this page is fragile; significant water damage and foxing (brown spotting caused by age and humidity) are visible along the bottom edge, which is common in historical documents stored in tropical climates.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a formal section header for folio 172 of the manuscript. It identifies the work by its abbreviated title and includes the auspicious number 108 as a sequence marker.</summary>

# Om! Salutations to the Illustrious Ganesha
<note>original: "ॐ श्री गणेशाय नमः" (Oṃ śrī gaṇeśāya namaḥ). A traditional invocation to the elephant-headed deity Ganesha, the remover of obstacles, used at the start of Hindu manuscripts.</note>

Having reflected upon this matter, the following is stated: He who is the master of the world, who takes the form of the sphere of the universe, and who remains the witness of the creation of the Supreme Brahman in the form of light—Him I behold. <note>The author opens with a philosophical reflection on the Sun as the physical manifestation of the divine or the "soul of time."</note> This eternal reality, which is the consciousness within us and the lord of Brahma and the other gods, has become manifest here. (1–2) In this world, the Lord, the treasury of light, known by the name of the Soul of Time—the Sun—is understood through this science. (3) 

Moving within the center of the celestial sphere, like a water-wheel, the Sun—though all-pervasive and indestructible—appears as the light-giver. (4) He is the promulgator of the scriptures, the object of meditation, the one who makes us understand Time, the embodiment of the three Vedas, and he who stands as the singular measure for all distances. (5) 

<margin>Original note: regarding the Vedas.</margin> 

Some people, out of ignorance, say "This is my friend" when the sun sets, speaking like simple villagers; however, those who know the sphere follow the calculations of the scriptures and understand the movements of the planets according to their own astronomical systems. (6–7) There is another tradition, well-known yet misunderstood by many, called the <term>Saura</term> <note>Saura refers to the "Solar" system of astronomy, specifically the *Sūrya Siddhānta*, which was the standard authority in India for centuries.</note> system; due to a lack of understanding of its true intent, modern observers have struggled to reconcile it. (8) To clarify the logic behind the planetary calculations and to establish the true nature of the sphere for the sake of practical use, this work is composed. (9) The ultimate truth of these matters has been described in many ways by sages like Brahma, each creating astronomical systems according to their own intelligence. (10) 

Therefore, having bowed to the Sun and to the illustrious Divākara, the son of the intermediate teacher, I, Kamalākara, the son of Nṛsiṃha, (11) have composed this *Discernment of the Truth of Siddhānta* (Siddhāntatattvaviveka), including its commentary, to awaken the intelligence of those who study the sphere. (12) 

Even though this science was given by Brahma or Prajāpati, the measures of the Solar, Civil, and Lunar systems must be understood. (13) Time is the creator of all beings; it is of two types: <term>Mūrta</term> (manifest/embodied) and <term>Amūrta</term> (unmanifest/abstract), ranging from the very coarse to the extremely subtle. (14) 

<margin>Commentary.</margin>

Manifest time begins with the breath (*Prāṇa*), while the abstract or unmanifest is known to start even smaller. (15) 
- 6 breaths (*Prāṇa*) = 1 <term>Vināḍī</term> <note>Also known as a *Pala*, roughly 24 seconds.</note>
- 60 *Vināḍīs* = 1 <term>Ghaṭī</term> <note>Roughly 24 minutes.</note>
- 60 *Ghaṭīs* = 1 Day and Night (24 hours). (16) 

- 30 such days = 1 Month.
- 12 months = 1 Year. 
This is the measurement of time according to the rotation of the wind. (17) 

For spatial and angular measurement:
- 60 seconds (*Vikalā*) = 1 minute (*Kalā*).
- 60 minutes = 1 degree (*Bhāga*).
- 30 degrees = 1 sign (*Rāśi*).
- 12 signs = 1 full revolution (*Bhagaṇa*). (18)

In the ancient systems, these divisions were spoken of by the wise. First comes the zodiac sign Aries, and then the others in sequence. (19) Regarding the movement within these divisions, some say that the civil day is the most useful for calculations in the science of the sphere. (20) To know the movement of the planets, one must look from the western horizon to the eastern; a <term>Sāvana</term> day <note>A civil or terrestrial day measured from one sunrise to the next.</note> is defined as the time from one sunrise to the next. (21) The gathering of these days is called the *Ahargaṇa* (heap of days). In this system, a day is considered to belong to the Sun. (22) A solar month is defined by the Sun's transit from one sign of the zodiac to the next.

<summary>This page contains the opening of Kamalākara's treatise, featuring an invocation to the Sun, the author's lineage, and the foundational definitions of time and angular measurement used in Indian astronomy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*. Having introduced the Sun as the soul of time on the previous page, the author now begins to define the various scales of time—from the perspective of humans, ancestors, and gods—leading into the calculation of cosmic cycles or Yugas.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>

...successively, thirty nights constitute a month. It is stated by the sages that a <term>Sāvana</term> month <note>A "Sāvana" day is measured from sunrise to sunrise; a Sāvana month is 30 such days.</note> consists of thirty such days. (23) In this manner, the rotation of the stars has been described. Therefore, the rotation is said to consist of 60 <term>Ghaṭikās</term> <note>A Ghaṭikā is a traditional unit of time equal to 24 minutes; 60 Ghaṭikās make a full 24-hour day.</note>. (24) It was previously stated that a month of humans is a day and night for the **Ancestors** <note>original: "पितॄणां" (Pitṝṇāṃ). In Vedic cosmology, the moon's phases dictate the day and night of the ancestral spirits.</note>. The distribution of their day and night is equal. (25)

Because of the reverse positions of the **Gods** and the **Demons** <note>original: "देबानामसुरार्णाच" (Devānām-asurāṇām-ca). The gods are traditionally situated at the North Pole (Meru) and the demons at the South Pole.</note>, what is day for one is night for the other. Thus, a human year is a day and night for the gods. (26) These are called "Divine Days." In the midst of these, a year is completed by the Sun's passage through the entire zodiac. (27) In this sky, which is without beginning or end, the planets and the circle of stars revolve continuously in the great flow of Time. (28) At no fixed moment can it be said "this is the beginning," for the circle of the stars is constantly turning like a wheel. (29)

<margin>
[End of]
The Command
</margin>

At the midnight of Lanka, the <term>Horās</term> <note>planetary hours</note>, the nodes, and the entire cycle of calculations were set in motion; at that time, those versed in the scriptures describe the beginning of Time. (30) Experts declare that the conjunction of these cycles occurs at the end of a <term>Kalpa</term>. Through their supersensible vision, the ancient masters grasped this Time in their own homes, as recorded in the scriptures of the Great Cycle. (31) 

The measures—**Solar**, **Divine**, and **Sāvana**—all function simultaneously in the world. The calculation of these measures has been explained from the start of creation. (32) Those who know the spheres of the planets say that at the moment of creation, the planets were established by the Creator, Brahma, through his own power. (33) 

The sages describe the measure of a <term>Kalpa</term> according to the rotation of the <term>Brāhmamahorātra</term> <note>A day and night of Brahma.</note>, which is suitable for the performance of one’s own duties and religious vows at the beginning of a cycle. (34) This time, known by the name of **Kalpa**, is divided into various stages such as "Creation" for the sake of obtaining results. (35) 

### The Cosmic Cycles (Yugas)

A <term>Kalpa</term> is comprised of one thousand <term>Mahāyugas</term>. In a single <term>Mahāyuga</term>, there are four Yugas (Ages) categorized as follows: (36)

*   **Kṛtayuga**
*   **Tretāyuga**
*   **Dvāparayuga**
*   **Kaliyuga**

These ages decrease in length successively. (37) According to the Vedas, the number of solar years in a **Mahāyuga** is 4,320,000. The specific lengths for the ages, including their <term>Saṃdhyā</term> <note>The "twilight" or transition periods at the beginning and end of each age.</note>, are: (38)

| Age (Yuga) | Duration in Solar Years |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Kṛtayuga** | 1,728,000 |
| **Tretāyuga** | 1,296,000 |
| **Dvāparayuga** | 864,000 |
| **Kaliyuga** | 432,000 |

<margin>
Twilight
</margin>

One-tenth of the total <term>Mahāyuga</term> (432,000 years) represents the base unit. (39) Adding these together, the sum of the four ages equals the 4,320,000 years of the <term>Mahāyuga</term>. (40) At the beginning, middle, and end of these, there are periods of twilight. The Lords of Time have also established the durations of the <term>Manus</term>. (41) This entire duration, containing the cycles of the Sun and the various <term>Manus</term>, is called a **Kalpa** by the masters of astronomical time for the purpose of calculation. (42)

Religious merit (Dharma) exists in four parts in the first age, and decreases by one part in each subsequent age. (43) The day of Brahma is said to be the length of one Kalpa, and his night is of equal length. Together, they form a <term>Brāhmamahorātra</term>. (44) According to the scriptures, 360 such days and nights make a year of Brahma, and his total lifespan is...

Then, the number of his days and nights is 45. To determine the light of the day of the **Gods**, the method of the Sun—which is unopposed—is stated. This world was not created by the mere speech of **Vasu** <note>original: "देववसो" (Devavaso); referring to a class of deities or perhaps a specific Vedic authority</note> (47), but by the turning of the wheel. At any time, by the power of speech, the seven great distinctions were made (46). From the undivided Earth, the realms of **Bhur, Bhuvah, and Svah** <note>The three worlds: Earth, the atmosphere, and Heaven.</note> rose together. From the beginning, these were arranged in order by the Moon and the Sun (47).

Now, it is said that the creation of **Brahma** <note>original: "ब्रलसृष्टेः" (Brahmasṛṣṭeḥ)</note> began with the sunrise of that first day. In the great time of all the Gods, the worlds such as **Maharloka** <note>The fourth of the seven upper worlds in Hindu cosmology.</note> exist (48). In the **Markandeya Purana** and others, and likewise in the **Bhagavata**, there are authoritative statements regarding the measurements of time which are accepted here as their own (49). In the very years of creation, as seen in the various accounts, it is said in the scriptures that they are joined with the technical methods of calculation (50).

The **Shakas** <note>referring to the era or perhaps the people associated with the era-keeping</note> were created by the Creator for the sake of the Universal Form, and they are never separate from the three Vedas (51). In this world, many such "Lotus-Born" creators <note>original: "कमलोद्भवाः" (Kamalodbhavāḥ); an epithet for Brahma, who is born from the lotus growing from Vishnu's navel.</note> have come and gone in this eternal time, and many more will come (52). From the beginning of the **Kalpa** <note>A vast cosmic cycle lasting 4.32 billion years.</note>, the lifespan that has passed of the current Creator is determined according to the branches of the solar years (53).

In the era of the illustrious King **Shalivahana** <note>A standard astronomical era starting in 78 CE. The text later gives a date of 1706 Shaka, which corresponds to 1784 CE.</note>, according to the tradition of the mouth of the Creator, the results of the fruits of time are given in order (54). The **Manus** <note>The progenitors of humanity who rule over specific periods called Manvantaras.</note> who have passed are: **Svayambhuva**, **Svarocisa**, **Uttama**, **Tamasa**, **Raivata**, and **Caksusa** (55). The seventh **Manu** is **Vaivasvata**, who is of the present time. Thus, six Manus have passed, along with their respective junctions (55).

As stated, it should be known that in the current Kalpa, the number of solar years passed is 3,174 <note>This likely refers to a specific sub-cycle or a corrupted OCR reading of a larger number</note> (56). From the sum of all parts, the solar calculation is performed according to the rule. Or rather, the years passed are measured by the mountains, the moons, and the openings: **1,972,947,109** <note>original: "१७९२१७१०९" (likely a typo for the standard 1.97 billion years usually cited in Siddhantas since the Kalpa began).</note> (57).

In the **Shalivahana era**, the years of the Sun that have passed are noted as **1,706** <note>This dates the manuscript's calculations to 1784 CE.</note>. These are the years elapsed in the cycle of creation (58). In the method of the creation-cycle, from the solar years of **1,970,640,000**, the elapsed years are subtracted (59). This method of the creation-wheel is used to calculate the movement of all heavenly bodies.

These secrets have been written down after considering the many jewels of thought, resolving contradictions between the sages (60). This is the very root found in the **Brahma Purana**; although not heard by everyone before, it is well-known to those who understand the origins of creation (61). In this section on the logic of the moments of time, even without direct observation, the truth is established in the root of the scriptures (61). If there were a contradiction between the Sun and the sages regarding the Kalpas and Manus, then the word of the "Lotus-Born" Brahma would be preferred (63). In the first book of the composition of the day of Brahma...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the measurement of cosmic time. While the previous page established the creation of the world and the role of the Vedas, this page delves into the specific astronomical traditions, comparing the teachings given by the Sun to Maya with the school of Brahma, focusing on how different time-scales (Kalpas) explain variations in planetary calculations.</meta>

### The Revelation of the Sun to Maya

Having released those impurities, the <term>Muni</term> <note>A sage or inspired seer.</note> [explained] how the **Sun** spoke to **Maya** <note>original: "सूर्योमयाय" (Sūryomayāya); Maya was a legendary architect and astronomer-demon to whom the Sun God revealed the *Surya Siddhanta*.</note>. That which is known through direct perception, logic, and the scriptures of the learned is the truth; whatever men do contrary to this is considered a false statement of their country. (65)

Furthermore, in this context, the origin of the **Sun's** school is stated. Some follow the school of **Brahma** <note>The *Brahma Siddhanta*, another major system of Indian astronomy.</note> regarding the sunrise. Mt. **Meru** <note>The sacred mountain at the center of the world, representing the North Pole.</note> stands as the center of the Earth's pericarp, while **Lanka** <note>The city on the equator used as the prime meridian (0° longitude) in ancient Indian astronomy.</note> is situated elsewhere; the meaning of the Sun's teaching is consistent because of this. (65)

Moreover, in this world and in the scriptures, it is not strange to say that one's own system is superior, without adopting the measurements of the root-authority based on desire or the passing of time. (66)

-> **On the Clarity of Meanings** <-

The methods of these two systems regarding time are distinguished by the difference in the <term>Kalpa</term> <note>A vast cosmic age, often called a "Day of Brahma," lasting 4.32 billion years.</note>. Due to the difference between the school of **Brahma** and the school of the **Sun** (*Arka*), the movement of the planets is understood. In every age (<term>Yuga</term>), the ancient Kalpa has been declared for the sake of the sages. (67)

There is no [difference] in the world of **Brahma**, only in the darkness [of misunderstanding]. (68) Since he knows the difference of that specific time, he now asks where the schools of Brahma and the Sun might be joined to achieve the fruit of their calculations. (69)

In the knowledge of mathematics, the Kalpa is declared as arising from the specific time of the planets; he should speak of those two systems as desired by others. (70) In truth, even on the mountain of the Kalpa, the essence is not found by force, but rather by the traditional methods that have come before. (71)

### The Source of the Differences

The scripture was received by the gods, and the **Sun** spoke it to the <term>Daitya</term> <note>Referring to Maya, who belonged to the race of Daityas (titans/demons).</note>. According to the words of the Holy **Sun**, the difference does not arise from the Kalpa itself. (72) By the revolution of the ages, the measurement of time remains solitary; this difference in the movement of the stars is not caused by time alone. (74)

There is no difference in the calculations themselves for the sake of the solar transition. The cause of that difference is the truth known by the followers of the Sun. (75) If the motion of the planets at the end of a Kalpa is determined, how can the difference in the circles of the <term>Yugas</term> be reconciled by the wise? (76)

This established difference in the positions of the planetary spheres arises from the difference in the Kalpas; to say it is not logical based on the previously mentioned counts of stars is incorrect. (77) That which was seen by the Sage with his four faces <note>Referring to Brahma.</note> and that which was revealed by the Sun is not contradictory. To say they are opposed is a false story. (78) Because the time is established by the movement of the celestial bodies, this current Kalpa is calculated accordingly. (79) If it were so, then through the lack of contradiction with the scriptural Kalpa, the logic of every single moment of time would be grasped in the calculation. (80)

Even if the wind of **Brahma** moves the planets, and even if they wander in a single path, the difference in their speed is small; the planets are always moving on their paths. (81) Through great effort and the measurement of their slight movements, the science of the sunrise is produced for all people. It is seen in the west when it sets, and it is seen by the people in the East. (82) In this way, the wise understand the distance in <term>Yojanas</term> <note>A traditional unit of distance, roughly 8 to 15 kilometers.</note>, and they calculate the enjoyment of the signs of the zodiac. Even when the movement is slow or fast, the perception of it remains true. (83)

When it is midnight in **Lanka**, it is sunrise in **Yamakoti** <note>A legendary city located 90 degrees east of Lanka.</note>; this is the knowledge of the Sun's appearance at the beginning of the **Shaka** era for Maya and others.

<summary>This page discusses the reconciliation between different schools of Indian astronomy (Brahma vs. Surya), explaining that variations in calculations stem from the vastness of cosmic cycles (Kalpas) and the geographical positions of the observers relative to the prime meridian at Lanka.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of astronomical time-cycles, specifically moving from the general theory of revelation to the specific mathematical constants—the revolutions of the planets—that define the current cosmic age (Kalpa). It reconciles the differences between various schools of thought by grounding them in the initial moment of creation.</meta>

<margin>
System of 
Knowledge 
3
<insert>3</insert>
</margin>

Even if the sequence of days appears different due to the influence of time, all these measurements—from the smallest unit of time to the vast ages of the <term>Vedas</term> <note>The ancient sacred scriptures of India.</note>—converge in this system. At the very beginning of creation, the Creator knew everything simultaneously in one place; therefore, he established the sequence of days. (84–85)

Although these sequences of months and lunar days were established long ago, starting with the twice-born <note>original: "dvija"; a reference to the upper social classes who traditionally studied these sciences.</note>, they should be understood here through the reconciliation provided by the sages. (86) Who other than the Sun, the ender of time itself, can truly declare the day when creation began? Therefore, the start of creation is measured from the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term> <note>A vast cosmic cycle lasting 4.32 billion years.</note> according to his measurement. (87)

From the first day of creation until the end, the stability of the people on Earth and the conduct of daily life depend on this calculation. (88) The alignment of the planets is established as it was at midnight in **Lanka** <note>The astronomical prime meridian on the equator.</note>. Just as the arrival of the year is calculated, at the start of creation, the Sun rose on its own day <note>Sunday</note> to mark the beginning of the world. (89)

How could one be deceived regarding this truth? The calculation of the planets' positions—including their <term>Apogees</term> <note>The point in an orbit farthest from the Earth.</note> and <term>Nodes</term> <note>The points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic.</note>—was observed by men at **Lanka** at the very transition between the zodiac signs of **Pisces** and **Aries** <note>original: "mīna-meṣa-sandhi"; this marks the traditional Hindu New Year.</note>. This occurred at the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term>. This "Creation Sun" is calculated by the years of creation and the planetary positions combined. (90–91)

The revolutions of the Sun and other bodies from the beginning of the creation are now stated according to the **Science of the Sun** <note>original: "arka-śāstra"; another name for the *Surya Siddhanta*.</note>. (92) In a <term>Kalpa</term>, the revolutions of the **Sun** are 4,320,000,000. (93) The revolutions of the **Moon** are 57,753,336 <unclear>...</unclear> <note>This number represents the lunar revolutions in a Great Age (Mahayuga) within the larger Kalpa.</note>. For **Mars**, the revolutions of its <term>Sighrocca</term> <note>A mathematical point used to calculate the planet's faster motion.</note> are 2,296,832 <unclear>...</unclear>.

The revolutions for **Mars** at the start of the creation <term>Kalpa</term> are heard to be 175,306,000. For **Mercury**, they are 36,422 <unclear>...</unclear>. The total rotations of the stars are 1,582,237,500 <note>The OCR shows "7022376000", which likely reflects a specific variation or a transcription error of the sidereal rotations.</note>.

To find the total elapsed days at the end of a lunar day, one should multiply the elapsed time by the number of **omitted lunar days** <note>original: "avama"; these are the days skipped in the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with civil days</note> in a cosmic cycle. Divide this by the total lunar days of the cycle. The result, when subtracted from the total lunar days, gives the **terrestrial civil day count** <note>original: "ahargana"</note>. (7)

The wise have established that the mean position of the Sun is calculated for the moment of midnight at **Lanka** <note>the traditional Indian prime meridian on the equator</note>, which corresponds to sunrise in the city of **Yamakoti** <note>a mythical city located 90 degrees east of Lanka</note>. (8)

The remaining fraction of the **intercalary months** <note>original: "adhimasa"; the extra months added to the lunar year</note> should be known. To this, one must add the solar months elapsed since the beginning of the month of **Chaitra** <note>the first month of the Hindu lunar calendar, usually occurring in March-April</note> in one’s own solar year. (9) This calculation follows the logic of the Sun's movement. The months elapsed since the start of the year, combined with the fractional parts, provide the total elapsed solar time. (10)

When these fractional parts are diminished by the correct proportions, they become equal to the whole. In this way, if the remainder of the intercalary month is equal to the elapsed part of the year, the calculation is balanced. (11) If the fractional parts are less than the omitted days at the end of a lunar day, then that is the day-count for that time. At midnight, however, one must account for the addition or subtraction of the Sun’s motion to find the true time. (12)

(14) To calculate the positions of the planets, if the day of the week does not match the calculation, one must add or subtract one day from the day-count until the observed day and the calculated day align. (15)

(16) At midnight in Lanka, the markers for the mean Sun are produced from the day-count. From these, the positions in degrees and minutes are determined by the strength of the mathematical logic. (17)

### On the Calculation of the Civil Day and the Mean Sun

...by those forms spoken of as the sequence of the days, there are 27 rotations. (27) Because the revolutions are completed by the **mean civil day-count** <note>original: "ahargana-madhyama-savana"</note>, the crown of the civil day calculation is established. Indeed, these are determined by adding or subtracting the corrections related to the rising of the celestial sphere. 

Through this result, (28) in the world, the earth's rotation is observed by the people. Because of the Earth’s rotation, the **Mean Sun** <note>original: "madhyarka"; the Sun's position if its motion were perfectly uniform</note> is divided by the parts of the daily motion, and those minutes of the daily motion are equated to the solar motion. (28) 

Therefore, the difference between the **true position** <note>original: "sphuta"</note> and the **mean position** <note>original: "madhya"</note> creates an interval between two solar days. This difference in motion, when applied to the daily movement, results in the subtraction of time-degrees <note>original: "asavah"</note> from the signs of the zodiac. (29) Through this knowledge, the rising of the stars and the difference between the mean and true sunrise is understood for the benefit of calculations. (30) 

Thus, it is proclaimed in the **Shiromani** <note>referring to the *Siddhanta Shiromani*, a masterpiece by the 12th-century astronomer Bhaskara II</note> that the result of the corrected sunrise is beyond doubt. (31) 

->**The Movement of the Celestial Sphere**<-

The intersection of the **equator** <note>original: "nari-vritta"</note> and the diurnal circle is fixed at the beginning of the sign of Aries, which is the start of the fixed lunar mansion **Ashwini**. This point is joined with the Mean Sun. By this means, the uniform motion of the **equatorial circle** <note>original: "vishuvat"</note> and the diurnal circle completes one rotation of the Earth. However, this rotation does not return to the exact same spot for the observer because every planet moves slightly in its own diurnal circle. 

The rotation of the celestial sphere, measured in minutes of arc, contains a slight difference. When one rotation is completed, the Mean Sun has moved slightly forward in the **ecliptic** <note>original: "kranti-vritta"; the path the Sun appears to follow across the sky</note> from the start of Aries. Because the Sun has moved forward by a few minutes of its daily motion, the Earth must rotate a little bit more than one full circle to bring the Sun back to the horizon. (32) 

The time taken for this additional rotation, caused by the Sun's daily motion, is what defines the status of a <term>सावन दिवसत्वे</term> <note>Savana Divasatve: the nature of a civil day</note>. A single <term>सावनो दिवसस्त</term> <note>Savana Divasa: a civil day, measured from one sunrise to the next</note> consists of one full sidereal rotation of the Earth plus the time required to cover the Sun's daily motion. 

It should be noted here that as the Mean Sun moves, it does not move only during the night; rather, it is a continuous movement. Therefore, the difference in the Sun's position over one day is joined with the daily motion. This is the truth of the **daily motion in minutes** <note>original: "gati-kala"</note>. These time-degrees, which constitute the civil day, were described by the blessed Sun God himself in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>one of the foundational and most sacred texts of Indian astronomy</note>. (33) 

Furthermore, by proportion, one civil day is not merely one rotation, but a rotation plus the additional arc of the daily motion. This is the actual time it takes for the Mean Sun to return to the same position, accounting for its daily forward movement.

5

Regarding the term "revolutions" <note>original: "bhagana"</note> in a given time, during a single rotation, <unclear>it is not</unclear> <unclear>attained</unclear>... in that same period, however many rotations occur as determined by the mean motion of the civil days <note>original: "savana-madhya-gati"</note>, those rotations are grouped into the count of celestial revolutions. From this, the mean position of the planet <note>original: "madhyakosa"</note> is derived. If, in a certain period, the time of the Mean Sun is subtracted or added <note>the text discusses the mathematical "Equation of Time" adjustment</note>, the "Actual Principle" <note>original: "vastava-tattva"</note> is attained. Without this adjustment, the calculation remains "Apparent" <note>original: "avastava"</note>, where the planetary revolutions and the minutes of the arc of the Mean Sun are considered only in their basic, uncorrected form.

When the Mean Sun's position is calculated through these "Actual Principles" at the moment of sunrise, it is called the **True Civil Day** <note>original: "vastava-savana"</note>. When it is calculated through the "Apparent Principles" using a simplified form, it is known as the **Mean Civil Day**. Through the use of mathematical proportions <note>original: "anupata"</note> applied to these mean civil measures, the actual state of time is established. 

If one asks, "Is there a contradiction in applying the correction for the difference in sunrises <note>term: <term>Udayantara</term> (the correction required because the Earth's orbit is elliptical and its axis is tilted, meaning sunrises don't happen at perfectly equal intervals)</note> to these apparent forms to reach the actual truth?" The answer is provided: even through that apparent form, by applying the correction for the difference in sunrises, the "Actual Principle" is indeed established for the daily calculation. Thus, for all calculations of the "Actual," there is a relationship between the planetary positions, the count of revolutions, and the minutes of the Mean Sun.

In the "Apparent" method, the revolutions and minutes are taken as single, uniform values. Here, the "Actual" enters into a relationship with the "Apparent" at the time of the Earth's rotation. One must examine which measure is being used—whether it is the solar correction or the terrestrial rotation—to avoid confusion in the calculation of the **Sidereal Day-Count** <note>original: "nirayana-ahargana"</note>. The difference between the minutes of the **Tropical Sun** <note>original: "sayana-arka"</note> and the mean motion is what defines the correction for the difference in sunrises. To ignore this would be a mistake. 

Because of the differences between the "Actual" and the "Apparent," a slight "Interval of Sunrise" <note>original: "udayantara"</note> is conceived. Through this, the "Actual Day-Count" is established for anyone seeking accuracy. These principles of solar and lunar motion are established because the Sun and Moon move along the **Ecliptic** <note>original: "krantivritta"</note> with their own specific velocities. 

Whether one uses the solar degree-motion or the lunar day-count, the mathematical proportion leads to the "Actual" result at the desired time. However, the rotation of the celestial sphere occurs day and night; therefore, the proportion must be adjusted to the reality of the Earth's sphere. This is how, through the use of **Intercalary Months** <note>original: "adhika-masa"</note> and **Omitted Days** <note>term: <term>Avama</term> (days "lost" in the calendar to keep the lunar months aligned with the solar year)</note>, the day-count is regulated. The remainder of these omitted days is what allows us to calculate the precise position of the Mean Sun at the moment of sunrise. Even if there is a perceived difference between the two methods (the True and the Apparent) at a single moment, they are both used to resolve the contradictions in the passage of time.

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the mathematical accuracy of the day-count (Ahargana). In this context, and regarding solar and other measures, the following must be understood: because **mean measures** <note>original: "madhyama-mana"</note> are not physically generated by themselves, their "actual" reality <note>original: "vastava-sattva"</note> is established through mathematical proportion <note>original: "anupata"</note>. The civil measures <note>original: "savana"</note> and others are considered "theoretical" <note>original: "avastava"</note> during the time of the sacrificial session <note>original: "satra-kale"</note>. Therefore:

That which is the **True Position** <note>original: "sphuta"</note>, calculated at the moment of the **Mean Sun’s rise** for one's own location, is the established **Day-Count** <note>term: <term>Ahargana</term> (the total count of days elapsed from the beginning of the cosmic epoch, used to calculate planetary positions)</note>. This is proven to be "Actual" <note>original: "vastava"</note> through the logic of its origin at the Mean Sun’s rise. Indeed, a day-count derived from an uncorrected "Theoretical" mean civil measure is not seen to be perfectly whole at the moment of sunrise. How then can it be considered "Actual"? Just as the remainder of the Mean Sun's division <note>referring to mathematical remainders in division</note> is handled, so too is this.

Now, if the calculation for the **True Day-Count** results in a fraction <note>original: "savayavah"</note>, how can it be "Actual"? When a planet is observed to be whole <note>at a specific integer day</note>, how can it be accepted that it is merely "near" the time of sunrise? This is why it is called "Mean" <note>original: "madhyama"</note>. If it is only "near" sunrise, then how do we determine the day of the week or the planetary count? **34**

Furthermore, there is a confusion <note>original: "bhramti"</note> here: Does the Solar Mean Sun align with the sunrise when the **Equation of Time** <note>term: <term>Udayantara</term> (the correction required to account for the difference between the uniform "Mean Sun" and the observable "True Sun" due to the Earth's orbital eccentricity and tilt)</note> is applied? Moreover, the **True Sun** <note>original: "spashta-ravi"</note> is calculated by subtracting the result of the mean motion from the sunrise. This rationale for the "correction" <note>original: "phala-vasana"</note> has been established by me previously. In that doctrine, because the celestial circle <note>original: "pratimandala"</note> moves at a fixed rate, and the true position is derived from the alignment of the orb's center, the "Actual" measure of the two planets <note>likely referring to the Sun and Moon</note> is stated. In that measurement, even the **Equation of Time** is established through different standards <note>original: "bhinna-pramana"</note> depending on the proximity to the moment of sunrise. **35**

Furthermore, if the today's and tomorrow's **Mean Suns** are not corrected, the difference in their motion-minutes would be significant. By applying the correction for the difference in sunrises <note>Udayantara</note>, the center of the Sun’s orb aligns within the 360 degrees of the year. **36**

In one day of the gods <note>a "divine day" or a specific solar cycle</note>, as many solar civil days as there are in a Kalpa, so many are the revolutions of the planets. <margin>:</margin> Even the rotations of the planetary groups are the same. Thus, how can the day-count calculated from the Mean Sun at its time of setting or rising be considered "Actual" or "Theoretical" by those with a pure intellect? The **Mean Sun** remains equal in its motion. Without the variation in the motion of the "Fire of Time" <note>original: "kalagni," a poetic reference to the sun's path</note>, one cannot speak of such movement. The completion of a civil day is always established through the calculation of the Mean Sun alone. **4**

From all of this, the assumption that the planetary positions are established at the local sunrise is a misconception. This is understood by those who are experts in the **Principles of the Sphere** <note>term: <term>Golattattva</term> (the science of spherical astronomy and the celestial globe)</note>. **5**

Therefore, it appears that applying the **Equation of Time** <note>Udayantara</note> correction to the current day-count derived from mean civil days is improper. This is a point that should be deeply contemplated by those who are experts in the True and Mean positions. Because of a lack of understanding of the "Actual" status <note>original: "vastu-sthiti"</note>, an "Inactual" <note>unreal</note> quality has been attributed to the day-count by those who are confused.

Even with great pride and effort, if one follows the path of **Bhaskaracharya** <note>Bhaskara II (1114–1185), a preeminent Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> without considering the contradictions with the ancient teachings of the divine sages, such a person is excluded from the true consideration of the **Celestial Sphere** <note>original: "gola-vasana-vichara"</note>. This interpretation, though accepted by some learned men of the past through the cycles of the ages, is now viewed by modern scholars as a path for the deluded. It should not be respected by anyone, for it creates a conflict with the world.

From the beginning of the current cosmic day, for the sake of determining the results of the **Yugas** <note>the four world ages</note> and **Manvantaras** <note>vast periods of time overseen by a Manu</note>, the calculation must be clear. It should not be done by just anyone; the determination of time is established by the authority of the **Solar Sciences** <note>original: "arka-shastra"</note>. 

The count of **Solar Years** <note>term: <term>Sauravatsara</term> (the time it takes for the Sun to return to the same point in the zodiac)</note> that have passed since the beginning of the **Day of Brahma** <note>term: <term>Kalpa</term> (a period of 4.32 billion years)</note> are as follows: the remainders and the full revolutions of the planets are calculated as **1,453,020,000** <note>original: "१४५३०२००००"</note>. In this system, one must calculate the positions of the Sun and Moon, along with the fixed stars, to determine the exact time. 

Twelve revolutions of the Sun comprise the years, which, when combined with the current zodiac signs, are divided by sixty to find the positions. When the Sun enters a new sign—the **Solar Transition** <note>term: <term>Sankranti</term></note>—beginning with Aries, it marks the solar month. However, the lunar months like Chaitra do not always align perfectly with these transitions. The great sages have taught that: 

When a lunar month occurs without a Solar Transition, it is called an **Intercalary Month** <note>term: <term>Adhimasa</term> (an extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons)</note>. Conversely, if a month contains two transitions, it is a "decayed" month. Because of the speed of the planets, an intercalary month must necessarily occur before and after certain periods. In all these matters, one must rely on the eternal tradition <note>original: "sanatana-smriti"</note> rather than modern innovations that contradict the scriptures. 

If the current scientific methods are used to determine the start of months like Karttika, the results will not be consistent with the sacred sciences. Therefore, the glorious Lord of the Universe has established the movement of the stars in a uniform manner. Through the instructions of the **Guru** <note>spiritual and technical teacher</note>, we understand that the celestial globe, which is the heavens, moves in a way that creates the divisions of time.

The vast number representing the cosmic scale is: **187,120,865,000,000,000** <note>This represents a traditional calculation of the "circumference of the sky" or the distance traveled by light/planets in a specific cosmic cycle</note>. This constant movement occurs because of the **Sphere of the Wind** <note>term: <term>Pravaha-vayu</term> (the celestial force in Hindu cosmology believed to drive the rotation of the planets and stars westward)</note>. This wind moves westward, carrying the planets with it.

By the power of God, the planets remain fixed in their stations at the **Celestial Poles** <note>original: "dhruva-sthana"</note>. From the position of the equinox at Aries, the seventh sign marks the opposite point. The circles of day and night are known by their technical measures, extending from the northern station of the gods to the southern station of the departed ancestors.

# The Structure of the Star-Sphere

In this manner, there is a method where the two poles <note>The North and South celestial poles</note> are positioned. The axis of **Mount Meru** <note>The central axis of the world in ancient Indian cosmology</note> is established at ninety degrees. Within the **Ecliptic Circle** <note>term: <term>Kranti-mandala</term> (the apparent path of the Sun)</note>, both the **Equator** <note>term: <term>Vishuvat</term></note> and the **Ecliptic** are fixed with the twelve zodiac signs—Aries and the rest—along with the fixed stars.

The twenty-seven divisions <note>term: <term>Nakshatras</term> (Lunar Mansions)</note> are also marked upon the **Ecliptic Circle**. Within the hollow of this sphere, there is the **Star-Sphere** <note>original: "Bhagola"</note>. This sphere is fixed, yet it is said to move from the center of the poles by thirty degrees.

### On the Shifting of the Zodiac

According to the sacred teachings, after twenty-seven parts of the rotation, the movement proceeds from the center of the poles. In the mouth of this circle, the twelve signs—Aries and the rest—are placed within the moving sphere for the purpose of calculating astrological results. The stars, beginning with **Ashvini** <note>The first Nakshatra</note>, are traditionally known as the fixed ones. However, the Star-Sphere itself shifts due to the power of the cosmic winds <note>term: <term>Pravaha-vayu</term> (the celestial wind that drives the stars)</note>.

Because of this movement, the point of the **Solstice** <note>original: "Ayana"</note> shifts from its original position. Those with "poor intellect" <note>original: "ku-buddhayah"</note> become confused about the center and the motion of the poles. There is much debate among the learned regarding the position of the North Pole <note>original: "Dhruva"</note> and whether it remains stable while the sphere rotates around it.

## The Arrangement of the Heavenly Orbs

As stated in the **Solar Sciences** <note>original: "Saurashastrokti", referring to the Surya Siddhanta</note>, the visibility of the stars depends on one's position on Earth. One must understand the fixed nature of the poles and the shifting of the Star-Sphere. 

Now, we shall describe the planetary spheres that reside within it. These spheres are arranged according to their respective heights, starting from the outermost and moving inward toward the Earth:

*   **Saturn** <note>original: "Shani"</note> — the highest and slowest
*   **Jupiter** <note>original: "Guru"</note>
*   **Mars** <note>original: "Bhauma"</note>
*   **The Sun** <note>original: "Ravi"</note>
*   **Venus** <note>original: "Shukra"</note>
*   **The Moon** <note>original: "Indu"</note> — the nearest to Earth

The Great Sphere of the Sky <note>term: <term>Akasha-gola</term></note> contains these massive bodies. Their order from highest to lowest is clearly established: Saturn is the first, followed by Jupiter, then Mars, then the Sun. Below the Sun is Venus, followed by the Moon. <gloss>Then</gloss> <margin>the</margin>...

<summary>This page describes the geometry of the Star-Sphere (Bhagola), explaining the relationship between the fixed zodiac and the shifting equinoxes (precession). It concludes by listing the traditional geocentric order of the planets from Saturn down to the Moon.</summary>

It references the school of Aryabhata to discuss—and largely refute—the idea of a rotating Earth, favoring a stationary Earth around which the spheres revolve.</meta>

...produced by the perfected **Vidyadharas** <note>Celestial beings in Vedic mythology known for their knowledge</note> and others. (47) These spheres of the sky are always and everywhere the foundations of the natural unions. When the connected sphere moves upward by its own motion at the pole, (48) a downward motion is produced from that. That which moves upward does not move by its own inherent speed; rather, it is situated below and moves in opposition to the previous one. (49) On account of this movement, there is no contradiction between the two motions. Thus, the motion of the upper sphere is driven by its own nature. (50) But the lower one does not move by its own speed because of the obstruction of that motion. The **Star-Sphere** <note>original: "Bhagola"</note>, which is the third, is set in motion in its entirety by a driving force. (51) When that sphere is moved, the various observations are made. Those spheres of the sky are also considered to be moved by the rotation of the heavens. (52) Because of their mutual interaction in the sky, observations and calculations are performed. Through these, one’s own sphere is established in its proper place. (53) Moving from the earth's surface to the limit of the upper path, the outer sphere is strong in its half-rotation, while the inner one is not. (54) The world of men is made of such great magnitude; therefore, the eternal ones <note>The fixed stars or gods</note> are worshipped there. (55) Without the wind, there is no movement; by its own power, the **Pro-vector Wind** <note>term: <term>Pravaha-vayu</term> (the celestial wind that drives the planets from east to west)</note> causes the zodiac to rotate. Even the Star-Sphere does not reach its own stopping point without it. (56) The spheres do not move by their own power alone; they move solely to wander through the obstruction of the sky, or for their own wandering. (57) Whether the stars are fixed or moving westward, or moving eastward—at the limit of the obstruction of the two spheres—the answer is given here according to the words of **Aryabhata** <note>original: "Bhatoktaih"; referring to the famous 5th-century astronomer Aryabhata who famously proposed the Earth's rotation</note>. (58) Just as a heavy object at the top of a house remains in its own place and does not fall when the night passes, so it is in this determination. (59) The idea that the Earth moves is considered incorrect by the noble teachers, because on the Earth, the connection to the poles is not seen to rotate. (60) In this way, Time and the true spheres are explained. The sphere is the foundation; the Star-Sphere is the second; and the third is called the **Pro-vector**. (61) Beyond the Pro-vector wind, there are the excellent and fixed winds. Some spheres are covered by clouds, while others are free from them. (62) Just as the **Atmosphere** <note>term: <term>Bhu-vayu</term> (the 'earth-wind' or terrestrial atmosphere)</note> has its particularities, so too are the winds of the Pro-vector born. These are called winds, though they are clearer than the earthly air because they originate from the pure sky. (63) Because of their density or thinness, these spheres have various individual motions. These winds, which are settled in the sky, are different from the lower air. (64) Though different by nature, they are all perceived as forms dwelling in their own places. They wander by their own motion and by the motion of others, even with their differences. (65) The rays of the sun are not obstructed by anything in the sky. Near the sun, the intensity of the rays is great. (66) Because of the smallness of the distance, there is a reflection from that. Thus, the illumination of the sky extends to the limit of the spheres. (67) All the spheres are contained within the **Egg of Brahma** <note>term: <term>Brahmanda</term> (the Universe/Cosmos)</note>, O wise one. The Star-Sphere is not made of darkness, yet it appears as such in the fourth part. (68) Just as a thing that is clear cannot be seen if one is unable to bow to it, so the sky appears **blue** <note>original: "nila"</note> to those situated below, even though it is actually made of darkness. (69) The perception in the world that "the sky is blue" is an illusion. It is due to the depth of the connected spheres and the eye's inability to see beyond. (70) Their center is one; the center of all is seen as one. But for those whose center is different, there are seven spheres, and the great sky is even higher than them. (71)

It also provides a physical explanation for why the sky appears blue, attributing it to an optical illusion caused by the depth of the atmospheric spheres.</summary>

Having previously addressed the motion of the celestial spheres and the arguments against the Earth's rotation from the school of Aryabhata, the text now focuses on the "Bhugola" (Earth-sphere), its spherical shape, the nature of gravity, and the relative nature of "up" and "down" in a spherical cosmos.</meta>

# 53

...the rays of the sun, having come once, go [around] in seventy-one <note>likely referring to a cycle or measurement of time/distance</note>. Just as a wheel rotates by a certain force, so do the water and other elements move continuously in a cycle. (73) In the beginning of creation, **Brahma** <note>The creator deity in Hindu cosmology</note> manifested space; this physical sphere, shaped like a **wood-apple fruit** <note>original: "kapittha-phala"; a common analogy for the Earth's spherical shape</note>, is the support of all centers. (74) It is stationary; thus all the spheres are situated. In the middle of everything, that **Earth-sphere** <note>original: "bhugola"</note> stands in the void of space. (75) 

The supreme power is the sustainer; it is the very nature of support. It is not by effort that this physical form stays in place. (76) It is fixed there because of the fear of "infinite regress" <note>original: "anavastha"; a logical fallacy where one cause requires another indefinitely</note>. Because of its manifold nature, that power is called the **Divine** <note>original: "Deva"</note>. (77) Having first conceptualized this power of the Lord on the earth, those of poor intellect <note>original: "kubuddhayah"</note> believe that the Earth falls because of its **weight** <note>original: "gurutvat"; the quality of being heavy or gravity</note>. (78) 

Observing that a heavy object goes toward the Earth, they speak falsely about the motion of the Earth itself. (79) There is no absolute "up" or "down" on the Earth, nor is it uneven; the wise know that the spheres are always stable in the universe. (80) Just as a man travels from his own place for many leagues <note>original: "yojana"</note> upon the ground, he does not perceive the curvature. (81) Because of its roundness, the globe appears even; because humans are small in body, they see from their own positions in all directions. (82) Even though it is a sphere, they see it as a circular disk, like the back of a tortoise; and it is indeed described as such. (83)

The **Earth-sphere** <note>original: "bhugola"</note> is situated within the waters, yet it is not moved by the water; there is more water inside the globe and a small amount on the exterior, as known by the experts of the spheres. (85) From its rotation within the water, that which is inside the water does not come out; thus the Earth is fixed. (86) When the mass of the Earth is situated above the water like the back of a tortoise, the upper part is visible, and the lower part is submerged within the water. (88) 

One may see different colors or white areas <note>likely referring to the continents and oceans</note>, but the place is uniform throughout. (89) Because it is high, the surface appears level; for those who have gone to the shore of the circle, there is no unequal standing. (90) Now, the divisions of the Earth are being described: the seven lands and the seven oceans which are the support of all. (91) There are lands with divine herbs, and many collections of gems, and the great mountains. (92) 

In the center of the **Earth-sphere** <note>original: "bhugola"</note> goes **Mount Meru** <note>The sacred five-peaked mountain of Hindu, Jain, and Buddhist cosmology, considered the center of the physical and spiritual universes</note>, which emerges at the top. The gods and sages are situated there at the center. (93) To the outside are the **Asuras** <note>Beings often in opposition to the gods</note> who are supported there. (94) 

Regarding the locations on the Earth: **Yamakoti** <note>A legendary city 90 degrees east of Lanka</note> is in the East; **Romaka** <note>Often identified with Rome or a city 90 degrees west of Lanka</note> is in the West. (96) Below, at the antipodes, is **Siddhapura** <note>The city "of the perfected," 180 degrees from Lanka</note>; **Meru** is in the North and the South pole is opposite. Thus are the positions always established on the circumference of the Earth. (97) People always imagine themselves to be at the top; for those standing on the sphere, it is the same everywhere. (98) What is the purpose of this sphere? **Meru** and other places are marked by lines... the wise determine the directions of the North and South...

# 95. Division of the World

From this, it should be known by everyone that **Mount Meru** <note>original: "Meruḥ"; the sacred mountain at the center of the world according to traditional cosmology, here identified with the North Pole</note> is situated in the north. At the opposite side, in the south, lies the **Great Meru** <note>original: "Kumeru"; the South Pole</note> in the southern direction. (100) When the sun rises in **Lanka** <note>A city on the equator used as the prime meridian (0° longitude) in ancient Indian astronomy</note>, it is mid-day in **Yamakoti** <note>A legendary city 90 degrees east of Lanka</note>. At that same moment, it is sunset in **Siddhapura** <note>A city 180 degrees from Lanka, in the antipodes</note>. (1) In **Romaka** <note>A city 90 degrees west of Lanka, likely representing the Roman/Hellenistic world</note>, it is midnight. These regions are always situated thus. Because of the eastward rotation <note>The text here discusses the relative motion of the sun from the perspective of different longitudes</note>, (2) the experts in the **Sphere** <note>original: "Gola"; refers to spherical astronomy</note> determine the western directions. Due to the curvature of the Earth's surface, the regions to the east always experience the sun earlier. (3) 

Since Yamakoti is to the east, and **Avanti** <note>The city of Ujjain, a major center for Indian astronomy</note> is situated on the meridian, Lanka is to the south of Avanti. Experts determine the distance between these places based on the circumference of the circle. (4) Now, regarding the circumference of the globe: since the dwellers on the sphere are located everywhere, one must know the circumference and the diameter to calculate the results of their positions. (5) According to the calculation methods approved in the **Siddhantas** <note>original: "Siddhanta"; established astronomical treatises</note>, when the diameter is known, the circumference is determined by its proportion. (6) Because the circumference surrounds the sphere on all sides, it cannot be known without a standard measurement. (7) 

This method was obtained from the tradition of the ancients, increasing by the square of the word <note>likely referring to a mathematical formula</note>. According to the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>original: "Shri Surya"; the most authoritative ancient Indian astronomical text</note>, the calculation is done as follows: (8) The square of the **diameter** <term>vyasa|the width of the circle through its center</term> multiplied by ten, and then the square root of that result, is the **circumference** <term>paridhi|the distance around the circle</term>. To find the diameter from the circumference, one should divide the square of the circumference by ten and take the root. (9) 

Multiply by eleven (11) and divide by the prescribed number... <note>The text describes various approximations for the ratio of a circle's circumference to its diameter, similar to Pi (π)</note>. This continues until the circumference is equal to the measurement of the sphere. (10) By taking the square of the diameter, and applying the half-sum of the two results, one calculates the **Sine** <term>jya|a chord or trigonometric function</term> and the **Versine** <term>kramajya/utkramajya|specific trigonometric ratios used in ancient mapping</term>. (11) When the circumference is equal to that, the calculation of the Sine is considered constant. This has been famously established by many scholars through various methods. (12) 

For a circle with a diameter of one unit, the precise circumference is described by those who possess the light of knowledge. It is not a rough figure, but a subtle and exact value. (13) If the diameter is one, the division of the parts was written by the followers of **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II, the 12th-century mathematician</note>. By using the formula for the diameter, the remainder is seen there. (14) Using the parts—**8; 29, 45, 36** <note>A highly precise sexagesimal value for the ratio of the circumference to the diameter, approximately 3.14159</note>—the method spoken by Bhaskara is perfectly applied. This is the root form of the calculation. (15) 

Some scholars, in the calculation of the circle, assume the circumference to be **68036** <note>A specific measurement for the Earth's circumference in ancient units</note> as fixed in the **Surya Siddhanta**. (16) This was spoken by the Sun to the sage; for the sake of established astronomical truth, one should perform the division of the globe accordingly. (17) The square of the square of the diameter is multiplied by five (5) and divided by eight (8); the root of that gives the result. From that result, the diameter can be found in reverse. (18) Or, from the square of the square of the circumference, the root gives the result from which the circumference is found in reverse. (19) Whether the diameter is calculated in terms of the Earth's units or from the north, perform the calculation according to that position. (20) 

The numbers **3, 5, 4, 54, 12** are mentioned. The root of ten is spoken of here as the basis. The circumference is exactly the root of ten times the diameter. (21) When calculated with this diameter, the same result arises. The root obtained there is the value of **6766 / 13**. (22)

# 95. Division of the World (Continued)

If the square of the Earth's diameter is calculated, the resulting values are 52, 20, 54. <note>These represent technical astronomical coefficients used to calculate the Earth's dimensions.</note> The circumference is calculated in many ways. In some calculations, the circumference is not fixed, but is given as 2,600 **Yojanas** <note>original: "yojana"; a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles, though its value varied significantly between different texts</note>. Other calculations provide values like 5,059 or 3,800. (25)

Because of the differences in the measurement of a Yojana, the values for the Earth's size given in the **Puranas** <note>Ancient Hindu texts containing mythology and cosmology</note> and the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>original: "Sauragama"; a foundational Sanskrit treatise on astronomy</note> appear to vary. This discrepancy was discussed in the **Siddhanta Shiromani** <note>The "Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas," written by the 12th-century mathematician Bhaskara II</note> to show there is no real contradiction if one understands the scale used. (26) Those who do not understand this method of calculation might find fault with the dimensions of the Earth, but those who are not well-versed in the half-mathematical methods <note>original: "ganita prakāre"; refers to the specific logic of spherical trigonometry</note> cannot truly speak on this. (27)

The **Meridian** <note>original: "yāmyottarekhā"; literally "the south-north line"</note> is the circle that passes through the poles and the observer's location on the equator. Where this line intersects, it is known as the Prime Meridian, used to define the Earth's true circumference. (30) Thus, starting from **Mount Meru** <note>The North Pole</note>, these lines are drawn through various cities. These lines do not deviate; they are the fixed reference points for all locations. (31) Where the latitude is equal to the degrees of the arc, that determines the position of a city relative to the Meridian. (32)

The distance in Yojanas between the Prime Meridian and one's own location is called the **Longitude** <note>original: "deśāntara"; literally "the difference between places"</note>. (33) To the west lie cities like **Rome** <note>original: "Romaka"; likely referring to the edge of the Hellenistic/Byzantine world</note> and other directions. (34) The lines originating from the two poles <note>Meru and Kumeru</note> determine the longitudinal difference for any country. (35)

The following are the coordinates for various cities <note>The text provides two numbers for each: likely the distance from the prime meridian and the latitude</note>:

| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Amravati** | 108; 20 | 23; 21 |

(37) To find the difference in time <note>original: "gatikalāguṇa"; the multiplier for calculating the time difference</note> between the Meridian and your own city, one must use the mathematical rule of 360. (38) If the city is to the east, the correction is added; if to the west, it is subtracted. (39) By multiplying the longitudinal difference by ten, one arrives at the time difference in **Palas** <note>A unit of time equal to 24 seconds</note>. (40)

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of terrestrial and celestial coordinates from the previous page, specifically focusing on the calculation of longitude (deshantara) and the distinction between observations made from the Earth's center versus its surface.</meta>

<margin>
Principles of Truth <note>original: "Tattva-vi"; abbreviation for Siddhanta-Tattva-Viveka</note>
9
</margin>

When the true Sun <note>original: "sphutarka"</note> is calculated through the mathematics of planetary conjunctions, if it is found to be less than or greater than the observed position, the difference arises from the correction for the observer's location. (51) Various ancient teachers have described the calculation of the longitude <note>original: "deshantara"; the longitudinal distance between the prime meridian and the local station</note> in many ways. Through the method of the "Six-Eyes-Children" <note>original: "shat-netra-bala"; likely a mnemonic for a specific mathematical constant or group of scholars</note>, this is successfully determined. (411) These degrees are then adjusted for one's own country. (52) The scholars of the scriptures, such as those of the **Ratnamala** <note>A famous astrological-astronomical treatise by Sripati</note>, have described the locations along the prime meridian.

The town of **Rohtaka** <note>Modern-day Rohtak, historically considered to lie on the prime meridian of India which passed through Ujjain</note> is well known to be situated between two mountains, near a lake. In this way, others are also identified according to the measurement of **Yojanas** <note>original: "yojana"; a traditional unit of distance</note> prescribed in the scientific texts. The knowledge of longitude is determined as described in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>original: "Sauraukta"; refers to the teachings of the foundational text, the Surya Siddhanta</note>. When the observed lunar eclipse occurs later than the calculated time, the observer is to the west; if earlier, they are to the east of the prime meridian. (53) By using the difference in time—measured in **nadis** <note>A unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note>—and multiplying it by the local circumference of the Earth, then dividing by 60, one finds the longitude in Yojanas. This calculates the specific distance on the Earth's surface based on the clear circumference.

For a place located to the east of the meridian, the correction should be subtracted; for a place to the west, it should be added. This allows the observer to find the local planetary positions. (54) Those who are wise in the knowledge of the Earth understand that the observer stands upon the surface of the Earth <note>original: "ku-prishtha"; the literal surface</note>, while the mathematical center of the Earth <note>original: "bhu-garbha"; the 'womb' or center of the world-sphere</note> is where the foundational calculations are based. Because the observer and the center are separated by the Earth's radius, there is a difference in what is seen. (50) Just as a man standing on the surface sees the horizon differently than if he were at the center, the line of sight <note>original: "drik-sutra"</note> from the surface to the celestial sphere <note>original: "gola"</note> creates a shift.

An observer at the center of the Earth would see the planet rise and set exactly on the mathematical horizon. However, for one standing on the surface, the "visibility" is altered by the physical bulk of the Earth. (55) This is like a pot or a jar placed upon the ground; its height changes the perspective of what lies behind it. Therefore, the rising and setting of the Sun and planets are seen differently from the surface than from the center. (56) People say the planet rises when it reaches the horizon, but this horizon is defined by the observer's sight-line on the Earth's surface. The calculated mathematical horizon belongs to the Earth's center. Because of this difference in the "points of sight," there is a discrepancy in the observation. (57)

The observer always looks through the atmosphere, and the planet is seen at the end of the line of sight. This is never exactly the same as the mathematical calculation from the center. (58) Because of these varied points of vision, the wise must apply a correction for the Earth's surface <note>original: "ku-prishtha-phala"</note> to the planetary calculations to ensure the math matches the eyes. (59) Above the sphere of the Earth, the Earth-Air <note>original: "bhu-vayu"; the atmosphere</note> extends for many Yojanas. This air is said by some to be dense and always carries the clouds. (60)

<meta>This page continues the technical and cosmological discourse from the previous folio. ### Atmospheric Omens and Celestial Phenomena

From the interaction of the upper winds, various forms appear in the sky. When these winds encounter moisture, circular haloes <note>original: "kundala"; refers to atmospheric haloes around the sun or moon</note> are formed. These appear in many colors in the sky, much like **Indra’s Bow** <note>original: "Indra-chapa"; the traditional Sanskrit name for a rainbow</note>. It is said that when such a halo surrounds an army or a stream, it serves as a potent omen. 

Neither the Sun nor the Moon creates the "City of the Gandharvas" <note>original: "Gandharva-puram"; a technical term for a mirage or Fata Morgana, often viewed as an ill omen in Sanskrit literature</note>; these variegated celestial cities are illusory. Similarly, when **Ketus** <note>original: "Ketavo"; comets or meteors</note> appear, they bring various fruits—often adverse—to the world. When the rays of the Sun are scattered by the wind and moisture, they create these appearances.

### The Nature of Motion and the Earth

Scientists observe that dust and particles are carried upwards by the force of the wind. However, just as a weapon thrown into the sky eventually returns, so too do well-rounded, heavy objects fall back to the Earth. 

Moving through the sky is possible for light things due to the power of the wind, but the Earth is solid and possesses a natural strength <note>This passage reflects early Indian physical theories regarding gravity and the "solidity" of the earth as a point of rest</note>. Just as sound travels and spreads across the Earth, the Earth itself provides the foundation for all motion. It is for this reason that movement on the Earth is accomplished without the same struggle as moving through the heavy atmosphere. This is the constant decision of the heavens, reaching even to the mountains.

### On Omens and the Great Plague

When the seasons are disrupted and heat or rain occurs out of its proper time, or when planetary alignments are malefic, a great death occurs among the people. This is known by the name **Mahamari** <note>original: "Mahamari"; literally 'The Great Killer,' referring to a plague or pestilence</note>. 

If an eclipse <note>original: "grahana"</note> or a planetary conjunction occurs at the wrong time—outside the calculated period—or if the colors of the planets appear distorted (such as the Sun appearing toothed or grey), it foretells the destruction of leaders and the loss of crops. The Lord of Time <note>original: "Kala-jnye"; the expert in time/astronomer</note> must understand these omens.

### Calculations of the Cosmic Sphere

The extent of the universe is described in detail in other scriptures, but here we summarize the motion of the planets governed by Time. In the **Kalpa of Creation** <note>original: "Srishti-Kalpa"; a vast cycle of time lasting 4.32 billion years</note>, the measurements are given in **Yojanas** <note>original: "yojana"; a unit of distance roughly equal to 8 or 9 miles</note>. 

The circumference of the Great Sphere of Brahma <note>original: "Brahma-gola"</note> is calculated through the following proportions:

| Constant Type | Value/Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Lunar Constant** | 315,000 |
| **Planetary Cycle** | 1,059,205 |
| **Solar Revolution** | 1,654,630 |

The motion of the planets <note>original: "grahanogatih"</note> occurs daily through these Yojanas. This motion is divided into hours <note>original: "Hora"</note> and days to determine the exact position. The celestial sphere is situated in the middle of the white/clear sky. By calculating the revolutions <note>original: "bhagana"</note> and the distance covered, the scholar determines the path of the Sun and other heavenly bodies. 

Through the mathematics of the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>original: "Arka-shastra"</note>, the exact timing of eclipses and the movement of the lunar nodes are established. The mean motion <note>original: "madhya-gatya"</note> of the planets serves as the basis for all astrological and astronomical rites.

### The Dimensions of the Planetary Orbits

The revolutions and distances of the planets are calculated here. For **Mars** <note>original: "Kuja"</note>, the distance is stated as 5,333,500 <note>This refers to revolutions in a Kalpa or a specific large-scale orbital calculation</note>. Similarly, for **Jupiter** <note>original: "Guru"</note>, the figure is given as 117,688,255. For **Saturn** <note>original: "Shani"</note>, and then for the **Sun** <note>original: "Bhanu"</note>, the distance of the "sharp-rayed one" is 159,890,000 **yojanas** <term>yojana: an ancient Indian unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles</term>. These planets wander in their respective circles <note>original: "mandale"</note> above one another.

This is the **Sphere of Universal Origin** <note>original: "loka-utpatti-golakah"</note>. For a planet situated higher up, the **orbit** <term>kaksha: the path or "vessel" of a planet's motion</term> is large. Because of this large orbit, a planet moves through the signs of the zodiac over a long period of time. Conversely, a planet in a small orbit moves through the signs quickly. Thus, the Sun, moving in its great orbit, appears to move slowly, but it is actually covering its distance in its own time. The speed of the planets, when calculated in yojanas, is converted into **minutes of arc** <term>kala: 1/60th of a degree</term>, which allows us to understand their varying motions.

### Planetary Diameters and Distances

The following are the distances in yojanas as heard in the traditional texts for the middle orbits. For the **Moon** <note>original: "Vidhu"</note>, it is 59,295 yojanas. For the **Sun**, the distance is 1,645,354. For **Mercury** <note>original: "Shashija"</note>, it is 6,824,900 yojanas.

<margin>41</margin>

For **Jupiter** <note>original: "Vachaspati"</note>, the distance is 1,298,139. For **Saturn**, it is 8,133,121. For **Venus** <note>original: "Bhargava"</note>, it is 2,016,135 yojanas. And for the **Sun**, it is 41,052,200 yojanas. 

Indeed, the spheres of the planetary bodies are fixed in their respective celestial shells. However, the diameters of these planetary bodies in yojanas, as stated by the gods and sages, are as follows:
*   For the **Moon**, the diameter is 6,500 yojanas.

### Maximum Celestial Latitudes

The **deflection** <term>vikshepa: celestial latitude, or the distance a planet moves north or south of the ecliptic</term> of the planetary orbits is also described. 
*   For **Mars**, the maximum deflection is 480 minutes and 48 seconds <note>480' 48''</note>.
*   For the **Mercury**, it is 144 minutes and 54 seconds <note>144' 54''</note>.
*   For **Jupiter**, it is 831 minutes and 45 seconds <note>831' 45''</note>.

<margin>52</margin>

*   For **Saturn**, the measurement is 483 minutes and 17 seconds <note>483' 17''</note>.
*   For **Venus**, it is 1,477 minutes and 6 seconds <note>1477' 6''</note>.

These values for deflection in yojanas are consistent with the standards of the Sun's motion. These measurements for Mars and the others, as spoken by the sages, are not merely imagined but are observed through instruments. Because of the difference in the observers' locations on Earth, these values must be carefully calculated by the learned to reflect the true state of the heavens. 

These deflections for Mars and others are multiplied by the correct factors and applied to their respective **hypotenuses** <term>karna: in astronomy, the distance from the center of the earth to the planet</term> to find the true deflection in the lunar sphere. These methods were described in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>A foundational Sanskrit text on astronomy</note> for Mars and the other planets in order.
