### Determining the True Diameters of the Planets

...calculated as 55; the completion is 2130. When these are converted into minutes of arc <term>kala: 1/60th of a degree</term> in the sky, one should determine the direction of the **true Sun** <note>original: "sphutarkan"</note>.

**Verse 56-57:** To correctly determine the **diameters of the disks** <term>vimba: the visible disk of a celestial body</term> of **Mars** <note>original: "Bhauma"</note> and the other planets, one must use the distances <term>karna: literally "hypotenuse," referring to the distance between the earth and the planet</term> and the diameter values. By using the sum of these distances, the **true distance** <note>original: "sphuta-karna"</note> is found. It is said that the planet moves at the head of this distance.

**Verse 58:** Through the combination of these results, one achieves the calculation of the positions. The determination of the **true distance** <note>original: "sphuta-karna"</note> is thus established for the **Sun** <note>original: "Arka"</note> and the **Moon** <note>original: "Chandra"</note>.

**Verse 59:** From the **mean distance in yojanas** <term>madhyama-yojana-karna: the average distance measured in yojanas</term>, and the **standard radius** <term>trijya: the radius of the reference circle, often 3438 arcminutes</term>, the true dimensions in yojanas are derived. Thus, from the true distance, the standard radius is used to calculate the mean dimensions.

**Verse 60:** The diameters of the disks are produced from this. The explanation of the diameters of the disks for **Mars** <note>original: "Bhauma"</note> and the others has been illustrated in this manner.

### The Mathematics of Angular Diameters

To clearly understand the **diameter of the sphere of the disk** <note>original: "vimba-gola-vyasa"</note> in both yojanas and arcminutes, the solar astronomers use a specific method. Here, by using the combination of distances, the **true distances** are found. Generally, the mean distance in yojanas is considered in relation to the standard radius.

As it is stated: if at the true distance the diameter in yojanas is known, then what would the diameter be in arcminutes <note>original: "vimba-kala"</note>? In this calculation, the **standard radius** <note>original: "trijya"</note> (in arcminutes) is the multiplier, and the **mean distance in yojanas** is the divisor. By this method, the measurement of the disk situated at the distance of the Sun's orbit is determined.

The same method for calculating the Sun's disk is stated elsewhere. For the planets, one should divide the **true distance** <note>original: "sphuta-karna"</note> by the **standard radius** <note>original: "vyasardha"</note>. The result gives the corrected diameter in yojanas. To find the diameter of the planet's disk in arcminutes, one multiplies the planet's physical diameter by the standard radius and divides by the true distance. In these calculations, the term "distance" <note>original: "karna"</note> is used according to the schools of **Bhāskara** and others.

# Chapter 11: The Investigation of the Celestial Spheres

576 | 48  S. 3 • 57 47 68 5 | 12  S. Ni. 10 54 62 20  S. U. 22 41 50 55 ||

**Verse 61:** Regarding the higher states of the celestial spheres <term>gola: the celestial globe or armillary sphere</term>, the ancient masters have explained how they are perceived through the eyes of the great astronomers, such as **Bhāskara** <note>original: "Bhaskaraprabhritibhir," referring to Bhāskara II, author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>, and others who relied on their own observations and the movements of the planets.

**Verse 62:** If one considers the distances in **yojanas** <term>yojana: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> as taught by the Sages to be contradictory to observation, then the calculation of the orbit's path for the planets would be hindered. However, those distances are consistent when viewed through the lens of their specific paths.

**Verse 63:** The **Lord of the Planets** <note>original: "Khechareshvara," referring to the Sun</note>, when situated in its own orbit, is always positioned high. Here, there is no contradiction in its being high, as this is established through logical demonstration <term>vasana: the underlying rationale or proof of an astronomical theory</term>.

**Verse 64:** Those spheres that consist of the planetary disks <term>vimba-gola: the physical sphere of the planet's visible disk</term> are situated somewhat externally or internally due to the obstruction of the celestial hosts. For instance, **Mars** <note>original: "Bhauma"</note> and the **Moon** <note>original: "Soma"</note> are seen in a specific order because of these obstructions.

**Verse 65:** These disks are fixed within their respective spheres, starting with the Moon and moving outward. Even though the spheres rotate and move amongst one another, the planets remain fixed in their designated positions. <meta>marginal numeral 5 at end of line</meta>

**Verse 66:** Or perhaps at all times, the Sun remains at the center. Even if there is a single path of motion, how could the spheres of the planetary disks be fixed in an elevated position? This should be understood clearly.

**Verse 67:** The sages in this world have described the spheres of the disks as being mixed with Earth-elements <note>original: "mridbhaga," implying physical materiality</note>. They are not contradictory, but due to the visual obstruction of the earth <note>original: "kshiti-rodhat"</note>, the observer's sight may be diverted.

**Verse 68:** Because of the confusion in the knowledge of these spheres, many have claimed there is no difference between the various "clear" positions <term>sphuta: the "true" or "corrected" position of a planet</term>. Furthermore, even when a planet is situated at its lowest point <term>nica: perigee</term>, it may appear otherwise.

**Verse 69:** It is a great wonder that the positions of **Mercury** <note>original: "Bhruguja," the son of Bhrigu/Venus, though "Bodha" usually refers to Mercury; here implying the inner planets</note> and **Venus** are so unique. At certain times, their movements are such that they appear to switch their relative heights.

**Verse 70:** This "up-and-down" movement <note>inverse vertical order</note> appears to be entirely contrary to the opinions of the ancient Sages <term>rishi</term>. Generally, those who are deluded by their own attachments are deceived by such illusions of movement.

**Verse 71:** Since the quality of "light" <note>original: "tejasatve"</note> is accepted by those who follow the path of **Venus** <note>original: "Bhryu-ge," likely referring to the school of Bhrigu</note>, this theory should be established through the logic of the **spheres** <term>gola</term>.

**Verse 72:** If the Sun is always above, then there is a loss of brilliance for the two planets below it <note>Mercury and Venus</note>. The majesty of the Sun does not carry over in the same way in the minds of those who do not understand this distance.

**Verse 73:** Some say that the Creator <note>original: "Dhatra"</note> fashioned the blue sky as a physical sphere, hard as iron, supported by the winds and the stars, extending to the south and north.

**Verse 74:** Within this sphere, wise men fix the circumference. This circumference is known as the **Equatorial Circle** <term>vishuvad-mandala: the celestial equator</term> in the middle of the world.

**Verse 75:** Between the poles, in the middle of the north and south, and fixed above and below, lies the **Ecliptic** <term>kranti-mandala: the "circle of declination" or the sun's path</term> at an angle of 24 degrees <note>original: "jinonshair," where Jina represents the number 24</note>.

**Verse 76:** In this circle, a needle or pin made of iron should be fixed. Using threads and various supports, the clever astronomer creates a model that represents the measure of the entire universe.

**Verse 77:** The divisions of the **Equator** <note>original: "Vishuvat"</note> and the **Ecliptic** <note>original: "Kranti-vritta"</note> contain the signs of the zodiac, starting with **Aries** <note>original: "Mesha"</note>. Thus, the layout of the signs is established on the ecliptic.

**Verse 78:** Now, regarding the arrangement of the spheres: seven spheres are accepted, one for each planet. They are arranged in the form of a "Great Torment" <note>original: "Mahapida," likely referring to a complex mechanical linkage</note> on a single axis.

**Verse 79:** **Saturn** <note>original: "Shanishchara"</note> and the others were created in a sequence based on their distance from the center. Having fixed these, the astronomer uses an iron sphere to show the path leading toward the ultimate goal.

It describes the construction of a Gola-yantra (armillary sphere) using iron and threads to model the Equator, Ecliptic, and the relative positions of the seven planets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse from Chapter 11, specifically focusing on the physical construction of an armillary sphere—an "iron globe"—and its limitations in representing the actual celestial movements. It transitions from the previous discussion on planetary distances to the mechanics of astronomical instruments versus the reality of the cosmos.</meta>

...the iron produced from various alloys, by which the middle of the path is achieved. This is oriented toward the west. Even so, it is as described in verse 80: regarding the form of the sphere, which is a singular entity. This is what was stated by **Brahma** and **Arka** <note>original: "Brahmarka," referring to the divine source of the Surya Siddhanta and the creator god</note> and the ancient sages, though some interpret it out of confusion. **[80-81]**

The rotation is not accomplished simply through the iron; rather, the iron globe <term>loha-golo: a physical armillary sphere made of iron or metal for astronomical demonstration</term> is accepted by those who have spoken through the liberated texts. **[82]**

It is well-known that the iron is easy to work with and remains consistent; through it, one might grasp the life-force of the **Sun** <note>original: "Bhano," the Sun god</note> and the stability of the sovereign ruler of the stars. **[83]**

Even that is false, for it is well-known that such a thing is not seen in direct perception. Through the error of the directions, the practice of creating the form of the globe arises. **[84]**

The celestial sphere <term>akasha-golam: the vast, ethereal sphere of space</term> was created by the **Supreme Lord** <note>original: "Paresha," the High Lord or Creator</note>. Its nature is to rotate; therefore, what is the use of making one out of iron? What weight or significance does such a creation hold? **[85]**

Nor is there any iron among the stars here. If one argues that the rotation is forced through the use of force or mechanical means, that is not the way of the stars; this is not the explanation to be given here. **[86]**

Therefore, this iron instrument is distinct from the primary source. The rotation of the Sun's sphere is distinct from the physical support that you have constructed. **[87]**

Where is there a moving support for the **Kadamba** <term>kadamba: the pole of the ecliptic</term>? What is the cause of the rotation? The iron globes with their pins and circles are merely wonders without truth, as has been said. **[88]**

This sphere exists with all its cords and supports; it is made to rotate by the joining of iron rods and pins. However, this is not the case in reality. **[89]**

Regarding the Sun, there is always one celestial sphere <term>bhagola: the sphere of the fixed stars</term> which serves as the support of the light. It moves from the two **Kadambas** <note>original: "kadambakabhyam," the two poles of the ecliptic</note>; this is the established truth. **[90]**

Furthermore, in this iron instrument, as far as the iron pins are concerned, the rods that support the stars are joined together. From the **Pole Star** <term>dhruva: the celestial pole or the North Star</term> and others, they are fixed and made to rotate. **[91]**

Through the two **Kadambas**, situated on the line of the observer’s position, it is not seen by the eye; thus, the iron construction does not truly represent the wind or the water <note>original: "vayu-salila," referring to the subtle elements or cosmic winds like Pravaha</note> that move the heavens. **[92]**

The rotation is not truly seen through the demonstration; the celestial sphere you have described is superior. Thus, through such futile effort, the fruit of one’s labor is lost. **[93]**

By the connection to the **Pole**, the sphere is brought to the Pole. Because it is supported by the movement of the **Kadamba**, there is the common usage that "it moves." **[94]**

This iron globe was not brought into existence without the help of the alloy; it moves according to the Pole because of the **Pravaha wind** <term>pravaha: the great cosmic wind in Indian cosmology believed to drive the celestial bodies around the Earth</term>. **[95]**

It is not by the human attraction of the Pole that the globe moves; following the Pole, the spheres of the wandering stars <term>khechara-golakana: the spheres of the planets</term> move without effort because of the iron-like pins of divine law. **[96]**

Due to the following of the Pole, the rotation of the **Pravaha wind** is certainly not prevented, nor is it seen in that which is supported by it. Through this movement alone, the Pole becomes the support, and not through any mechanical attraction. **[97]**

The rotation of the **Pravaha wind** is well-known. Because it is not seen, its support is the Pole; this is known through the three methods of knowledge. **[98]**

Furthermore, in the sphere made of blue iron, the planetary spheres are bound together by the **Pravaha wind**. Through their movement, they are indeed connected. **[99]** By the iron circle and the two **Kadambas**...

<summary>This page presents a critique of using physical iron armillary spheres to represent the cosmos, arguing that these man-made instruments cannot truly capture the divine movement driven by the Pole Star and the cosmic Pravaha wind. The author emphasizes that while these models are useful for demonstration, the real celestial spheres are governed by subtle forces rather than mechanical pins and rods.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of using a physical iron armillary sphere to represent the cosmos. The previous page established that while metal models are useful for teaching, they are ultimately "false" representations because the heavens are not made of iron. This page specifically addresses and refutes theories that explain planetary motion through magnetic attraction or mechanical force.</meta>

<page-num>१५</page-num>

<margin>
Dispute
Special
12
<note>Handwritten numeral "12" in pencil is visible below the original marginalia.</note>
</margin>

Whether it be like a bird or a revolving machine, the motion of the planets is made clear through their connection to the sphere. Some claim that the man who understands the rotation of the sphere does not wander in confusion. **[1]** Through the power of **attraction** <note>original: "akarshana," here referring to a gravitational or magnetic-like pull</note>, a ball of blue iron moves by its own nature; through such attraction, the support of the atmosphere is achieved. **[1]** When the **Zodiac** <term>bhachakra: the "circle of stars" or the wheel of the constellations</term> is rotated by the movement of the iron, and the iron is held fast, then there is no fault in the rotation, as it is joined through mechanical means. **[2]**

Now, if a **magnet** <note>original: "chuvaka," a variant of chumbaka (loadstone)</note> is placed in the circle of the ecliptic, the iron would be drawn toward it. Even if it were at the tip, it would be pulled by that powerful force. **[3]** If this is how the blue half-sphere is drawn, then why is it not seen to be perfectly balanced in its own place through that same attraction? **[4]** All heavy iron objects fall downward by nature; in that same way, the theory of attraction in the half-sphere is established by the ancient teachers. **[5]**

Furthermore, if the upper sphere is great, it is unable to perform attraction by its own power. Therefore, it is said that the attraction of the planetary spheres is like that of a **magnet**. **[6]** Since the spheres do not move in exact alignment with the ecliptic, the attraction of these globes is like iron moving toward the face of a magnet, yet they do not truly collide. **[7]** Because they are dependent on others, their motion becomes irregular. Even if fixed in an iron cage, they would still follow their own path and stay within their designated signs of the zodiac. **[8]**

If both stability and motion are observed here, it is because of the various supports like the cosmic wind. It is said that the atmosphere serves as their higher support. **[9]** Just as the water in a jar moves when the jar is rotated, do the spheres of the sky not move because of the rotation of the wind? Their own motion is distinct. **[10]** Because the Earth is stable, such motion is impossible for it; the stars are fixed in the flow of the **cosmic wind** <term>pravaha-anila: the "provector" wind believed in ancient Indian astronomy to drive the celestial spheres</term>. Are the planets then moved by the gods? Are they propelled through the sky by divine power? **[11]**

The example of the wind's flow is clear, yet the sky itself is subtle. Here, there is no visible proof of the wind's permanence. **[12]** A magnet is a specific kind of iron; it is not simply the same as any iron. Therefore, the stars do not follow a path made of iron. **[13]** Here, because the nature of the magnet is absent, this theory of attraction is entirely **false** <note>original: "alikam," meaning unreal or a lie</note>. How could the whole universe be made of iron? **[14]** This idea has been used to deceive those of dull wit. In the world of the wise, such talk is inconsistent with the truth. This is the conclusion of the greatest experts of the spheres. **[15]**

Because the spheres are situated far away, the arrangement of the globes cannot be perfectly proven by those staying on Earth. Therefore, the teacher describes the example of a model made of metal. **[16]** With fine needles or rods, he made the globe stable in its own place, demonstrating the forward motion. **[17]** One must construct the **Zodiacal sphere** <note>original: "bhagolam"</note> and the support of the stars for the purpose of mathematical calculation and craftsmanship; for that, this method is considered correct. **[18]** The intersections of the South, North, East, and West circles, and where they meet—these points are called the **crossings** <term>svastika: the "Swastika" or auspicious intersection points of the celestial circles</term>, which are situated below. **[19]** For one who possesses the knowledge of these intersections, there is no error in calculation regarding the lunar days or the auspicious times...

It concludes by describing the proper construction of an armillary sphere for mathematical use, defining the "Svastika" as an astronomical intersection point.</summary>

Whichever parts are equal, let them be placed in the North [10]. Then, in that specific section, the **Pole mark** <term>dhruva-chihna: the fixed point representing the celestial pole</term> should be placed. In the South <note>original: "yaamye"</note>, it should be imagined in the same way. The axis <note>original: "kilaka," literally a pin or spike</note> is said to be there; it is visible to us as the Pole. **[11]**

The **Celestial Sphere** <term>bhagola</term> should be constructed with the two poles as the base. When the axis is placed through the center, the **Equinoctial Circle** <term>vishuvan-vritta: the celestial equator</term> is formed. With the calculated degrees, the North and South directions are established from the Pole. **[12]**

That sphere's **Meridian** <term>yaamyottara-mandala: the north-south circle</term>, or as the wise call it, the circle of the poles, is marked with the Pole sign. In the North direction, and here in the South direction, the motion is fixed. By these degrees, the Celestial Sphere, which has moved toward the East, is fixed upon its axis. **[13]**

From the two **Ecliptic Poles** <term>kadamba: the poles of the ecliptic, distinct from the celestial poles</term>, in that celestial circle—indeed, in the **Zodiac Circle** <term>rashi-vritta: the ecliptic</term>—the twelve signs are marked. These divisions are completed by the wise. **[20]**

All the circles of the two Ecliptic Poles should be made to follow the path of the planets <note>original: "shara," referring to celestial latitude</note>. This sphere of innumerable stars is joined to the Ecliptic Poles. In this way, because of the movement of the Pole, the stars follow their own course in the sky according to their location. **[25]**

Those who know the earth should construct these spheres with the calculated measurements. For the movement of the ancient degrees <note>original: "amshaka," referring to the precession of the equinoxes</note>, the motion of the Poles is always used. **[26]** Thus, the Celestial Sphere is situated within the motion of the Ecliptic Poles. The signs of the Zodiac in the Celestial Sphere are aligned in a straight line. **[27]**

Each is situated in its own sphere, revolving by its own motion. To understand the sphere, one should look through the three spheres. **[28]** This is done to grasp the reality of the objects, as previously stated by the wise. This concludes the **Spherical Observation** <term>gola-vedha: the method of using an armillary sphere for sighting</term>. The various distances created by the differences in direction, place, and time are calculated based on these positions. **[29]**

Through the movement of the middle and true spheres, the various wonderful motions are investigated. **[25]** <note>The numbering in the original manuscript jumps or repeats.</note> Through subtle calculation, all of these are known to be one, though they appear as many different parts to the observer through the use of the **Instruments** <term>yantra</term> during the solstices. **[30]**

In the middle of this, between the North and South solstices, some fools—out of worldly pride—speak of errors. They say the **Correction Seed** <term>bija: a mathematical correction applied to traditional astronomical formulas to align them with current observations</term> is not good because of the difference in time. **[31]**

Those who are steady and patient, even if they see a small discrepancy in the calculation, do not lose heart. They know that the "Seed" of correction is like the Veda—it is meant to bring light, not to burn the house down. **[31]** Thus, the rising and setting of the sun and the planets are calculated. By making three broad divisions, the internal differences are resolved. **[33]**

If the calculation is destroyed, then how can the light remain steady? That which is lost must be restored, or the calculation of daily motion <note>original: "bhukti"</note> will be coarse. **[34]** If it is kept steady, then knowledge flows. From this world, we gain knowledge of the unseen through these calculations. **[35]**

To achieve the eight-fold results, one must perform the calculations. This has always been seen by those who have the vision of truth. **[36]** Just as a mirror is used to see what must be seen, the one who is not a fool uses this system. The one who sees through the mind's eye sees nothing else. **[36]** This is known through the power of three; through the power of the Lord of Speech <note>original: "vagesha," likely referring to Brihaspati or the power of wisdom</note>, it becomes pure. Thus, by following the path, the obstacles are removed in order...

Through a subtle instrument <note>original: "sūkṣmāyakena"</note>, even a small change or reflection <note>original: "parāvartatva"</note> is observed. If the rotation were seen even slightly off, then within six months, the entire position would become detached. **[38]** Because of their purity, the rays are visible as if they have gone into the mountains. In that place where the rays fall by their own power, that region is reached by those living on earth. **[39]** There, the clear sun is seen. Even if it is hidden, the triple-reflection of the solar disc <term>arka-bimba: the visible disk of the sun</term> occurs. In the same way, the image of the sun travels after being reflected, appearing distinct in the water. **[40]** Just as a reflection is seen in a mirror, so it exists in water; though the scholars say the extent of the sun is vast, one should consider the clarity of the reflection carefully. **[41]** Generally, the collection of the sun’s rays creates a visual web; even on a wall, the sun's rays that have arrived seem to pass through, as perceived by the eyes. **[42]**

### ### Thus concludes the Chapter on Mean Motions in the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.

Because the movement of the celestial bodies is beyond the senses <note>original: "atīndriyatva"</note>, their true motions are described for the sake of determining good and bad results. This cannot be achieved without the "eye of knowledge," and therefore, mathematics <term>gaṇita</term> is the fundamental path. **[1]** Therefore, I shall now describe the methods for finding squares <term>varga</term>, roots <term>mūla</term>, and other operations as they are used in this context, for the benefit of those who wish to understand the calculations of the spheres. **[2]**

The square is said to be the product of two equal quantities. For numbers staying in various places, the square is the result of the multiplication of the digits. **[3]** In the multiplication of digits, the numbers are placed in their respective columns <note>original: "koṭya"</note>; from there, the operations are performed again by those who understand the methods. **[4]** The process of squaring continues until the square of all digits is achieved. Similarly, by reversing this process, the method for finding the root is easily understood. **[5]**

From the last place, moving upwards and downwards, the places are designated as "odd" <term>viṣama</term> and "even" <term>sama</term> in sequence. **[6]** Subtract the square of the root from the last odd place; then, the root of that is placed elsewhere. The remaining digits are divided by twice the root, and the square of the quotient is subtracted from the next odd place repeatedly. **[7]** This process continues until all the digits of the root are found. If the digits in the last place are exhausted, the root is considered pure. **[8]**

If the square cannot be perfectly divided, O learned ones, I will explain the method for finding the root of such non-square numbers. **[9]** In a non-square number <note>original: "avarga"</note>, one should multiply the remainder by a large square number to find a more precise root. **[10]** By this method, the root is increased; the wise remember that by dividing the result by the square root of the multiplier, a "subtle" <note>original: "sūkṣma," meaning precise or fractional</note> root is attained. **[11]** When the multiplier is small, the precision is less; as the multiplier increases, the precision of the root increases. Thus, the root of the remainder is found through this refined calculation. **[12]** By dividing the remainder by twice the approximate root, a clearer approximation is achieved. If the approximate root is used correctly, no error remains in the calculation. **[13]**

This has been explained in this book for the sake of mathematical clarity. However, if not used properly, the result will not be seen as superior. **[15]** In calculating the root from the product of two squares, or the sum of two squares... **[16]** if the digits are not correctly placed, the root will be faulty. The root should be extracted from its own place. **[17]** The square of sixty <note>original: "ṣaṣṭi-varga," 3600, central to the sexagesimal system used in Indian astronomy</note>...

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical discourse following the "Chapter on Mean Motions." Kamalākara is now explaining the extraction of square roots, specifically focusing on the problem of irrational numbers (non-squares) and why they cannot have an exact numerical root, even when using fractions. He references the great mathematicians Āryabhaṭa and Bhāskara to support his proofs.</meta>

From this, taking one for the sake of the **square root** <term>mūla: the "root" or base of a squared number</term>, if the remainder is one, it is multiplied by sixty <note>The Indian mathematical system often used a sexagesimal (base-60) system for fractions, similar to how we measure time or angles today.</note> and added to the next place. When divided by twice the root, 18 is obtained. This result, the root together with its fractional parts <term>sāvayava: literally "with limbs," referring to a number with fractional components</term>, is 6. When the fractional part is divided by sixty, the root is 18.

Now, regarding the **square** <term>varga: the product of a number multiplied by itself</term> of non-square quantities, there is a common delusion that they possess a discrete, identical root. This is a mistake found among the unreflective, and it is refuted by careful investigation. **[10]**

This was stated by the venerable masters Āryabhaṭa and Bhāskara... **[11]** Regarding the **root**, there is a distinction between that which is "unitary" and that which is "non-unitary." Those numbers whose roots are whole or unitary are called squares. Those which do not possess such a unitary root—such as 2, 3, 5, and so on—are non-squares. **[12]** Therefore, while a perfect square has a fixed root, a non-square has only an approximate root.

If one considers the root of 5 among the non-squares, the investigation involves two quantities: a quantity slightly less than 5 whose square is known, and a quantity greater than 5. In seeking the root, the square of any chosen quantity will always be more or less than 5. It is impossible for it to be exactly 5, because a non-square square cannot exist without a remainder.

Now, if it is argued that a "fractional" <note>original: "mitra," likely a scribal variant for "bhinna" (fraction)</term> root might exist: a fraction multiplied by itself remains a fraction. By this well-known mathematical logic, since a fraction multiplied by itself cannot lose its fractional nature to become a perfect whole, it is impossible for a non-square whole number to have an exact fractional root. Just as a number that does not divide another perfectly will not divide that number's square perfectly, it is certain that the square of a fractional number will always retain a fractional remainder. **[13]**

For example, when a division is performed and the result is 2 and 48 parts <note>2;48 in sexagesimal notation, or 2.8 in decimal</note>, the square of this fractional quantity is 7, 50, and 25 parts <note>7.84 in decimal</note>. If one takes the fractional quantity 2 and 45 parts, its square is 7, 56, and 9 parts. If one takes 2 and 50 parts <note>approximately 2.833</note>, its square is 8, 2, and 40 parts <note>8.04 in decimal</note>. Here, by taking fifty parts, the square exceeds 8. Therefore, by the logic of impossibility <note>original: "anupapatti"</note>, even if one continues to take increasingly subtle and microscopic fractional parts in a series, the square will always be slightly less or slightly more than the target whole number; it will never be exactly equal to it. **[14]**

Therefore, no fractional quantity can be imagined which, when squared, perfectly equals a non-square whole number. If a fractional quantity's square could be a whole number, it would contradict the very nature of fractions. This rule regarding the squares of fractional quantities is a pillar of calculation...

<summary>Kamalākara discusses the mathematical nature of square roots, proving that non-square numbers (like 2, 3, 5, or 8) cannot have exact roots even when using infinite fractional approximations. He uses sexagesimal (base-60) calculations to demonstrate that the square of a fraction will never perfectly equal a whole number.</summary>

# Tattva-viveka, Page 14

...the loss of parts; in that case, by the multiplier of twice sixty, the result is obtained. By that, the **square** <term>varga</term> with its fractional parts is established. By the remainder of the sixty-fold division, it is combined. In the division of parts, whatever is produced by the fractional component, even if it is a small amount, is considered to have a fractional remainder. Therefore, it is certain that in the reduction of parts, it is appropriate to say they are "without parts" <term>niravayavatva: being a whole number without fractional remainders</term> only when the division is exact.

This is applied here for the sake of understanding the text: where a fractional divisor is greater than the dividend, its **square** will also be even greater <note>The author is explaining that squaring a proper fraction (where the denominator is larger than the numerator) results in an even smaller value, further increasing the gap between the divisor and dividend</note>. On this point of fractional nature, we have no disagreement.

Whether the **dividend** <term>bhājya</term> and **divisor** <term>hāraka</term> are **reduced** <term>apavartita: simplified by a common factor</term> or unreduced, the result of their division is well-known. Because of this, even for a reduced fraction with small parts, the square will not result in a whole number. If the **dividend** and **divisor** are not naturally **firm** <term>dṛḍha: reduced to their lowest terms, meaning they are coprime</term>, one must first make them **firm** by using a **common factor** <term>apavartaka</term> before squaring. In this case, there is no possibility of them becoming a whole number <note>Kamalākara is arguing that if a fraction is in its simplest form, its square will also be a fraction in its simplest form and can never be an integer.</note>.

This is the consideration: otherwise, if the **firm dividend** were to be squared and then divided by the **firm divisor**, and if that division were to be without remainder, then the **firm dividend** would have to share a common factor with the divisor. But that is impossible, as they were already defined as "firm" (reduced to lowest terms). If they were capable of further reduction, it would contradict their status as "firm" numbers. For example, consider a **firm dividend** made of parts; its **square** is investigated here. If it is not divisible without remainder by the **firm divisor**, then a remainder of one or two may exist. If the square of the **firm dividend** consisted of three parts, and each part were divided by the square of the **firm divisor**, the division would only be exact if the categories were identical.

If the first part is of the same category as the square of the **firm divisor**, it might be divided perfectly. Even if the second part were sometimes perfectly divided, the third part, which is the **square of the reduced remainder**, being smaller than the divisor, would fail to be divided. Thus, the **square** of a **firm dividend** consisting of three parts cannot be divided without remainder. If there were a remainder in the second part, then only if the sum of that remainder and the **square of the firm remainder** in the third part equaled the square of the **firm divisor** could the division be exact. However, such a detailed investigation shows that this is not the case. The square of a **firm remainder** is of the same category as the **firm divisor**... <note>The text here becomes highly technical, debating the modular arithmetic of remainders to prove that the square of a simplified fraction cannot suddenly become an integer.</note> because the square of the **firm divisor** is also of the same category as the **firm divisor**. Therefore, by this logic, the third part is imagined... the square of the **firm remainder** is equal to the square of the **firm divisor**...

<summary>Kamalākara provides a formal mathematical proof using the logic of "firm" (coprime) numbers to show that a reduced fraction, when squared, can never result in a whole number. This serves to prove that the square roots of non-square integers must be irrational/infinite fractions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on why fractional roots of non-square numbers are never perfect. # The Conclusion on Squares and the Geometric Proof

It is not merely that a measurement multiplied by ten million <note>The author uses "millions" to represent an infinitely large scaling of units</note> becomes something different; rather, the principle remains that the **square** <term>varga</term> of a measurement that is less than a full unit by some remainder does not magically become a whole number. This is the established conclusion <term>siddhānta</term>. When a measurement is multiplied by one or more units, it is proper to consider it in the context of its own kind. In the reflection of the method of squaring, it occurs that if the side <term>koṭi</term> of a measurement contains a remainder, its square is calculated based on that remainder.

If we consider the measure of a quantity of "hands" <note>The *hasta* or "hand" was a standard unit of length, roughly 18 inches</note>, the square produced from a remainder that is smaller than a clear "hand" is itself a squared value. The product of three squares is not a single square. <insert>×</insert> In this case, if one square is multiplied by another, the result is a second square of a different nature. For a chosen unit measurement, there is a multiplier. If there is a remainder in the clearly measured "hand," there is a possibility of **reduction** <term>apavartana: the simplification of a fraction by a common divisor</term> again with the root of the unit. The second multiplier of the square also leads to a possibility of reduction.

Therefore, because the nature of the remainders in the current case is established as having been reduced, and because of the difficulty with the square calculated from the remainder, there is nothing that can be added to the root of the unit measurement. A square that is divided by the unit measurement should be pure; in that way, the remainder would be of the same kind as the unit. In the third section of this work, the nature of the square of the remainder, being less than a full unit, is described. Because there is no exact division in the square of the remainder, the roots of these squares must be expressed as fractions.

### The Construction of the Square of the Hypotenuse

To illustrate this, consider a right-angled triangle. One should take four congruent right-angled triangles <term>jātyasra: a "born-straight" or right-angled triangle</term> where the **base** <term>bhuja</term>, **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>, and **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> are the same. These four triangles should be arranged in such a way that they form a large square.

When these are placed together, a **square figure** <term>sama-chatur-bhuja: a four-sided figure with equal sides</term> is formed. In this arrangement, where the figure consists of the two sides—the base and the perpendicular—the **area** <term>kshetraphala</term> of that square is equal to the square of its side. Thus, the relationship is:

-> (Base $1$ + Perpendicular $1$) + (Base $2$ + Perpendicular $2$) <-

Because the sum of the **squares** <term>varga</term> of the base and the perpendicular is equal to the area of the square formed on the hypotenuse, it is proven that:

**The square of the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the base and the perpendicular.**

<note>original: "bhujakoṭivarga yoga tulye karṇavarga"</note>

This is proven with logic. It should be seen here that in the place of the sum of these two squares, the area is established. The hypotenuse is the line that connects the base and the perpendicular. Because it is perceived as a measurable quantity in the form of the root of that sum, it is certain that in a right-angled triangle...

<summary>Kamalākara concludes that fractional remainders in square roots cannot be eliminated by simple scaling and provides a geometric proof of the Pythagorean theorem by arranging four congruent triangles into a square.</summary>
<keywords>square root, reduction, right-angled triangle, hypotenuse, base, perpendicular, area, Pythagorean theorem, Kamalākara</keywords>

# The Geometric Line and the Theory of Atoms

Or [it may be said that] the **area of a square** <term>samacaturbhuja-kṣetraphala: literally 'the fruit of the field of four equal sides'</term> is inherently of the nature of a square. In this case, the root is simply the form of the line itself; it cannot be limited or defined by a known discrete number. Because of the reasoning stated previously, such a discrete number cannot be established. Since it is proven that a single whole-number unit is not the root, then for any quantity that "has parts" <note>meaning a fractional or composite number</note>, its **square** <term>varga</term> will not be equal to the square of a whole number. 

If one were to ask "what is the root?" when such a line is drawn, the answer "the root is the form of this very line" is much more appropriate than trying to force it into a whole-number unit. Wise men should reflect on this, as the proof is established right here. You should understand that when we use the term "non-square" <term>avarga</term> for a line, we mean that its root cannot be found through the method of whole units. Therefore, in mathematics, a perfect whole-unit root is entirely impossible for a non-square; even if we call it "authoritative," it is only an **approximate root** <term>āsanna-mūla: a value that is 'near' but not 'exact'</term>. One should contemplate that the line-based root is the closest we can come to the truth. 

The meaning of the term "approximate" <term>āsanna</term> is used because a root made of a "non-square" number is impossible. That which is "near" is what is intended to be used, even if it is a quantity with parts. By looking at the form of the line, one perceives its power. Because of the lack of numerical knowledge of the exact scale—even if we use a scale of tens of millions of divisions—the root remains "approximate." This approximate line is used as long as the calculation requires it, using the union of the intellect and the mind. This is done according to the maxim: "destruction of the whole is worse than the loss of a part" <note>original: 'sarvanāśāt rūpanāśavara iti nyāyāt' — a legal/logical maxim suggesting it is better to accept a small error or loss than to lose the entire calculation/logic</note>. Therefore, accepting the approximate value is proper; otherwise, in practical applications, the area of the field would result in a total void or a smeared error.

### The Infinitesimal Composition of Lines

Now, this very concept of the "nature of parts" <term>sāvayavatva</term> exists even in the **atom** <term>paramāṇu: the smallest indivisible point of matter</term>. For example, in our school of thought, we accept that an atom has a limited, defined measure. There, two atoms combined create the measure of a "dyad" <note>original: 'dvyanuka'</note>, and three of those create a "triad" <note>original: 'tryanuka'</note>. A line is a collection of such atoms—hundreds of millions of them. The diagonal <term>karṇa</term> of a square, which is the root of the sum of the squares of the **sides** <term>bhuja</term> and **uprights** <term>koṭi</term>, is perceived as having parts. 

Because we cannot find a discrete, whole-unit root for the diagonal, we must conclude that the measure of an atom is not the absolute smallest possible limit. Through this logic, a measure even smaller than an atom is established. By following this leap in logic, more and more subtle parts are proven to exist. This is not a mistake, as it is confirmed by direct perception. It is not as if the parts of a body are all identical; rather, through the eyes of the wise, what appears "gross" or "thick" is made of many "small" parts, and those "small" parts are made of "subtle" parts, and so on, until only a single remainder remains. If we do not imagine this progression, the logical proofs mentioned before would fail. Without assuming these many subtle parts, the production of sounds or the movement of the body would be impossible. Thus, there is no lack of evidence for imagining many constituent parts.

<summary>Kamalākara argues that for non-square areas, the only "true" root is the geometric line itself, as numerical roots are always approximate. He uses this mathematical necessity to argue that atoms must be further divisible into even smaller parts to account for the existence of irrational lengths like the diagonal of a square.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, square root, approximate root, āsanna-mūla, atoms, paramāṇu, divisibility, infinitesimals, diagonal, karṇa</keywords>

# On Atoms and the Geometry of the Circle

It must be said that if [the atom] were the same in both cases—at the peak of Mount Meru and in a mustard seed—then [their sizes] would be identical. <note>original: "meru-sarṣapayoḥ". This refers to a classic philosophical paradox: if atoms are partless points, then a mountain and a mustard seed both contain an infinite number of points and should be the same size. Kamalākara uses this to argue that atoms must have physical dimensions.</note> If you claim that the measure of an object is not created by the quantity of atoms, then the distinction between them would fail. In every way, atoms must have parts; even in the smallest thing, parts exist. By this, the existence of both the **gross** <term>sthūla: large or perceptible matter</term> and the **subtle** <term>sūkṣma: minute or infinitesimal matter</term> is proven. How is it that the Logicians <term>tārkika: specifically the Naiyāyika school</term>, abandoning direct perception and relying solely on inference, can accept that the **atom** <term>paramāṇu</term> is without parts? || 22 ||

The effort made by those who do not understand the calculation of roots is meaningless; the concept of a partless atom is desired only by those ignorant of the division of time and space. || 23 || 

Furthermore, to say something has a magnitude but no parts is a contradiction. Let us end this dispute. A **line** <term>rekhā</term> is that which always possesses length but lacks width. It is extremely subtle, and it is seen by the eyes of the wise. || 24 || 

Lines are of two kinds: **straight** <term>asara</term> and **curved** <term>vakra</term>. A straight line is one that does not deviate, while a curved line is the opposite. || 25 || 

An atom cannot be the form of a curve, nor can it be like the wind; it has neither [significant] length nor width. || 25 <note>The numbering repeats in the original manuscript.</note> || 

By the intellect of those who understand logic: a line that stays at a constant distance from a **center point** <term>madhyagata-kendra</term>, moving at the distance of the **half-diameter** <term>vyāsārdha: the radius</term>, is a curved line [forming a circle]. || 26 || 

In that perfectly round circle, the line that stays exactly in the middle is the one passing through the center. || 27 || 

That very line is called the **diameter** <term>viṣkambha</term> or the **width** <term>vyāsa</term> when it passes through the center of the circle. A line within that circle that is produced from a **full arc** <term>pūrṇa-cāpa</term> is called a **chord** <term>jyā</term>. || 28 || 

Thus, from the center to the edge, everything is measured by the half-diameter. A **sphere** <term>gola</term> is described as a body where every point on the surface is equidistant from the center. || 29 || 

When one line stands upright upon another line, the four sections created by the circle [at that intersection] are called **right angles** <term>samakoṇa</term>. || 30 || 

If, in all four directions, the lines are equal, then the two "arms" <note>sides</note> forming that right angle should be given equal values. || 31 || 

| | |
|----------|----------|
| In a **triangle** <term>trikoṇake</term> | ...this is not the case for those who do not know the pairing of even and odd in the scriptures. || 32 || |
| In an **equal quadrilateral** <term>caturbhuje sama</term> | ...[the diagonal] is not found by the angles alone, nor by the right angle. || 33 || |
| Its **sides** <term>bāhu</term> | In a triangle, those sides are equal to the [other] equal triangles. || 34 || |
| Whether of the **same kind** <term>sajātīya</term> | or of a **different kind** <term>vijātīya</term>, the wise man does not know this by simple observation. No mockery should be made here, for this is the way of mathematics. || 35 || |

<summary>Kamalākara critiques the "partless atom" theory of the Logicians, arguing that all physical matter must have dimensions. He then provides formal definitions for geometric elements including lines, radii, diameters, chords, spheres, and right angles.</summary>

In this system, it is established that a **right angle** <term>samakoṇa</term> exists where two sides meet in a **right-angled triangle** <term>jātyaka</term>. || 36 || In a triangle with a slanted side, where the sum of the sides is uneven, the angles are likewise unequal. In a large figure, when the height is great, its fourth part <unclear>is taken</unclear>. || 37 || When the planets are not being calculated, one should understand the two right angles of the right-angled triangle. <insert>a small hand-drawn right-angled triangle diagram illustrating the intersection of the altitude and the base</insert> Between those two, a line stands equal to the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>; that which is established there is known by the wise. Just as the sum of the slanted sides produces an unequal angle, so too is it in a large figure with a large height. || 40 || From the two hypotenuses made in a circle, the side is marked. From the unequal angle, a larger right-angled triangle is produced. || 41 || Two right-angled triangles situated between parallel lines are **similar** <term>sajātīya: of the same type or proportional</term>; these two belong to the same class. || 42 ||

The side of that triangle is also similar to the large right-angled triangle. It is proportional to the large height and the base. || 43 || From a small area, a smaller hypotenuse will be produced. This is how the nature of the right-angled triangle is perceived in this system. || 44 || When a layout of similar figures is established, even if it is small, it is made according to the proportions of the large triangle. Thus, they are similar. || 45 ||

<margin>
The ratio is worthy of being calculated between the hypotenuse, the ground, and the side. When there is an angle, it is not a right-angled triangle. || 46 || 
When the lines of the two directions are unequal, there is no similarity to the right-angled triangle. Therefore, the determination of the upright <term>koṭi</term> and the hypotenuse is stated through the calculation of the shadow. || 48 || 
By performing the operation between parallel lines, the similarity of the angle and the line is achieved.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical discourse of Kamalākara, moving from the general principles of similar triangles to the specific trigonometric functions used in Indian astronomy, such as the sine (jya) and cosine (kotijya). It provides both geometric constructions and the algebraic "upapatti" (proofs) for these relationships.</meta>

On the circle of the cross-north line <note>original: *tillotarasūtra*; likely referring to a specific meridian or auxiliary line in the diagram</note>, one should mark 51 degrees of the arc on both sides of the East point <note>original: *śakra-cihna*; Indra’s mark traditionally represents the East</note>. From there, a line should be drawn across; this is the double-sine. In the absence of terrestrial longitude... || 51-52 ||

...half of that is the sine of the altitude. Having seen that arc, the calculators <note>astronomers/mathematicians</note> determine the half-sine <term>ardha-sinjinī: the modern sine function</term>. There is no practical use for a calculation that is not perfected. || 53 ||

From the mark of the arc to the end of the side, as far as that tip reaches horizontally, is the East-West line. From there, reaching the tip from the North-South line || 55 || is the half-line, which is the **Cosine** <term>kotijyā: literally 'the sine of the tip/corner'</term>. In the meeting of these lines, the **Radius** <term>trijyā: the 'three-sine' or the radius of the circle of calculation</term> acts as the **Hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> for that single object. || 56 ||

The square root of the **Radius** square minus the **Sine** square is the **Cosine**. Truly, the square of the **Cosine** subtracted from the square of the **Radius**... || 57 || ...and its square root is known by the wise as the **Sine** <term>doḥ-jyā: the sine of the side</term>. When these two, the Sine and Cosine, are known through subtraction, they are established as the order of the sines. || 58 ||

The sine and arc are situated in the middle. These are the actual values of the sine. From the Sine and Cosine produced in this way || 59 || the square root of the sum of their squares is the **Hypotenuse**. This was stated in many parts by the ancients. || 60 ||

The Sine is half the chord. Twice the square root of the difference of the squares of the radius and the segment... || 61 || ...if the square of the Sine is divided by the **Versine** <term>śara: the 'arrow', or the segment of the diameter between the chord and the arc</term>, and the Versine is added to the result, that sum is surely the diameter. || 62 ||

In the same way that the side and base are equal to the diameter, so too is the Sine here. Looking at the arc from the coverage of the side... || 63 || ...what would the Sine and Cosine be like in the circumference for a particular portion? || 64 ||

### **Now the Proof**

The **Diameter** <term>vyāsa</term> is twice the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term>. The Diameter minus the Versine <note>original: *śara*</note> is the sum of the Cosine and the Hypotenuse <note>This refers to the geometric property where the diameter is partitioned by the chord</note>. The square root of the difference of their squares is the square of the **Sine** <term>bhujajyā</term>. This is how the Sine is produced. In this demonstration, the square root of the product of the Versine and the "Diameter minus the Versine" is established as the Sine. By this method, the desired result is found.

When the difference between the two is small, the calculated value of the Sine is found by taking the Diameter as the basis... as stated in the text, twice the Sine... the square root of the difference of the squares of the double-radius <note>abbreviations for mathematical terms: bhū. va. = bhūja-varga (square of the side); tri. va. = trijyā-varga (square of the radius)</note>. The root of the difference of the squares is twice the **Cosine** <note>ko. 2 = dviguṇā-koṭijyā</note>. Twice the known value... twice the Sine <note>śra. 2 = dviguṇā-kramajyā</note>. Because it is seen as a single factor, half of that is the Sine, as stated with joy. || ||

Thus, the square of the Sine is equal to the difference of the squares of the Cosine and the Radius. In this instrument, when divided, the sum of the Cosine and Radius is the "Diameter minus the Versine." When added to the Versine, it becomes the Diameter. This is proven. Just as those who are dedicated to a single object understand its nature through constant reflection, so too should the knowledge of this book be practiced by those clever in the circle of the arc. By the feeling of addition, the Sine and the Sine of the difference of two arcs...

It concludes with a formal geometric proof (Upapatti) for these calculations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical exploration of trigonometry. Following the definitions of sine and cosine on the previous page, he now introduces the "Bhāvanā" (composition) rules, which are the Indian mathematical equivalent of the addition and subtraction theorems for trigonometric functions.</meta>

<margin>
Principles of
Science
17
<note>Pencil annotation below:</note>
17
</margin>

Regarding the two arcs, whether one is positioned within the other or outside, the experts in calculation determine their results according to their specific names. We shall now explain the two types of **Composition** <term>Bhāvanā: a ruleset for combining mathematical quantities, originally used by Brahmagupta for Pell's equation and later adapted for trigonometry</term> as they are traditionally taught. 

The mutual multiplication of the **Sine** <term>jyā</term> of one arc and the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> of the other, divided by the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term>, results in the sum or difference of the arcs, which are known as the **Sines of the Sum and Difference** <note>This refers to the identity $\sin(A \pm B) = \frac{\sin A \cos B \pm \cos A \sin B}{R}$</note>. Similarly, for the two **Cosines**, the difference or sum of their products, when divided by the **Radius**, results in the **Cosine of the Sum or Difference of the Arcs**. This calculation was previously established in the glorious **Śiromaṇi** <note>The *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*, a seminal 12th-century astronomical text by Bhāskara II</note> through these two types of composition, and it shines forth in the memory of calculators.

The meaning of that derivation has been set forth by the masters of the established scientific systems <term>Siddhāntas</term> according to the brilliance of their own intellects for the sake of preservation. In the **Marīcikā** <note>A 17th-century commentary by Muniśvara on Bhāskara’s work</note>, the method is shown using "Minor" <term>kanishṭha</term> and "Major" <term>jyeshṭha</term> terms. This is comparable to the rule of **Cross-multiplication** <term>vajrābhyāsa: literally 'diamond-multiplication', where terms are multiplied diagonally across a grid</term>. In that system, the Sine and Cosine are treated as the "Minor" and "Major" roots. To find the remainder, one considers the square of the Sine added to the square of the Cosine, which equals the square of the Radius.

<insert>
Sine 1 . Cosine 1 . Radius-Square 1 | | Here, the terms |
of cross-multiplication: | Sine1 Cosine2 . Cosine1 Sine2 | | Sine1 Cosine2 Cosine1... | | Similarly, for the second: Sine2 . Cosine2 . Radius-Square 1 | | Cosine1 Cosine2 |
Sine1 Sine2 | | Combined Sine 1 |
</insert>

Through this process, the "Minor" term generated by the composition of the sum and difference is established. Since it is stated that the square of the **Radius** acts as the additive constant <term>kshepa</term> in these derivations, the Sine is perfected by dividing by the Radius. Therefore, the result of the cross-multiplication, when divided by the Radius, yields the **Sine of the combined arc** <note>$\sin(A+B)$</note>. This logic demonstrates how the calculation of the Sine and Cosine of the sum and difference of two arcs is flawlessly achieved through the method of Composition.

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s critique and expansion of the "Composition" rules (Bhāvanā). He is now reconciling the traditional methods of generating sine tables—specifically the "successive arc" method—with the algebraic principles used by the 12th-century mathematician Bhāskara II.</meta>

The determination of the **Radius** <term>trijyā: the total sine of an arc of 90 degrees, often used as a constant in Indian trigonometry</term> was not performed as a primary calculation by the teachers because of the excessive burden involved in its derivation. This is because we know the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā: literally the "sine of the complement"</term> is the square root of the **Radius** squared minus the **Sine** <term>bhujajyā</term> squared. This holds true even when the Cosine is the smaller value.

When the Sine is taken as the base, the derivation of both the Sine and the Cosine of the arcs is achieved specifically through the "Rule of Successive Arcs" <note>ekottara-cāpa-nyāya: a method for generating a sine table by calculating values for arcs at regular intervals, such as 3° 45'</note>. Even though there is a distinction between the Sine and Cosine in both instances, the calculation remains consistent because the Sine of the next arc and the Cosine of the current arc are mutually related. This derivation is presented here to clarify the logic of the **Composition** <term>bhāvanā</term> in the absence of a comprehensive text on the subject.

One might raise a doubt: how do the statements of **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century mathematician-astronomer</note> align with this? Since the **Composition of the Sum and Difference** <note>the trigonometric addition and subtraction formulas</note> can be understood through a different method of logic, we can see four specific variations in the mathematical roots:

1.  In the first case, the **Radius** squared minus the **Sine** squared equals the **Cosine** squared.
2.  Alternatively, the **Radius** squared minus the **Cosine** squared equals the **Sine** squared.
3.  In the third case, the sum of the squares of the **Sine** and the **Cosine** equals the **Radius** squared; here, the Sine is the "Lesser" value and the Radius is the "Greater" value.
4.  In the fourth case, where the Cosine is the "Lesser" value, the square of the **Sine** is derived from the **Radius**.

Even though these appear similar, they relate to seven different types of "Lesser" values <term>kanishṭha: the smaller root in an algebraic equation, usually representing the Sine in these compositions</term> which are combined through the logic of Composition. This leads to the derivation of the Sine of the next arc. Some claim that the Composition of Sums and Differences should be accepted as the Sine itself because of their similarity. However, our position is that the "Lesser" value is the Sine of the successive arc, according to the tradition. If your preferred method is through the derivation of the **Composition** <term>bhāvanā</term>, then the square of the Sine would be divided by its root. In our view, the Sine of the successive arc is the "Lesser" root itself, so there is no inconsistency.

<warning>The bottom left section of the manuscript page contains significant physical damage and white patches, which obscures parts of the text in the final three lines.</warning>

Even if that method is not followed, the "Lesser" value remains the Sine of the successive arc for all cases. It is not merely the sum of the Sine and Cosine of two different arcs divided by the Radius. If you argue that the Sines of successive arcs are established by the logic of the Sine, Cosine, and Radius of the arcs, then the doubt remains. A rule cannot be established without a fixed principle. The logic of the Sine of the successive arc must be proven through the relationship between the Sine and the next arc. In the previously mentioned method of Composition, the square of the Sine of the arc difference is multiplied and then divided <unclear>... ... ...</unclear> because of this, the Sine of the next arc is correctly established by that logic. This leads to some repetition in the subsequent sections.

<summary>Kamalākara discusses the mathematical derivation of the Sine and Cosine of sum/difference arcs, reconciling Bhāskara II’s algebraic "Composition" (Bhāvanā) with the geometric methods of generating sine tables.</summary>
<keywords>trigonometry, Bhujajyā (Sine), Koṭijyā (Cosine), Trijyā (Radius), Bhāvanā (Composition), Bhāskara II, sine tables, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 145 regarding the application of the algebraic "Composition" (Bhāvanā) to trigonometric arcs. Kamalākara is explaining why the algebraic relationship between "Lesser" and "Greater" roots is essential for validating the "Successive Arc" method of sine calculation.</meta>

<margin>
Inclination
Section
18
</margin>

Regarding the nature of the internal roots after the derivation of the "family" of equations: one should not argue that the knowledge of the **Lesser Root** <term>kaniṣṭha: the variable $x$ in the equation $Dx^2 + k = y^2$</term> alone is what should be considered, or that the knowledge of the "one-greater" arc <note>ekottara: an arc incremented by a standard unit, like 3° 45'</note> in the future is somehow diminished. For those who rely only on the names of these terms without understanding their logic, there is no proof for the establishment of the "one-greater" [sine value] without this specific reasoning. If one brings about this knowledge through the logic of **Composition** <term>bhāvanā: the algebraic process of combining two solutions of a "Square-Nature" equation to produce a third</term>, then the method of achieving the desired sine through the Root-Multiplier <note>mūlakāra: the fundamental logic of the calculation</note> is perfectly stated. What is the purpose of your objection here? The demonstration of the very nature of this **Composition** was not previously completed in this manner.

By knowing the two **Lesser Roots**, the third **Lesser Root** is known; however, there is a doubt as to which of the forms of the "one-greater" knowledge is established by this logic. This doubt is resolved by the following reasoning: in the **Lesser** and **Greater** roots described in the "Seeds of Algebra" <note>bījokta: refers to Bīja-gaṇita, the branch of mathematics dealing with algebra</note> taught in the seventh chapter and elsewhere, the **Composition** is not merely for decoration. In such cases, through the intersection of those definitions, the third **Lesser Root** generated by the **Composition** results in the identity of the two previous **Lesser Roots**. Thus, the result is established without a doubt. Just as the sine is achieved through the **Composition** of two known sines, if the third **Lesser Root** is sought, its nature is not diminished. Therefore, the sine is established by the force of the "one-greater" arc calculation. Without this logic, there would be no rule, and more or less [variation] would be possible. This is the direction of the proof.

The demonstration of the **Composition** is also described by the **Teacher** <note>Guru: likely referring to Kamalākara himself or his primary source</note> as follows: even through a simpler method, the achievement of that calculation is observed. Therefore, following that context, the demonstration is as follows: by the squares of the **Lesser** and **Greater Roots** related to two different multipliers, the roots are known. When multiplied by the square of the difference, those known roots become the desired **Lesser** and **Greater Roots**.

Furthermore, even in the absence of the square-root context, the knowledge of those additives is possible. In that case, where the square of the **Lesser** and **Greater Roots** are combined, we see the relationship of the **Multiplier** <term>prakṛti: the coefficient $D$ in $Dx^2 + k = y^2$</term> times the square of the **Lesser** added to the square of the **Greater**.

For example, let the two roots be:
**| Multiplier · Greater · Lesser · 2 |**

From these, the product is the additive of the next arc. This should be known as 1. If the square of the first is known to be equal to the square of the **Greater Root**, then:
**| First Greater Square: 15 | Second Lesser Square: 1 | Second Additive: 1 |**

<insert>Note: The second calculation is taken here.</insert>

In this text, when that is added, it becomes the provider of the square root. Here, the square root of the sum divided by the **Multiplier** is, in the master's view, the **Lesser Root**. In the sum, the square root that results is the **Greater Root**. The square of the **Multiplier** times the known **Lesser Root** and the square of the simple **Greater Root**—the sum of those squares is the **Multiplier** times the result. This would be the result of the composition. If it is divided, it will always provide the square root. Therefore, here in the middle, by some method, two or three [values] may have entered that place.

<summary>Kamalākara provides a formal algebraic justification for the trigonometric "Addition Formulas" by linking them to the Varga-Prakriti (Square-Nature) equations. He argues that the knowledge of successive sine values (the "one-greater" arc) is only mathematically sound when proven through the logic of algebraic Composition (Bhāvanā).</summary>

<keywords>Varga-Prakriti, Bhāvanā, Kaniṣṭha, Jyeṣṭha, Square-Nature Equation, Sine derivation, Kamalākara, mathematical proof, algebraic logic</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical justification of the "Composition" (Bhāvanā) method. Kamalākara is now demonstrating how the algebraic "cross-multiplication" (vajrābhyāsa) used in the Varga-prakṛti (Square-nature) equations is the underlying logic for the sine addition and subtraction theorems—what modern mathematicians know as sin(A ± B).</meta>

Of the subtractor... <note>The text begins with fragmented shorthand for variables: Gra (Greater Root), Va (Square), Pra (Prakṛti/Multiplier). This represents the setup for the algebraic identity.</note> 1, Gra, Ka, Dvi, Ka, 1. From the cross-multiplication of these two: 1, Dvi, Je, 1. Here, it is said that the sum is equal to the product of the square of the **Sine** <term>jyā</term> and the doubled **Coefficient** <term>prakṛti: the fixed multiplier in the equation, here representing the relationship between sine and cosine</term>.

**Sine** squared, **Sine** squared, 1. **Greater Root** [multiplied by] **Sine**, doubled **Sine**, 1. Doubled **Sine** squared, 1. By taking the square root of this divided by the **Coefficient**, many **Lesser Roots** <term>kaniṣṭha: the $x$ variable in $Dx^2 + k = y^2$</term> are produced: **Greater Root** [multiplied by] **Sine**, 1, Doubled **Sine**, 1. This is used for the "subtractor" logic, and subsequently, for the application of the "Summation" <term>yoga-bhāvanā: the process of adding two arcs/solutions</term>. For the purpose of finding the square of the knowledge of the "Additive" <term>kṣepa: the constant $k$ in the equation</term>, taking the root of the desired value: **Greater Root**, **Lesser Root**, 1, **Greater Root**, **Lesser Root**, 1. 

Thus, here, the cross-multiplication of the sum of the **Lesser Roots** is to be known. From the result produced by the **Multiplier** <note>prakṛti-guṇaka</note>, the form of the sum of the knowledge of the "Additive" is found. Later, it is applied to the logic of the **Summation** with its respective roots. By that logic, one should understand the procedure. By the "Difference Composition" <term>antara-bhāvanā: the process for $sin(A-B)$</term>, the difference is brought about. What is the use of laboring further over the proof of these compositions here? 

In this regard, in the *Vāsanā-vārtika* <note>The "Explanatory Commentary," likely referring to a gloss on the Sūrya-siddhānta or Kamalākara's own commentary</note>, it is stated: For the purpose of establishing the **Sine of the sum and difference** of two desired arcs, when the **Sines** of those two arcs are known, the nature of the sine of one [arc] is modified by the other. The difference between the **Sines** consists of a "minuend" and a "subtrahend." Because the subtrahend added to the difference equals the minuend, this is well-known. 

Therefore, that difference is being calculated. In the absence of one **Sine**, since its **Radius-Sine** <term>tri-jyā: the sine of 90 degrees, or the total radius</term> exists, the **Radius-Sine** is the limit of the difference between the two. Here, in the absence of the **Sine** of one arc, its **Cosine** <term>koṭi-jyā</term> is at its maximum, being equal to the **Radius**. Thus, the **Sine** and **Cosine** of the other arc are equal to the **Radius**. For a different location, what would the **Cosine** be? For a desired **Sine**, that is the difference. Even when the **Cosine** is at its maximum, if it were absent, there would be no difference. In the absence of that **Cosine**, the **Sine** becomes the maximum; hence, the result of the sum is obtained from the **Sine** and the **Radius**. This is established as equal to the **Sine** described in the *Tadvit* <note>Likely referring to the "Tadvit-kotijya" or a specific technical section on trigonometry</note>. 

Indeed, even if it is established by that method, the "front" of the difference is the sum. When that is established, the **Sine** mentioned in the *Tadvit* remains in the "Mani" <note>The "Jewel" or a specific section of the astronomical text</note>. Because of the reversal of the minuend and subtrahend, this method is faster. Without this algebraic process, the placement of the sum and difference would be improper. Therefore, the "Gross Rule" <note>sthūla-sūtra: an approximate formula used before refining</note> stated in the *Tadvit* for the sines of the sum is as follows: from the two [arcs], the difference of the two **Sines** is known in relation to the **Radius**. The **Cosine** found there is the **Sine of the sum** in the gross rule. In the context of the maximum difference mentioned in the *Tadvit*, the gross rule for the **Sine of the sum** is applied. That which is the **Sine** of the sum of the rules—the **Cosine** mentioned in the *Tadvit*—becomes the **Sine of the sum**. Because the nature of the sum of the rules has the form of the difference of the **Sines**. That difference is maximum when the **Radius-Sine** is the difference. For the purpose of achieving the desired difference, a proportion <term>anupāta</term> is used: "If for this **Radius-Sine**, this is the difference mentioned in the *Tadvit*, then for these two desired **Sines**, what would it be?" By this, the difference between the **Sines** of the sum in the "Gross Rule" is found.

<summary>Kamalākara demonstrates how the algebraic "Composition" (Bhāvanā) rules for solving Square-nature equations provide the geometric proof for the sine addition and subtraction formulas. He explains the relationship between the Sine, Cosine, and Radius when calculating the arcs of two combined angles.</summary>

<keywords>Jya, Kotijya, Trijya, Yoga-bhavana, Antara-bhavana, Vajrabhyasa, Varga-prakriti, trigonometry, sine addition formula, Vasana-vartika</keywords>
