<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical defense of his "Composition" method. Having introduced the algebraic roots of sine addition/subtraction on the previous page, he now addresses potential objections regarding the precision (sukshmatva) versus the approximate (sthula) nature of these calculations, particularly when using the "Rule of Three."</meta>

<margin>
Principles of [the work]
Reflections
15
</margin>

...attained. <note>original: "trupanagate"</note> 

Now, it may be argued that if this same logic applies to a **Gross** <term>sthūla: an approximate or large-scale value</term> measurement, then **Subtlety** <term>sūkṣmatva: mathematical precision</term> can also be achieved through its **Refinement** <gloss>saṃskāra</gloss>. Otherwise, in the school of the opponents, it is established that the **Subsequent Sine** <term>anantara-jyā: the sine of the next arc segment</term> follows from the **Gross Rule of Three** <term>sthūlānupāta</term>. This is proved by the **Sine of the Difference** <gloss>lekha-jyā</gloss> and so on. Since this is perceived directly, in its form as the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term>, there is a resulting **Versed Sine** <term>vackramajyā: the versine, or $1 - \cos(\theta)$</term> which, when applied to the sum of sines, yields the highest value. 

In the absence of that **Subsequent Sine**, there is no difference between the **Sine of the Arc** <gloss>kaca-jyā</gloss> (which is measured by its respective **Cosine**) and the **Precise Sine** <gloss>sūkṣma-jyā</gloss> as perceived directly. This is established through the **Method of Agreement and Difference** <note>anvaya-vyatireka: a logical tool where a conclusion is proven by both the presence of a cause leading to an effect and the absence of the cause leading to the absence of the effect</note>. 

If it is argued that the result of the second **Rule of Three** <term>trairāśika</term> cannot be considered "precise" because it would lead to errors in calculating **Deflection** <term>valana: the angle between the ecliptic and the local horizon/vertical</term> and other factors—this is not so. In that context, even if there is a **Highest Grossness** <gloss>parama-sthūlatva</gloss>, the rule of the **Sine of the Three Signs** <note>tri-jyā: the Radius, or Sine of 90 degrees</note> remains consistent. In our system, the rules of increase and decrease are fixed. We see that even in ancient calculations for the **Sine of the Three Signs**, there are no contradictions found when applying the **Rule of Three**. 

One might ask: "What is the proof of this, since the internal nature of the **Rule of Three** is established here as being without difference?" 

The proof lies in the fact that no "grossness" <gloss>sthūlatā</gloss> is observed anywhere in those specific cases. Just as in the calculation of **Deflection**, if one were to admit grossness there, it would lead to a chain of errors. Therefore, in every **Precise** level, there is a subsequent **Precise** level. 

When the **Precise Sine** is subtracted from a state of **Subtle Precision**, it does not exceed the maximum. However, in the case of a **Gross** calculation of the **Sine**, if one subtracts it from the **Second Segment** <gloss>dvitīya-khaṇḍa</gloss>, then the **Subsequent Sine** is multiplied by the other and divided by the **Versed Sine**. In that **Second Segment**, because there is no difference in the remainder, the values are essentially equal. 

By the rule of **Common Denominators** <gloss>samaccheda-vidhi</gloss>, when the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> is multiplied and the other **Sine** is multiplied, and the **Versed Sine** is subtracted, one is free to act upon either of the two multipliers. When calculating the product of the **Sine** and the **Radius**, the result is established as such. Because the multipliers are equal in both cases, the result is the **Versed Sine** measured by the **Radius**, which is not different from the **Cosine**. 

Therefore, the result obtained from the **Gross Rule of Three** is the same as the **Sine of the Difference** multiplied by the **Cosine** and divided by the **Radius**. This is the previous form. If one considers the faults in the logic of "one or the other," it is seen that in the derivation of the **Subsequent Sine**, one must calculate the **Sum** <gloss>yoga</gloss> of the **Sine of the Other Arc** subtracted from the **Sine of the Two Arcs**. 

This clears the doubt. In the absence of the **Sine**, the sum takes the form of the **Sine of the Two Arcs**. For the **Cosine** of that, the question is: "What is the result for the **Cosine of the Local Difference**?" By this, the **Cosine** of the other <unclear>arc</unclear> is obtained as the difference. But this is the **Gross** version. Because the **Sine of the Other Arc** is at its maximum, the **Sine of the Difference** becomes its own **Cosine**. Thus, the sum of the two **Subsequent Sines** is proved to be the measure of the **Versed Sine**. This cannot be established by the aforementioned **Rule of Three** due to the absence of the **Cosine of the Other Arc**.

<summary>Kamalākara logically defends the precision of trigonometric identities (specifically sine addition/subtraction) by arguing that the Rule of Three, though seemingly approximate, yields precise results when correctly refined. He refutes critics who claim his "gross" approximations lead to errors in astronomical phenomena like deflection (valana).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical defense of his methods for calculating sines. He is specifically addressing the precision of the "Rule of Three" when applied to the sines of sums and differences of arcs, responding to critics who claim his "gross" or approximate results lead to significant errors.</meta>

Indeed, according to that logic, even if a **Precise** <term>sūkṣma: mathematically exact</term> sum is established, it is not appropriate in that context. This is because we observe a reversal of the **Subtractor** <gloss>viyojaka</gloss> which is measured by the **Cosine of two or three [segments]** <note>original: "dvitra-koṭijyā-mita"</note>. This reversal is only possible within the sums of the other sines. One should not be confused by this. 

Furthermore, when the **Other Sine** <gloss>anyatar-jyā</gloss> reaches its maximum value, the **Precise Sine** is calculated through the aforementioned **Gross Proportion** <term>sthūlānupāta: the rule of three used for approximation</term>. Because the **Other Sine** is then equal to the **Radius** <term>trijyā: the total sine of 90 degrees, or the radius of the circle</term>, the sum that is traditionally handed down as being measured by the **Radius** is considered "gross" or approximate. It deviates from the **Precise Value** <gloss>sūkṣmāt</gloss>. 

It is determined that there exists a sum measured by the **Cosine of two or three [segments]**. In this case, there is no fault in the conclusion, as it is established by the reversal of the **Subtractor**. Therefore, when the **Precise Other Sine** is added to the maximum **Versed Sine** <note>original: "dvitra-kramajyā," referring to the versine or the "step-sine"</note>, the **Precise Other Sine** remains. Thus, it is established. 

Now, one might ask: "Since the **Radius** is already known, why is there a need for a **Proportion** <gloss>anupāta</gloss>?" 

The logic is as follows: If for a **Sine measured by the Radius**, we obtain this specific difference measured by the **Versed Sine**, then for any **Desired Sine** of a different region, what would we obtain? Through this, the **Precise Difference** is understood. This is established by the **Method of Agreement and Difference** <note>anvaya-vyatireka: a logical principle where a premise is proven by showing that the effect exists when the cause is present, and the effect is absent when the cause is absent</note>, similar to how one determines the north-south direction. 

Indeed, for the purpose of establishing a **Precise Sine** from a **Gross Sine**, the proportion is applied to the two segments of the **Gross Sine**. The resulting sum is remembered as the **Middle Sine** <gloss>madhyama-anantara-jyā</gloss>. When the second result is subtracted, the difference resides in the second segment. By the rule that the "difference between a positive and negative value is their sum," the result for the subsequent **Other Sine** is simply the form of the difference of the **Cosine of two or three segments**. 

First, the movement of the **Other Cosine** <gloss>anyatar-koṭijyā</gloss> takes the form of the difference of the **Sines of two or three segments**. This is only possible in the sums of those two. It is also established that the difference between the positions of two **Chords** <term>jīvā: another term for sine</term> is equal to the difference of their **Arcs** <term>cāpa</term>. 

Here, the means of achieving the **Subsequent Sine** <gloss>uttara-jyā</gloss> is established by the fact that the other arc is smaller. Therefore, by the reversal of that, it is established by the smallness of the two or three arcs. This is not contradictory to the **Wise Man** <gloss>dhīmata</gloss>. 

In the same way, even when calculating the **Sum of Sines** <gloss>aikya-nayana</gloss>, by accepting the difference of the **Other Subsequent Sines**, the result is the same as the sine of the sum and the sine of the difference. This was the intention here. Therefore, the **Ignorant Person** <note>original: "abudhena"</note> who thinks there is some slight discrepancy in the second proportion should reconsider. The claim that "there is some slight difference" is hereby refuted. 

Now, following this logic, we consider the **Sine** and **Cosine** in their absence. When the **Sine** is absent, the **Cosine** is at its maximum, equal to the **Radius**. In that case, what **Sine** is obtained by the **Sine of two or three segments**? The resulting sine would be excessively "gross." This cannot be established by the **Method of Agreement and Difference**. 

However, when the **Other Sine** is at its maximum, even if the "gross" and "precise" values are treated as a single sine according to the stated method, the precise value is still perceived. There is a sine measured by the **Cosine of two or three segments**. When that is added to a very small sine (which is nearly zero) at the maximum point, the resulting **Precise Sine** would be measured by that **Cosine of two or three segments**. 

To establish this even when it is not known, the proportion is: "If by the **Other Sine measured by the Radius**, we get this difference measured by the **Cosine of two or three segments**, then for another region..."

<summary>Kamalākara defends the precision of his trigonometric methods, arguing that the Rule of Three correctly accounts for the differences between "gross" approximations and "precise" values. He utilizes the logical method of "Agreement and Difference" to prove that his calculations for sines and cosines remain valid even at their maximum and minimum values.</summary>

**The Rationale** <term>upapatti: a formal mathematical proof or demonstration</term> is obtained as desired. The difference between the two sines is established through the logic of **Agreement and Difference** <note>original: "anvaya-vyatireka," a standard method in Indian logic to prove a cause-effect relationship</note>. The same sine, produced from the given values, is added; thus, it is logically proven in the same form. 

This is the statement regarding the **Sine of the sum of arcs** <gloss>āpajīvā</gloss>. Even in the calculation of this, when the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā: literally the 'end-sine,' or the sine of the complementary angle</term> is measured by the **Sine of the difference** <gloss>antarajyā</gloss>, and is at its maximum within that [calculation], the result is established. 

However, because of the reversal of the **Subtractor** and the **Minuend** <note>the terms "viyoja-viyojaka" refer to the numbers involved in subtraction</note> compared to what was previously stated, the result is not established when the **Internal Cosine** is absent in the stated proportion. This is because the two values are not the same. Even there, the **Gross** and **Precise** <term>sthūla-sūkṣma</term> nature of the sines of the differences are considered. By the rule that the **Gross Sine of the difference** is reduced by the **Sine of the difference** [produced by the proportion], the maximum value is established. Therefore, by the second proportion, the result is of that same form. By subtracting the sine produced by that proportion from the **Gross Sine of the difference**, the **Precise Sine of the difference** <gloss>sūkṣmāntara-jyā</gloss> is calculated. Thus, the nature of the subtractor and minuend is established in this way for the subsequent difference of sines.

On this point, we say: On level ground, draw a circle using the **Radius** <term>trijyā: the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 minutes</term>. Mark the internal divisions of the circle's degrees. Draw the **East-West line** <term>pūrvāpara-rekhā</term> and the **North-South line** <term>yāmyodag-rekhā</term> <note>these axes are essential for establishing the quadrants of the circle</note>. 

In that circle, from the East-West line, measure the **Sine of the smaller arc** <gloss>laghucāpa-jyā</gloss> in the clockwise direction, and the **Sine of the larger arc** <gloss>vṛhaccāpa-jyā</gloss> in the counter-clockwise direction. Then, staying on the circumference, the **Cosine of the smaller arc** should be drawn reaching toward the North-South line. From the base of the **Sine of the larger arc** on the East-West line—or from the position of the smaller arc—another line should be drawn reaching the **Cosine of the smaller arc**. This distance to the North-South line represents the **Cosine of the larger arc**. 

Then, from the point on the circumference, a transverse line should be drawn for the two sines of the larger arc. By this, in the geometric figure related to the single combined arc, one side is the **Base** <term>bhuja: the vertical side of the triangle, the sine</term>, and the other is the **Difference of the Cosines** <gloss>koṭyor-antara</gloss>, while the transverse line is the **Hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. 

Now, this same hypotenuse can also be formed by the **Sine** <gloss>kramajyā</gloss> and **Cosine** related to a single arc. In this context, to show this clearly: from the point on the circumference where the hypotenuse stands, extending to the point on the circumference separated by the [arc] difference, draw a line equal to the East-West line. From that point, another line should be drawn to the second tip of the hypotenuse, similar to the **Sine of the base**. This falls within the **Sine of the single arc**. 

Furthermore, from that base, there exists a difference in the form of the **Versed Sine** <term>utkramajyā: the 'reversed' sine, or 1 minus cosine</term> of the single arc along the North-South line. When this is observed in the geometric figure, the hypotenuse is clearly seen. The calculation of the **Sine of the arc segment** <gloss>capakhaṇḍa-jyā</gloss> and the other internal **Sine** related to the **Versed Sine** is established by the rule: "The square root of half the product of the Radius and the Versed Sine..." <note>original: "trijyotkramajyā... dhalasya," likely referring to the half-angle formula or the chord-to-sine conversion</note>. This is already known. Thus, the relationship of the **Base** and **Cosine**...

He demonstrates how the sine and cosine of combined arcs form a triangle where the hypotenuse represents the relationship between the two.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from the previous page, specifically focusing on the derivation of the sine of a compound arc (sum or difference of two arcs). The author, Kamalākara, is using algebraic-style expansions of the squares of sines and cosines to demonstrate that the relationship holds true based on the fundamental identity where the sum of the squares of the sine and cosine equals the square of the radius.</meta>

Even by the **inverse method** <term>viloma-vidhi: a method of working backward from a result to prove a premise</term>, the derivation of the **Sine of a single arc** <note>original: "ekacāpajyā"; this refers to the sine of the combined arc resulting from two smaller arcs</note> is established. The calculation involves the difference between the **Sine** <term>jyā</term> and **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> of the desired arcs, which are processed through their respective positions to ensure no confusion in the expansion.

<insert>
1st Sine (1) | 1st Cosine (1) 
2nd Sine (1) | 2nd Cosine (1)
</insert>

In this calculation, we take the square of the difference between the **Base-Sine** and the **Cosine**.

<insert>
(1st Sine)^2 
(1st Sine × 2nd Sine) 
(2nd Sine)^2 + (1st Cosine)^2 
(1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) 
(2nd Cosine)^2
</insert>

The sum of these products results in the **Square of the hypotenuse** <note>original: "karṇi-varga"; here identifying the radius of the circle</note>. Specifically, the expression involves:
(1st Sine × 2nd Sine) + (2nd Cosine × 1st Sine) + (1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) + (2nd Radius-squared). 

Because the sum of the squares of the Sine and the Cosine is equal to the **Square of the Radius** <term>trijyā-varga</term>, those terms combined in the first and second parts of the proof are simplified. Thus, their sum is twice the square of the radius. 

<insert>5th Section</insert>

When the values are equal, the clarity of the proof is evident. Just as the single arc is defined by the root of the sum of the squares, the root of the **Square of the Cosine** is established.

The three parts are: (1st Sine × 2nd Sine) + (1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) + (1st Radius-squared). Then, by subtracting the product of the two cosines from the square of the radius, the remaining value is obtained. 

The square root of this gives the **Sine of the single [combined] arc**. This is further refined by the relationship of the radius, where the sine of the single arc is squared and processed:
(1st Sine × 2nd Sine) + (1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) + (Radius-squared). 
When this is multiplied by the Radius, we obtain the form of the **Cosine of the single arc**. Its square is:
(1st Sine × 2nd Sine)^2 + (1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine)^2 + (Radius-squared)^2. 

By removing the square of the radius from this, we find the **Square of the Sine of the single arc**:
(1st Sine × 2nd Cosine)^2 + (1st Sine × 2nd Sine × 1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) + (1st Cosine × 2nd Sine)^2 + (Radius-squared)^2.
<insert>Radius-squared</insert>

In this state, there are **four segments** <term>khaṇḍāni: the distinct terms in an algebraic expansion</term>. Among them, the fourth segment, which is the "square of the square of the radius" ($R^4$), is calculated as follows:

The sum of the squares of the first Sine and first Cosine is the first Square of the Radius.
(1st Sine)^2 + (1st Cosine)^2.
The sum of the squares of the second Sine and second Cosine is the second Square of the Radius.
(2nd Sine)^2 + (2nd Cosine)^2.

The product of these two is the **Square of the Square of the Radius** ($R^4$). This is expanded as:
(1st Sine × 2nd Sine)^2 + (1st Sine × 2nd Cosine)^2 + (1st Cosine × 2nd Sine)^2 + (1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine)^2. 
In this context of squaring products, the square of the product is taken as equal to the product of the squares. Thus, by subtracting the segments, the general form of the **Square of the Sine of the single arc** is produced:
(1st Sine × 2nd Cosine)^2 + (1st Sine × 2nd Sine × 1st Cosine × 2nd Cosine) + (1st Cosine × 2nd Sine)^2. 

Here, the sum of the products of the two Sines and two Cosines is considered. 
<insert>Radius-squared</insert> 
This is because of the mutual multiplication of the Sines and Cosines of the two arcs, which is twice the product. 
(1st Sine × 2nd Cosine)^2 + (1st Sine × 2nd Cosine × 2nd Sine × 1st Cosine)...

### Section 21: On the Derivation of Sines and Arcs

The **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> [squared] multiplied by two, the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term>, and the square of the **Cosine** minus one, are used at the root of the arc-sum to determine the arc. This is because the **Sine** <term>jyā</term> and **Cosine** [of the first and second arcs] have their squares subtracted by one. <gloss>Shiva</gloss> <note>original: "shiva"; in Sanskrit mathematical notation, "Shiva" often represents the number 11, or it may refer to a specific auspicious point in the calculation</note> This method for finding the **Cosine** is as follows:

In the calculation of the sine of another arc, because it is performed within a circle defined by the **Radius**, the result is established based on those specific segments. In the direction of the eight points of the compass, <gloss>Radius 1</gloss> the difference of the two arcs should be marked on the circumference by the wise mathematician. Similarly, that value should be subtracted from the **Cosine** [on the circle]. On the **Cosine-side** <term>koṭi</term> of the circle, this should be done according to whether the rising is toward the **South** <note>original: "dakṣiṇa"</note> or the **Left (North)**. 

By this method, regarding the sine of another arc, the **Sine-side** <term>bhuja</term> and the **Cosine** are different. The sum of the squares of the **Cosine** and the **Sine** should be understood as the square of the distance. 

Now, this **Hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> itself, which is related to the sine of another arc, can also be determined by the **Sine** <term>kramajyā</term> and **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> of the arc. To understand this, regarding the **Hypotenuse**, after calculating the difference between the two centers and the **Radius**, those who possess knowledge of the **Sine-side** should draw another line. This line represents the **Sine** of the other arc on the **Sine-side**. From the root of that sine, the difference between the two **Hypotenuses** or the two **Radii** is the **Cosine** measured by the **Versine** <term>utkramajyā: the "vertical" sine or 1-cos(theta)</term> of the other arc. Through both of these, the distance is calculated. This is demonstrated in the accompanying figure.

Now, the **Hypotenuse** of the other arc is its **Sine**. Its relationship to the **Versine** of the other arc is also established by the **Inverse Sine** <term>viloma-kramajyā</term>. In the same way, from the **Sine** of the other arc, the calculation of the arc-sine is achieved through the **Inverse Method**. 

Thus, the method for deriving the sine of an arc is easily understood. Where the **Versine** of the other arc is used in a circle made with the stated **Radius**, and the direction is known, a diagram should be drawn. In that diagram, a single line should be drawn. This line, in the mathematics of direction, represents the sine of the desired portion of the arc. For the purpose of finding the desired arc, half of the sine is used. In calculations, this is used as the **Sine of the last arc** <note>original: "antyacāpajyā"</note>. 

Furthermore, in the circle of the **Radius**, the sine of the arc is half of the total chord. In a circle with half the **Radius**, the sine is also shown. Therefore, in a circle made with a radius of half the **Radius**, the direction should be marked, and the desired arc should be calculated. A line is then drawn between those two points. This line is double the sine of the desired arc in the half-radius circle, but it is equal to the **Half-Sine** <term>jyārdha</term> in the full-radius circle. This is well known among those learned in mathematics as the **Chord-Sine** <note>original: "jyā"</note>. In the half-radius circle, the wise mathematician finds the small arc by the **Left-handed** <note>meaning counter-clockwise or inverse</note> method and the large arc by the **Right-handed** method. Then, he should draw the lines to the ends of those arcs in the form of a chord. These are the measurements within the radius...

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric derivations of Kamalākara, specifically focusing on the relationship between the radius, sines, and cosines within a circular frame. It builds on the previous page's discussion of arc sums by visualizing these as parts of an irregular quadrilateral.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

In this manner, the arc of the circle and the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>The radius of the defining circle, usually 3438 minutes in traditional Indian astronomy</note> are considered; the third **Radius** and the nature of the **Sine** <term>ardhajyā</term> <note>Literally "half-chord," the standard term for the sine function</note> are established. From these two, extending to the **Western point** <note>original: "paścima-cihna"</note>, another operation is performed involving the doubled arc of the circle. This relates to the arc of the **Eri** <note>likely referring to a specific circular segment or a localized technical term in this manuscript's geometry</note> and the **Sine of the Eri arc**, where the third **Radius** takes the form of the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term>.

By touching these two points, a calculation is made. Here, even if it is the **Sine of the Eri arc** associated with the doubled arc, the third **Radius** becomes essentially the same as the **Sine of the Sine-form**. Thus, the **Sine-side** <term>bhuja-jyā</term> and the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> associated with the arc of the circle form an **Irregular Quadrilateral** <term>viṣama-caturbhuja</term> <note>A four-sided figure with unequal sides, used here to derive the properties of chords</note>. In this figure, one diagonal is the **Radius** that goes through the Eastern and Western points. The second diagonal, because of the circular construction by the half-radius, is said to be the **Sine of a single arc** <insert>and its measure is stated as such. In this demonstration, one diagonal is the first</insert>. 

In the first case, the square root of the sum of the squares of the parts is calculated as before. <insert>Known as the Radius-form diagonal, it is either the Radius-side or the result</insert> of the derivation. The second diagonal, in the form of the **Sine of a single arc**, is placed upright <insert>in the calculation. For thus: by the rule 'the sum of the two sides in a triangle,' etc.</insert> regarding the **Sine-side** of the small arc: <insert>Small Sine-side</insert> <insert>Small Cosine-side</insert> <insert>Great Cosine-side</insert> <insert>The result</insert> <insert>Small Sine and Small Cosine</insert>. Similarly, for the **Sine-side** of the other arc: <insert>The result</insert>. Here, the two <insert>Radii</insert> are <insert>Radii</insert>. <insert>Square of the small Sine-side</insert> <insert>Small Sine-side, third, and great Sine-side</insert> <insert>Great Sine-side</insert>. 

The sum of the two ends: <insert>Square of the small Sine-side and small Cosine-side</insert> <insert>Small Sine, Small Cosine, Great Sine, Great Cosine 1</insert> <insert>Great Cosine 2</insert> <insert>Square of the Great Sine and Great Cosine</insert> <insert>Radius</insert> <insert>Square of the small Sine and third Cosine</insert> <insert>Radius</insert> <insert>Small Sine, Small Cosine, Great Sine, Great Cosine 1</insert> <insert>Square of the Great Sine and Small Cosine</insert> <insert>Radius</insert>. The square root of this is established as the second diagonal in the form of the **Sine of the combined arc**. 

This is how the **Cosine** is calculated. This is explained clearly in the context of the circles. In this case, in the triangle, the diagonal is the smaller diagonal from one corner. In another instance, the diagonal is different. In that triangle, the sides and the diagonal from one corner are given. The **Cosine** and the **Diagonal** are thus determined. Even the form of the **Radius** of the northern arc is beautified by the division of the circle. Now, another line is drawn. From the previous point in one direction, the **Sine** of the small circle's arc is drawn. By those two, the **Cosines** are extended to the western point. Similarly, the **Sine of the combined arc** is determined from those two. Thus, in this demonstration, there are two triangles: one where the sides are the **Radius**, the **Small Sine-side**, and the **Cosine**, and another where they are the **Great Sine-side** and the **Cosine**.

Astronomy Chapter (Tantra)
*Siddhāntatattvaviveka* (Vi.)
21

Because they [the arcs and sines] are described in the manner stated, it is not the case that after the falling away [of the logic], the **Sine-side** <term>bhujajyā</term> <note>The horizontal component or Sine of an arc</note> or the **Perpendicular-sine** <term>koṭijyā</term> <note>The vertical component or Cosine of an arc</note> would be otherwise. As they [scholars] know, for those whose intellects are engaged in the right path, there are many types of methods in this arrangement. Here, the difference between the upper halves, which is established by the multiplication of the **Sine** and the **Cosine**, is indeed proven through visualization and constant practice of the **Composition method** <term>bhāvanā</term> <note>original: "bhāvanā"; a central algebraic method in Indian mathematics for combining sines and cosines of different arcs, similar to modern addition theorems</note>. This **Composition method** is spoken of for the purpose of establishing the difference between the two [arcs].

By this much, the nature of the **Composition of Sines** <term>jyā-bhāvanā</term> is established as a mathematical proof, not merely as a description. Just as when the distance between sines is not zero, for those with intellect, the proof is established by that very power. There is another **Composition method** of a different form, which is used for the sake of knowing the end [of the calculation]. For those without this knowledge, even when it exists in the commentary, the proof of the Earth's circumference and the like remains unclear. This proof is described here by me out of necessity. Now, the calculation for the **Sine of the five-sided figure** <term>pañcatta-bhujajyā</term> <note>Likely referring to the chord of a regular pentagon inscribed in a circle</note> and its square is stated.

The **Sine** and **Cosine** are multiplied; this result is then multiplied by the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>original: "tri-jya"; the Sine of 90 degrees</note> and divided by [another factor]. When the square of the result—obtained by dividing by the **Sine of the sign** <term>rāśi-jyā</term> <note>The sine of 30 degrees (one zodiacal sign)</note>—is subtracted from the three [terms], the result of the calculation is the **Sine of the five-sided figure** <term>pañcatta-bhujajyā</term>. In this way, when mixed and multiplied, the difference between the **Sine** and **Cosine** is taken. Multiplied by the sum of those two and then diminished, it is divided. By this, the **Sine of the given arc** is produced. 

When the square of the **Sine** is placed, and the **Radius** is subtracted from it, and the sum of the squares is refined, the result is the product of the squares. The **Sine** and the **Radius** are multiplied by the difference; then divided by the square of the **Sine** and the square of the **Radius**. The result is the **Sine of the five-sided figure** in reverse. The square root of the sum of the squares of the **Sine** and the **Versine** <term>utkramajyā</term> <note>original: "utkrama-jyā"; the 'rising sine' or versine, calculated as Radius minus Cosine</note> of the half-arc provides the **Sine of the half-arc**. Or, the square root of the product of the **Radius** and the **Versine** is the **Sine of the half-arc**. Where the arc is greater than the signs [30-degree divisions], it should be calculated by subtracting the degrees and using the **Versine** and the **Sine** divisions. By this, the result joined with its own benefit is the **Sine** division. Again, through the calculation of cubes and the like, the exact value becomes clear.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivations of Kamalākara from the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*. The previous discussion on the logic of the "Composition method" (Bhāvanā) is now applied specifically to the derivation of the side of a regular pentagon (the sine of 72°) and the geometric proofs for doubling arcs.</meta>

This section of the manuscript is difficult to master; by it, the sines of arcs are derived through two geometric shapes. In the heavenly circle <note>the circle upon which the arcs are measured</note>, by doubling the base and the perpendicular <note>sine and cosine</note> and multiplying them together, the sines are made equal. For the first sine, the refined fifth portion <term>pañcāṃśaka</term> <note>The sine of 1/5th of a circle, or 72°</note> is obtained from the square of the arc’s sine, multiplied by four and divided by the square of the sine of the fifth part. From this, when the result is diminished by its own **Versed Sine** <term>śara</term> <note>original: "śara"; the segment of the radius perpendicular to the sine, calculated as Radius minus Cosine</note>, the unrefined value is found. When this is added to the first value, it becomes the **refined sine of the fifth part** <term>sphuṭa-pañcamoṃśa</term>. To find the exact value of the desired part, one should perform this calculation repeatedly, just as the refined value of the fifth part of the sine was found. This same method for finding the divisions should be applied to other parts as well. Thus, the method for the initial parts of the imagined divisions has been stated; it should be applied to any desired parts in the same way. This was understood and explained by the son of the wise teacher <note>likely referring to Kamalākara himself as the successor to his father Nṛsiṃha’s tradition</note> through the **Geometric method** <term>kṣetrarīti</term> in the previously mentioned arrangement of divisions.

Now, the proofs follow in sequence: In a circle where the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the semi-diameter, the sine and the cosine of the remaining arc are known. When these are doubled, the sine and cosine take their respective forms. Let that be known. In a circle where the radius is doubled, the arc itself is doubled. In the two triangles produced therein, the first has the **Sine** <term>bhujajyā</term> <note>The horizontal component of the arc</note> as the side, the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> <note>The vertical component of the arc</note> as the perpendicular, and the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> as the hypotenuse. Thus, through the sum and difference of the sine and cosine of the arc, and by doubling the arc, the three sines are produced. These belong to the same class of figures and are thus fit for addition or subtraction.

There, in the second triangle, the sine of the double-arc is the side, and the **Versed Sine** <term>utkramajyā</term> <note>The sine of the complementary arc</note> subtracted from twice the radius is the perpendicular. In the third triangle, the **Versed Sine** of the double-arc is the side, and the cosine of the arc multiplied by two is the perpendicular. Double the sine of the arc is the hypotenuse in both cases. As shown in the geometric diagram, for the sake of knowing the sine of the double-arc, a proportion is made from the second triangle: if the **Radius** is the hypotenuse and the **Sine** is the side, then what is the side when the double-cosine is the hypotenuse? Thus, the sine of the double-arc is proven to be twice the product of the sine and cosine [divided by the radius].

Alternatively, from the sine and cosine, the **Summation Composition** <term>samāsa-bhāvanā</term> <note>An algebraic method for finding the sine of the sum of two arcs: Sin(A+B)</note> is produced. For the sines of the doubled base-parts:

In the calculation of the **composition of arcs** <term>bhāvanā</term> <note>a mathematical method for combining known trigonometric values to find new ones</note> for a **twenty-four-sided polygon** <term>caturviṃśatibhuja</term>, the **sine** <term>dojyā</term> and **cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> are to be known. **Sine · Cube · Cosine** <note>original: "bhū · gha · ko"</note> plus **Cosine · Cube · Sine · 2** | **Sine · Square 1** | **Sine · Square · Cosine 2 · Cosine · Square 2** | Here also, as before, the sine and cosine are determined.

In the calculation of the composition for a **twenty-five-sided polygon** <term>pañcaviṃśatibhuja</term>, the sine and cosine are to be known. **Sine** | **Method** | **Cube · Cosine · Square · Cosine · Square <insert>Method 1</insert> · Square · Sine · Square · Sine · Square · Sine 1 | Sine · Square · Square · Cosine · Square · Sine · Square · Cosine** 
**Square · Cosine · Square · Square · Cosine 1** | In this sequence, starting from the beginning, the **chords** <term>jīvā</term> <note>the full chord of an arc, related to the sine</note> of the twenty-five-sided polygon are: **Sine · Cosine 2 | Square · Square 1 | Cube · Sine 5 · Square · Square 4 3 | Cube · Sine · Cosine · Cosine · Cube <insert>Square · Square</insert> · Cube · Sine <insert>Cube · Sine · Cosine · Square 1</insert> Cosine · Square · Square 4 5 · Sine · Square · Sine 1** | Following this sequence of calculation <insert>Triangle |</insert> the sines of the sides of the polygon are stated. <insert>Square |</insert> They are obtained through the **refined** <term>sphuṭa</term> <note>accurate or corrected value</note> method, just as they are in the world <insert>Universal 1</insert>.

Why should this not be written <insert>Square · Square |</insert> at length? To satisfy the student, the method for finding the sine and cosine of a quarter-circle is now explained. As seen in the previous example, in this same way, if the arc is **doubled** <term>dviguṇa</term>, the result is well-known.

There, to find the arc's portion, whatever is considered the half-arc's portion, by that method, the **Sine** <term>kramajyā</term> <note>literally "order-sine," the standard sine function</note> and **Cosine** <term>koṭi</term> are found. The sum of their squares is the square of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> <note>here referring to the radius or the chord of the arc</note>. Half of that hypotenuse becomes the **sine of the half-arc** <term>ardhajyā</term>. This method is so simple that it can be grasped even by children. In the second method, the result is achieved by the fall of the perpendicular. Just as mentioned before for the twenty-four-sided polygon, the **Sine** and the **Versed Sine** <term>utkramajyā</term> act as the base and the perpendicular. Their sum’s square is the square of the hypotenuse; its half is the sine of half the arc. For the sake of brevity in calculation, this second method is used. By this **similarity** <term>sājātya</term>, even in the fourth type of right-angled triangle, the result is as in the third.

And that is as follows: If, when the sine of half the arc is the base, the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the hypotenuse, then when the Versed Sine is the base, the hypotenuse is the sine of the whole arc. Therefore, the sine of the half-arc is obtained by dividing the product of the radius and the Versed Sine by the sine of the whole arc. If the square of the sine of the half-arc is desired, it is the product of the radius and the Versed Sine divided by two. This is proven by similarity. By this, wise men can understand other methods as well. Wherever a certain result is stated, it should be understood through these principles.

Know this: when the Sine is subtracted from the **Radius** <term>vibhajyā</term> <insert>uṃ | vi |</insert>, the Cosine is not what is produced. <note>original: "koṭijyakā nakta ti"</note> **u · va · 1 3 · vi 2 | vi · va |** And when the square of the Sine is subtracted from the square of the Radius **u · vaṃ 1 · u · vi 2**, it becomes the square of the Cosine. When the square of the Sine is added to the square of the Versed Sine **u · vi 1**, and the result is doubled, the result is the square of the chord. By taking the square root of that, the value is established. This second method, thus described, is the way to bring about the square root of the product of the Radius and the Versed Sine.

For the calculation of the **sine of a portion of the side** <term>bhujāṃśajyā</term>, this is the nature of the sine of that portion. The various sines of the arc-segments of that side are the forms of the chords. In those cases, when the division is cleared through various proofs, those which are established—

Here, those two are obtained through the proofs of the segments of the chords. By performing these, knowledge arises. When these are equal, they fall into the category of <note>original: "dhetriya-dha-vvi"</note>. The **square of the sine** <term>jyāvarga</term> | plus the known quantity is established as the value of the face-segment.

The square of the sine, when divided, is not known. That which would be the measure of knowledge is the first hypothesis. The square is not the side; the face-segment is added to the side-segment. This would be the measure of knowledge. Again, from that, the **precise chord** <term>sphuṭatantī</term> of the arc-segment would be the sine. This is the indication of the division of the side <term>dorbhāga</term> according to the tradition.

### **Derivation of the Sine of the Fourth Part**
This is the nature of the sine of the fourth part of the side-arc. The face-angle and the corner-angle <insert>difference 1</insert>. This very primary angle 8 and the side-arc <insert>difference 1</insert>. Here, in the case of the sine of the fourth part, those sines are multiplied together. According to the stated proof, the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> <insert>difference 1</insert> and the corner-angle <insert>difference 1</insert> | 8 and the corner-angle 5 ||

And here, according to the formula for the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> of the fourth part of the sine, the value of the arc-sine is derived by dividing by the Radius and doubling it. <insert>...Radius 9... sine 1... determined</insert>. When this is done, it becomes the measure of the sine of those known arc-segments. Therefore, in the proof, the square of the Radius multiplied by the sine, divided by the result, then the difference between the result and twice the square of the Radius—from that root, the sines of the fourth parts are obtained as stated.

**Knowledge of the Fifth Part**
In the nature of the sine of five times the arc-parts: the face-angle 10, the side-arc. The **square of the sine** subtracted from the **square of the Radius** is the **square of the cosine**.
1 The nature of the square of the cosine... at the end, the primary difference is 5 <insert>difference-square 1</insert> the primary being 18 | Here, for the fifth part 3, the value of the side is not found alone. In this proof, the difference 20, the difference-square 5, <insert>difference-square 1</insert> and 18 | In the calculation of these two, the difference is 20, or 18.
In the proof of the known values, multiplied by the difference-square 9, the square of the sine 1... the difference 20... 18 and the difference-square 1 and the difference-square 5 | By dividing by the **square of the square** <term>vargavarga</term> <note>the fourth power</note>, the sine of the fifth part is obtained. In this same equal proof, the fifth part of the known sine is established in the expressed segment. Because of its grossness <note>meaning a rougher approximation</note>, having assumed the fifth part of the sine as the "known fifth part," it should be accepted in the first expressed segment; that is proven. In the second expressed segment, that is also proven. Thus, the sine is joined with the difference between those two results.

This page (15) moves into the complex calculation for the fifth part (1/5) of an arc, utilizing algebraic equations and the relationship between sines and cosines.</meta>

<margin>Tattva-Viveka 15</margin>
<margin>24</margin>
The **fifth part** <term>pañcamośa</term> is the **precise sine** <term>sphuṭajyā</term>; and that fifth part which is to be calculated again, from the starting point of the fifth parts, through which the precise sine and the fifth part are applied—this is the procedure for the fifth part.

In this regard, it is stated for the **sines of the arc-segments** <term>bhujāṃśaka</term> that the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> is to be known by the square root of the difference of the squares of the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> and the sine. If the cosine is equal to the sine, then it is multiplied by two. For those arc-segments, the half-cosine is added to the sine of the quantity, then multiplied by the Radius, and the square root of this is taken. That result is the cosine. According to the method for the sines of the **sun's degrees** <note>original: "sūryāpa," referring to degrees of the ecliptic or 30-degree signs</note>, the cosine for the **sine of sixty degrees** <term>ṣaṣṭi-lava-jyā</term> <note>original: "ṣaṭ 60"</note> is its own square. The **square root** <term>vargamūla</term> is the sine. In the case of **forty-five degrees** <note>original: "cakra-koṇa 45"</note>, the square of the sine and the square of the cosine are equal. Added or subtracted from the square of the Radius, then halved, the square root of that result provides the sine and cosine respectively.

The square of the difference between the sine and cosine, when its square root is taken, is known as the result. If the difference of the segments of the arc-sine is calculated, the square root is the result even without the division. Or, the sine of the arc multiplied by two, its own square, divided by the result, gives the fruit. Thus, through one's own intelligence, the sine of the fourth part or the cosine is doubled. By the difference of the sines, the result is obtained.

Where the method of proof is shown, let the value of the **sine of one sign** <term>rāśi-jyā-māna</term> <note>30 degrees</note> be assumed here as 1 <insert>ya 1</insert>. The **versed sine** <term>utkramajyā</term> <note>1 - cosine</note> of the Radius is also 1 <insert>vi 1</insert>. Another method for bringing forth the half-sine of the said quantity: the square of the sine becomes <insert>ya vi 1 vi va 1</insert>. This is equal to the square of the established value. By destroying the common denominators and equalizing the sides: <insert>ya va 1 ya vi 1/30</insert>. These are equal. Multiplied by eight and added to the square of the Radius, they are again placed as equal: <insert>ya va 8 ya vi 8 vi va 1</insert>. By taking the square root <insert>ya va 0 ya vi 0 vi va 1</insert>, and then <insert>ya. bha vi 1</insert>, similarly, by equalizing and dividing, the **Radius** <term>vibhajyakā</term> becomes <insert>ya va 0 vi ye 0 vi va 5</insert>. At the end, the sine multiplied by its own quality <insert>ya. vi 3</insert> becomes the side.

There, the side is thirty, and the other is its half, which is the **sine of forty-five degrees** <note>original: "śara-veda 45"</note>. There, the sine and cosine are equal, and the Radius is the hypotenuse. Since it is equal to the square root of those two, by the inverse method, the square of the Radius is indeed the root. The cosine of the half-sign is to be known as that. For the sine of the side, the versed sine of the Radius is the versed sine. Thus, for the sine of the side joined with the Radius, the sine and the versed sine are produced. By the method of the half-parts, the precise sine is found. From the horizontal and vertical cords <note>geometric chords</note>, the sine and cosine are established. By those two, the difference of the sines is like the difference of the sine and cosine. The square root of the sum of those squares is the hypotenuse, and its half is the side.

That difference <gloss>Ru 3 Sa</gloss> <note>original: "ru 3 sa," likely referring to three units and a fractional part</note> in the sine is well-established here; in the sum of the cosines, having assumed <gloss>Ru 3</gloss> <note>three units</note>, the sine and cosine are to be squared. By means of the half-radius <unclear>va</unclear>, for the sines and cosines of the arcs, it should be the same.

For the sine and the cosine <gloss>bhī</gloss> <note>sine</note> and <gloss>Ru 3</gloss> <note>cosine</note>, by those and the <gloss>Ru 3</gloss> together, the multiplier for the cosine is found. <gloss>ga</gloss> Because of the equality in the result, and having performed the proof as stated, the composition <term>bhāvanā</term> <note>a method for finding new solutions from known ones in algebra</note> is thus declared. As the sine and cosine are represented as <gloss>Bhu 1 Ko 1</gloss> <note>Sine 1, Cosine 1</note>, here the cosine and sine are used for the sake of the subsequent composition. The layout is: <gloss>Bhu 1 Ko 1</gloss>. Because the square of the sine subtracted from the square of the Radius is the square of the cosine, we have <gloss>Bhu-varga 3 Tri-varga 1</gloss> <note>Sine squared 3, Radius squared 1</note>. Since it is measured by three times the **Radius-sine** <term>trijyakā</term> <note>The sine of the total radius of the circle, used as a base unit in Indian trigonometry</note>, a simple division of the sine is not appropriate here. There is a rule by which the sine of a specific part is produced from half of its **cosine** <term>koṭi</term>. Thus, the rule for sines generated from parts applies to both cases here. Having performed the sum and subtracted it from 81, the value is determined. From this, the understanding of the "construction" mentioned by others is clarified. Knowing this, the wise should speak with reasoning. Because it causes doubt, the cause of the square <gloss>varga</gloss> is taken here for the five parts <note>original: "pañcakāno," referring to the division of an arc into five segments</note>, with the parts being **2 | 24 | 10** <note>This represents a value in sexagesimal notation: 2 degrees, 24 minutes, and 10 seconds</note>. 

In this context, the **Radius-sine** multiplied by the sine of the three-segment arc, and then divided, gives the sine of the part. The method for the sine of the difference between the **sine** <term>doh</term> <note>the "arm-sine" or simply the sine</note> and the **cosine** <term>koṭi</term> is established. In this five-part division, the sine is established by the subtraction of the part-sine. With the two sines, let one reflect: **0 | 24** and **1** and **2**. The squares of those two cosine-parts are **2** and **5**. From these, the **Versed sines** <term>bāṇāḥ</term> <note>original: "baana," literally 'arrow', referring to the sagitta or versed sine</note> are **5**. From their square root, **2 | 35**, when added or subtracted, the results are the square roots **1 | 57**, **16 | 0 | 35**, and **16**. <insert>8</insert> 

When the **Radius-sine** is multiplied by 8 in sequence, the sines for the arcs of 71 <note>original: "shaila," a chronogram for 7</note> and 36 are produced. And so, for the five-part division, the sines are obtained as stated. The multipliers are: **0 | 25 | 24 | 15 | 30**. This is indeed proven for the **Radius-sine**. After that, the wise should ensure the sine and cosine for the divisions are not confused. The mutually produced sines and cosines, multiplied and divided by the radius, result in the difference of the squares of the sine and cosine. This is known to be equal. Just as the difference of the squares of the two internal parts is known, this second rule is established for the sake of logic. 

This is the derivation. Even for those who are experts in the wandering of logic, there is often a lack of understanding of calculations involving square roots. Therefore, I speak here of the method for the sake of clarity, to dispel the confusion caused by the approximations produced by others. By the logic of mathematics, in this context, I shall now state the **method for determining the area of a quadrilateral** <note>original: "catur-bhuja-phala," referring to calculating the area of four-sided figures</note>. This applies to quadrilaterals with two equal sides, three equal sides, or all sides equal, as well as to five-cornered <note>pentagonal</note> figures. 

The values are stated in digits <term>aṅgula</term> <note>a standard unit of measure, roughly the width of a finger</note> for the sake of the results. It is established by these fingers, being seven and twenty <note>27</note> fingers. For each finger, one should combine the mathematical meaning. There, for a single "jewel-born" result <note>perhaps referring to a specific geometric shape or unit</note>, the result of 15 is **576**. When multiplied by two or more, for two or more cubits <term>hasta</term> <note>a unit of length from the elbow to the fingertip</note>, the results are **1152**, **1728**, and **3604**. The single result is multiplied by the number of cubits, and by the square of the fingers, the sum is found.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivations of geometric figures, transitioning from the theoretical sines of the previous page to the practical application of calculating the dimensions for various ritual sacrificial pits (Kundas). Long life, health, and sovereignty are attained through equilateral <term>samabāhu</term> <note>literally "equal-armed," referring to regular polygons where all sides are the same length</note> pits; such is the fruit of these sacrificial basins. However, in pits where the sides are unequal <term>viṣamabhuja</term>, the wise have specified particular side lengths. Even in four-sided pits, unequal sides are sometimes constructed. 

For those pits with one, two, or three times the area, which are wide and expansive, the two types—circular and four-sided—should be made equal in proportion. The calculation for the **approximate root** <term>āsannamūla</term> <note>a method for finding square roots of non-square numbers to determine side lengths</note> should be understood. For a diameter <term>vyāsa</term> of **32 | 54 | 30**, the side of a circular pit of one **cubit** <term>hasta</term> <note>a standard unit of measurement, roughly the length of a forearm</note> or more is determined. When calculating the square of the area for these circles, multiply the desired square by eight and divide by five. This gives the measure of the diameter through the method of the approximate root, which is approximately **7**.

For a one-cubit pit, the square of the result is **33 | 7 | 6**. For other sizes, the measure is multiplied by twenty-two and divided accordingly. From the square of that result, the root or half-root is taken to determine the diameter. Through this approximate root method, the diameter for a one-cubit pit is shown to be **7 | 8**. 

If one divides the square of the area of the pit by seven and then multiplies, the side of the root is produced. One should then add a seventh part to the diameter to establish the proper position. When the squares of the results are divided by five or eight, the diameter is produced. For a one-cubit pit, the side would be **36 | 28**. The diameter, in **fingers** <term>aṅgula</term> <note>a smaller unit of measure, the width of a finger</note>, is derived from the root. 

In the case of a **Yoni-shaped pit** <note>a pit shaped like a vulva or a leaf, symbolic of the creative source</note>, one should multiply by forty-eight **4800** to make the sides equal. For a circular pit divided into seven parts, the diameter for a five-hand pit would result in a side of **15 | 56** and a diameter of **22 | 50** fingers. This is the calculated method for the Yoni-shaped pit.

Alternatively, for another type of Yoni pit, the area is calculated by taking the square of sixty **3600** and subtracting **1252**. The resulting diameter and its square provide the side. For a Yoni pit of this measurement, the diameter is **15 | 22 | 30** and the side is **10 | 56 | 8**. 

This is the second method for the Yoni pit. By multiplying the square of the pit's area by twenty and dividing, the measure of the side is produced from the root. In another method, the diameter is produced by taking the square of the result and dividing by eight. Thus the side and diameter are determined. By taking the approximate root in a pit with two sides, the side is **15 | 53** and the diameter is **19 | 46** fingers. 

Similarly, for an **octagonal pit** <term>aṣṭāsra</term>, the area is multiplied by **75** and divided by **53**. The root of this value becomes the diameter. For a one-cubit octagonal pit, the diameter is **18 | 28** and the side is established as **10 | 55**. This same logic should be applied to pits of other sizes.

Now, for a **pentagonal pit** <term>pañcāsra</term>, the area is multiplied and then divided by **3600** or **4425**. The root of that value provides the diameter and the side. In a one-cubit pentagonal pit, the diameter is **11 | 16 | 25** and the side is **8 | 16 | 38** fingers. These multipliers are used for the pentagonal pit. For all these, the roots are multiplied by **0 | 6 | 7 | 24** to find the side from the area.

### On the Mathematics of Pits and the Method of Construction

In the text **The Mathematics of Pits** <term>Kunda-ganita</term>, the method of construction is as follows: 

A **circular pit** <term>vṛtta-kuṇḍa</term> is formed by rotating a cord at a distance of half its diameter from the center. 

For a **half-moon pit** <term>ardha-candra</term>, the wise should draw a circle with half the diameter and mark the points where it touches the boundary.

For pits with five, six, or seven sides, the width should be divided into seven equal parts. One should draw equal lines in each direction. Then, the chord <term>jyā</term> should be placed within the circle to create the entire shape. These shapes should be made for the patron <term>yajamāna</term> using his own **finger-breadth** <term>aṅgula</term> <note>a personalized unit of measurement based on the patron's physical body</note>. If these divisions are not used, the calculator must apply knowledge of proportions to determine the sides. 

A circular pit is equal-sided by nature. For the **vulva-shaped pit** <term>yoni-kuṇḍa</term> <note>a leaf or vulva-shaped pit associated with the creative power of the Goddess</note>, one should draw three circles. By rotating the cord from the centers of these circles, the shape is produced. After marking the sides, the remaining portion forms the "womb" of the pit. By using half the side-measure and drawing arcs from the corners, the curved "bow-like" sides are formed.

In all pits, one should first determine the specific length of the side and the width. These measurements are always calculated according to the finger-breadths of the patron. For pits of two cubits or more, the area is calculated by tripling the base unit. Using the method described for a one-cubit pit, the expert should calculate the sides and width. 

To draw the shape, one may use the **"fish" method** <term>matsya</term> <note>a geometric technique where two overlapping circles create a lens shape resembling a fish; the points where the circles intersect are used to draw a perfect perpendicular line</note>. By creating these "fish" intersections and rotating the cord, the four directions and the corners are accurately established.

# Thus, the Consideration of Mars

In this way, by the different methods of bringing forth the Sine <term>jiva</term>, and having performed the derivation of the final values <term>antya-anayana</term> through direct observation <term>pratyaksha-jnana</term>, one should divide by sixty <note>The standard sexagesimal base used in Indian astronomy</note>. Moreover, in the calculation of the Sine, by which the attainment of the arc-limit <term>kashta-gata</term> is established, those astronomical efforts are observed by scholars in mathematical systems. From the degrees <term>lava</term> and the traversed minutes <term>bhukta-kalika</term>, one makes the segments <term>khanda</term> that are yet to be traversed <term>bhogya</term>. By the divisor of sixty, the desired Sine is produced. For the purpose of the desired calculation, the purified Sine <term>jiva-vishuddha</term> is found. The sum of the direction of the cosine-result <term>Koti-phala: the horizontal component of a planet's displacement from its mean position</term> and its own direction constitutes the "corrected cosine." # The Rationale of the Corrected Circumference

Those circumferences, grasped through the second and third combinations, lead to a reduction in the smaller movements. What is the reason for this? This rationale of the results <term>Phala-vasana: the underlying geometric or physical logic behind a mathematical result</term> is quite marvelous. Due to the different positions on the celestial sphere <term>Gola: the armillary sphere or the global model of the heavens</term>, one observes the circumferences in the odd and even quadrants <term>Oja-yugma: 'odd' refers to the 1st and 3rd quadrants (0-90° and 180-270°), while 'even' refers to the 2nd and 4th (90-180° and 270-360°)</term> in succession.

Because the wise ancients did not specify the even quadrant <note>literally "the even step" or *pada*</note>, the odd quadrant alone was described for the sake of determining the circumference. 

<margin>
Now, with supporting evidence, this is presented as being free from errors. It is mostly derived from the three [methods] and situated upon the sphere; the reading is stated here as it is traditionally followed and well-known.
</margin>

By taking the sum of the sine-results of the odd and even quadrants and dividing by the even, the "true" or "corrected" <term>Sphuta: accurate or mathematically adjusted</term> value is produced through this calculation. These circumferences were formulated by the ancients so that, through various methods, the result remains the same. Just as a connection is established in the movement, so too should this be understood by those seeking to resolve the differences between the odd and even quadrants through the ratio of the sine. The true nature of the circumference is always established through the rationale of the result for the sake of higher knowledge. 

In this way, I have stated that the true circumference is 180 <note>original: "१८०", referring to the degrees of a semicircle or a specific ratio used in the calculation of the epicycle</note>. By this logic, the correction is made without confusion. In my own teaching, the reading of the even quadrant is not omitted; it is stated for the ease of understanding by those who follow the logic of others.

This is my reasoning. Even when the ratio is applied to the planetary results, the true circumference according to the Solar doctrine <term>Saura-mata: the school of thought belonging to the Surya Siddhanta, one of the most important Sanskrit astronomical treatises</term> should be expressed for the sake of clarity. In reality, the Solar doctrine must be considered here by those who have attained understanding. 

By the power of this position, who else but a scholar can grasp this? The science of things beyond the senses <note>original: "Atindriya-artha vijnanam", referring to astronomical phenomena that cannot be seen by the naked eye but are proven through math</note> is the proof. The logic applied here follows the ancient tradition, observing the flow of the ages <term>Kalpa: an immense period of time in Hindu cosmology, often used to calibrate long-term planetary cycles</term>. 

When this is said, the true circumference is joined to the arc of the sine-result. By applying the hypotenuse once, the astronomer finds the hidden correctness in the "Brahma-junction" <note>original: "Brahma-yoga", likely referring to the calculations of the Brahma Siddhanta or a specific planetary alignment</note>. Through the increase of this correctness, the results for the months are produced. Whether one begins the calculation from Aries <term>Mesha-adi: the start of the zodiacal signs, the vernal equinox</term> or from Libra <term>Tula-adi: the autumnal equinox</term>, the final result is unified.

# The Calculation of the Corrected Planet

In the fast-correction <term>Shighra-vedha: the process of adjusting a planet's position based on its conjunction with the Sun</term>, the mean position, when refined by the slow-correction <term>Manda-phala: the equation of center, which accounts for the eccentricity of an orbit</term>, becomes the "slow-corrected" <term>Mandasphuta: the longitude of a planet after the first major correction is applied</term> position. Alternatively, through the movement of the planet, the sine of the anomaly <term>Dorjya: the sine of the angular distance from the planet's apogee</term> is calculated from that center. When that arc is added in degrees, the result is the slow-corrected position. 

Alternatively, for the Sun and the Moon, that arc in this first quadrant—or in the sequence of quadrants starting from the first—is subtracted or added accordingly. All of this is done as before, utilizing the cosine <term>Koti: the perpendicular or cosine in Indian trigonometry</term> in the calculation. Thus, the corrected position of the Sun and Moon is obtained. For these two, the "fast-corrected" position does not exist, as they lack a fast-anomaly <term>Shighra-kendra</term>. 

For the other planets starting with Mars, the corrected process is desired. From the refinement of the slow-correction, the mean position becomes corrected; then, from the refinement of its own fast-correction <term>Shighraphala: the correction for the planet's second inequality, relating to its distance from the observer</term>, the planet's position is calculated "once" <note>This refers to the *Sakrit* method, an approximate single-step calculation</note>. In the mean calculation, half of the fast-correction and half of the slow-correction are applied to the mean planet. Then, the full slow-correction and the full fast-correction are applied.

According to the authority of the Solar sequence <term>Saurakrama: the traditional system or order of calculation in the Surya Siddhanta tradition</term>, the true action (calculation) follows only two corrections. In this way, I have explained it without contradiction. Here too, as with the slow-correction, the sine of the fast-anomaly is declared to be the corrected arc. 

In whatever third quadrant it may be, when that is added to or subtracted from the longitude, the corrected position or the "true" shadow <note>possibly referring to the gnomon shadow or arc calculation</note> is found. When the slow-correction is calculated "repeatedly" <note>original: *as akrit*; refers to an iterative process for higher accuracy</note>, the planet becomes truly "corrected." If the Sun's position is refined in reverse, the mean position is found. This is favored by the solar astronomers. If the result of the two corrections is applied once, only the Sun's center is corrected; otherwise, it remains the mean.

## The Rationale of Planetary Motion

Now, hear the rationale <term>Upapatti: a geometric or logical proof</term>. From the position of the mean planet, which is established in its own circle, the fast-anomaly is calculated. Likewise, the sine of the result of that anomaly is determined. Just as on the celestial sphere <term>Bhagola: the global model of the stars and planets</term>, the mean distance from the center is always maintained, so too should the lines and the sky be understood on the sphere.

Because of the speed of the motion, the planet shifts from its position. However, that shift is understood through the rotation <term>Bhramana: the revolving motion of the epicycle or the planet itself</term>. The planet does not move on its own center-born path; because of the difference in the sine of the second result, it exists between the center and the second center. One must perform calculations for both sines. Thus, from the center of its own established circle, in the sequence of the quadrants starting with Aries, the second corrected position is found.

The thread <note>original: *sutra*; a line of calculation or a chord</note> is refined by the mind. Similarly, for other regions, in the direction of the perigee <term>Nicha: the point closest to the Earth</term>, the thread reaches the end of the center. In the thread of the apogee <term>Uccha: the point farthest from the Earth</term>, one should envision the field of the planet. Similarly, in the perigee-thread, one should imagine it in the same way. Indeed, because of the distance or proximity created by these two actions, the motion in the center of the thread is determined by one's own intelligence. 

In the middle of the half-portions, that becomes the sine of the fast-correction. From the end of the apogee-perigee thread, the difference between the two is the sine of the corrected arc. For the sake of the wise, this is explained: when the disk of the planet is in its own circle or the circle of its rotation, the rotation of the disk occurs on that day through the agreement of the anomaly. Now, because of the rotation from the circle, at whatever time the disk rotates, its position is within the womb of that state. In that circle, starting from Aries, the corrected path is conceived for the sake of the result. Thus, this is given. To explain the method of calculation, I say: from the apogee, in reverse order, indeed...

# The Geometric Rationale of the Hypotenuse and Equation

Starting from the first point of Aries <note>The beginning of the zodiacal signs</note>, the ascensional difference is not calculated inversely. Here, the sine of the anomaly <term>Kendra-bhuja: the sine of the angular distance from the planet's apogee</term> is used to determine the half-difference. The line following the high and low points <term>Nicocha-sutra: the line of apsides connecting the apogee and perigee</term> acts as the guide. The base <term>Koti: the horizontal component or cosine in a right-angled triangle</term> is established here through observation, extending from the mean center.

The half-difference of the movement arises from the transverse line. That base-sine <term>Kotijya</term> and the resulting equation are combined or subtracted depending on whether the anomaly is in the quadrants beginning with Capricorn or Cancer <note>original: "mṛgakarki-kendre"</note>. Then, the square root of the sum of the squares <note>original: "kṛti-yoga-mūlaṃ"—this is the Pythagorean theorem</note> of those two sides is the hypotenuse <term>Karna: the distance from the center of the Earth to the planet</term>. This hypotenuse represents the distance from the center of the Earth <term>Ku-garbha: the "womb" or core of the Earth</term>.

Now, in this context, when the mean center is placed on the base-line of the yojanas <note>a traditional unit of distance</note>, where the line does not follow the mean path of the cycle, the "true result" <term>Sphuta-phala</term> is produced. Therefore, from the center of the planet’s disk, the base-line does not reach the center of the Earth directly. The sine of the anomaly of that center, divided by the hypotenuse, gives the equation.

From the radius <term>Trijya: the standard radius of the calculation circle, usually 3438 minutes</term>, the sine of the equation <term>Phalajya</term> is found. Even in the eccentric circle <term>Prativrtta: a circle whose center is offset from the Earth to explain non-uniform motion</term>, the sine of the equation is calculated by the same method. In the formula for this hypotenuse, the sine of the anomaly <term>Bhujajya</term> is the perpendicular side. By using the line of the planet's disk and the "small hypotenuse" line, the sine of the equation is produced. This is the reasoning established here for the calculation.

The mean planet is imagined at the center of the mean circle. The sine of the equation is then found in the circle of high and low points <term>Nicocha-vrtta: the epicycle</term>. The Earth is at the center, and the center of the Earth <term>Ku-garbha</term> is situated there. Knowledge of the height and depth is obtained by observing the planet's position above or below the horizontal line. The line connecting the high and low points meets the eccentric circle at the point where the planet's disk is located.

Because the mean circumference is adjusted by the distance of the anomaly, the position on the eccentric circle is determined. The perpendicular <term>Bhuja</term> and base <term>Koti</term> are then derived from the dimensions of the circumference. Through the result of this base and perpendicular, and from the line passing through the Earth, the hypotenuse is formed. In the center of the Earth's disk, the mean position is fixed. The distance from the Earth's center to the planet is the hypotenuse, where the base-sine is the base and the perpendicular-sine is the arm. 

When the mean planet is in the eccentric circle, the equation is formed. At that moment, the center of the second cycle <note>likely referring to the epicycle or a secondary correction circle</note> is not at the Earth's core. The sum of the bases and the perpendicular result in the center, and from that hypotenuse, the true mean position is calculated. This is the distance from the center of the Earth...

# On the Proportionality of the Hypotenuse and the Sine of the Equation

Through the proportion of the hypotenuse <term>Karna: the line representing the distance from the center of the Earth to the planet</term>, the desired result is obtained. The sine of the equation <term>Phala-jya: the trigonometric correction for the planet's position</term> is derived by dividing the components of the arc. From the resulting sine of the equation, the four <gloss>of the segments</gloss> differences in the results of the two base-sines are produced <insert>from the Romaka/Greco-astronomy traditions</insert>.

<insert>As per the remaining explanations</insert> The arrangement of the multipliers and divisors for the body of the planet is established through these methods. For the observer <note>original: "naro", literally 'man' or 'gnomon'</note>, the result is found by using the Radius <term>Trijya: the standard radius of the calculation circle</term> as the means of calculation. From the resulting calculation of the hypotenuse, similar results are born <insert>in the three-component sine</insert> <insert>following the rules of calculation</insert>. The result itself is indeed the sine of the equation in its proximity. According to the method of the sine of the equation, from the resulting sine of the base <term>Doh-phala</term>, the sine of the equation is declared here. This is what the text says here. 

In this way, when the sine of the equation is to be corrected, it is multiplied by the Radius and divided by the hypotenuse. Through these two corrected values, the sine of the base is established. Then, what would that sine of the equation be? It would be through the proportion of the hypotenuse. Even without making it "true" <term>Sphutatva: the process of correcting a mean value to a true value</term>, the result from the base-sine and the proportion of the hypotenuse is considered. Although it may seem like a small difference, the method is used for one's own purpose. In that context, the logic is joined with reasoning, because it has been examined through a logical process. 

The difference between the planet and the sun <note>original: "graharkayantaracaya"</note>, the calculation of the night, and the understanding of the directions are all stated here. Whether the corrected value should be used through the proportion of the hypotenuse or not—here, if the clarity of the result is not hindered by the calculation, the result is considered equivalent. Some say that from the sine of the base <term>Dor-jya</term> and the sine of the equation <note>original: "phalajyakasva"</note>, when calculated for the hypotenuse, the multiplier is used for the movement. Through the proportion of the planet's movement and the hypotenuse, the sine of the base is not destroyed. This occurs between the multiplier and the divisor. 

Thus, let it be said according to that school of thought. It does not appear correct to some because the hypotenuse is derived from the sine of the base and the sine of the equation, where the Radius is taken as the divisor. The difference in the hypotenuse is to be touched for the sake of the sine of the base; this is the logical refutation. Even for the sine of the base, the corrected state must be imagined from the proportion of the hypotenuse. This is the accepted view regarding the results of the planets. 

The true sine of the equation here does not arise simply from the mean planet. Having derived the hypotenuse from the mean center <term>Kendra</term>, the sine of the base is corrected. As it has been said in other treatises, I shall explain it here so that no doubt remains. Let us imagine the disk of the Sun, which is stronger than the mean line. From the mark of the mean planet, just as the horizontal line <term>Koti-sutra</term> is formed, so too is it formed from the mark of the true planet. In that place, the horizontal line is transverse, equal, and directed northward. 

When the disk is at the horizontal line of the mean position, the movement from the apogee <term>Uccha</term> and the anomaly <term>Kendra</term> are present. Now, when the disk has moved to another place, the true anomaly is formed from the apogee, starting from the line called the center of the Earth. From the intersection of these two horizontal lines, the angles formed are unequal. Their sines become uniform through their own actions. This is how it is understood by the wise.
