### On the Mathematical Application of the Hypotenuse and Corrections

Similarly, for the purpose of the explanation: at the twentieth degree, the <term>Phala-jya: the sine of the correction or equation</term> is extended. By adding the error of the <term>Karna: the hypotenuse, or the distance from the Earth to the planet</term> to the values derived from the mean center, the base-line <note>original: "bhujas"</note> is formed through the central chord. This is done upon the circle calculated by its own sine of the equation.

Following that specific method, even from the calculated results, as stated previously, the values for the base and the equation are projected onto the epicycle <note>original: "ucca-vritta", the higher circle or epicycle used in ancient models</note>. For that eccentric circle <note>original: "prati-vritta"</note>, when the <term>Karna</term> is positioned at the half-way point of the <term>Koti-sutra: the vertical cosine-line or chord</term>, its own sine of the equation remains in the middle. From that point, the base-result is born from the <term>Kendra: the anomaly or the angular distance from the planet's apogee</term>. The sine of the equation is then derived perfectly from that base. Joining these two values removes any doubt in the knowledge of the <term>Karna</term>. Where the intersection occurs, the <term>Sphuta: the true or corrected position</term> is established.

The "known" planets are those corrected by the equation. From the base-sine born of the <term>Kendra</term>, the <term>Sphuta</term> is determined. In this context, the corrected values of the planets are calculated by applying the equations to the <term>Karna</term>. As it is said: the value of the equation of the base, born from the true anomaly, is the corrected result. However, the calculation should be performed as before, with careful attention to the purity of the calculation. Therefore, in this system, having first projected all the <term>Madhyama: mean positions</term> of the planets, and having corrected them by the desired result, the base-sine of the corrected <term>Kendra</term> is produced. From this, the arc of the result of the base-sine is derived. This arc, when well-corrected, yields the result as stated.

The base-result arc is repeatedly calculated. Through the <term>Sphuta</term> derived from the sum of these parts, the final <term>Sphuta</term> is understood. Whether corrected or stationary, the base-result and the <term>Phala-jya</term> are calculated only once here. Alternatively, by the method of the <term>Karna</term>, the circles are drawn from the center of the Earth <note>original: "bhumi-garbha", literally the womb or center of the Earth</note>. Thus, the arrangement is otherwise clarified; the corrected position is understood through the chord-lines.

On the circle, wherever the mean planet is not situated, the <term>Sphuta</term> will be found along the line of the <term>Karna</term>. The difference between the <term>Sphuta</term> and the <term>Madhyama</term> is the "result" or equation. That which arises from the line of the formula is known as the "result of the base." Because it is derived from the proportion of the <term>Karna</term>, the sine and its arc represent the equation. In this way, through the cause of the mean calculation and the method of the circle, the position on the circle must be known by the wise.

In the radius-line of the sphere, ahead of the mean position, the <term>Sphuta</term> is established through that calculation. Anything else follows the previous logic of the planetary powers in sequence. This "joy of the results" <note>original: "moda-phala"</note> is the only liberation taught by the wise for all time. When the two results are equal, the lord of the circle <note>an epithet for the planet</note> is always at the center of the <term>Kendra</term>. Thus, the planet moves within its orbit.

To bring about the <term>Sphuta</term> position, the calculation is as follows:
1. The <term>Madhyama</term> is well-corrected by the <term>Manda-phala: the equation of the center</term> to become the "true-mean."
2. That "true-mean" is then well-corrected by the result of the <term>Shighra-phala: the equation of the apex or anomaly of parallax</term>.
3. This result is the "True Class" <note>original: "sphuta-varga"</note>.

Those who know the sphere say that the refinement of the results and the corrections lead to the <term>Sphuta</term>. This explanation of the sphere's divisions is provided by those who know the secrets of the calculated true positions of the planets. There is no fault in this explanation, only merit, especially for the Sun. This logic is always consistent. I shall now speak of the results born of the sphere, which are the causes of the equations, having observed them in the heavens.

## The Spheres of the High and Low Points

For the purpose of calculating the application of the high and low points <note>the apogee, or furthest point, and perigee, or nearest point</note>, by adding half the width of the disk <note>original: "vimbarddhena," referring to the planetary or solar diameter</note> to its own distance, those who know the spheres <note>original: "gola-vida"</note> determine that the **sphere of the maximum apogee** extends from the **center of the earth** <term>Kugarbha: the geocenter, used as the primary reference point in Indian astronomy</term>.

In the same way, from the radius of the perigee-hypotenuse of the slow motion <note>original: "mandagra-nica-karna"</note>, the **sphere of the perigee** is established. The movement of the sun’s disk <note>original: "arka-vimba-sancara"</note> never goes outside the sphere of the maximum apogee, nor does it ever stay in the space between the two spheres <note>the sun is always contained within the shell defined by these two boundaries</term>.

The **true distance** <term>Sphuta-karna: the actual, corrected distance from the Earth to the planet at a given moment</term> is indeed calculated by taking the distance from the apogee and subtracting the sine of the equation <note>original: "phalajya"</note> produced by the motion. This is connected to the sphere of the sun's disk. This distance, when halved, identifies the **true position** <note>original: "sphuta-dasa"</note>. The two spheres involved here are established as the **eccentric circle** <term>Pratimandala: a circle whose center is offset from the Earth's center to account for varying planetary speeds</term>; these two spheres are positioned within the middle of the "stomach" of the surrounding sphere. 

However, the sphere of the disk itself is not fixed. In the description of the clear sphere, the center of the eccentric circle and the center of the earth are separated by a distance of six <note>referring to a specific units of eccentricity</note>. These two centers are always fixed in their relation. Because of this, the **eccentric circle** is known by the wise as the path where the center of the sun's disk moves. The lords among the wise <note>original: "budhaindra," referring to great astronomers</note> understand that the variation in speed <note>original: "madhyavakata"</note> arises from the movement of the sun's center around this eccentric point.

When the movement towards the high and low points occurs, the diameter is divided into two parts. With the two poles <note>original: "kadambaka"</note> positioned one above the other, the displacement caused by the **slow motion equation** <note>original: "mandottha-phala"</note> can be easily understood. In the place where the two straight lines are tied—just as a string is tied between two poles—the distance is equal to the sine of the equation of the slow motion. The motion of the sphere-bearer <note>the planet</term> is known to be located at that true center.

The diameter of the eccentric circle <note>original: "pratimandala"</note> is always measured from the center of the earth by the sine of the equation. Because of the stability of the two poles, the movement of the sphere-bearer is constant. Even the **sphere of the maximum apogee** moves perpetually, but because of its great distance, it appears stable to those whose minds are at peace. Similarly, because of the stability of the positions, the sphere of the eccentric circle also moves.

The "mean anomaly" <note>original: "madhyama-kendra"</note> is described as the movement of the sun's disk-sphere within its own orbit. Just as the eccentric circle revolves, the disk of the sun moves with that same velocity. Wherever it revolves, the disk's position is made clear by the ray-string <note>the line of sight</note> originating from the center of the earth. For this reason, the sphere of the apogee and the eccentric circle are considered as one by the intelligent. 

Now, that place on the eccentric circle is known as the "high" <note>apogee</note>. Similarly, the "low" <note>perigee</note> is the place where the distance is least. Between these two, the sphere of the apogee sits in the middle of the eccentric sphere. Thus, within the sphere, the sun's disk moves according to the logic of the spheres. This explanation I have given is the correct method for the calculation of the spheres. 

Because of the movement of the disk, there is a thickness to the sphere of the "thousand-rayed one" <note>the sun</note>. This sphere of the perigee is equal to the eccentric sphere. It is known that the center of this thickness is the true center. By using the two poles, the calculation becomes clear...

# The Celestial Mechanics of the Eccentric Spheres

Regarding the points situated in the motion of celestial space, the regions surrounding what is called the **mean orbit** <term>Madhya-kakṣā: the theoretical circular path of a planet assuming constant speed</term> are established accordingly. Those two points which are fixed in the **eccentric sphere** <term>Nīcocca-gola: the 'high-low' sphere, an eccentric model where the center of the orbit is offset from the Earth</term> are related to the two poles. Here, the distance from the two poles is everywhere equal to the mean radius; this is well-established in this science. Just as the **celestial sphere** <term>Bhagola: the sphere of the fixed stars</term> moves due to the two poles, so too does the eccentric sphere move. Because of the motion of the two poles, the eccentric sphere also resides in a lower position, and therefore it moves along the circumference. For this reason, the movement of the apogee <note>the furthest point in the orbit</note> toward the east is described as the "direction of the high point." This high-low system is established because if the planet were located elsewhere, it would not consistently follow the calculated path from the two poles in the sky. By its own inherent motion, the center of the eccentric circle moves towards the west.

One should understand that the true sun is located on the circumference of the disk's sphere. If that sphere were fixed, then the motion would appear consistent in the sky; however, due to the rotation of the eccentric sphere, there is a deviation. In the circle that represents half of the celestial sphere, and in the eccentric circle, the positions of the high and low points are described in this manner. This conceptualization of the eccentric circle is made entirely to explain the observed motion. Where the **true planet** <term>Sphuṭa: the mathematically corrected, "clear" position of a planet as seen from Earth</term> is perceived, it is calculated by means of the **hypotenuse-radius** <term>Karṇa: the actual distance from the observer to the planet</term> and the sight-line. For the purpose of establishing the **true sun** <note>original: "sphuṭārka"</note> using the first method, the nature of the celestial sphere is shown here.

In this context, corrections such as those for the apogee are applied just as they are for the sun. For the purpose of attaining the results of the "slow" correction <note>original: "manda-phala," the first equation of center</note>, those who understand the spheres state that the planet moves with **mean velocity** <term>Madhyama-gatikā: the average speed of a planet</term>. That mean planet produces the desired result. The sun is called "slow-true" <note>original: "mṛdu-sphuṭa"</note> due to the connection with its disk. That "slow-true" position is not actually the true position of the planet <note>for planets other than the sun and moon, a second correction is needed</note>; rather, it serves as the center for the **fast-motion circle** <term>Śīghra-vṛtta: the second epicycle used to account for the Earth's own motion (the solar equation)</term>. 

Because of the planet's rapid motion, the distance from the "slow-true" position to the mean position must be understood. The great teachers say that the "slow-true" position is located on the circumference of the hypotenuse-circle, which extends from the center of the mean position's circumference to the planet's disk. Some authorities, however, say that the "slow-true" position is on the circle of the mean orbit, while others say it is on the circle of the hypotenuse. The **fast hypotenuse** <note>original: "śīghra-karṇa"</note> exists to determine the final true position. By knowing the "fast-result" <note>the second equation of center</note> relative to the "slow-true" mean, the position is calculated. Therefore, the slow motion must be projected onto the mean orbit. 

The results calculated from the circumference of the "slow" correction are understood through the "vector results" <note>original: "phala-jya," the sine of the equation</note>. However, these are established through the "slow-hypotenuse circle" and not the mean circle. Therefore, the results here are multiplied by the "slow-hypotenuse" to become true. For the purpose of calculating the result arising from one's own "slow-hypotenuse," one should use the method stated by **Bhaskara** <note>likely Bhaskara II, the 12th-century mathematician-astronomer</note> where the circumference is "corrected" by the hypotenuse. Alternatively, the result is found through the knowledge of the simple equation; by using the method to calculate the velocity, the result of the fast motion is clarified. Through these "vector results," the true hypotenuse and the position of the node <note>original: "pāta"</note> are determined. This writing on the results is in agreement with Bhaskara. Occasionally, there may be a difference...

# Mathematical Rationales of Planetary Equations

Because of the differences in results <note>original: "phala-prabhedān"</note> and contradictions in one's own goals arising from the dullness of one’s own intellect, it is stated that what has been explained is indeed correct. Since the distinction of the flow of logic here should be shown to me, I shall state it according to the established method. By the famous rule of the **center-line** <term>Kendra-sūtra: the line connecting the center of the earth with the center of the eccentric circle</term>, the hypotenuse <term>Karṇa: the variable distance from the Earth to the planet</term> is established in the field of calculation.

<margin>
✚
</margin>

If the **corrected circumference** <term>Sphuṭa-paridhi: the planetary epicycle adjusted for its distance</term> is not explicitly stated even when the division of the circle is made, then one must find it. By imagining the divisions of the circle based on the **sine of the anomaly** <term>Dorjyā: the R-sine of the planet's angular distance from its apogee</term>, the **sine of the equation** <term>Phala-jyākā: the sine of the arc representing the difference between the mean and true planet</term> is produced. There, the distance—the hypotenuse—is not suitable for kings <note>meaning: it is not authoritative or "standard"</note> because the union of the degrees of the signs within the circle is what matters. 

Furthermore, if the degrees of the signs in the circle arising from the force of the distinction are not clear, then their clarity is spoken of according to the situation. Therefore, for the sake of the result of the sine, a calculation is stated by the correctors. When joined with the **radius** <term>Trijyā: the standard R-sine, usually 3438 minutes</term>, the result is established through the corrected sine of the equation and the corrected sine of the anomaly. 

## The Authority of Bhaskara’s Commentary

As stated by the great astronomer **Bhaskara** in his verses, this corrected circumference is the means by which the sine of the equation becomes clear. The multiplier that is the circumference, when applied to the area, becomes the extent of the **rationale** <term>Vāsanā: the underlying logic or "proof" behind a mathematical formula</term>. For those who know this, the rule is joined with the distance of the own-circle. 

If this is not done, the clarity of the result arising from the **high-low system** <term>Nīcocca: the system of apogee and perigee</term> remains as previously stated, without any distinction. In the same way, the calculation of the **fast equation** <term>Śīghra-phala: the equation of conjunction, used to find the true position of a planet</term> involves a great deal of effort because of the difference in its nature. Therefore, this must be respected by the wise, as the essence of the results differs according to one’s own intellect. In the corrected works of Bhaskara, for those whose minds are wrongly inclined, their own favorable arguments are not effective here. One who repeatedly tries to destroy this logic only destroys their own understanding. 

### On the Inclined and Eccentric Circles

These positions are thus established as "corrected." However, whether they are in the **ecliptic** <term>Vrtta: the path of the sun</term> or the **inclined circle** <term>Vimaṇḍala: the secondary circle or orbit of a planet inclined to the ecliptic</term> is a doubt we shall now resolve for the benefit of the students. 

Through the rotation of the **eccentric circle** <term>Pratimaṇḍala: the circle whose center is offset from the Earth</term>, the movement in the inclined circle creates the degrees, minutes, and **seconds** <term>Vikalā: 1/60th of a minute of arc</term> beginning from Aries. By the rotation of the eccentric circle and the inclined circle, the **planetary disk** <term>Bimba</term> moves. When the disk is in the ecliptic, it creates the degrees and minutes starting from Aries. 

<note>The text here contrasts the movement of the physical planet (disk) with the theoretical points on the eccentric circle.</note>

The movement of the disk from the eccentric circle does not occur in the ecliptic in every case. Because, in brief, the rotation that occurs through the eccentric circle is the movement of the disk; that circle is the corrected one. The two positions of the disk in the ecliptic are clear in the inclined circle at the **nodes** <term>Pāta-sthāna: the points where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</term>. Immediately after that, the planet is joined with its node, and the distance represents the **latitude** <term>Śara: literally 'arrow', the celestial latitude or perpendicular distance from the ecliptic</term> in the circle of the **poles of the ecliptic** <term>Kadamba-vṛtta</term>. This continues until the node is reached. From the two values of the planet-with-node and the latitude, the mathematical calculation proceeds.

In the **circle of visibility** <term>Dṛgvṛtta: the vertical circle passing through the planet and the zenith</term>, the calculation should be performed here. Following the sequence of its own quadrants, the **node** <term>Pāta: the point where the planetary orbit intersects the ecliptic</term> is subtracted according to the rules of spherical geometry. Thus, the corrected planet <note>original: "saṃskṛtagrahaḥ"</note> is established in the zodiacal circle. Therefore, according to the stated method, the **true planets** <term>Sphuṭa-graha: the actual observed position of a planet</term> should be understood at the beginning of the visible circle and at the end of the inclined orbit <term>Vimandala</term>. However, they are not naturally found in the zodiacal circle; thus, I shall properly explain the reason why their clarity is derived from the zodiacal circle.

The **cosine** <term>Koṭijyā</term> of the true planet, which has been added to the result of its own conjunction, is multiplied by the **Radius** <term>Tribhajyakā: literally "the sine of three signs" or 90 degrees</term>. In this context, the cosine is diminished... <note>The text here uses cryptic word-numerals to describe specific arcs or ratios</note> the positions are determined by the planet joined with its node. When the node is subtracted from the planet in the zodiacal circle, it is considered "attached." In such a calculation, when the planetary motion is small, the latitude is perceived. Everywhere in the calculation of the inclined orbit, the planets are accepted as being in the zodiacal circle for the sake of the results. 

In the mathematical calculations for the Sun and others, when considering them merely in the circular motion, their positions are refined over many days. Between these two positions, if the daily velocity <term>Bhukti</term> is greater in the middle, it indicates **retrograde motion** <term>Vakra-gati</term>—if the planet is indeed moving retrogradely. In the circle of the retrograde path, the result for the planet is always determined <gloss>by its own sounds/vibrations</gloss> to be in a state of "non-existence" or zero-point. 

The values for these calculations are given as: 154, 145, 130, 163, and 115 <note>These likely refer to the parameters for the five visible planets: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>. These relate to the "Death-Cycles" <note>original: "mṛtyu-cakraiḥ"</note> and the roots of the planets. For Mars <note>original: "kujāt"</note>, the remaining values are: 
156 | 126 | 1230 | 187 | 245. 

With these very values, the retrograde motion is determined. In retrograde motion, the planet suffers "affliction" <note>a term used when a planet's motion or position is astrologically or mathematically challenged</note>. Even while moving in its own orbit, the planet's motion is not uniform. The **true motion** <term>Sphuṭa-bhukti</term> is calculated from its position at the **perigee** <term>Nīca: the point closest to Earth</term> or **apogee** <term>Ucca: the point farthest from Earth</term>. 

In this system, the logic is that the velocity of Mars and the others is always greater than the **mean velocity** <term>Madhyama-bhukti</term> when they are near the perigee. Therefore, in the celestial sphere, one imagines the planets moving in their own orbits. When the planet is corrected by the **equation of the center** <term>Phala: the difference between mean and true positions</term>, the motion is established through the **conjunction** <term>Śīghra-phala: the second equation of the planets, related to their distance from the Sun</term>. 

Thus, the true motion is refined by the difference in the equations. The planet, when refined by the equation of the center and the equation of conjunction, becomes "true" <note>sphuṭa</note>. Otherwise, the velocity is merely the "refined daily motion." Thus, the true planet is not different from the form of the planet refined by its daily velocity. This is easily understood everywhere. Just as the difference in the equations is applied to the motion, the true planet is ahead or behind based on whether that motion is increasing or decreasing.

# On the True Daily Motion and Retrograde Motion

In the second quadrant <note>original: "dvitiyapade", referring to the 90-180 degree arc of the planet's anomaly</note>, as the correction of the result decreases, the **mean daily motion** <term>Manda-bhukti: the planet's daily speed corrected by its first inequality</term> itself determines the **true daily motion** <term>Sphuṭa-bhukti: the actual observed daily velocity</term>. As long as the mean motion remains greater than the difference of the equation, the subsequent true position is greater than the preceding one, though the rate of increase is diminishing. After this, when the difference of the equation <note>original: "phalottara"</note> becomes equal to the mean motion, there is an absence of true motion; at that moment, the two subsequent positions become equal. This occurs near the **perigee** <term>Nīca: the point in the orbit closest to Earth</term>, where **retrograde motion** <term>Vakra-gati: the apparent backward motion of a planet</term> begins. Because the mean motion is exceeded by the decrease of the equation as it approaches perigee, the subsequent true position is actually less than the current one.

In the third quadrant, due to the increase of the equation, the mean motion is once again adjusted. When the difference of the equation is equal to the mean motion, the lack of true motion—the stationary point—occurs again. This marks the end of retrograde motion and the beginning of **direct motion** <term>Mārga-gati: the normal forward movement of a planet</term> near the perigee, as the subsequent positions begin to increase again. In the fourth quadrant, due to the decrease in the result, the true motion is the mean motion combined with the difference of the equation; thus, the true motion continues to increase. 

Through this peculiar behavior of the **mean motion** <term>Manda-gati</term>, the rules of calculation are established. Some scholars have pointed out errors in the texts regarding the knowledge of the exact time when true motion ceases. Truly, it must be understood that when the result decreases, it leads to retrograde motion, and when the result increases, it leads to the start of direct motion. 

This logic remains consistent: even when the **anomaly** <term>Kendra: the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</term> is the same, the planet's center is perceived to move according to the quadrants. Retrograde motion begins in the first quadrant and direct motion begins in the second quadrant, specifically when approaching the perigee. It should not be feared that the true position would be calculated incorrectly here. Between the two results of the equation, there is no lack of mathematical logic. I have here provided the true explanation <note>original: "subāsanā"</note> of the retrograde and direct paths in a concise manner, as the previous methods were fully described. 

When the planet's disk is at the intersection of two circles, there is an absence of equation in its motion. Regarding what was said by **Bhaskara** <note>referring to Bhaskara II, the 12th-century astronomer</note> in his own treatise, some learned men are not satisfied, as they claim it contradicts the logic of **spherical mathematics** <term>Gola-yukti</term>. Through this, the movement of the planet and its equations should be understood by the wise...

## On the Intersection of Orbits and the Spherical Reality

The sages do not observe the result of the motion exceeding twenty <note>original: "vimshottaram", likely referring to a specific arc or limit in the calculation of the equation of center</note> by the results of the observed motion and the minutes of arc. This **intersection** <term>Sampata: the point where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> is established precisely because of the equality of the results in the difference of one day; when there is equality, there is an absence of the result. As it has been said: "Where there is a meeting of the preceding and succeeding positions, they meet at a single point." In the difference of one day, there should be equality. When the results are small, it is constant for all time. Because of the Sun's correction, at the time of the intersection, there is no ... <note>The text is partially broken here</note> ... the sphere exists. The Sun is from that ... those who ...

-> **The Critique of the Unlearned** <-

In this way, through the method of the **planetary spheres** <term>Graha-gola-riti: the study of the physical or geometric arrangement of celestial spheres</term>, the state of the **mean** <gloss>average position</gloss> and **true** <gloss>observed position</gloss> is divided. From the intersection, as previously stated, the mean circle and the circle of the path meet as is appropriate. If one says that the result of the Sun's motion should be destroyed, then in their own treatise, when the intersection is reached, they do not properly conceive of the circles. Those who do not understand the rationale <note>original: "vasana", the underlying geometric explanation</note> behind this are fools <note>original: "murkhah"; a common polemical term in Sanskrit scientific texts for those who calculate by rote without geometric understanding</note>. In the absence of the result of the meeting, they imagine a false division.

Combined with the result of the motion, at the **apogee** <term>Ucca: the point furthest from Earth</term> and **perigee** <term>Nīca: the point closest to Earth</term>, the planet's correction is used. Where the intersection occurs, the result is brought about by the higher corrections. In the **ecliptic** <term>Nabhoga: literally "sky-goer", the path of the planet</term>, the perigee and apogee are on the circumference. Where they are perfectly in one place, according to the rule of the **geocentric ray** <term>Nirgarbha-sutra: a line drawn from the center of the Earth to the planet</term> that goes outward, at that true location, the planet possesses its calculated result. Its mean position is then smaller or larger than the true position.

Those who do not know the truth of the planetary spheres, their minds are dull. Just as from the **celestial globe** <term>Bhagola: the sphere of the fixed stars</term>, the form of the Sun's sphere exists... <note>original: "arka-sphat", referring to the solar orbit</note>. The spheres are positioned six signs apart from one another. The **inclined orbit** <term>Vimandala: the secondary circle or eccentric orbit</term> is known by its latitude. In that sphere, which goes through the center of the celestial globe, there the intersection occurs. It is not at the center, but rather from the center of the earth (geocentric). Therefore, the sine of the correction should be calculated.

Just as the **eccentric circle** <term>Prativrtta: an epicycle or off-center circle used to explain non-uniform motion</term> is a sphere in the heavens, situated in the expanded circle, so it is in the celestial sphere. One sphere is the celestial globe, the other is the eccentric sphere. Where the two meet is the eccentric intersection. In the half-sphere of the apogee, they are the nodes <note>original: "pata"</note>. The sphere is above that circle. To find the other position in those two, at the meeting of the apogee-sphere and the celestial globe, there is the sphere of the nodes. From those two positions, the mean is that which resides in the half-sphere. With the two celestial poles, at the point of the node-intersection, it rotates. The first is to the East, the second is to the West. Between the two teachings, for the people... in the circle, the sequence of the expanded body... from the place of intersection, it is indeed geocentric. It is not at the center, nor is it of the two spheres. The location of the node-sphere's rotation from the node is the difference **12 : 28** <note>This likely refers to a specific constant in degrees or minutes related to the motion of the lunar or planetary nodes</note>. Because of the rotation of the node-sphere in the expanded circle, an impossibility does not arise; the intersection rotates.

# The Geometry of the Planetary Spheres and Latitudes

The detailed distinction of the **true motion** <note>original: "sphuta-gati"</note> arises from the various divisions of the deity-like rotations of the heavens. When a person observes the **slow-corrected position** <term>Mridu-sphuta: the planetary longitude after the first correction for its elliptical orbit</term>, it is clear that there is no other path. This is because the circle of the **fast motion** <term>Shighra-gati-mandala: the circle representing the planet's synodic cycle, relating to its position relative to the Sun</term> exists above the sphere for Mars and the other planets.

-> **On the Motion within the Celestial Disk** <-

When the planet is situated there, one observes its rapid movement. In the **celestial disk** <gloss>the visible body of the planet in the sky</gloss>, the slow-moving one wanders; however, the "slow" and "true" results are not different in their essence. Between these two, the **inclined orbit** <term>Vimandala: the secondary circle or orbit inclined to the ecliptic</term> intersects. From the center of the Earth, the master of the forms observes the intersection of the circles. One point is high, and the second is low. In the union of these **perigee and apogee circles** <note>original: "nica-ucca-vritta", referring to the epicycles</note>, the famous calculation is established.

The master of forms is served by the sphere of the inclined orbit. Indeed, it is said that the **nodes** <term>Pata: the points where the orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> are seen from the center of the sphere. The half of the sphere is moved elsewhere, and thus it is seen there. From the center of the observation point, the sphere of the inclined orbit is considered. From that very point, the sphere of the nodes is positioned.

-> **The Calculation of Latitude and Longitudinal Corrections** <-

Even if there is a difference in parts among other theories, the intersection occurs in the same direction by its motion. Because of the **fast eccentric circle** <note>original: "shighra-prativritta"</note> and its relationship to the earth-centered sphere, the spheres are united in a single place. From the center of the earth, in the circle of the planetary sphere, the inclined orbit is established. If there is a shift, the position of that inclined orbit is stated by others to be in a different place. The sphere of the inclined orbit is situated within the planetary sphere.

In this text, the two spheres are the basis for the calculation. The **sine of the latitude** <note>original: "shara-jya"</note> in the inclined orbit cannot be calculated otherwise. For one situated there, looking at the fast-motion circle, the measurement is taken from the magnitude of the planet's disk. In that sphere, the latitude is established not by the intersection alone, but by the celestial circle.

According to the sacred tradition, the **slow-corrected** <gloss>Mridu-sphuta</gloss> position is always used. They say the **true planet** <gloss>Sphuta-kheta</gloss> is found in the path of the nodes. In other systems, the parts are stated differently. For the planets, the difference is to be understood in their own circles. For Mars and others, the minutes of arc of the nodes are given; in the **Solar Treatise** <note>original: "Saura", referring to the Surya Siddhanta</note>, these are stated as the **mean minutes** <gloss>madhyama-liptika</gloss>.

When these are multiplied by the results of the "fast" calculation, they become **true** <gloss>sphuta</gloss> in their own orbits. By the inverse of the fast-motion result, the node is corrected. Because of this trueness, the learned find the nodes. By moving according to these nodes, the slow-corrected positions are reached. This is accomplished through the center of the circle, and when subtracted, it becomes the **true position** <note>original: "sphuta"</note> starting from the sign of Aries in the celestial circle.

The **latitudes** <term>Shara: the celestial latitude or distance from the ecliptic</term> of these are explained by some in a contradictory manner. Therefore, the goal of the **experts in the spheres** <note>original: "gola-vid"</note> is to seek the result through the slow-corrected motion. Following the rules of the sphere, under the authority of the Sun, I have spoken to dispel the confusion arising from the errors of others regarding these latitudes.

# The Principles of True Motion and the Celestial Sphere

Without a true understanding of the meaning, even a person with a steady mind cannot grasp the essence of these rotations. The descent of knowledge is not for those who merely follow, nor for those who contend with the great masters like Bhishma; rather, the meaning becomes **clear** <note>original: "sphuṭaḥ"</note> only when one observes the movement of the planets among the stars. In the middle of the celestial path, the highest truth remains hidden unless one resolves the contradictions between the observed positions and the mathematical models. The **universal master** <note>likely referring to the Sun as the primary mover</note> exists in the path of the stars, and through his refined movements, the fate of the "rising and setting" is determined. For the devotees of this science, the fractions of the arc—the **minutes** <term>Kala: a unit of angular measurement equal to 1/60th of a degree</term> and **seconds** <term>Lipti: another term for arc-minutes or small divisions of time</term>—reveal the true children of the stars. Whether the planet is visible or hidden by the rays of the Sun, its true position is calculated by its "rising" from the mathematical horizon.

## Calculations of the Solar and Lunar Positions

The results of the **true motion** <note>original: "sphuta-phala"</note> are derived from the movement of the Sun. By multiplying the Sun's daily motion by the desired time, one finds the position at the **equinox** <term>Vishuvat-kalika: the time when the Sun crosses the celestial equator</term>. If the difference between the mean and true Sun is small, the calculation remains simple; however, when the motion varies, one must apply the difference in velocity to achieve accuracy. The success of determining the true position of the Moon and Sun depends on the union of their velocities. 

-> **On the Lunar Calendar and Time** <-

Through the power of the true positions of the Sun and Moon, the **lunar days** <term>Tithi: a lunar day based on the longitudinal distance between the Sun and Moon</term> and the **half-lunar days** <term>Karana: an astrological period equal to half a Tithi</term> are established. These are not merely counts of time but are derived from the "cruel" and "gentle" movements of the luminaries. Starting from the "Bird" <note>Shakuni, the name of a specific Karana</note>, these periods follow in sequence. The **time-minutes** <term>Nadika: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> that have passed or are yet to come are determined by the planet's own velocity. The accuracy of these **lunar days** <note>original: "tithyādikā"</note> should be refined by experts who understand the multiplication of time-units and the divisions of the celestial circle. If one calculates backwards due to **retrograde motion** <note>original: "vakra"</note>, the timing of the "knots" or nodes must be handled with care.

## The Movement of the Celestial Sphere

The light of the Sun illuminates the world, and in the forest of the stars, the movement is measured with extreme precision. By observing the subtle divisions of the arc, one can see the truth of the year and the cycles of time. If the **ascendant** <term>Lagna: the point of the ecliptic that rises on the eastern horizon</term> is not calculated correctly, then the resulting ritual or astronomical work will not match the reality of the **celestial globe** <term>Drig-gola: the visible sphere of the sky</term>. Without this harmony, the conjunction of planets cannot be accurately determined. 

In this era, the **celestial sphere** <term>Bhagola: the sphere of the fixed stars</term> is seen to revolve, and within the circle of the zodiac signs, the parts are seen to shift. This is the **precession** <note>original: "ayanam", referring to the shift of the equinoxes</note> that causes the directions to change over vast scales of time. Some scholars speak of the "pole-intersection" as the node itself. This movement is well-known in the **Solar Treatise** <note>original: "Sauram", referring to the Surya Siddhanta</note>. When the sphere is situated at the celestial equator, the signs seem to move toward the west, but then they appear to return or "revolve" based on the degrees of precession. When the Sun reaches the first point of Aries, it marks a specific direction, and from there, the rotation continues.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 9 regarding the correction of planetary positions. It moves from the geometry of the orbits to the specific calculations of the precession of the equinoxes (Ayanāṃśa), citing the Surya Siddhanta and comparing different astronomical traditions including the Roman (Romaka) school to harmonize their findings.</meta>

Through the degrees and established positions at the **vernal equinox** <term>Meṣamukha: literally "the face of the Ram" (Aries), the point where the Sun crosses the celestial equator</term>, it has been declared by the gods and the steadfast sages in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>One of the most foundational and sacred ancient Sanskrit texts on astronomy</note> that the motion is self-evident. This movement, or the shifting of the equinoxes, is calculated through the **revolutions** <term>Bhagaṇa: a complete orbit or cycle</term> within a vast cycle of time. In a Great Age, these revolutions are measured as six hundred thousand (600,000). To find the true position in a given year since the start of the era, the elapsed years of the **Kali Yuga** <note>The current "Age of Iron" in Hindu cosmology, which began in 3102 BCE</note> are divided by 7,200. The resulting figure provides the **precession of the equinoxes** <term>Ayanāṃśa: the angular distance between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term>.

By adding these degrees of precession to the planet’s position, one obtains the **tropical position** <term>Sāyana-graha: the position of a planet relative to the equinoxes rather than the fixed stars</term>. Without accounting for this shift, one cannot correctly calculate the **declination** <term>Krānti: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</term> or the **ascensional difference** <term>Chara: the difference between a planet's actual rising time and its rising time at the equator</term>. Those who truly understand the principles of the "meeting points" <note>referring to the intersection of the ecliptic and the equator</note> know that the mathematics must align with observation. Others may be confused by these shifting points, but for the sake of achieving accurate results in our rituals and calendars, these calculations are essential. 

The learned say that even if there are slight variations in the descriptions—perhaps due to the vastness of the heavens—the movement of the Sun through the zodiac remains the standard. Some scholars argue over the specific era or the precise number of minutes, but as the *Surya Siddhanta* states, the contradiction is only apparent, not real. 

Even the **Roman school** <note>original: "Romaka," referring to the astronomical system influenced by Western/Alexandrian traditions</note> and the authors of the **Five Treatises** <note>original: "Pañca-siddhāntikā," a famous compendium by Varahamihira</note> have deliberated on these corrected movements of the stars. While they may describe the location of the gods or the results of the "day and night of the gods" <note>A traditional way of describing the polar day and night cycles</note> using different starting points, the underlying mathematical truth remains consistent. There are many ways to approach the calculation, but they do not negate one another.

Therefore, to resolve any perceived conflict between the ancient scriptures and observed reality, we follow the methods clarified in the *Surya Siddhanta*. This ensures that the measurement of the Sun’s **transit** <term>Saṃkrānti: the moment the Sun enters a new zodiac sign</term> is precise. Because the Sun’s disk has a physical size, its crossing is not a single instantaneous point but a process. The holy time of the transit occurs when the center of the **Solar disk** <note>original: "Arka-bimba"</note> touches the boundary of the new sign. This subtle timing is vital for the spiritual fruits of our ceremonies. 

For those who understand the **celestial sphere** <term>Gola: the globe of the heavens</term>, the distance in **Yojanas** <note>An ancient unit of distance, roughly 8–15 kilometers</note> and the true diameter of the planetary bodies are calculated through these same principles. When the Sun and Moon have the same "result" <note>likely referring to their longitude</note>, it is the basis for calculating the lunar days and eclipses.

ज्याक <note>This is a catchword—a partial word from the next page included to help the reader or binder maintain the correct sequence. It refers to "Jyā," the Sanskrit term for a chord or sine in trigonometry.</note>

<summary>This page explains the calculation of the precession of the equinoxes (Ayanāṃśa) according to the Surya Siddhanta and argues that different astronomical schools, including the Roman/Romaka, ultimately describe the same celestial reality.</summary>
<keywords>Ayanāṃśa, Precession, Surya Siddhanta, Romaka, Declination, Saṃkrānti, Celestial Sphere, Kali Yuga</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical calculations from page 37. While the previous page focused on the precession of the equinoxes (Ayanāṃśa) and tropical positions, page 38 shifts focus to calculating the "corrected distance" of planets in units of yojanas and reconciling different mathematical traditions, specifically comparing the Surya Siddhanta with Bhaskara’s Siddhanta Shiromani.</meta>

# Tattva-Viveka — 38

<margin>
Tattva-
Vi-
38
</margin>

The **corrected distance in yojanas** <term>Yojana-sphuṭa-karṇa: the true linear distance of a planet from the Earth, measured in ancient Indian miles</term> is now explained. For Mars and the other planets, there are four ways that this true clarity is achieved. In the calculation of these values, great effort <note>original: "mahā-prayāsa"</note> is required according to traditional methods. To avoid this difficulty, and for the sake of practical usage, I shall speak specifically regarding the calculation of the **hypotenuse** <term>Karṇa: in Indian astronomy, this refers to the distance between the center of the Earth and the center of the planet</term> according to the Great Surya tradition and the teachings of the great sage Shaktya. 

The distance is derived from the sum of the radius <term>Trijyā: the sine of 90 degrees, often used as a constant in spherical trigonometry</term> and the planet’s specific variables. When the mean orbit is multiplied by the radius and divided by the distance, the result is the **corrected distance in yojanas**. This is also confirmed by the authorities of the *Vishnudharmottara Purana* <note>An ancient encyclopedic text containing sections on cosmosgraphy and astronomy</note>. It should be understood that whether one follows the calculation of twenty-four thousand or other systems, the principle remains consistent. 

In the **Siddhanta Shiromani** <note>The "Crown of Treatises," a seminal astronomical work written by Bhaskara II in 1150 CE</note>, which is the jewel of universal systems, the calculation of the distance in yojanas is described according to the light of that tradition. The maker of the planetary clarity <note>original: "grahaspaṣṭakartā"</note> determines that the corrected distance becomes clear when divided by the radius of the sphere. Thus, moved by the prodding wind <note>Pradhānavayu: the cosmic wind believed to drive the celestial spheres</note>, the distance in yojanas is multiplied by the sun's rays. 

When the **distance of the slow-moving planet** <term>Manda-karṇa: the distance to a planet calculated based on its "slow" or eccentric anomaly</term> is divided by the radius, the corrected distances in yojanas are obtained. For Mars and the others, the measurement in yojanas is established through this process. This method of calculating the **hypotenuse-yojana** <note>original: "karṇa-yojanā"</note> is well-known in the calculations of the great masters.

Furthermore, there is a perceived difference between the **mean distance** <term>Madhya-śruti: the average orbital distance</term> and the result of the sine-distance calculations. When examined closely, this difference arises from the observation of the **planetary disks** <term>Vimba: the apparent disk or diameter of a planet as seen from Earth</term>. Sages and astronomers have noted that the size of the disk appears differently depending on the distance. Because of this, the calculation cannot be done otherwise than by mathematical methods. 

Without understanding the corrected distance, the measurement of the disk's circumference remains unclear. The great teacher Aryabhata and others have spoken of this. When the sun is at its **perigee** <term>Nīca: the point in an orbit closest to the Earth</term> or **apogee** <term>Ucca: the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</term>, the apparent size of the disk changes. Specifically, when the planet is at its lowest point (perigee), the disk appears larger, and when at its highest point (apogee), it appears smaller. 

If one claims that the disk size is constant, it would contradict the lived observation <note>original: "loke viruddha"</note> of the world. Therefore, the method shown here follows the reasoning of Bhaskara to avoid contradiction. The corrected distance in yojanas is the true path. Whether the calculation involves Mars or the Sun, the distance in the eccentric circle and the movement in the orbit must be harmonized. When the two are equal at the time of calculation, the truth of the planetary position is revealed.

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical procedures for calculating the corrected distance (Karṇa) of planets in yojanas, citing authorities like Bhaskara and the Surya Siddhanta to explain how planetary distances affect the apparent size of their disks.</summary>

...the meeting [of these theories] is not [always possible], and those who look upon it with a biased eye are troubled; they are even ashamed before the *Yavanas* <term>Yavana: a term historically referring to Greeks, but in Kamalakara’s 17th-century context, it refers to Islamic/Persian astronomers</term>. For those who serve only one school, whether it be small or great, it is the creator of their own confusion. I do not know why these fools <note>original: "mūrkhāḥ"</note>, despite possessing some knowledge, are struck by the signs of the northern path in such a way. It is clearly stated in the Vedas <note>original: "veda-vido"</note> that those two [methods] are for the sake of determining the observed meaning. When the observation does not match the determined meaning, it is a calamity; for the sake of achieving the desired result, one must not rely on a seedless <note>meaning: "baseless" or "unproductive"</note> tradition. In the vastness of authoritative proof, those intent on performing their duties must be diligent. 

Some others, despite being capable, are distracted by the minds of others and give up, suffering the bitter fruit of their actions. Because of this, they do not attain the goal. Therefore, one should understand the nature of Time <note>original: "kālātmanam"</note> through various actions, which is established through the solar time systems.

**Thus ends the Chapter on the True Positions of the Planets in the *Siddhanta Tattva Viveka* composed by the illustrious Kamalakara.**

Direction, place, and time are useful in all matters; without them, the science of travel and astronomical observation cannot exist. Therefore, I shall now describe the arrangement of the celestial sphere <term>Khagola: the imaginary sphere of the sky with the Earth at its center</term>, without which the composition of mathematical astronomy is incomplete.

Even though the earth is moving, as some say, or if it is stationary <note>Kamalakara refers here to the debate between the stillness of the Earth and the theories of rotation</note>, it is situated in its own orbit. One must imagine the Earth’s interior, the atmosphere, and the celestial spheres. For the sake of mathematical brevity, the wise have established the center of the Earth as the starting point. From that center, there are six directions. For those who are proficient in the calculation of the sphere, the subtle mathematics of the globe arise from the intersection of these lines. The center of the Earth is the heart of the mystery. From the center of the sphere, one should draw the Prime Meridian <term>Yāmyottara-vṛtta: the 'south-north circle' or local meridian</term> and the horizon. 

One must understand the equatorial region <term>Nirakṣadeśa: 'the place without latitude,' meaning the Equator</term>. The prime meridian passes through the city of Lanka <note>In Indian astronomy, Lanka is a theoretical point on the equator with zero longitude, not necessarily the modern island of Sri Lanka</note> and the mountain of the gods [Meru]. The distance between one's own location and this meridian is what allows us to calculate the difference in local time. The celestial poles <term>Dhruva: the fixed points around which the heavens appear to rotate</term> are situated at the top and bottom of this system. Because of the power of the *Pravaha* wind <note>In ancient Indian cosmology, the 'Pravaha' is the celestial wind believed to drive the rotation of the planets and stars</note>, the entire sphere rotates, and it is through this rotation that we determine the time of day at various longitudes, east or west. 

<summary>This page concludes the chapter on planetary positions and introduces the principles of spherical geometry, geography, and the Prime Meridian system used to calculate local time and position.</summary>

He is defining the fundamental circles and poles used to measure the stars.</meta>

</margin>

From the **Northern Pole** <note>original: "saumyadhruva," the North Celestial Pole</note>, if one moves toward the North, the **declination** <term>declination: the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator</term> becomes less. At that time, on the circle where the units of time and the **pole of the ecliptic** <term>kadamba: the pole of the ecliptic, located 23.5 degrees from the celestial pole</term> meet, the rotation of the signs is determined. This is fixed upon the circle of the **equinoxes** <note>original: "nāḍikā vṛtta," the celestial equator</note>. 

The points where this circle meets the path of the signs are known as **Aries** and **Libra**. In the opinion of the unified schools, these signs are calculated from the Sun's position. From the equatorial circle, when the Moon reaches the half-point of Aries, it is said by the wise to be the **Equinox** <note>original: "viṣuva"</note>. This is the place of the separation of Indra <note>original: "śakra viraho," a poetic reference to the east or a specific astronomical alignment</note>. There, in the west, the order is always the same.

In the southern and northern paths, the two **poles of the ecliptic** <note>original: "kadamba-dvaya"</note> are joined to the seven circles. At the point of the **ascendant** <term>lagna: the point of the ecliptic that rises on the eastern horizon</term>, from that point of the solstice, the equatorial line arises. Some scholars say that in that place, there is a single form of the sphere <note>original: "eka rūpe prabvadeti kecit"</note>. 

However, because of the contradictions in their views, I say that the equatorial line arises from its own position. In the world, when we look at the two solstices, the intersection of the two circles is determined by the signs. From the pair of signs, the arc of the celestial sphere is produced. From the **North and South Poles**, these circles of time are determined, and there, the celestial pole is shifted by the northern declination. Thus, the wise have described the appearance of the celestial sphere. 

In this circle, which is equal and balanced, the planets revolve. According to the measure of the "arm" <term>bhuja: the sine of the arc used in spherical trigonometry</term>, the planetary orbits are calculated. These orbits are always tied to the motions of the planets. This is how the entire movement of the planets is described by the learned. The middle point is fixed above, and it is perceived through the **pure observation of the eyes** <note>original: "dṛg-amala," referring to the importance of aligning mathematical theory with visual observation</note>. 

When the planets move, one must imagine the "Earth-born" <note>Mars</note> and others on their respective paths. With the hand, one can demonstrate how the equatorial circle and the circle of observation meet. This is the purpose of the **Gola** <term>Gola: an armillary sphere or a wooden model of the celestial sphere used for instruction</term>. In the middle of this globe, which is constructed with accurate measurements, the knowledge of the sphere becomes clear. At the center of that circle, one should imagine the Earth. 

By the rule of that sphere, the two poles are positioned obliquely. From the equatorial circle, the path goes toward the north and south. This circle is called the **equinoctial** <note>original: "nāḍī-valaya"</note>. Where the two poles of the ecliptic move, and where the path is tilted, that intersection is fixed. In the sphere, there are two half-circles that are distinct from each other. Under the power of these, the northern and southern paths of the stars are determined. Whether it is the circle of the stars or the path of the Sun, the distance from the two poles is always calculated from the **equatorial circle** <note>original: "nāḍī-valaya"</note>. Through this, the observation of the stars is perfected. The Sun, being clear and visible, reveals the power of these circles in the globe of the sky.

When the planet has moved from its starting point, then through the calculation of those degrees and the time of its passage, the **altitude** <term>unnatāṃśa: the angular height of a celestial body above the horizon</term> is determined. Whether the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa: the angular distance from the point directly overhead (the zenith) to the celestial body</term> or the altitude is known, the difference between the northern and southern circles determines the hemispheric position. If the planet moves from the southern point toward the northern direction, starting from Scorpio and other signs, the rotation of the celestial sphere is observed according to the established rules of the circular system. In that same way, the planetary bodies are seen on the sphere; the wheel of the zodiac does not actually appear divided in the sky, but is perceived as a continuous connection. Just as the three-fold calculation is made in the middle of those circles, so too is the wisdom of the observer applied. 

In this context, there is a distinct difference between the **surface of the earth** <note>original: "bhūpṛṣṭha"</note> and the **center of the earth** <note>original: "kugarbha"</note>. At the place where an observer stands on the surface, what is seen along a single line of sight differs from what would be seen if the eye were positioned at the center of the globe. This difference, occurring within the visible sphere, creates what we call the **parallax** <note>original: "dṛglambana," though here described through the relationship of the "kugarbha" (center) and "svadeśa" (one's own place)</note>. 

The degrees of zenith distance and altitude are calculated from the arc of the visual circle. However, the true altitude—which belongs to the center of the earth—is not the same as the observed altitude. Because of this, for those seeking the truth of the celestial positions, the "visual picture" must always be reconciled with the "calculated picture." The difference between the line from the Earth's center and the line from the observer's eye on the surface is the cause of this discrepancy. Therefore, when observing the **Sun** <note>original: "sūrya"</note>, the altitude degrees must be corrected; they are reduced by the value of the Earth's radius to find the true position as seen from the center.

<insert>The correction for the Sun's altitude is found by considering the Earth's radius and the planet's distance.</insert>

Furthermore, the calculation involves the **sine of the Earth's radius** <term>kujyā: literally 'Earth-sine', used here in the context of horizontal parallax</term>. When these values are multiplied and divided according to the rules of the **radius of the great circle** <term>trijyā: the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, usually 3438 minutes</term>, the true mathematical position is revealed.

Now, regarding the geometry of the sphere: when three arcs of great circles intersect on the surface of the sphere, they form what is known as a **triangle** <term>trikoṇa: a three-sided figure; here, a spherical triangle</term>. These triangles are classified by their properties. If the angles are unequal, they are called **scalene** <note>original: "viṣama"</note>. When two of the circles meet at a **right angle** <note>original: "samakoṇa"</note>, the relationships between the sines of the sides and the sines of the angles follow specific proportions.

In a triangle formed by three arcs, where there are three sines representing the sides, the relationship is such that the sine of the side opposite a specific angle is proportional to the sine of the angle itself. This rule of three is applied to the **arcs** <note>original: "chāpa"</note> and their respective **sines** <note>original: "jyā"</note>. 

50. In the sphere, these arcs and sines are used to determine the exact positions. I shall now describe the methods by which these calculations are refined, so that no doubt remains regarding the positions of the heavenly bodies.

Having imagined the planetary position as stated, one should place it on the ground at the upper opening of the sphere. That which is brought to the surface of the earth from the center of the earth is considered the <term>central point: the kugarbha or center of the terrestrial globe</term>. From that point, the position on the ground is established. Just as a cord is drawn through the difficult-to-reach path, the **meridian circle** <note>original: "yāmyottara-vṛtta"</note> is fixed, passing through the **celestial poles** <note>original: "dhruva"</note>. In the same way, on the inclined circle, the north, south, east, and west points are marked on the circle of the meridian. There, placed transversely on the meridian circle, are the **sine** <term>bhuja: the vertical side of a right triangle in Indian trigonometry, often translated as sine</term> and the **cosine** <term>koți: the horizontal side or complement of the sine</term> degrees. From the midday globe, the visible and invisible declinations are observed until the pole located above is reached through the inclined circle.

This is precisely what is explained: from the pole, and by the degrees of the sun’s position, those values are brought forth. By using those specific units and the divisions of the digits marked on the circle, the values for the **sine of the arc** <note>original: "bhujāṃśa-vṛtta"</note> are calculated. On both sides of the center, at the desired circle, the calculation is equal to the property of the arc. At that very edge, the cord of the circle is placed. Then, from the center of the earth to the middle cord, the **cosine** <note>original: "koṭi-jīvā"</note> is found. In the second case, from the center, the distance to the **ecliptic** <term>krānti-vṛtta: the sun's apparent path among the stars</term> is the sine of the planet's position. In the **equatorial circle** <note>original: "viṣuva-vṛtta"</note> as well, the method for the sine of the arc is the same. Thus, by the cord situated there, the poles are determined.

In the instrument of the pole, combined with the desired circle, from the first center to the pole, the circles are made equal by the cord. Between them, the divisions of the degrees are marked on the half-circle cord. In this desired circle, the sines and cosines are found. Then, the difference between the markers on the two rings should be noted. From the first center, the **day-radius** <term>dyujyā: the radius of the diurnal circle of a planet</term> is also known, and from that, the cosine and the hypotenuse. That marker is the **sine of declination** <note>original: "krānti-jyā"</note>. From the first center on the ground, the cord reaches the **equinoctial** <note>original: "nāḍī"</note>. Then, through the circles, the maximum declination degrees are determined. The other equinoctial cords are placed transversely on the ground. From the first center, one must calculate the sine of the arc. In this place, the maximum declination and the sine of the arc are known. By these two, the desired sine is refined on the circles. That refined sine, multiplied <unclear>ma</unclear> <unclear>psa</unclear> and calculated by the arc, provides the result. Thus, by this refined calculation, the position at the beginning of the **celestial sphere** <note>original: "gola"</note> is known here. Therefore, the calculation for the inverse declination has been explained by the wise.

Now, on the north-south cord, the sine of the declination is taken from the first center. Having given that, the **diurnal circle** <term>ahorātra-vṛtta: the 'day-night circle' or the path a star follows in 24 hours</term> should be projected. From that center, the cord reaching the diurnal region of the north-south circle is the day-radius. The cord reaching from the middle of that to the end is the sine of the declination. This is equal to the square root of the difference between the squares of the sines. The same calculation is applied to the first center at the equatorial degrees. The sine found there on the middle cord is transverse. It is produced at the pole for the sake of calculation. From the latitude of the equatorial degrees, and from that arc, the equatorial degrees are determined along with the remaining minutes. Now, according to another method, there are many ways for the wise to find the equatorial degrees. Having imagined the pole in the middle, and in the north-south direction the circle called the equator, if one desires to find the position from that, the pole is the primary point. Then, whatever lies above that in the desired circle is called the midday sine. On that circle, the sine of the arc is determined. From that, the pole and the diurnal circle are calculated.

<meta>This page continues Kamalakara’s mathematical astronomy from the Siddhānta Tattva Viveka. Following the previous discussion on the celestial sphere's geometry, this page shifts to the specific calculation of equatorial degrees (Right Ascension) and their relationship to declination and the ecliptic.</meta>

<margin>
Man. 4
P. 47
</margin>

When the observation is made at that time, those indeed are the degrees of the maximum declination and the **equatorial degrees** <note>original: "viṣuvoṃśaka," referring to Right Ascension</note>. In the desired circle, the degrees of the arc are produced from the root of the difference between the squares of the **sine** <term>jīvā: the chord or sine of an arc</term> and the **cosine** <term>koți: the horizontal component or complement of the sine</term>. Those degrees of maximum declination, in the context of one's desired measurement, become the equatorial degrees. If, in the circle called the equinoctial, the celestial sphere is divided into its circles, then at the center of that globe, the maximum declination is established as the cosine of the equatorial degrees, and the desired declination becomes the sine of the base. Here too, the equatorial degrees are to be calculated at the desired time.

By the instruction regarding maximum declination, those degrees are determined as 570 <note>This likely refers to a specific value in minutes or a geometric constant used in the calculation</note>. In the movement of the sphere, these become the equatorial degrees. When the arc is equal to the equinoctial, then it is indeed called the declination. If the movement of the declination is not known, then from the arc produced by the sine of three signs <note>90 degrees</note>, the maximum declination is found at the junction of the equator. In that desired circle, the cosine is established, and likewise, when the planet’s sine is observed, the "middle" value is found here. From the limit of the maximum declination, the arc of declination is produced. That is precisely what is stated: the equatorial degrees are fixed based on the sine-arc of the maximum declination.

Previously, this single method for finding declination was stated; now, the maximum declination is derived in reverse. Therefore, the wise should understand the calculation of equatorial degrees through these mathematical methods. Having subtracted the square of the desired declination's sine, the square root of the remainder is obtained. That result, divided by the two factors of the radius, gives the equatorial degrees of that arc. 

Alternatively, by multiplying the planet’s cosine by the radius and dividing by the **day-radius** <note>original: "dyujyā"</note>, the result in degrees and minutes is obtained. The wise call these the equatorial degrees. When the result is multiplied by the sine of the radius and divided by the two values, the equatorial degrees are produced for the desired arc. 

These degrees depend on the quadrant in which the planet resides:
In the **second quadrant** <note>original: "dvitiya pāda"</note>, the result is subtracted from 180 degrees. 
In the **third quadrant** <note>original: "tṛtīya pāda"</note>, the result is added to 180 degrees. 
In the **fourth quadrant** <note>original: "caturtha pāda"</note>, it is subtracted from 360 degrees to find the true position.

These are the equatorial degrees, also called the "planetary degrees" in this context. I will now explain this method in reverse for clarity. If, in the globe, there is a measurement involving six signs, as far as the equatorial degrees are concerned, they are divided by the multiplier produced from their own cosine and multiplied by the radius. The arc of that result gives the moving planetary degrees in the first quadrant. In the second quadrant, it is subtracted; in the third, it is added to the signs; and in the fourth, it is subtracted from the full circle. This is the established calculation.

The logic here is based on the circle of the equator. At any point where the planet is located on the declination circle, there are two strings: one from the pole and one from the celestial point. The distance between these two strings is the cosine. The difference between the pole-string and the other is the difference in quadrants. This difference does not exist at the end of a quadrant. This calculation for the rising of the planets is to be performed by the wise. While there is a difference in the names of the equatorial and ecliptic circles, there is no difference in their underlying nature. The equatorial degrees are always linked to the straight cord of the equinoctial, just as the declination is linked to the celestial pole.

<summary>This page details the trigonometric formulas for converting ecliptic longitude into Right Ascension (equatorial degrees) using the maximum declination (obliquity). It specifies how to adjust these calculations based on the planet's position in the four quadrants of the celestial sphere.</summary>
<keywords>Right Ascension, equatorial degrees, maximum declination, sine (jiva), cosine (koti), quadrants, celestial geometry, Kamalakara</keywords>

Having established the basic definitions of declination and equatorial degrees on the previous page, the author now focuses on the geometric proportions and the calculation of time via diurnal circles.</meta>

<margin>
Salutations.
Section.
41
</margin>

When the **declination** <term>apama: the angular distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</term> is observed through the planet’s contact, those same degrees are known as the **equatorial degrees** <note>original: "viṣuvāṃśa," equivalent to Right Ascension</note> upon the earth. By means of this, the expert who understands the **principles of the sphere** <term>Golatattva: the science of celestial geometry</term> should recognize the position of the **ecliptic** <term>krāntivṛtta: the sun's apparent path</term> facing the equinoctial circle. The **sine of the maximum declination** <note>original: "parama-apama-jyā"</note> is calculated using the **radius** <term>trijyā: literally the "sine of three signs" or 90 degrees</term>. That which is the sine of the equatorial degrees is stated as the base for those degrees. The radius is multiplied by the sine and then divided by the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā: the sine of the complementary angle</term>; the result obtained is the maximum declination. Because the multipliers and divisors of the planet’s sines are equal, this process is established as the method for determining the equatorial degrees in all instances.

In the measurement of the radius within the pair of circles, at the end of the **base** <term>bhuja: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> or the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, the value resides in the middle. Within the "Universal Sphere" <note>original: "viśvagole"</note>, this is obtained through the radius-circles. If this is not understood through the logic of the triangle, how can the proof be established? It is said that the sine of the arc relates to the radius in the same way the smaller sine relates to the larger. In my opinion, this is the most accurate method for successful calculation. Following the rules of the field of declination, one arrives at the desired arc. The declination of a planet or the hours of the equatorial degrees are determined by its own circle. If there is a variation, it is considered an exception. From the difference between the squares of those two values, the square root is found. This should be known through the cosine, which is expected as the measure.

This equality is not stated merely for the base and hypotenuse; it applies to all. Thus, for the two sines of the base, one takes the cosine and divides it by the radius. This results in the sine of the base for that specific circle. At the root of this sum, the hypotenuse is found to be equal. Conversely, from the cosine of a single base, and by the radius, the resulting value is given for those who lead the calculation. This measure is given here as described in the field of declination. These are the various methods. In the sphere, on the sides of the arc-hypotenuse, the differences of those properties are brought into one’s own view. If there is a single angle, they are mutually unequal. In a triangle with three movements, the two sides are the base and the upright from the unequal and equal angles. By subtracting the square of the **unequal-sided** <term>viṣama-bāhu: a scalene or non-uniform side</term> figure from the square of the hypotenuse, and taking the root, the side is determined. 

That value, divided by the radius, gives the "unequal" cosine. That arc is always half of the equal-sided figure. In places where the sun is at the meridian, through the equinoctial and the **diurnal circles** <term>ahorātra-vṛtta: "day-night circles" or circles of daily rotation</term>, which are "small circles" <note>as opposed to "great circles" like the equator</note>, the measurement is established. Even if the positions differ, because they are part of the same two circles, the degrees of the equator and the declination remain the same. In those very diurnal circles, these same degrees are used for the successful **knowledge of time** <term>kāla-jñāna</term> at the end of the rising of the stars.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of celestial coordinates.
