### The Calculation of Rising Times

If the **radius** <term>trijyā: the standard trigonometric radius used in Indian astronomy, typically 3438 minutes</term> is established upon the equatorial circle, the difference between day and night can be discerned. From the fixed point of **Aries** <term>meṣa: the first sign of the zodiac, marking the vernal equinox</term>, the equatorial degrees are determined. These degrees, as known by the experts of the **ascendant** <term>lagna: the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term>, are the equatorial portions. Now, to determine the **rising times** <term>udayakāla: the time it takes for a specific sign of the zodiac to rise above the horizon</term> of the signs starting from the fixed Aries at the equator, one must project these portions onto the **six-o'clock circle** <term>unmaṇḍala: the circle passing through the celestial poles and the east/west points of the horizon</term>. 

The time in which these portions rise is measured in **respirations** <term>asava: a unit of time equivalent to four sidereal seconds, or the time taken for one sidereal breath</term>. These portions of the **celestial sphere** <term>bhamaṇḍala</term> are created from the divisions of the ecliptic. Their rising times at the equator are equal to their equatorial degrees. However, in one's own local latitude, these times must be adjusted.

For the first six signs starting with Aries, and then for the six signs starting with **Libra** <term>tulā</term>, the calculation involves the **latitude factor** <term>palaprabhā: the length of a gnomon's shadow at noon during the equinox, used to determine the observer's latitude</term>. When the sine of declination is multiplied by this latitude factor and divided by twelve, the result is the **sine of ascensional difference** <term>carajyakā: the difference in rising time between the equator and a specific latitude</term>. 

#### Local Adjustments and the Geometry of the Sphere

To find the rising times in one's own country, the portions of the **ascensional difference** <note>original: "cara-khaṇḍa"</note> must be subtracted from the equatorial rising times for the first three signs (Aries, Taurus, Gemini) and added back in reverse order for the following signs. In this way, the rising times of the signs from **Scorpio** <term>kīṭa</term> to **Pisces** are determined. This variation occurs because of the tilt of the celestial sphere relative to the local horizon.

<margin>
The Observation of the Globe.
</margin>

Half of the celestial sphere is always situated above the center of the earth. Therefore, only the **upper hemisphere** <term>ūrdhva-golaka</term> is visible to the observer, while the other half is hidden by the horizon. The **diurnal circles** <term>ahorātra-vṛtta: the daily paths of stars or planets</term> are thus seen to be slanted depending on the observer's latitude. 

If this were not the case, the rising times would be uniform everywhere. But because the earth is a globe and the observer sits upon its surface, the signs of the zodiac appear to rise at different speeds. Great sages have sung of these movements in relation to **direction, place, and time** <note>original: "dig-deśa-kāla"</note>. Without this understanding of the sphere, the results of our calculations would not match the observed reality of the heavens. Therefore, the learned should fix their sight upon the established principles of the globe.

It emphasizes the geometric reality that only half of the celestial sphere is visible from Earth at any given time.</summary>

# On the Measure of Day and Night and the Visibility of the Signs

When the sun is situated in the various **origins** <term>yonis: here referring to the signs of the zodiac as the "womb" or source of the sun's current position</term>, the units of time known as **ghaṭikās** <term>ghaṭikā: a unit of 24 minutes; 60 ghaṭikās make a full day</term> are determined by its path. In the **Northern Hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola</term>, when the latitude is less than the solar declination, the sun remains above the **equatorial horizon** <term>nirakṣīya-bhūja</term>. In the **Southern Hemisphere** <term>yāmya-gola</term>, the difference in time between these two positions is known by the experts of the **scientific treatises** <term>śāstra</term> as the **ascensional difference** <term>cara: the difference between the time of rising at a given latitude and the time of rising at the equator</term>. When this value is divided by the relevant factors, the resulting **ghaṭikās** are either added or subtracted depending on the hemisphere. In the Northern Hemisphere, half the day is increased by this amount, while in the Southern, it is decreased.

When the latitude is such that the sun never sets, the day is measured by the duration of the **eons** <term>yuga: used here metaphorically to describe an immensely long duration of light</term>. If the sun remains above the horizon for six months, that is the duration of the day. In the Northern Hemisphere, the measure of the day is greater, while in the Southern, it is smaller. Conversely, the measure of the night follows the opposite rule. Because of the symmetry of the spheres, the length of the night in one hemisphere is equal to the length of the day in the other at corresponding positions of the **celestial poles** <term>dhruva</term>. This variation in the duration of the night must be understood through the laws of the **astronomical sciences** <term>śāstravit</term>.

The movement of the celestial circle from **Aries** <term>meṣa</term> to **Scorpio** <term>kīṭa</term> is always influenced by the rotation of the **celestial sphere** <term>bhamaṇḍala</term>. For an observer at a specific latitude, the sun is seen to rise and set according to the **obliquity of the ecliptic** <term>krānti</term>. As long as the sun's northern declination is within certain limits, it remains visible. However, when the declination exceeds the **co-latitude** <term>lambāṃśa: the complement of the observer's latitude</term>, the sun remains constantly above the horizon. This has been stated by the **ancient teachers** <term>ārya: referring to the venerable astronomers of the past</term>.

In specific regions, certain signs like **Taurus** <term>vṛṣabha</term>, **Gemini** <term>mithuna</term>, and **Cancer** <term>karka</term> are visible in their entirety, while others are not. When the celestial sphere reaches ninety degrees, the movement of the signs changes. If one observes from the north or south, the position of the **zenith** <term>khavastat</term> relative to the signs determines their visibility. To claim visibility where none exists is a **false position** <term>mithyā sthiti</term>. The measurement of day and night must be calculated from the **center of the earth** <term>kugarbha: the geocentric center</term> rather than just the surface, as the sun's appearance and disappearance are governed by this central alignment.

Regarding this, the **Siddhānta Śiromaṇi** <note>original: "siddhānta śiromaṇiṣu"; a seminal 12th-century astronomical text by Bhāskarācārya</note> states that because of the Earth's spherical nature, the observation of sunrise varies. The calculation of the **measure of the day** <term>dinapramāṇa</term> is explained clearly therein: having determined the sun's position and the observer's location, the true distance is found by the root of their squares. One must account for the **earth's radius** <term>yojana: a unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> and the specific parts of the globe. Only through such precise calculation, accounting for the **geocentric center** <term>kugarbha</term>, can the true duration of the sun's presence be known.

It concludes by citing the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi to justify geocentric calculations over surface-level observations.</summary>

# On the Distinction Between the Geocentric and Surface Horizons

Even if an observer is positioned at a specific location, the **geocentric horizon** <term>garbhakshitija: the horizon as measured from the Earth's center</term> and the **visible sphere** <term>drishta-gola: the celestial sphere as seen by an observer on the surface</term> are not identical. Due to the shift in the line of sight, there is a deviation in the calculation of the **rising points of the signs** <term>lagnanayane</term>.

When calculating the visible position, one should take the **cosine** <note>original: "kotimobo," likely a variant of kotijya</note> of the observed angle, multiply it by the **radius** <term>vijya: the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, typically 3438 minutes</term>, and divide by the appropriate divisor. By adding or subtracting these results, the true **visual observation** <term>nija-drishti</term> is established. Thus, the positions of the geocentric horizon and other factors are determined. Through the **inverse method** <term>viloma-ritya</term>, the positions relative to the **poles** <term>dhruvadya</term> are also established.

Whether for the **geocentric horizon** or the visible one, for a single zodiac sign or many, one must calculate the **sun’s declination** <term>apakrama</term>. Then, by dividing the cosine of that position by the radius, and adjusting by the distance in **leagues** <term>yojanas</term>, the results of the observation are determined. These results are influenced by the **sun’s polar declination** <term>arkadhruvakanti</term>.

The units of time known as **nadikas** <term>nadika: a unit of 24 minutes</term> differ between the geocentric and surface horizons. In one's own sphere, the sun’s appearance is constant, but if one views it from the **solar sphere** <term>arka-gola</term>, the measurement of the **length of the day** <term>dinaman</term> must be adjusted. For instance, when the sun is in **Pisces** <note>original: "Mina"</note>, **Aquarius** <note>original: "Kumbha"</note>, or **Sagittarius** <note>original: "Dhanus"</note>, the length of the day varies according to the sun's power.

Because of the curvature of the Earth and the observer's height, there is a specific difference in the signs from **Leo** <note>original: "Simha"</note> to **Scorpio** <note>original: "Kita"</note>. In the polar regions, the distance in leagues must be accounted for by the observer on the ground. As the **great teachers** <term>acharyas</term> have stated, the true geocentric position is what must be calculated. This was previously mentioned in our own teachings: when the sun's observed parts match the geocentric calculation, even for a duration of one or two months, that is the true length of the day.

Those who are learned in the **science of the stars** <term>jyotish</term> recognize that when the latitude exceeds twenty-four degrees <note>original: "jina," a code-word for 24</note>, these calculations become essential for the **conjunction of the planets** <term>shaka-yoga</term>. Through the distance in leagues and the resulting visual corrections, the geocentric horizon is reconciled with the surface observation.

<margin>
Horizontal 
measure
and the 
visual result
</margin>

When the visual angle is small, the results of the geocentric horizon and the surface observation are combined. The sphere supported by these calculations is indeed a marvel. When the observer is situated at a higher elevation, the **visual result** <term>drishta-phala</term> must be applied. If the **latitude** <term>kudvatra</term> is greater than the visual correction, one must subtract the cosine of the difference to find the true position. By multiplying and dividing by the radius, the combined visual correction is found. If the results exceed the local horizon, the observer is considered to be in the **lower sphere**; if they are less, the observer is in the **upper sphere**. This intersection of the visible sphere and the geocentric sphere constitutes the totality of the earth's observation.

It details how the length of the day and the rising of zodiac signs like Leo or Pisces vary based on the observer's latitude and elevation.</summary>

# The Geometry of the Celestial Sphere and Solar Altitude

The following explains the logic of the **six-fold celestial sphere** <term>golaka-shashtya: a traditional division of the celestial globe into six parts or directions</term> and the arrangement of sight. The **altitude** <term>unnata: the height of a celestial body above the horizon</term> is determined by the power of the **Sun** <term>Arka</term> and the specific observer's position. For the sake of demonstrating the mathematical logic, the visual observation is described through the **angles** <term>kona</term> of the sphere.

In the **right-angled circle** <term>jatyavritta</term>, which serves as a frame of reference, the plane of rotation and the **azimuth degrees** <term>digamsha: the horizontal angle from a cardinal direction</term> are established. These are calculated based on the **diurnal circle** <term>dyuratravritta: the daily path of a star or planet</term>. If there is a difference in the direction, specifically toward the north, a **correction** <term>samskara</term> must be applied to reconcile the observed position with the mathematical model.

<margin>The degrees arising from the applied corrections determine the zenith distance and the altitude for observers at different locations; if the zenith distance and altitude are inverted, an inverse correction must be performed.</margin>

Through these degrees of the arc, the Sun's position is known. By using the **inverse method** <term>viloma</term>, the degrees of the **zodiac signs** <term>rashi</term> are established within their respective **quadrants** <term>pada: one-fourth of a circle, or 90 degrees</term>. In the **first quadrant** <term>adye-pada</term>, the value is used as it is. In the **second quadrant**, the value is subtracted from six signs <note>original: "shaddhe," representing 180 degrees</note>. In the **third quadrant**, the value is added to the full circle. 

In the first quadrant, the **gnomon shadow** <term>chhaya</term> is considered to be at its smallest, representing the **increase** <term>vriddhi</term> of the day. In the second quadrant, the calculations of the shadow's length are performed according to the logic of the **seasons** <term>ritu</term>. One must know the Sun's movement—whether it is **decreasing** <term>hrasa</term> or increasing—to accurately determine the **ascendant** <term>lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term>.

When the Sun is in the eastern or western part of the celestial circle, the **geocentric horizon** <term>garbha-kshitija</term> and the degrees of the plane of rotation must be aligned. This is calculated using the **solar time** <term>savana-kala</term> and the current position of the Sun. If the calculation involves a **star** <term>nakshatra</term> rather than the Sun, one must instead calculate the specific **sidereal time** <term>nakshatra-lagna</term>. By applying these corrections to the time and the degrees, the **true solar position** <term>stuta-kalika-arka</term> is revealed. Finally, one must subtract or add the necessary factors to find the desired result in the current **Kali Yuga** <term>Kali: the current historical age in Hindu cosmology</term>.

It explains how to apply mathematical corrections to determine the rising sign (ascendant) based on the Sun's position and the time of day.</summary>

# Calculations for the Ascendant and the Corrected Sun

At the moment of the **desired rising** <term>sveshtodayakala: the specific time calculated for a zodiac sign to rise on the eastern horizon</term>, one must consider the degrees of the planets situated within the **zodiac signs** <term>rashi</term>. By following the reverse order through the six signs, the **Ascendant** <term>Lagna: the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at a given time</term> is determined. This is essential for understanding the resulting **auspicious and inauspicious fruits** <note>original: "shubhashubha-phala"</note> of a particular moment.

Just as one calculates the **true positions of the planets** <term>sphutakheta</term>, if the time elapsed is small, the desired time intervals should be **multiplied by sixty** <note>original: "shashtighna," referring to the sexagesimal system used in ancient Indian mathematics</note> and divided by the local rising time of the signs. By adding or subtracting these results from the known position of the Sun, the **Sun at the Ascendant** <term>arkalagna</term> is found for that specific time.

Using the **inverse method** <term>vilomata</term>, one calculates the degrees already traversed and those yet to be traversed by the **corrected Sun** <term>chala-samskritarka: the Sun's position adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes</term>. These middle degrees, when combined and multiplied by the desired local rising values, provide the required result for determining the observer's local time.

Between the degrees of the Sun and the Ascendant, the difference in **minutes of arc** <term>kala</term> is calculated. This is then multiplied by the local rising time and divided by the degrees of passage to determine the **desired time** <term>ishtakala</term>. Thus, the time is reckoned from the rising Sun. This results in **solar time** <term>savana</term> <note>original: "savana-kala," or civil time based on the Sun's daily motion</note>. If calculating for a desired time at night, one must repeatedly apply these steps using the **circle of time-units** <term>nadivallaya: a mathematical representation of the 24-hour cycle divided into 60 nadis</term>.

## Adjustments for Precession and Midday

Even in a single step, the **precession of the equinoxes** <term>ayanamsha: the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs</term> and the **true Sun** <term>sphutarka</term> are used to find the corrected position. The time is divided into degrees and minutes, then combined with the sixty **time-measures** <term>nadis</term> starting from the sign of **Aries** <term>Mesha</term>.

When observing the Ascendant in reverse, after applying the correction for precession, the inverse method of **calculating the Ascendant** <term>vilagna-anayana</term> is used. By subtracting the time from the **midday** <term>madhyahna</term> position using the **hour-angle** <term>nata-nadika: the time elapsed since a planet or point was at the meridian</term>, the precise degrees are revealed. These methods are declared by the **ancient teachers** <note>original: "svacharya," referring to the lineage of astronomical authorities like Aryabhata or Bhaskara</note>.

Finally, for the desired time of night, one adds six signs to the Sun's position to look at the opposite point of the zodiac. By observing the **inverse Ascendant** <term>vilagna</term> and applying the precession, the time is reconciled with the **equinoctial degrees** <term>vishuvamsha</term>.

# Calculation of Parallax and Celestial Geometry

The **sine of the zenith distance** <term>natāṃśajīvā: the sine of the angular distance of an object from the point directly overhead</term> is multiplied by the corresponding values and divided by the **radius** <term>trijyā: the standard sine of 90 degrees used in calculations</term>. The resulting arc represents the divisions of the **vertical circle** <term>dṛgvṛtta</term>. By calculating the **sine of the amplitude of the ascendant** <term>lagnāgra-jyā</term>, one determines the **parallax in longitude** <term>dṛggati: the motion of the planet as seen from the observer's position relative to the ecliptic</term>. 

When the arc of the **zenith distance of the nonagesimal** <term>dṛkshepa: the distance from the zenith to the highest point of the ecliptic</term> is subtracted from the total, the remaining portions are used to find the **meridian zenith distance** <term>madhyanatāṃśa</term>. By using the **inverse method** <term>vilomata</term>, one calculates the distance from the observer to the celestial bodies. The **sine of the latitude** <term>akshajīvā</term> of the observer's location is then combined with the **declination** <term>kranti</term> to find the precise **zenith distance** <note>original: "natāṃśa"</note> in the desired direction.

The measurement of the **Drikshepa** <gloss>parallax in latitude</gloss> is achieved by considering the contact of the **ecliptic** <term>vimmala-lagna: the point of the ecliptic on the meridian</term> with the vertical circle. This calculation always involves the **sine of the ecliptic's tilt** <note>original: "apa-mama-jīvā"</note> and the **radius**. Through these proportions, the true **zenith distance of the highest point of the ecliptic** <note>original: "sphuta-dṛkshepa"</note> is revealed in its specific direction.

### **The Mathematical Proof**
<note>original: "Atropapatti"</note>

**Here is the logic:** Imagine the **celestial sphere** <term>gola</term> fixed in space. From the observer's position, the **horizon** <term>kuja</term> and the **celestial equator** <term>nāḍīvalaya</term> are established. Within this frame, the **ecliptic** <term>bhavṛtta</term> resides. The degrees of the Sun are observed relative to these circles. In this imagined globe, the **vertical circle** <term>dṛgvṛtta</term> intersects the ecliptic. 

The maximum value of the **parallax in longitude** <note>original: "parama-dṛggati"</note> is calculated when the zenith distance is at its peak. In the geometry of the sphere, the distance between the zenith and the ecliptic represents the degrees of **parallax in latitude** <note>original: "dṛkshepa-bhāga"</note>. These degrees are consistently derived from the difference between the **meridian zenith distance** <note>original: "madhyamanatāṃśa"</note> and the observer's **latitude** <note>original: "vishuvan-bhāga"</note>. 

Through the study of these intersecting circles—the vertical circle and the ecliptic—the specific properties of the **three-column problem** <term>tri-prasna: the three problems of time, place, and direction</term> are solved. These geometric figures are not separate but are parts of a single system. By understanding the arcs and sines within this globe, one attains a clear and doubt-free understanding of the movements of the heavens, including the **circles of day and night** <note>original: "ahorātra-vṛtta"</note> and the rising and setting of the stars near the **meridian** <note>original: "meru"</note>.

<summary>This page explains the trigonometric calculations for Dṛggati and Dṛkshepa, which are used to determine astronomical parallax. It includes a formal "upapatti" (proof) describing the geometric relationship between the horizon, equator, and ecliptic on the celestial sphere.</summary>
<keywords>astronomical parallax, Dṛkshepa, Dṛggati, zenith distance, ecliptic, celestial sphere, trigonometry, sine, mathematical proof</keywords>

# The Construction of the Celestial Sphere and Geometric Proportions

The central pole <term>meru: the axis of the celestial sphere or the central pillar of an astronomical instrument</term> should be fixed within the surrounding structure. Those who are knowledgeable in these matters <note>literally "the knowers of that"</note> should tie threads <term>sūtra: cords used to represent celestial circles or lines of sight</term> from the top to the bottom. These vertical and horizontal threads must be attached firmly and without gaps, following the line from the zenith to the nadir.

Similarly, on the ground, the angular threads <gloss>kōna-sūtra</gloss> should be tied according to the circular measurement of the horizon. In the same way, the various planetary movements should be calculated. Whatever motion occurs horizontally or within the intermediate spaces must be understood through the gaps between these threads.

Where two threads are parallel to each other, the distance between them is constant. This distance represents the interval between the two rising and setting points on the horizon. Everywhere in this structure, from the corner of the wall to the point of sunrise and sunset, the gap remains consistent. Thus, the **sine of the amplitude** <term>agryā: the distance between the east-point and the point where a celestial body rises</term> is calculated at the horizon. 

Just as a planet <note>original: "kheta"</note> travels along its path around the circumference, one must calculate the **prime vertical** <term>samamaṇḍala: the great circle passing through the zenith, nadir, east, and west points</term>. This is established by the threads passing through the north-south points. Within the center of these three threads, the oblique lines are fixed. Where the line of the shadow meets the base of the gnomon <term>śaṅku: a vertical pillar used to measure time and altitude by its shadow</term>, that is the limit for the rising and setting points in the direction of the celestial sphere <gloss>gola</gloss>.

### Proportional Relationships in the Celestial Triangles
<note>The following section defines the "triplets" (base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse) used to calculate positions through similar triangles.</note>

From the base of the gnomon to the line of the observer’s arc, the following relationships exist:
*   The **sine of the latitude** <term>akshajyā</term> serves as the **base** <gloss>bhujā</gloss>.
*   The **sine of the co-latitude** <term>lumbajyā</term> serves as the **perpendicular** <gloss>koṭi</gloss>.
*   The **radius** <term>trijyā</term> serves as the **hypotenuse** <gloss>karṇa</gloss>.

Through these proportions, one determines the **declination** <term>krānti: the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator</term>. In the **six-o'clock circle** <term>unmaṇḍala: the equatorial horizon</term>, the "human" gnomon <note>original: "nara"</note> is the base, the distance to the celestial pole is the perpendicular, and the declination is the hypotenuse. 

When the planet is on the **meridian** <term>yāmyōttara</term>, the distance between the two points is always constant. This is also true for the **equinoctial circle** <term>nāḍī-vṛtta</term> at all times on the celestial sphere. At any given time, the intersection of the observer's vertical circle and the planet's path reveals the **altitude** <gloss>unnata</gloss> and the **zenith distance** <gloss>natāṃśa</gloss>. From these, the degrees of the time since sunrise are calculated. 

The gnomons <gloss>śaṅku</gloss> that are well-known to experts by their specific names must be used to calculate the **base** (horizontal), the **perpendicular** (vertical), and the **hypotenuse** (diagonal). By using these three, the **sine of the declination** and the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā: the distance between the rising-setting line and the east-west line</term> are found.

# On the Sine of Altitude at the Prime Vertical and Declination

The **sine of altitude at the prime vertical** <term>sama-shanku: the sine of the altitude of a celestial body when it crosses the east-west vertical circle</term> is derived from the segments of the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā: the sine of the angular distance of a planet from the celestial equator</term>. In the calculation where the base is the vertical side of the triangle, the result of the sine of altitude arises from the declination. From that, the shadow diminished by the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā: the distance between the planet's rising-setting line and the celestial equator's plane</term> is produced. This occurs within the **visible sphere** <term>drg-gola: the observable celestial sphere centered on the observer</term> because of the specific geometry involved. 

In the visible sphere, the celestial body <note>original: "kheta"</note> is located at the center of the horizon. From that position, the sine of the altitude is formed. Therefore, one must understand the various divisions. Here, the three types of flows or divisions mentioned by the ancients are not entirely applicable. The sine of the altitude arises from the visible flow, which serves as the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term>. Thus, on a given day, the base of the altitude or the arm <note>original: "bhuja"</note> is produced.

When the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the longest side of a right-angled triangle</term> is formed, if the calculation is made using the planetary positions, the result is specified in minutes of arc <gloss>kalā</gloss>. When the sun is elevated, the calculation is made simply from the horizon. 

-><-

In this context, the earth-sine is measured through the movement of the sphere. Similarly, through the calculation of the declination and the radius, the arc is determined. From the difference or sum of these factors, the position in the sphere is established. By using the **radius** <term>trijyā: the total sine of 90 degrees, often 3438 minutes in ancient Indian astronomy</term> and the **sine of the amplitude** <term>agryā: the distance from the east point to the rising point</term>, one should calculate the base and the perpendicular in sequence. Through the arcs of the latitude and the sine of the latitude, the result is obtained. Alternatively, by using those sines along with the base and the perpendicular, and applying the rule of proportions, the sine of declination and the hypotenuse are found. This second method, involving the sine of the altitude on the prime vertical, should also be accepted.

By performing the calculation, the shadow is obtained when the perpendicular is decreased. In whatever way the base and the hypotenuse are divided, the shadow is produced. If the shadow is combined with the base and perpendicular in sequence, and those are divided by the hypotenuse, the sine of the altitude on the prime vertical is understood. That calculation comes from the perpendicular. Alternatively, from that shadow, or from the difference of the shadow, the perpendicular arises. This sine of the altitude at the prime vertical is distinct from the sine of declination.

Each should be multiplied by the base and perpendicular, and then divided. From that, the earth-sine is generally produced. For the sake of the observer, this is done quickly. By the sum of these two, the shadow is formed. When the base and perpendicular are multiplied by the earth-sine and divided by the hypotenuse, the result is obtained. Or, in the first segment, when there is a connection with the horizon, the result is obtained. In the first segment of the sine of declination, when divided by the base and perpendicular, the sine of altitude is formed for the Sun moving in its circle. 

The earth-sine and the first segment, when divided by the base and perpendicular, produce the result. For the man at the horizon, that is the same as the sine of the altitude at the prime vertical. In the first segment, the sine of declination becomes the sine of the altitude. Through the difference in the segments, the observation is made. By the inversion of the sines, these are to be known in the sphere. The declination in minutes, when multiplied by the perpendicular of the sine and divided by the hypotenuse, gives the first result. The second is obtained similarly by dividing by the hypotenuse. The first is at the base of the altitude, and the second is in the segment. Through the inversion of the multipliers and divisors, the motion should be established by those who are **expert in mathematics** <note>original: "gaṇitapravīṇaiḥ"</note>.

# Geometric Foundations and the Calculation of the Gnomon

The square root of the sum of the squares of the **base** <term>bhuja: the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term> and the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi: the vertical side</term> is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the diagonal side</term>. Likewise, the square root of the difference between the squares of the hypotenuse and the base is the perpendicular, and similarly for the base. This is established by the square of the sum and difference of the base and perpendicular. 1.

Regarding the desired celestial body's position, through the various means of the path, the base and perpendicular are calculated. In the absence of all three <note>likely referring to the base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse</note>, the true measurement cannot be found through simple **proportions** <term>anupāta: the rule of three</term> in any case. Therefore, calculations must be performed everywhere using the planet's own daily motion, avoiding any deviation from the rule.

Through the circle of the **midday gnomon** <term>madhyāhna-shanku: the gnomon's shadow at local noon</term>, whatever altitude is attained in the three directions, that is multiplied by the desired shadow. In the time produced by the sun's position from Aries and other signs, the northern and southern **hemispheres** <term>gola</term> are determined by whether the values are added or subtracted. 

The **gnomon** <term>yasti: literally "staff" or "pole"</term> is determined by the raised gnomon, and from that, the midday gnomon is derived. By the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā</term>, the square of the sine of the base is formed, and the square of the **radius** <term>trijyā: the radius of the celestial circle, usually taken as 3438 minutes</term> is utilized. From the half-radius, three-fold calculations are made. Thus, for those without specific latitude, the calculation of the two divisors remains uniform. 

When the sun is at the meridian, the gnomon or the altitude is calculated. The result of the **ascensional difference** <term>cara-jyā: the sine of the difference between the rising of a sign on the equator and at a specific latitude</term> is also explained by the learned. In the same way, in the calculation of the day-sine using the **twelve-digit gnomon** <term>dvādaśa-angula-shanku: the standard 12-inch measuring stick</term>, the **equinoctial hypotenuse** <term>palakarṇa: the hypotenuse of the shadow at the equinox</term> is known in reverse order.

One should know their own base and hypotenuse. From the first order of the **sine** <term>jīvā</term>, or by dividing the radius, the resulting value becomes the sine of the **zenith distance** <term>nata-jyā</term>. This is the gnomon at the middle of the day. As stated by the ancients, the strength of the base and the visible sine <note>original: "drg-jyā"</note> are considered here. Alternatively, the squares are added or subtracted according to the order of the hemispheres. The square of the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palabhā</term> is added to the square of the equinoctial hypotenuse. 

At midday, the visible sine is diminished by the sine of the zenith distance. Therefore, from this, the gnomon is derived. By multiplying the square of the latitude by the visible sine and the gnomon, and dividing by the radius, the square of the midday shadow is produced. The **base of the sun's declination** <term>arka-bhuja</term> is multiplied by twelve. 1770. Furthermore, when multiplied by the equinoctial hypotenuse, the base of the latitude is formed. 

As previously described, the square of the **shadow hypotenuse** <term>chāyā-karṇa</term> is multiplied by the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā</term> and the sine of the latitude, then divided by the radius. This yields the midday solar shadow and hypotenuse in sequence. For the remaining parts of the day, in both the northern and southern hemispheres, the midday value is found. 

Now, for calculating the shadow and hypotenuse at any desired time: the **ascensional difference** <term>cara</term> is added or subtracted from the time elapsed since sunrise, according to the hemisphere. From that, the sine is derived. By the rule of the **sine of the altitude** <term>unnatāmsha-jīvā</term>, and dividing by the earth-sine, the result is multiplied by the equinoctial hypotenuse to find the gnomon's position on the **day-radius** <term>dyu-sūtra</term>. This relates to the speed of the rising gnomon or the ascensional difference. The gnomon of the sun, added or subtracted according to the direction of the hemisphere, is then calculated using the sine of the desired time.

# Calculations of the Gnomon and the Hour Angle

The **gnomon** <term>nara: literally "man," referring to the vertical pillar of a sundial; original: "nara"</term> is determined by the **versed sine of the hour angle** <term>natokramajyā: the sine of the angle representing time elapsed since or until noon; original: "natokramajyā"</term>. When the desired versed sine of the hour angle is multiplied by the **sine of the day-radius** <note>original: "dyujyā"</note> and divided by the **radius** <note>original: "triguṇa"</note>, the result is the **corrected drop** <note>original: "bāṇa"</note>. From this, the **desired gnomon** <note>original: "iṣṭa-hṛti"</note> is calculated. When this is divided by the **hypotenuse of the equinoctial shadow** <term>palashruti: the shadow's hypotenuse when the sun is on the equator; original: "palashruti"</term> at the observer's hand, the result is the **middle gnomon** <note>original: "madhyanara"</note>. Just as with the midday gnomon, the calculation for the desired shadow’s base is performed. From that gnomon, the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>drigjyā: the sine of the angle from the point directly overhead; original: "drigjyā"</term> is found, and then the length of the shadow is determined.

In this context, the scholars state that one should never apply the subtraction rule in reverse. Rather, addition is used where subtraction is indicated, and subtraction where addition is indicated, depending on the direction. When the sun is in the southern **hemisphere** <term>gola</term>, the **sine of the earth-shadow** <note>original: "kujyā"</note> is subtracted from the half-day circumference to find the true gnomon. For the sake of determining the precise position, the wise calculate the **equinoctial point** <note>original: "samamastalam"</note>. The **gnomon** <term>shanku</term> that rises at the equator is also used for determining the observer's **latitude** <term>aksha: the angle of the pole above the horizon; original: "aksha"</term>.

The form of the midday gnomon is described here as it appears on the **Prime Vertical** <term>samavṛtta: the east-west vertical circle; original: "samavṛtta"</term>. At any desired time, whether day or night, when the thread of the calculation reaches the eastern or western point, it intersects the **meridian** <note>original: "kotivṛtta"</note> and the hemisphere. The gnomon is then situated where the thread of the sun’s path meets this circle. Through the **versed sine of the hour angle** <note>original: "natāsajī"</note> and the addition of the **corrected drop** <note>original: "bāṇa"</note>, the **cosine** <term>kotimorvī: the vertical component of the sine; original: "kotimorvī"</term> is found. By using the expansion of the half-circle, the **sine of the zenith distance** is once again derived.

From the result of the second **sine of the hour angle** <note>original: "natoshajīvādvitīyake"</note>, a third value is produced on the auxiliary circle. Scholars call this the **Result-named Circle** <term>phalākhyavṛtta: a specialized auxiliary circle used in Kamalākara’s trigonometric system; original: "phalākhyavṛtta"</term>. The **cosine** <note>original: "kotijīvā"</note> of the zenith distance on this Result Circle becomes the divisor. When the **base** <note>original: "bhuja"</note> is multiplied by the radius and divided by this divisor, the arc of the result is obtained. By these means, the degrees of **latitude** <note>original: "akshabhāgāḥ"</note> are determined by the wise, using the base and cosine components.

The gnomon is produced by the division of the radius by the cosine of the arc’s result. The sine of the zenith distance is then found through the base of that same arc. By taking the square root of the difference of the squares, the degrees of the hour angle are revealed. The latitude degrees are corrected by the arc of the cosine-result. In this way, the intersection of the arcs and the results of the cosine-calculation provide the "piercing" <note>original: "vedhāḥ," referring to the precise observation or intersection point</note> of the celestial positions. From the base and the sine of the hour angle, the square of the zenith distance is found on the Result-named Circle. Through this method, the position of the sun is fixed, whether at noon or at any desired moment during the day.

# The Determination of Direction and the Celestial Sphere

The calculation of the parts of the **circle** <term>maṇḍala: the celestial or horizon circle; original: "maṇḍala"</term>, the **cosine of the zenith distance** <term>drig-koṭi: the sine of the angle complementary to the zenith distance; original: "drig-koṭi-jīvā"</term>, and the solar day are established through the **meridian** <note>original: "nāḍyām"</note>. The perfection of these two elements follows what has been stated previously regarding their components. By this method, the rule of time is directed according to the directions. I shall now speak further of the calculations regarding the directions of the **zenith distance circle** <term>dṛnmaṇḍala: the vertical circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body; original: "dṛnmaṇḍala"</term>.

Through the difference in directions between the local horizon and the celestial circles, the zenith distance circle is determined. Within one's own center, the two known quantities are called out. For the "king of birds" <note>likely a poetic reference to the sun or a specific astronomical instrument</note>, the degrees of declination are like the soft observation of the degrees of the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa: the angular distance from the zenith; original: "natāṃśa"</term>. From the center of the sky to the **difference in longitude** <term>deśāntara: the geographical distance between two meridians; original: "deśāntara"</term>, the true circle and the circle of the planet’s position are clarified. In whichever direction the planet exists, the direction of the planet is determined at that time.

In the **southern hemisphere** <term>yāmya-gola: the half of the celestial sphere south of the equator; original: "yāmya-gole"</term>, the directions are indeed always southern. In the **northern hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola</term>, they may be both southern and northern. Above the horizon, the planetary positions and the count of time are determined by which **bowl** <term>kapāla: refers to the eastern or western halves of the celestial sphere; original: "kapāla"</term> the planet occupies. In the fixed celestial sphere, the direction is calculated by multiplying the cosine of the latitude.

<margin>50</margin> Here, the **declination** <term>apama: the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator; original: "apama"</term> is equal to the calculation. By multiplying the **radius** <term>tribhajyā</term>, the desired sine of declination is found. Through the calculation of the **zenith distance** <note>original: "natāṃśa"</note>, the shadow of the sun and the degrees of declination are derived. If the direction is northern, one should imagine the sun’s direction accordingly. When the sine of the direction is greater than the declination, the calculation follows the previously stated rule. Otherwise, the degrees are calculated differently, even up to the **measure of eleven** <note>original: "rudramitā"; likely referring to 11 degrees or a specific constant</note>.

<margin>43</margin> Regarding the "above the horizon" position, one must consider whether the sun is in the northern or southern hemisphere. In the northern hemisphere, if the sine of the direction and the degrees of declination are equal, the shadow remains consistent. 

<margin>51</margin> However, due to the **difference in the bowl** <note>original: "kapāla-bheda"; meaning whether the sun is in the eastern or western sky</note>, even if the degrees of declination are the same, they may fall in a single "bowl" at different times, which is proven by the time difference between two shadows. Thus, the shadows of the two "bowls" should be understood. Where the southern direction and the movement are combined, the shadow is determined by the southern or northern path. By considering the union of the two circles in the celestial sphere, the scholar should reflect on the movement. Even when the two shadows are calculated within a single "bowl" (either eastern or western), the one that occurs due to the distance from the zenith circle appears further away. 

In the northern hemisphere, when the sine of the direction is equal at two different times, the shadow of the sun is likewise determined.

It specifically details how these calculations change depending on whether the sun is in the northern or southern hemisphere, and how to account for time differences when the sun produces identical shadows in the eastern or western sky.</summary>

# On the Elevation of the Sun and the Geometry of Shadows

When the sun reaches its maximum elevation, the state of **midday** <term>dinārddhatva: literally 'half-day-ness' or noon; original: "dinārddhatva"</term> is established. Conversely, when the movement is reversed, the state of midday is not observed according to the established order of the celestial sphere. In those regions where the sun’s rays do not fall during its transit, even at the highest point of its path, there is a total absence of a measurable shadow. In such cases, the shadow is always oriented toward the north, and the length of the shadow is determined by the specific degrees of the sun’s position. Here, even when the shadow is very short, its direction can still be precisely calculated.

When the **declination** <term>krānti: the angular distance of a celestial body north or south of the celestial equator; original: "krānti"</term> is equal to the **latitude** <term>akṣa: the degrees of latitude of the observer; original: "akṣa"</term>, the sun passes through the **zenith** <term>kha-madhya: the point directly overhead; original: "kha-madhya"</term>. At that moment, the degrees of the shadow are reduced to zero. Whether the sun is in the eastern or western half of the sky, if one subtracts the squares of the known values and finds the square root, then multiplies this by the **radius** <term>trijyā: the standard radius used in calculations, often 3438 minutes of arc; original: "trijyā"</term>, the result will reveal the true position. If the **sine of the declination** <note>original: "krāntijyā"</note> is not equal to the **sine of the latitude**, then the shadow will have a specific magnitude and direction. 

Expert astronomers should contemplate the absence of a shadow when the sun and the zenith coincide. How can there be a direction for that which has no length? This is a point of refined inquiry. Regarding the statements made by the great teacher **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II (1114–1185), the preeminent Indian mathematician and astronomer whose works Kamalakara often critiques or expands upon</note> in his own works, one must observe the direction of the shadow—whether it falls to the south or the north—very carefully. If the shadow is very short, it is traditionally calculated by the sum or difference of the directions. When the shadow is at its minimum, and if the directions are equal, there is no confusion in the observer's mind. But when they are not equal, how can a single shadow represent two different states? In a single hemisphere, there is only one gnomon and one shadow. Because the sun, the observer’s eye, and the horizon are joined in a single circle of direction, there is a distinct difference in the shadow's behavior between the **northern and southern hemispheres** <note>original: "saumya-yāmya-gola"</note>.

<insert>In the northern hemisphere, the sun rises from the horizon and moves through its path; likewise, in the southern hemisphere, its position is determined by its own movement from the horizon.</insert>

If one calculates the direction in a single hemisphere, the shadow's length and direction are determined by the sun’s height above the horizon. In the **northern hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola: the northern celestial half; original: "saumya-gola"</term>, the sun’s position relative to the zenith is projected onto the **meridian** <term>yāmyottara-vṛtta: the north-south line or meridian circle; original: "yāmyottara-vṛtta"</term>. By conceptualizing the intersection of the **celestial equator** <term>nāḍī-vṛtta: the circle of the equator; original: "nāḍī-vṛtta"</term> and the **ecliptic** <term>krānti-vṛtta: the path of the sun; original: "krānti-vṛtta"</term>, one finds the difference between them. This difference is known as the **declination** in degrees. 

From the intersection of the **vertical circle** <term>dṛgvṛtta: the circle passing through the zenith and the planet; original: "dṛgvṛtta"</term> and the equator, a new circle is formed, attached to the poles. The distance from the horizon to the pole along this circle is the measure of the latitude. The arc from the zenith to the equator is the **latitude of the observer** <note>original: "lambāṃśa," often referring to the complement of the latitude</note>. Through these calculations, the degrees of the zenith distance are established. In the region of **Kashi** <note>Modern-day Varanasi, a major center for historical Indian astronomy</note>, the intersection of the vertical circle and the equator is used to determine the distance between the three circles. This distance represents the maximum declination. In the context of the **solstices** <term>ayana: the sun's northern or southern progress; original: "ayana"</term>, the degrees of position are determined by the distance from the zenith to the celestial equator on the meridian. Even when these circles coincide, the skilled astronomer must distinguish between the vertical circle and the equatorial circle to find the true elevation.

# The Geometry of the Celestial Sphere and Shadow Calculations

Through the degrees of the **maximum declination** <term>paramāṃśa: the highest point of the sun's path from the equator; original: "pamośā"</term> and the motion of the stars, the relationship between the **upright** <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angle triangle; original: "koṭi"</term> and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the longest side of a right-angle triangle; original: "karṇa"</term> is established. From the combination of these two, a third value is derived. By knowing the arc created by this intersection, and through the two known **sides** <term>bhuja: the horizontal base or side of the triangle; original: "bhuja"</term>, one can comprehend the nature of the hypotenuse. In this way, the knowledge of the intersection of the circles within the sphere is attained.

In the center of this cosmic grid, the knowledge of the directions arises. For those situated on the earth, the position of the sun in the **northern hemisphere** <term>uttara-gola: the half of the celestial sphere north of the equator; original: "uttara-gole"</term> is analyzed through the **equinoctial circle** <term>viṣuvad-vṛtta: the celestial equator; original: "viṣuva-vṛtta"</term>. However, if the sun is in the northern hemisphere and the direction is found to be southern after calculation, one must correct the result. Similarly, in the visible sphere, when the upright is small, the diurnal circle and the horizon intersect at two points. This is well-known in the tradition regarding the **two shadows** <note>This refers to the "two-shadow problem" where the same shadow length can occur at two different times or positions</note>.

<margin>
Principles.
Vi.
48
</margin>

If one does not understand the true form of the **sphere** <term>gola: the celestial sphere or globe; original: "gola"</term> and yet insists on explaining the methods of shadows by force, such arguments are utterly rejected. To find the true rationale, one must use the **sine of the altitude** <term>dṛg-jīvā: the sine of the sun's elevation above the horizon; original: "dṛg-jīvā"</term>. By multiplying and subtracting the **sine of the azimuth** <term>dig-jyā: the sine of the horizontal angle from the prime vertical; original: "dig-jyā"</term>, the true "base" (side) is found. 

The calculation follows a reverse process: the value is divided and then multiplied by the hypotenuse. If the shadow is in the northern hemisphere, the side and the hypotenuse are combined or subtracted depending on the observer's **latitude** <term>akṣa: the degrees of latitude; original: "palā" or "akṣa"</term>. Whether in the northern or southern path, the "base" is identified by its orientation to the sun. How can the shadow be known without the hypotenuse? The square of the side is subtracted from the square of the shadow's hypotenuse, and the square root is taken. 

In the western part of the visible sphere, when the sun enters the circle, the direction and the time must be calculated. The **sine of the azimuth** <term>dig-jyā</term> acts as the base, and the length of the shadow is derived through the hypotenuse and the arc. Because the sun is situated on the circle of declination, its movement appears uniform. Whether looking from the south or the north, the observer sees the sun’s direction change. One must always know the directions starting from the **Pole** <term>dhruva: the fixed celestial pole; original: "dhruva"</term>. Therefore, the learned ones describe the knowledge of directions as the very foundation of this entire mathematical science.

It critiques astronomers who attempt shadow calculations without a thorough understanding of the celestial sphere (gola).</summary>

In this manner, by the methods described by the wise, the alignment of the **sunrise and sunset** <term>arkodayāsta: the points where the sun meets the horizon; original: "arkodaya-asta"</term> is observed. When these points are unified or aligned through the observation of the directions, others who are situated not far away might calculate the world differently. Generally, the directions must be established at a time when the sky is clear. For the sake of practical worldly dealings, one must determine the directions by considering the difference in location between two places. The method for this involves understanding the variation in the **azimuth** <term>dig-sādhana: the process of determining cardinal directions; original: "dik-sādhane"</term>.

Without the knowledge of the intersection of the **direction lines** <term>dik-sūtra: lines indicating north-south and east-west; original: "diksūtra"</term>, the shadow cannot be correctly interpreted. The **upright** <term>koṭi: the vertical side of the triangle, represented by the gnomon; original: "koṭi"</term> is the gnomon itself, and the shadow is the result of the sun's position. The square root of the sum of the squares of the gnomon and its shadow is called the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the line from the tip of the gnomon to the tip of the shadow; original: "karṇa"</term>.

However, for those living on the **surface of the earth** <term>bhūmi-pṛṣṭha: the top level of the ground where observers stand; original: "bhūmipṛṣṭha"</term>, there is a difference in vision. The line of sight from the eye of the observer on the surface is not the same as the line of sight that would exist from the **center of the celestial sphere** <term>golakendra: the geometric center of the earth/cosmos; original: "golakendra"</term>. At the time of observation, the line of sight does not fall exactly where it is desired for perfect calculation. Therefore, one must use a slightly adjusted direction line for practical use. Because of the observer's position, the visible altitude of the sun appears slightly lower.

To find the true **altitude degrees** <term>unnata-amśa: the vertical angle of a celestial body above the horizon; original: "sonnatāṃśāḥ"</term>, one must subtract the difference caused by the earth's radius from the observed altitude. By using the **radius** <term>trijyā: the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, usually 3438 minutes; original: "trijyā"</term> and the distance in **yojanas** <term>yojana: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles; original: "yojana"</term>, the wise calculate the correction. One must subtract the segment related to the earth’s radius to find the true **sine of altitude** <term>śaṅku: the vertical height of the sun in the celestial sphere; original: "śaṅku"</term>. Without this correction, the line of sight to the center remains incomplete.

When the line of sight from the center and the line of sight from the surface are understood by those who know the sphere, the truth is revealed. This whole calculation is used to find the corrected altitude. One should then place these values vertically to find the time. Through these, four values are produced. From these, the **upright** and the **base** are derived to know the directions of a specific place and time. The **sine** <term>jīvā: the chord or sine of an arc; original: "jīvā"</term> and the **versed sine** <term>utkramajyā: the vertical segment of the arc; original: "utkramajyā"</term> are used here. Where a triangle is formed with a base of **30 degrees** <note>original: "१० ३०° समितिॊ"</note>, the wise understand its properties. In the center of the circle, there is one right angle. If the center of the earth were not the center of the celestial circle, the observation of the horizon would be distorted.

The wise have determined that the degrees of the horizon and the degrees of the altitude are related. Between the horizon and the zenith, there are 90 degrees. The **sines** and **versed sines** of these arcs must be calculated in order. Through the sequence of sines, the true positions are established.

# On the Construction of the Measuring Instrument

At the desired time, the displacement between the two centers on the ground—measured in **digits** <term>angula: a standard unit of measurement, roughly a finger-breadth; original: "angula"</term>—is determined through these calculations. The **sine** <term>jyā: a trigonometric function used in Indian astronomy, similar to the modern sine but based on a specific radius; original: "jyā"</term> values known to experts are then applied. From the **horizon** <term>kuja: the circle where the earth meets the sky; original: "kuja"</term>, the digits are measured; the sine value produced there is specific to **one's own location** <note>original: "svadeśajā"</note>. When the local sine is multiplied by the radius and divided by the appropriate factor, the resulting segments are established in their proper places.

The instrument should be made from a piece of wood 500 units in length, which is straight and clean, and three digits in width. Its width at the top should be crafted according to the desired dimensions. One side should be made perfectly flat, and the **center** <term>kendra: the focal point of the instrument's geometry; original: "kendra"</term> should be marked there by the wise. From this center, the length extends to the **rim** <term>nemi: the outer edge or circumference of the circular instrument; original: "nemi"</term>, where the **earth-sine** <term>kujyā: a specific sine value related to the sun's rising; original: "kujyā"</term> and the base are positioned.

### Placement of the Sines and Latitudes

Once the instrument is prepared, one should mark the divisions on the rim using the digits measured from the center. Upon this frame, one must mark the **declination** <term>apama: the sun's distance north or south of the celestial equator; original: "apama"</term> and the **latitude** <term>palabhā: the shadow of a gnomon at noon on the equinox, used to find latitude; original: "palabhā"</term>. The earth-sine is then established from the center toward the ground. 

Where the line reaches the rim, the **ascensional difference** <term>cara-jyā: the difference between the actual rising of the sun and its rising on the equator; original: "carajyā"</term> is marked. When the sun is in the **northern hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola: the half of the celestial sphere north of the equator; original: "saumyagola"</term>, the ascensional difference is considered. By observing the distance from the center to the rim, the wise astronomer calculates the various positions. For a clever practitioner, the center of the instrument and the base of the gnomon should be aligned at equal intervals.

### Determining Solar Altitude

The **sun's path** <note>original: "arka-netra"</note> is observed through the instrument to always remain aligned with the sun's friend (the observer's eye). At the time of **sunrise** <term>arkodaya: the moment the sun's disk appears; original: "arkodaya"</term>, the thread should be placed. As the sun moves, the **altitude** <term>unnata: the angular height of the sun above the horizon; original: "unnata"</term> and the **zenith distance** <term>nata: the angular distance from the point directly overhead; original: "nata"</term> are determined. 

The sine of these altitude degrees is marked where it touches the scale. Depending on the **celestial sphere** <note>original: "gola"</note>, the ascensional difference is added or subtracted. The time passed since sunrise, measured in **nāḍikās** <term>nāḍikā: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes; original: "nāḍikā"</term>, is then calculated from the horizon. If one wishes to know the time remaining in the day, the process is reversed. Through the center and the rim, the degrees of the sun's position are revealed by the instrument. By measuring the square of the difference between the center and the observation point, the **gnomon's shadow** <note>original: "śaṅku-chyāyā"</note> at that specific moment is found.

# On the Geometry of the Instrument and Time Calculation

The six factors proceed according to this method. In the same manner, for the shadow at sunset, the height of the **gnomon** <term>nara: literally "man," referring to the vertical pillar of a sundial; original: "nara"</term> is considered from the center. The **earth’s shadow** <note>original: "bhūmicchāyā"</note> and the related chords should be calculated from the intersections of the **prime vertical** <term>samavṛtta: the vertical circle passing through the east and west points; original: "samavṛtta"</term>. To ensure the accuracy of the increasing values, a **measuring plate** <term>paṭṭī: a strip or plate used as a ruler or pointer on an instrument; original: "paṭṭī"</term> should be fixed. The **sine of the declination** <note>original: "apamajyakā"</note> is then applied.

-> Measurements from the Center <-

From the center, the **sine of the latitude** <term>palajyā: the sine of the observer’s local latitude; original: "palajyā"</term> is determined. When this sine is moved along the **rim** <term>nemi: the outer circumference of the instrument; original: "nemi"</term>, the intersections indicate the positions of the celestial bodies. Alternatively, the **ascensional difference** <term>chara: the difference between the celestial and terrestrial equatorial risings; original: "chara"</term> is used to adjust the calculations for the signs of the zodiac, starting with **Aries** <note>original: "meṣa"</note> and **Taurus** <note>original: "vṛṣa"</note>.

## The Calibration Table

For the purpose of determining the desired time, the following values are arranged in columns:

| Column | Measurement Category | Unit/Sign |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| First | Time Duration | 10 **Ghaṭikās** <term>ghaṭikā: a unit of 24 minutes; original: "ghaṭikā"</term> |
| Second | Fractional Time | **Palas** <term>pala: a unit of 24 seconds; original: "palāni"</term> |
| Third | Zodiacal Segments | **Aries and Taurus** <note>original: "meṣa-vṛṣa-vibhāgaiḥ"</note> |

From the degrees of the **zodiac signs** <note>original: "rāśi-bhāgebhyo"</note>, the **instantaneous time** <note>original: "abhīṣṭa-kālāya"</note> is found. When the degrees are adjusted, the time between the motion of the sun becomes **accurate** <term>sphuṭa: "clear" or "mathematically corrected"; original: "sphuṭo"</term>.

### Construction of the Physical Device

To distinguish the points clearly, one should note the intervals. For the knowledge of the night, a **circular plate** <note>original: "paṭṭī"</note> is joined to the **northern hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola: the northern half of the celestial sphere; original: "saumya-gole"</term>. According to the calculated sine, a thread should be attached. The instrument itself should be **three digits** <note>original: "aṅgula-traya"</note> thick.

<column-break/>

Because of the nature of eclipses and planetary motion, the difference between the **center** and the **argument of the center** <term>kendra: anomaly or center; original: "kendra"</term> is vital. From that center, the sines are drawn. The wise practitioner should ensure that the **base** <term>koṭi: the perpendicular side of a right-angled triangle; original: "koṭi"</term> and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: original: "karṇa"</term> of the instrument are in harmony.

When the mathematical results are equal, the work is successful. The measurement on the face of the instrument should follow the scientific rules of the **altar-circle** <term>vedikā-vṛtta: the foundational circle of the instrument; original: "vedikā-vṛtte"</term>. By drawing from the center to the two points where the plate is attached, the lines of the **equator** <note>original: "madhyalviṣva"</note> are established. If the lines are not drawn from the center, the instrument will not be accurate for measuring the **shadow** <note>original: "chāyā"</note>. Therefore, the plate must be fixed properly from the ground center to the center of the instrument.

If the desired result is sought, when the duration of the night is not known from the **horizon** <term>kuja: literally "earth-born," referring to the horizon circle; original: "kuja"</term>, one should take the desired value and apply it to the calculation. In the context of the **Sun’s declination** <term>ayanadṛggati: the motion related to the sun's path; original: "ayanadṛggati"</term>, the value is placed in the sixty-part division. By the measure of the **radius** <term>trijyā: the radius of a standard circle used in trigonometry; original: "trijyā"</term>, the desired result is obtained.

Now, the diversity of the degrees on the instrument is explained. Just as the center and the horizon are established, the spheres move through the upper degrees. Between the two lies the **division of night** <note>original: "rātrikhanda"</note>. That time is the measure of the **degrees of elevation** <term>unnatabhāga: the degrees a celestial body has risen above the horizon; original: "unnatabhāga"</term>. When positioned obliquely, it is great; when small, it follows the horizon. The time elapsed since rising from the horizon is calculated by those skilled in time-keeping.

The distance from **midday** <note>literally "half of the day"; original: "dinārdha"</note> is calculated through the oblique position. This is directly proven in the study of the **celestial sphere** <term>gola: the spherical model of the universe; original: "gola"</term>. Therefore, the time at which the sun's elevation occurs is near midday, and the elevation from the horizon is always smaller. In all countries, this explanation remains constant for the inhabitants of the world. The time of elevation is measured by the observation of the time of day. 

This is the method established by the **Siddhanta** <term>Siddhanta: a traditional Indian astronomical treatise; original: "Siddhānta"</term>. Those experts in the science of the celestial spheres have joined this with the methods of observation. What remains here is explained through the logic of the instruments. From this, the calculation of direction, place, and time is born. Through these ratios, the mathematical knowledge becomes subtle and precise. This has been stated before; there is nothing here that is not related to time, direction, and place. This is the essence of the decoration of the science.

# On the Physical Nature of the Sun and Celestial Spheres

Now, I shall explain the reasoning behind the mathematics of the Sun and the others, which has not been made sufficiently subtle by others. Those who possess a vast understanding of the **celestial sphere** <note>original: "golādhyāya"</note> describe it as follows. In the same way as described by the ancient Sages regarding the **mandala** <term>maṇḍala: a disk or orbital circle; original: "maṇḍala"</term>, the Sun is a sphere, and the planets and stars are also spheres.

The Sun is self-luminous through its own power, while the other planets are illuminated by the sun's rays. This has been stated by the ancient masters of the elements based on their own experience. Because the sight of those standing on the earth is directed upward from the horizon, the spheres of the Sun and other planets always appear bright when joined with the sun’s rays. 

During the day, the Sun is seen because of its brilliance, but other things are not seen. The heat produced by the sun's rays destroys the visibility of the stars. The **thousand-rayed one** <note>a poetic name for the Sun; original: "sahasra-rashmi"</note> stands clearly visible. In the same way, the **eclipses** <note>original: "grahaṇa"</note> are explained. In the day, the Sun is seen, and at night, the planets are seen—this is the standard example. 

When the **cool-rayed** <note>the Moon; original: "hima-rashmi"</note> orb is released from the sun's glare, it is seen. Without the planets being positioned in a certain way, they are not perceived by the eye. This is the sign of visibility. Thus, at night, when the Sun, Moon, and Mars are positioned above, or even during the day, the human eye sees the orb when it is freed from the sun's proximity. 

However, because the human heart/mind is limited, even when the elevation is the same in the north or south, one might not see. For this reason, the ancient wise ones have considered this carefully. Especially, a body that is seen with its own light always moves above. Because of the brilliance produced by the Sun, even though a body exists, it may not be seen. This is not a fault of the object, but a defect of vision caused by the sun's rays. This is why the **yellow-colored** <note>referring to the Moon or planets; original: "pīta-dyuti"</note> orb is sometimes obscured.

<summary>Kamalākara concludes the chapter on solving problems of time and direction (Triprashna) and begins a new discourse on the physical nature of the Sun as a self-luminous sphere that illuminates all other celestial bodies.</summary>

To be worthy of seeing that, if the appearances are similar, then by careful consideration of the three-fold nature, they are always seen as one; for a hole is never seen in the steady <term>kūṭastha: literally "standing at the peak" or "unchanging"; original: "kūṭa"</term> [sun] due to the net of rays, just as a reflection in water is seen through the power of sight. Because of that fault of vision, the truth is not perceived. In this way, the appearance is shown. Not without the planet, but rather through all the circles of rays <term>raśmimandala: the halo or orb of light surrounding a celestial body; original: "raśmimandala"</term>, it is always seen as a whole. A state of having a hole <term>sachidratā: the quality of being perforated or having a gap; original: "sachidratā"</term> does not arise in the sun. Sometimes, though the globe of the sun is beyond the path of the eye, a hole might be glimpsed, but never at night could a solar eclipse occur. Therefore, one should not be grieved <note>The author suggests that the optical illusions or "holes" seen in the sun are not to be feared as bad omens but understood as optical phenomena.</note> because the orb is great. In the solar disc, which has a small remainder [during an eclipse], and in the circles of rays, the remaining part is great from the ash-like [dimness] of the small remainder; thus the small might appear great or the great diminished. By this reasoning,

in the observation of the flickering [light], by the grace of the Lord, through the power of the brilliance <term>tejaḥ prabhāvāt: the influence of the intense light or energy; original: "tejaḥ prabhāvāt"</term> in the solar discs, a hole is never truly seen. Sometimes, due to the destruction caused by intense heat, the eyes lose their gentleness. Thus, in an annular eclipse <term>valayagraha: a "ring" eclipse where the moon is too far to cover the sun completely; original: "valayagraha"</term>, except for the state of the solar disc being a single unity, they are spread across each other. Just as the passage of time does not occur [visibly], in the same way, one should use an instrument involving water <note>A common historical method for observing the sun safely was to look at its reflection in a bowl of water or oil to reduce the intensity of the light.</note> to see the rays. Because the rays are not seen as similar, they do not produce a clear perception of the disc in the world. Indeed, during the night, when looking at the reflection in water...

in this way, for the sake of the planet, but those staying in the half-sphere are always enflamed by the sun's rays. Because of the nature of its disc, they are always three... and in the proximity of the sun... when the two of them are at the same degree as the sun, the orb is not bright... always the observers of the globe... when they have gone far by the degrees of the signs, the measurement of the disc... in the middle of the night, if it is touched by the sun's rays, the appearance should be described. (14) Not so, because of the arrangement of the two, the measurement of the disc is great; thus, due to the power of the rays, when the sun has gone far, it does not perform an overlapping. (15) Having touched... in the order of the mentioned circle of the sun... after that, there is a great distinction in this, because at night, the "Cool-Rayed One" <term>śītaruchi: a common poetic name for the Moon; original: "śītaruchi"</term> is here. (16) The solar disc... as it becomes smaller and comes closer, so it is said. From that, the rays of the sun would arise. (17) The measurement of the disc, for fear of a hole, is not known... mostly with contradiction... in my opinion, it is seen. (18) Because the time... is smaller than the sun... it is suitable for the moon. Even in the interval of time, it is not seen... given by that... is joined... not by anyone, nor in the city of the Yadus, by the power of the face... goes to destruction. (20) In that joining, the light does not appear... thus this is not broken. In this way, how is there a difference? Therefore, in the joining, they release a web of joy. (21) From the spread of the sun's rays, there is a great [strain] in the eyes; if joined in the middle, having abandoned... the appearance of the moon arises.
