<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s exploration of optics and spherical geometry, specifically focusing on why celestial bodies like the sun appear as flat disks (bimbas) rather than spheres (golas) to the human eye, and how the distance and the nature of light affect our perception.</meta>

### The Perception of the Solar Orb

[32] Since the power of manifestation <note>original: "unmeṣa-śakti"; the capacity for an object to become visible or reveal itself</note> is affected by the visual defects associated with the sun's intensity, there arises a contradiction with what is established by direct perception. Because of its inherent fiery nature, it is not seen in its true state. [33-34] If one argues that the sun is seen only by the measure of its light <term>munimānatena tejasa: a technical measurement of brilliance; original: "munimāna"</term> but is actually perceived by everyone at the end of their line of sight, we must look to the source. [35] Due to the visual defect born of the sun's rays, that which is possessed of light appears to rotate; this is explained here through authoritative measurements.

[36] Those spheres that are made of fire and those that are distant appear small from all sides.

->When a sphere is intersected by the path of vision, it becomes a disk<-

[37-38] When a globe <term>gola: a three-dimensional sphere; original: "gola"</term> is viewed by the eyes, there is a distinct difference in perception. [39] Now, regarding a globe that is understood by the wise to be viewed with a single eye: that which is seen is always slightly less than half, even if the object is placed at a distance of six times its radius. If the globe’s size is equal to the distance between the two eyes, some say that half the sphere is seen by using both eyes. [40] Yet, because of the smallness of the eye-stalks <note>The author is likely referring to the optic nerves or the physical aperture of the pupil</note>, one does not actually see exactly half the sphere's surface.

[41] As a globe moves further and further away, the portion covered by the eyes <note>the visual angle</note> increases, but it remains a specific portion of the whole. [42] Thus, by the inverse nature of that distance, when the object is small or has the appearance of a tiny disk <term>bimba: a two-dimensional disk or reflection; original: "bimba"</term>, the reason lies in the line of sight.

[43] Even if there is a defect in the eyeball of a living creature, the power to grasp an object remains within the disk of the globe. [44] When the diameter of the eye is equal to the distance between the lines of vision, then as the disk-sphere moves further away, [45] the distance between the lines of vision always becomes smaller, whereas it is greater when the object is near. By the circle of one's own visual sphere, this is called the "smallness of the disk."

[46] In the disk, the distance between the vision-lines and the eyeball involves a contraction; because of the lack of contraction, the perceived size appears larger. [47] For this reason, smallness is known to be of three types, appearing through the state of the vision-lines as one or the other. [48] Because of the difference in the time of rising of the celestial bodies, the "knowers of the stars" <note>astronomers</note> say the third type of knowledge comes from tradition and the application of logic.

[49] On the visible part of the disk-sphere, where the rays of vision touch the globe, one can calculate the minutes of arc <note>original: "limptā"</note> contained within that globe. In this way, the distance in <term>yojanas: a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-13 kilometers; original: "yojana"</term> is known. [50] By calculating the disk in its linear yojanas from its angular minutes of arc, the wise understand why it appears small when distant and large when near.

[51] What has been imagined by the ancient teachers regarding this cause of smallness has been stated; however, in my opinion, their view contradicts the logic of spherical geometry <term>gola-yukti: the mathematical principles of spheres; original: "gola-yukti"</term>.

### The Nature of the Sky and the Blue Sphere

[52] Now, it is said by everyone that there is a white light within the sky; the "Blue Sphere" <note>original: "nīla-gola"; the atmosphere or the perceived blue vault of heaven</note> exists, and in its middle is the sphere of transformation. [53] Because that sphere is always reached by the rays of the sun, the place where the rays are destroyed <note>shadowed or not reached</note> remains as a half-sphere.

[54] This blue color is famous to all people. At its center is the celestial sphere <term>bhagola: the sphere of the stars; original: "bhagola"</term>, which is always seen as a solid by those standing on the earth. [55] Where the stars and the endless rays stay together as if in one place, that is the Blue Sphere; it acts like a mirror for the eyes, and this sphere of the sky was created for the sake of vision. Without this blue vault, the disk-sphere would not be visible, for the blue allows the sight to terminate and perceive the form of the disk.

<summary>Kamalākara explains the optical geometry of why spherical celestial bodies appear as flat disks and how the distance between the eyes and the object affects the visible portion of a sphere. He also introduces the "Blue Sphere" (the atmosphere) as a necessary medium that reflects or bounds human vision, allowing us to see the sun and stars.</summary>

## The Geometry of Visual Perception

### The Calculation of the Perceived Orb

[56] These are the measurements of that orb. [57] The square of the visual perception is calculated from the center of the orb-sphere; there, the visual ray acts as the base <term>bhuja: the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle; original: "bhuja"</term> of the triangle. Within the sphere of the orb, this visual ray is clearly defined by its direction.

[58] Along the path of that circle, the circumference is divided into seven parts. The measurement of the orb and its visual distance is stated there. In this calculation of the visual ray, the perpendicular <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle; original: "koṭi"</term> is established where the vision meets the object.

[59] The point where the visual ray touches the surface of the orb-sphere is considered the point of contact. The distance from the center of that orb-sphere to this contact point is the radius, which acts as a smaller base in our geometry. [60] In this visual ray calculation, the perpendicular originates from the center of the Earth <term>kugarbha: literally "the womb of the earth," referring to the Earth's center; original: "kugarbha"</term>. By joining the base and the perpendicular, the hypotenuse is formed, which allows us to determine the true proportions of the orb.

### Converting Distance into Angular Size

[61] To determine the size in units of distance <term>yojana: a traditional Indian measurement of distance, roughly 8-15 kilometers; original: "yojana"</term>, one must consider the semi-diameter of the solar or lunar circle. One should subtract the difference between the visual ray from the Earth's center and the ray from the observer's position. [62] When the visual ray passes through the center and moves toward the edge of the orb-sphere, the resulting measurement in yojanas provides the true extent of the disk's diameter.

[63] Because of the difference between the visual rays—one from the observer and one from the Earth's center—there is a slight variation in the perceived disk. To find the truth for mathematical purposes, one must imagine the center of the celestial sphere as the primary point of reference.

[64] Now, to calculate the perceived orb in minutes of arc <term>kalā: a unit of angular measurement where 60 minutes equal one degree; original: "kalā"</term> based on the Earth's center: Multiply the semi-diameter of the orb (in yojanas) by the standard radius <term>trijyā: the radius of a circle used in trigonometric tables, usually 3438 minutes; original: "trijyā"</term> and divide by the actual distance of the planet from the Earth's center. The result is the angular size of the orb.

### Observations from the Earth's Surface

[65] Therefore, the reasoning is as follows: The visual ray from the observer on the Earth's surface <term>pṛṣṭha-bhūmi: the surface of the ground/earth; original: "pṛṣṭha-bhūmi"</term> and the ray from the Earth's center meet at the center of the orb-sphere. [66] Furthermore, the point where the visual ray becomes tangent to the orb-sphere is limited by the radius. This forms a triangle where the radius is the base and the visual ray is the perpendicular.

[67] From this, the visual position is well-established. Where the rays intersect the sphere, the difference in minutes of arc is determined by the semi-diameter of the circle, always joined by logical reasoning. [68] In the universal calculation, when the circumference is measured within the sphere, the resulting disk of the orb is precisely defined.

[69] When the calculation is perfected, the line of sight is measured against the circumference. [70] The size of the sun's orb is determined by the intersection of these visual rays. The distance from the Earth's center to the observer's position creates a visual gap <note>Kamalākara is describing the concept of parallax—the difference in the apparent position of an object viewed along two different lines of sight.</note>.

[71] Without the intersection of these visual rays, the observation of the night or day would not be consistent. The connection of the visual rays is essential for true sight. [72] For an observer whose vision is aligned with the Earth's center, the visual ray passes through the middle of the orb.

[73] Here, the base is the semi-diameter of the sun's orb in yojanas. The perpendicular and the hypotenuse are derived from the visual ray. [74] Even when the vision is aligned with the Earth's center, the perfection of the visual ray calculation is necessary, otherwise, the capability of the observation is lost. [75] When the visual ray meets the sphere of the orb, the calculation of the rise and fall of the light is made easy, whether the observer is at the center or elsewhere. Through the power of this visual connection, the velocity of the rays is understood, and the truth of the disk's appearance is made manifest.

Kamalākara uses the relationships of right-angled triangles to convert physical distances in yojanas into the angular measurements (minutes of arc) used by astronomers.</summary>

# The Calculation of Angular and Linear Sizes

### The Relation Between Distance and Appearance

[76] The linear diameter <term>yojana-bimba: the physical size of a planet or star measured in distance; original: "yojana-bimba"</term> is constant everywhere. However, the minutes of arc <term>kala: a unit of angular measurement where 60 minutes equal 1 degree; original: "kala"</term> that arise from it vary based on the observer's sight. [77] Because the orb is located at a great distance, its angular size—produced from the linear diameter—appears small to the eye.

[78-79] Since the minutes of arc are always dependent on that linear diameter, they must be calculated from the viewer’s perspective. [80] The venerable teacher Bhāskara <note>original: "Bhaskararyaih"; likely referring to Bhāskara II, a titan of 12th-century Indian mathematics</note> provided a simplified method for ease of understanding. However, he set aside certain complexities to avoid the effort required to resolve contradictions. In this work, by calculating the sight and the measurement of the minutes of arc, the truth of the linear diameter is correctly established.

### Geometry of the Visual Ray

[81] When we calculate using the arc and its sine, the result is derived from the math of the "half-chord." The difference between the two visual rays <term>drksutra: the line of sight from the eye to the object; original: "drksutra"</term> is what determines the perceived arc. [82] In the case of the Sun, the distance in miles is converted into the angular measurement of minutes. This gap between the visual rays is always present for an observer on Earth <term>sarvabhaume: relating to the whole earth; original: "sarvabhaume"</term>.

[83] It is said that there is no difference in the Sun’s physical size regardless of its distance, but the angular minutes we see are not its true, fixed size. [84] Rather, the minutes are a clever way to measure the orb's appearance. By dividing the sphere into parts and using the "life-segment" <note>likely referring to the Jya or sine tables used in Indian trigonometry</note>, the wise can state the true proportions.

[85] If the minutes of arc were the only measure of truth, they would be pure and unchanging. But since they change based on distance, how can they be used to determine the stable position of a planet? [86] The orb has one form, yet its appearance changes through the power of these minutes; otherwise, how could the world navigate the calculations of the heavens?

### Observations from the Earth's Center and Surface

[87] The "low" <term>nica: perigee, the point where a planet is closest to Earth; original: "nica"</term> and "high" <term>ucca: apogee, the point furthest from Earth; original: "ucca"</term> positions of the angular orb were described by the great Bhāskara and others. This explains the variation in the speed and appearance of the planets. [88] The true measurement of the minutes of arc comes from the visual rays. Without this calculation of the visual ray, the methods of the ancient masters cannot be fully reconciled with what we see.

[89] From the perspective of the Earth's center <term>kugarbha: the center or "womb" of the Earth; original: "kugarbha"</term>, the sign-division of the celestial circle is fixed. The result of the calculation depends on the distance of the orb in miles. [90] Then, the minutes of the orb are joined with the visual ray. When these are well-calculated, there is no contradiction between the two types of sight.

[91] <margin>🌸</margin> Within the celestial sphere, there is only one distance between the visual rays when measured from the Earth's center. However, for an observer standing on the surface of the Earth at sunrise, there is a distinct difference. [92] Thus, why would someone on the surface <term>ku-prstha: the surface of the earth; original: "ku-prstha"</term> not see the orb differently? One must explain how it appears from the center versus the surface in different directions.

[93] I shall explain the difference in these two ways of seeing the orb. The logic follows the path of the visual ray from the Earth's surface. [94] The experts in the spheres <note>astronomers</note> understand this "visual impression" <term>vasana: the underlying theory or mental impression; original: "vasana"</term> of the orb. [95] When the visual ray stands at the edge of the orb-sphere, that is what is perceived by the eye...

<summary>This page explains the geometric difference between a planet's physical size (linear) and its perceived size (angular). Kamalākara critiques previous simplifications by Bhāskara II, arguing that true accuracy requires calculating visual rays from both the Earth's center and its surface.</summary>
<keywords>angular diameter, linear diameter, Bhāskara II, visual ray, Earth's center (kugarbha), apogee and perigee, Indian trigonometry</keywords>

# Calculation of the Visual Ray and the Celestial Sphere

### The Intersection of Light and the Celestial Sphere

[56] When the rays from the center of the planetary orb <term>graha-bimba-gola: the spherical volume of a planet's body; original: "graha-bimba-gola"</term> reach the visible portion of the sphere, they come together as the "true half-rays" on the circle of the blue sky <term>nilambara-vrtta: the celestial sphere, literally 'the circle of the blue garment'; original: "nilambara-vrtta"</term>. [57] Within that celestial circle, the orb—as perceived by the eye—is determined to be identical in form to the actual spherical orb situated at that distance.

[58] The distance between the center of the orb and the point where the human eye intersects it is called the visual ray <term>drk-sutra: the line of sight or visual axis; original: "drk-sutra"</term>. This line of sight, generated by the sphere, is what defines the center of the visual division. [59] My friend, this does not always align with the celestial equator in every movement; thus, the calculation of the internal difference is achieved through specific intersections.

### The Geometry of the Visible Mark

[1] If the center of the planetary orb moves along the vertical circle, then the visual ray touching the sphere meets the celestial sky-circle. The mark of the planetary body is seen where those rays reside. The true effort here is to locate the orb on that visible part of the sky. [2] In this observation, the mark of the orb's edge is shifted by half the inclination from its own true center. In the middle of those two inclinations, one should imagine the synchronized center of the orb.

[3] With the center as the starting point, and using the distance of the "end of the eye-ray" as the radius, a circle is formed. In that sequence, the visual rays are established. Because of this, the orb is calculated precisely according to its known measurement. [4] The distance between the points where the rays touch the sphere is always equal to the apparent orb situated at a different sign (position). Because of its great size, it is seen by humans as touching the "blue-sky globe" where the visual intersection occurs.

### Calculation for Experts in Observation

[5] Because of the orb's great size, it stays fixed at the horizon; however, due to the power of sight, the circle appears changed. I shall now explain this according to the mathematics of non-linear progression. [6] Just as one calculates the interior of the planetary orb's sphere from the perspective of the observer, the visual ray must be calculated here by those skilled in the methods of planetary projection.

[7] For those wise in observation <note>literally "those with eyes like Kubera," the god of wealth/treasures, implying high skill or precious knowledge</note>, when the inclination is reduced, the true visual ray is clearly obtained here. This is found by applying the multiplication and division of the specific planetary levels. [8] Following the characteristics of the North and South, as taught by the sages, the distance in miles <term>yojana: a traditional unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles; original: "yojana"</term> of the orb becomes the "arm" <note>the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle in Indian geometry</note> of the true visual ray.

### Determining the True Center and the Earth's Radius

[9] That which is called the "anamika" <note>likely a technical term here for a specific sine or segment of the ray</note> is the soul of the calculation. It is produced by the visual ray, subtracted from the height. When the ray is situated at the center (the earth's core), it is considered the "womb-ray" <term>garbha-drksutra: the line of sight originating from the center of the Earth rather than the observer's eye; original: "garbha-drksutra"</term>.

[10] If the "anamika" is calculated in reverse, it must be abandoned. When multiplied by the inclination, the result for the visual ray from the eye and the visual ray from the Earth's center becomes two-fold. [11] The difference between the ray from the eye and the ray from the Earth's center is known as the "parallax" <note>the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different points</note>. This is found by taking the square of the local latitude, subtracting it, and finding the square root.

[12] Here, even the "true" value is a projection. The square of the "womb-latitude" <note>latitude relative to the Earth's center</note> is treated separately for additive or subtractive results. [13] Through this, the visual ray is refined. The distance in miles of the orb's location is then brought to the visual ray. [14] By using the distance in miles from the Earth's center and the true vertical of the observer, one does not simply add them. At the root of the difference between those two values, the result of the true inclination and the observer's sight is found.

<meta>This page continues the geometric discussion of the "drk-sutra" (visual ray) and how the apparent size of a planetary disc changes based on the observer's position on the Earth's surface. # The Geometry of the Visual Orb and Distance

### Determining the Intersection of the Visual Ray

[15] By knowing the point of intersection, the visible portion of the sphere is determined. When the visual ray is aligned, the orb's size in minutes <term>kalika: a unit of angular measurement, 1/60th of a degree; original: "kalika"</term> is calculated through the [16] visual axis. On the globe, the arc measured by the observation is calculated using the sine of the angle <term>jya: the trigonometric sine or the chord of an arc; original: "jiva"</term> relative to the radius of the sphere.

[17] When considering the perceived orb of the Sun and Moon, one must project the visual ray from the center of the eye to the center of the planet. [18] That ray serves as the single axis connecting the observer to the celestial sphere. [19] By using one's own intellect, one can understand that the center of the orb as seen from the Earth’s surface differs from the center as calculated from the center of the Earth.

### The Calculation of Distance and Appearance

[20] One should consider the true distance of the planet in leagues <term>yojana: a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8 to 9 miles; original: "yojana"</term>. By using the hypotenuse <term>karna: the straight-line distance from the observer to the planet; original: "karna"</term>, the measurement of the orb is refined. It is not sufficient to simply use the mean value of the minutes of arc at all times.

[21] Even though there is a difference in the observed positions, the calculated position of the Sun and other planets is made free of error through these adjustments. [22] Just as the observer moves upon the surface of the Earth, the path of the visual ray changes, and thus the perceived size of the orb changes for those looking through the path of sight.

[23] This matter, understood through the sequence of calculation, allows one who knows the formulas <term>sutra-jna: an expert in mathematical rules or "sutras"; original: "sutra-jna"</term> to realize that only half of the orb is ever truly visible to the eye. [24] Here, the methods of the ancients are analyzed. By applying these proofs to the Siddhanta <term>Siddhanta: a traditional Indian astronomical treatise; original: "Siddhanta"</term>, the labor of calculation yields success in practical observation.

### Comparison of Ancient Authorities

[25] For the planets starting with Mars, the measurement of the orb is obtained by calculating the distance in leagues. [26] If one subtracts or adds the result of the earth's radius from the center, the true measurement of the visible portion is found. [27] These observations are consistent; they are not merely the products of one's own imagination, but follow the logic of the science.

[28] Just as the methods for calculating shadows and the like were described by great teachers, so too have Bhaskara <note>Bhaskara II (1114–1185), a famous Indian mathematician and astronomer</note> and others used the sequence of half-chords to define the differences in the produced orbs. [29] Whether in the true or mean position, the vision of the eye follows the path of the visual ray. I offer my respects to those great teachers, even when my calculations differ from theirs.

[30] Shripati <note>Shripati (1019–1066), an important astronomer who wrote the Siddhanta Shekhara</note> stated it differently, claiming the Earth is seen by its own half-diameter. Because the Earth remains fixed, we perceive it as the base of our observation. [31] The Sun, though appearing close to the sky, is described by Bhaskara as being like the pericarp of a lotus <term>pankaja-karnika: the central seed-pod of a lotus flower; original: "pankaja-karnika"</term>, visible from all sides.

[32] Those who observe the Sun’s form from the Earth's sphere see only that part which is not obstructed by the horizon. [33] When the Sun reaches the horizon, it may appear without rays or distorted; this is a result of the visual geometry known to the practitioners of this science <term>yogin: here referring to a skilled practitioner or expert; original: "yogi"</term>. [34] There is no other method for the visual perception of the stars and planets for mankind than this rule of observation, which provides beauty and clarity to the science of the stars.

[35] ...by the application of the sevenfold method <note>original: "sapta-ritya"; likely referring to seven modes of calculation or observation</note>, thirty-five results are produced. If the orb-sphere <term>bimba-gola: the physical three-dimensional sphere of a planet; original: "viva-gola"</term> is always situated within the celestial sphere, then the intersection of the orbs is established in this text. In the sphere, the [36] union of the orbs is constant. Therefore, in the visual ray <term>drk-sutra: the line of sight from the observer to the object; original: "drsti-sutra"</term>, let two points be imagined. The distance between those two rays in the solar sphere becomes [37] the width of the orb-sphere, though it is quite small. These are also placed within the observational sphere.

[38] The distance between those two rays is projected for the purpose of establishing the union of the two circles. The distance between those rays is calculated in leagues <term>yojana: a traditional measure of distance, approx. 8-9 miles; original: "yojana"</term> and minutes of arc <term>kala: 1/60th of a degree; original: "kalika"</term> which are the measures of the angular separation. [39] When the true ray <term>spashtaka-sutra: the mathematically corrected line of sight; original: "spastaka-sutra"</term> is extended through those two circles, the calculation is performed from the Earth’s center <note>original: "kugarbha"; literally "the womb of the earth"</note> to the surface. From this true ray, [40] passing through the center of the orb-sphere and along the line of observation, a transverse line is drawn from the center of the orb-sphere to the half-diameter of the Sun's orb.

[41] Then, the distance between the two centers in the sky is calculated using the sine-chord <term>jiva: the trigonometric sine or the chord of the arc; original: "jiva-atmakam"</term>. In the same way, the contact of the rims <term>nemi-yoga: the point where the edges of two disks appear to touch; original: "nemi-yoga"</term> is established at that location. [42] When the center of the orb-sphere, viewed through the true ray, reaches the edge of the observer’s sphere, the union of the circles is produced by the increase in the measured diameter. [43] Because of this, the difference between the two true rays is calculated within a single sphere as previously described. For the sake of clarity, the two orbs are considered to have a transverse distance equal to the separation between their centers. 

The knowledge of the division into a single segment and the "long side" <term>koti: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle; original: "koti"</term> shows that no distance is seen between the two true rays.

> Because the difference between the two suns occurs at the contact of the rims, the distance from the Earth's center via the true ray is considered "universal" <note>original: "sarvabhauma"; this is a play on words referring to the author Munishvara, also known as Sarvabhauma</note>. When the half-diameter of the orb is [calculated], then the distance between the centers of the orbs on the true rays is half the sum of their diameters. As stated, when the distance between the orbs is not equal [to that sum], the distance between the centers does not match the sum of the diameters of the two rays; thus, the "universal" statement <note>referring to the author's own previous rules</note> is not contradicted. Because of this, when the rim is grasped <note>during an eclipse</note>, the sum of the rays is not located at the center, but at the rim. Even in the union of the visual rays, the contact of the rims is what matters. In the union of the true positions, the distance between the centers is used for the visibility of the two half-orbs. If the hypotenuse from the Earth's center <term>kugarbha-karna: the geocentric distance; original: "kugarbha-karna"</term> is used, one should imagine the solar hypotenuse and the planetary hypotenuse using the true rays. In the sphere of the Moon, the same calculation in leagues is applied.

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric analysis from Munishvara’s "Tattva-viveka." In the celestial sphere, having established the desired visual ray <term>drk-sutra: the line of sight from the observer to the celestial body; original: "drk-sutra"</term>, and having calculated the intersection of those two orbs <term>bimba: the visible disk of a planet or the moon; original: "vimba"</term> using the observer’s hypotenuse <term>karna: in astronomy, the distance between the observer and the planet; original: "veddhaka-karna"</term>, one should imagine the exact point where the ray "pierces" the center of the orb. [39] The visual ray to be established is not known to the unlearned. By the power of the Sun <note>original: "Ravi"</note> and the Moon <note>original: "Shitoshu," literally "the cold-rayed one"</note>, when they have reached their positions, the distance between their centers is given as one and the same.

Here is the proof for the sake of understanding for those who possess all scientific knowledge: In the sphere, the actual position of the orbs is shown. Through the power of the visual ray originating from the Earth’s center <term>kugarbha: the center or "womb" of the Earth; original: "kugarbha"</term>, the measure is seen. In the observation of the visual ray’s union, when the Moon <note>original: "Shashi"</note> and the Sun <note>original: "Tigma-rashmi," literally "the hot-rayed one"</note> are in the sphere, the distance between their centers and the visual rays is quite small. If the observer is on the Earth’s surface, and if they are devoid of the "excess brightness" <note>likely referring to the glare that obscures the true rim of the disk</note>, then the true appearance is seen. In that observation, the Moon’s orb appears slightly smaller. 

Because the visual rays of the Sun and Moon are equal, the visual ray also becomes the means of knowledge for the stable position. Due to the difference in the observer's location <note>original: "nara-bheda"; referring to topocentric parallax where the observer's position on the surface changes the angle compared to the Earth's center</note>, the appearance of the orbs is increased or decreased. This has been investigated by the experts through three methods. In the explanation of the meaning, if the visual ray is within the shadow's path, the observer should remember that an eclipse <term>graha: an eclipse or planetary seizure; original: "graha"</term> occurs. Because of its smallness, even with the solar ray, the discernment is not simple; it must be considered with one’s own intellect according to the rules given in the treatise. 

Now, when the Sun is at its own height and the Moon is at the twelfth sign <note>original: "dvadasha-ga"; potentially referring to a specific longitudinal separation or the 12th house</note>, then a cord <note>original: "sutra"; used here as a geometric line</note> is always used. At the base of the maximum value, through the power of the visual ray from the Earth's center and the corrected visual ray, there is a "furtherness." Due to the power of the "varied visual ray" <note>original: "vichitra-drk"; referring to the complex visual path influenced by refraction or parallax</note>, a variety of appearances is produced. Having brought forth the previously stated dimensions of the orbs, the knowledge is made clear by these rays. In this text, for the one who knows the treatise, when the planetary orb is being observed, it appears small because of the distance. If it is larger, that is the reality. 

This is the third variety according to the theory of the visual rays. For the wise, this is the rule of the Sun. Following that, the Moon’s disk is determined by the planetary orb in the expansion of the text. In the planetary sphere <term>kheta-gola: the celestial sphere where planets are located; original: "kheta-gola"</term>, for the purpose of calculation, one should project the leagues <term>yojana: a measure of distance; original: "yojana"</term>. Having multiplied the planetary orb by its own distance and divided by the hypotenuse, that resulting value is for the discernment of the leagues. However, if one leaves aside the hypotenuse and acts according to the visual ray, then from that also, the same result is obtained. When the orb exists, from the center of the planetary orb, it is measured by half-divisions and so on—1, 3, 5. 

Following this, the wise should divide accordingly. At the end of the discernment of the leagues, in the celestial sphere, when the planet is placed, it should be imagined mutually. That is for the purpose of calculating the desired distance. In the orb, the distance of the Moon’s hypotenuse is also calculated. For those situated at the zenith <term>kha-madhya: the point directly overhead; original: "kha-madhya"</term> or at another location, the equality of those two is given by the learned. Between those two, due to the difference in the observer’s position, there is a difference in the time of the orb’s appearance.

...one who is skilled in thousands of logical methods explains that existence is perceived through these rays. If the visual ray is reversed, or because the planets are in mutual aspects <term>dṛṣṭi: the "glance" or aspect between planets; original: "dṛṣṭi"</term> or square positions, and the visual ray is at a different direction from the ascendant <term>lagna: the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon; original: "lagna"</term>, a difference arises in the two planets being observed. At that point, the planet is seen where the visual ray meets the celestial sphere. If the ray is obstructed, the object is not seen. Even if a planet is positioned behind another, it might be seen if the rays do not overlap; however, if the visual rays are identical, one obscures the other. Therefore, due to the difference in the visual rays of men, objects can be observed in contrary ways.

### Thus concludes the **Chapter on Orbs** in the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* composed by the illustrious **Kamalākara**.

By whatever speeds the visual rays move, those same speeds apply to the seven rays of the Sun. If a man's vision is not struck by these rays, he cannot perceive the desired object. Because the visual ray moves through space from the tip of the eye, there is a natural darkness <note>original: "andhakāra"</note> when the Sun's rays are blocked. As far as the Sun's rays are obstructed by a body, that space between the rays remains in shadow. The length and width of this shadow depend on the size of the intervening body. If a globe <term>gola: a sphere or celestial body; original: "gola"</term> is smaller than the Sun, its shadow will be tapering/conical; if it were larger, the shadow would expand. Just as the length of the shadow of the Earth <note>original: "arkakra-gola," likely referring to the terrestrial sphere in relation to the Sun</note> is calculated, so too is the shadow length of a second planetary globe derived. 

The distance between the Sun and the planet, measured in <term>yojanas: a traditional Indian measure of distance, roughly 8–9 miles; original: "yojana"</term>, is used to determine the length of the shadow cone. I shall now explain the principles for calculating the illumination of these globes. The distance between the Sun and the planet, the sine <term>jīvā: the chord or sine of an arc; original: "jīvā"</term> and the cosine <term>koṭi: the perpendicular side of a right triangle or cosine; original: "koṭi"</term> must be determined. By multiplying the planet’s distance by the sine of the result and dividing by the radius, one finds the "true" <note>original: "sphuṭa"</note> distance. 

In the first quadrant, the calculation follows the standard order; in the second quadrant, it is subtracted from 180 degrees; in the third, it is added to 180 degrees; and in the fourth, it is subtracted from 360 degrees <note>the text uses "bhā-aṁśa," referring to degrees of the circle</note>. The difference in the centers of the Sun and the planet, along with their respective radii, allows the mathematician to calculate the "true" shadow. For wise observers, I have explained this method using the three-stringed formula <note>likely referring to the rule of three or a specific geometric construction involving three lines of calculation</note> to bring clarity to the positions of the planets and the Sun. Wherever these two meet, the union or separation of their orbs occurs exactly as described by these mathematical rules.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition following the "Chapter on Orbs." It specifically addresses the geometric calculations for the "pierced" solar disk—the line of sight between an observer on the Earth's surface and the center of the Sun—and how this relates to the length of the Earth's shadow during an eclipse.</meta>

-> ### The Logic of the Solar Distance Line <-

Now, I shall properly explain the logic of the line connecting the pierced point to the center of the Sun's disk <term>vedhārka-bimbāntara-sūtra: the mathematical chord or line of sight extending from the point of observation to the center of the solar orb; original: "vedhārka-bimbāntara-sūtra-yukti"</term>. In this calculation, the altitude and the ground serve as the vertical side <term>koṭi: the upright side of a right-angled triangle; original: "koṭi"</term>. Others consider the zenith distance of the solar disk <term>maṇḍala-nator-vidhyoḥ: pertaining to the angular distance from the zenith to the orb; original: "maṇḍala-nator-vidhyoḥ"</term>. This value, when multiplied by its own hypotenuse <term>sva-karṇa-nighno: multiplied by the distance (hypotenuse) from the observer; original: "sva-karṇa-nighno"</term> and divided by the radius <term>tri-guṇa: the standard sine of the radius, typically 3438' in this system; original: "tri-guṇa"</term>, gives the true vertical side in units such as leagues <term>yojana: a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles; original: "yojanādye"</term>. This should be calculated for both directions. From the difference of these vertical sides <term>koṭyośca: of the two upright sides; original: "koṭyośca"</term>, the true distance between the center of the Sun and the observer is established.

One must then take the square root of the sum of the squares <note>This refers to the Pythagorean theorem used to find the distance (hypotenuse) in astronomical triangles.</note> to find the primary distance. Thus, when the hypotenuse is multiplied and divided by the radius, the result is in leagues. In terms of the vertical and horizontal positions of the two bodies, the distance is determined by the sum or difference of their positions. The square of these values provides the line representing the interval between the disks <term>vimbāntara-sūtra-rūpaḥ: the form of the line/chord between two celestial orbs; original: "vimbāntara-sūtra-rūpaḥ"</term>. Because of the lower and higher positions, one gains knowledge through their respective calculations regarding the celestial sphere <term>gola-vitāḥ: those who know the science of the celestial sphere; original: "gola-vitāḥ"</term>.

If the calculation originates from the center of the Earth <term>gola: the sphere or globe of the Earth; original: "gola"</term>, then the distance is constant. However, the line from the observer on the surface to the Sun's center <term>vivantara-sūtra: the line of the intervening distance; original: "vivantara-sūtra"</term> changes based on the altitude. As the altitude decreases, the length of the shadow of the orb on the sphere <term>viva-gole-chāyā: the shadow of the orb cast upon the celestial sphere; original: "viva-gole-chāyā"</term> increases. 

The standard measurements for the length of the Earth's shadow <term>bhū-chāyā-dairgye: the length of the terrestrial shadow cone; original: "bhū-chāyā-dairgye"</term> are given as 113,631 <note>original: "११३६३१"</note>. When the Sun is at its furthest point (apogee), the distance is 29,380,102 <note>original: "२९३८०१०२१"</note> <note>The OCR shows 293801021, likely representing a high-precision yojana count for the solar distance.</note>. At its closest point (perigee), the distance is 215,161 <note>original: "२१५१६१"</note>. Based on these given values, the corrected distance for the shadow length <term>chāyā-dairghāṇi-likhite: the written lengths of the shadows; original: "chāyā-dairghāṇi-likhite"</term> is recorded as 505,221, 3,218,226, and 315,561 <note>original: "५०५२२१ ३२१८२२६ मु ३१५५६१"</note>. When the Sun moves to its higher position, the shadow length becomes 48,074.

When the Moon, the "Cold-Rayed One," moves between the Sun and the Earth, it obscures the solar rays. If the Moon's center and the Sun's center are aligned with the observer, the solar disk is covered. However, because the observer is on the surface of the Earth <note>original: "pṛṣṭha" meaning surface</note> rather than at the center <note>original: "garbha" meaning womb or center</note>, a correction for parallax must be applied to the true <term>sphuṭa: the mathematically "true" or corrected position; original: "sphuṭa"</term> motion.

This logic is stated in the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* <note>A famous 12th-century astronomical treatise by Bhāskara II.</note>. To find the shadow as seen from the Earth's surface, one must reconcile the positions of the Earth's center and the observer's location. By using the sine of the altitude and the distance to the Sun, the visible shadow is determined. The shadow is not constant but varies for the surface-observer. This is the essence of the calculation as found in the *Source of the Sun’s Treatise* <term>arka-siddhānta-sārye: the essence of the Sūrya Siddhānta; original: "arka-siddhānta-sārye"</term>.

### The Logic of the Earth and Sky

The logic of the Earth and Sky is presented here by the son of Ranganatha <note>Referring to the astronomer Muniśvara, author of the Siddhānta Sārvabhauma (c. 1646 CE)</note> according to the method of the three fields of geometry. When the solar ray is obstructed, its speed is restricted at night; I shall now explain the essential proof of the measurement of the Sun’s gnomon <term>arka-śaṅku: the vertical shadow-casting pole or the vertical component of the Sun's position; original: "arka-śaṅku"</term> and the resulting true shadow. In this context, the unequal method must first be mastered. The vertical circle <term>dṛgmaṇḍala: the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial object; original: "dṛgmaṇḍala"</term> is determined by the sight of the Sun’s gnomon. By subtracting the Earth's radius from the line of the geocenter, one finds the "center-derived gnomon" <term>sagarbha-śaṅku: the vertical coordinate measured from the Earth's center; original: "sagarbha-śaṅku"</term>, also known by the name of the "surface gnomon" <term>pṛṣṭha-sañjña: the coordinate as seen from the observer on the surface; original: "pṛṣṭha-sañjña"</term>, which accounts for the obstruction of the planet’s appearance. To calculate this, one must account for the intersection of the sight-lines. When the observer’s height is subtracted from the solar disk's position and divided by the surface-man, the resulting shadow of the Sun is obtained. The square root of the sum of the squares <note>A reference to the Pythagorean theorem ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$) used to find the hypotenuse or distance.</note> of those values yields the true distance. In this way, to understand the position of the planet, the method for the center of the solar disk is established by the teacher for the sake of clarifying the truth.

-> When a horizontal line is drawn through the center and a vertical line is drawn from the geocenter to the Sun, one should make a calculation at the center <-
-> In that place, where the Earth's sphere is intersected by the line of sight, the solar ray acts as the vertical side; likewise, the line of sight is not horizontal from the geocentered horizon, but equal to the vertical side <-
-> This line, which is attached to the observer's eye, touches the solar disk; the hypotenuse of this line is the distance between the eye and the Sun’s center, extending from the observer to the horizon <-
-> At the base of the gnomon, that hypotenuse is the basis; when the shadow is perceived, the shadow-hypotenuse is established upon the Earth <-
-> For the sake of understanding this, the shadow is shown in the diagram; according to the *Sārvabhauma Śiromaṇi* <note>The "Crest-Jewel of Universal Systems," the title of Muniśvara's treatise</note>, the shadow is calculated thus <-
-> The term for the Sun’s contact is the "line-hypotenuse," which is the base, while the sine of the zenith distance is the vertical side; the observed gnomon is the true vertical, and the shadow belongs to that class <-
-> Thus, from the *Sārvabhauma*, the calculation of the observed shadow is stated through the proportion of the lines for the purpose of extreme accuracy <-

If this proportion is used to find the geocentric portion, it should be added to the result. As far as the horizon of the Earth, the calculated portion is taken as the shadow. One should recognize this as the "weighty words of the elders." Since the shadow is calculated by this line, how could the calculation be done otherwise? The square of the hypotenuse is doubled and the Earth's radius is subtracted; the remainder is the square root. When the Sun’s height is equal to the shadow’s end, the light is at its most beautiful. The man standing on the surface of the Earth sees the object. Because the observer is on the Earth, the gnomon and the shadow are formed where the Sun’s ray-line meets the ground. Where that ray-line is obstructed, the shadow begins. Just as the shadow is seen by the eyes, the subtle consideration of the geometry of the shadow is analyzed here. In his view, the observer on the Earth's surface sees the shadow differently based on their position.

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the geometry of the solar disk and shadow calculations. # On the Differentiation of the Solar Disk and Shadow

Due to the variations in the appearance of the solar disk, the certainty of the calculation here is established through the specific divisions of the observed height. Even with the babble of those who argue otherwise, the consistency of measurements for tall or short observers remains. If there are minute divisions at the end of the calculation, they reach a state of extreme insignificance; therefore, by following the calculation of the disk's appearance, one should produce the specific differences generated by the "string" <note>The 'string' (sūtra) refers to the geometric line of sight or a chord in mathematical diagrams.</note> of the gnomon. 

The number [of these divisions] is well-known through measurement; however, why should there be a subtraction or addition here if the number is insignificant? Thus, may the **Siddhāntatattva-dinakara** <note>Literally "The Sun of the Essence of Settled Tenets," referring to Kamalākara, the author of the Siddhāntatattvaviveka (1658 CE).</note> be victorious! The "third position" stated in the **Sārvabhauma** <note>The Siddhānta Sārvabhauma by Muniśvara, Kamalākara’s intellectual rival.</note> regarding the daily calculation—which uses the solar motion combined with the "string"—is slightly flawed. Even in a very small difference of observer position, a change in the shadow is not perceived by the eye; therefore, it must be directly experienced. 

The Sun is said to be seen at the moment of its rising. Because the text of the *Sārvabhauma* is unclear regarding the half-diameter of the orb, it is an incorrect calculation. Having performed these two calculations of the disk's appearance, I shall demonstrate the true and clear appearance of the sky for the sake of clarity. In opposition to the "Gauras" <note>Likely referring to followers of a particular school or perhaps a regional group of astronomers (Gauḍa), or even "Westerners" in some contexts, though here it refers to astronomical predecessors.</note>, and from the calculation of the half-region of the orb’s surface, this has all been brought forth through careful consideration. 

### The Geometry of the Line of Sight

The desire for the true essence of the shadow arises from the distance of the half-region of the orb's surface. The "string" <term>sūtra: a geometric line or formula; original: "sūtra"</term> is drawn from the upper region of the solar disk. To establish the well-known shadow-string, no other method is mentioned. Because it originates from the uppermost region, this is the actual shadow-method. 

This region of the "true essence" is not merely the center of the disk. If one were to calculate using the "string" from the observer's eye to the center of the orb, there would be a loss of traditional doctrine. This is because the shadow on the Earth's surface and other calculations are centered in the middle of the celestial sphere. 

In these calculations, the geocentric line of sight <term>garbha-dṛksūtra: the imaginary line connecting the center of the Earth to the center of a celestial body; original: "garbha-dṛksūtra"</term> should be the square root of the sum of the squares <note>A reference to the Pythagorean theorem used to calculate the hypotenuse of the triangle formed by the observer, the center of the earth, and the sun.</note> of the gnomon and the shadow. From that geocentric line, one should subtract the height. When the calculation is made for the surface line of sight <term>pṛṣṭha-dṛksūtra: the line of sight from the observer on the Earth's surface; original: "pṛṣṭha-dṛksūtra"</term> touching the surface of the solar sphere, that which is brought forth is not the same as the geocentric version. 

How can it be appropriate for those who are active on the surface of the Earth to use geocentric coordinates? Therefore, the rays from the half-region of the solar disk are what actually meet the observer here. In calculating that shadow, it is as if it comes from the upper surface. Furthermore, if the center of the solar orb were at the zenith <term>khamadhya: the point directly overhead; original: "khamadhya"</term>, there would be a total absence of shadow at that time. 

This has not been properly calculated by the ancient followers of the Vedas <note>Referring to older traditional astronomical texts (Siddhāntas) that Kamalākara seeks to refine with his more precise geometry.</note> using the methods of the "division of the day." When the solar center is in the middle of the sky, or even when it is not, a shadow must be calculated from both sides. If the center of the disk is exactly at the zenith, the shadow truly does not exist. However, how can one calculate the shadow that is produced when the disk is not at the zenith? That shadow, which originates from the gnomon <term>śaṅku: a vertical pole used to measure shadows; original: "śaṅku"</term>, must come from the entire surface of the solar orb. Because there is a mutual contradiction in the various positions of the disk's surface, a wise man must decide on a single certain value. Where there is an absence of shadow, that must be accepted by the intelligent observer. 

Now, regarding the "string" in the middle of the solar sphere: it stays upon the surface of that sphere. Where it touches is the point to be considered. A location is imagined in the middle of the small solar disk, and from there, the gnomon...

<summary>This page discusses the complex geometry required to calculate solar shadows, debating the differences between using the center of the Sun versus its surface as the point of origin for light rays. Kamalākara critiques his rival Muniśvara, arguing that topocentric (surface-based) observations are necessary for accuracy.</summary>

...even so, it is possible on the surface of the **string-sphere** <note>original: "sūtragola"; referring to a conceptual sphere defined by geometric lines or chords.</note> to consider it as a plane. For this reason, in the forest of mathematical proofs, even a slight understanding is attained with difficulty. In the absence of that [logical] state, by the power of the gnomon's essence, the shadow of the **string** <note>The "string" (sūtra) here represents the central ray of light used in the geometric model.</note> would indeed be produced from that point. This must be the case, as any other explanation would be illogical. Thus, having set aside a portion of the half and the entire solar circle, the calculation of the shadow produced for people in various regions arises from this. In the absence of a gnomon or a specific direction, this becomes clear. Thus, even from the horizontal gnomon in the forest of calculations, the shadow would be produced horizontally, indeed, by the movement of people. Because of the lack of shadow in the middle of a very strong [light source], it does not appear [to change], as it follows the path of the middle **string** <note>The central axis of the light beam.</note> in the center of the disk. For that purpose, by all these causes, the shadow becomes rounded. If one follows the various methods of those who are not skilled in spherical mathematics, a **spherical contradiction** <note>original: "golavirodha"; a technical term for a geometric impossibility or a flaw that violates the rules of spherical trigonometry.</note> is established there. Therefore, because of the contradiction in the half-sphere, this method, produced by the common people, is always rejected in favor of the [correct] arrangement of the **string**.

->॥ Thus ends the **Chapter on Shadows** in the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* composed by the illustrious **Kamalākara** ॥<-

By its own nature, the solar sphere is always bright. The lunar sphere, which is below the solar path, is touched by the directions; whatever part [of the disk] is in contact with those rays, that half becomes bright. If that half is even slightly touched, it is released [from darkness] and remains constantly in the white or dark phases that are being described. This also applies to the other planetary bodies which are spherical. In the same way, the upper half of the moon is white, while the lower part may be dark or white due to the connection with the rays from the solar circle. Furthermore, by the union of the lunar shadow and other factors, and following the logic of the planetary spheres, if the distance is increased, the orb would appear like a **pair of deer-skin shapes** <note>original: "mṛgadvyākāram"; likely a metaphor for the crescent or specific phase-shapes of the moon.</note>. The knowledge of the **string** <note>The line of sight or chord.</note> of the disk is entirely unified in this investigation. For the sake of establishing this knowledge, the lack of difference is now made clear. Indeed, for those situated on the line between the center of the Earth and the center of the orb, the difference in the **ear** <note>term: "karṇa" (hypotenuse/distance); in Indian astronomy, karṇa refers to the distance from the observer to the planet.</note> is calculated through the [geometry of] the triangle. By the squares of those distances, the position is determined. Again, for those standing horizontally, the calculation is made through the difference of the squares of those two. Thus, this calculation is stated accurately. In the chapter on the **Moon's crescent** <note>original: "śṛṅgonnatyadhikāra"; literally the chapter on the elevation of the horns of the moon.</note>, the connection with the observer's sight situated on the Earth's surface and the segments of the sphere must be discerned at the end of the new moon. It is not stated elsewhere how the white portion appears in the lunar disk due to its nature. I shall explain the differentiation of these two [the sun and moon] through this calculation. Because of the new [methods], the three positions made by the Sun are understood by men in different regions... the shadow of the horizontal gnomon... not from the six... even here, it is given as clearly as the [calculation of] the planets... the discernment of the observer's sight... the arc of the solar position... the moon, being fit for the "deer" <note>A reference to the "deer in the moon" (mṛga), the Indian equivalent of the "man in the moon."</note>, through its union or separation from the Sun...

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical exploration of the solar disk (bimba). Following the previous discussion on the geometric "string-sphere" and shadow calculations, he now moves into a technical comparison of how the sun’s diameter is perceived versus its true mathematical projection on the celestial sphere. He specifically addresses discrepancies between various astronomical schools regarding units of measurement and the observer's perspective.</meta>

If, having set aside the false notions, the parts are divided by their own quarters, or if there are seventy-two parts according to the solar influence, then the stories told by the ancients regarding the **mean hypotenuse** <term>madhyamakarṇa; the average distance from the Earth's center to the planet</term> should be applied to the **solar orb** <gloss>the sun's disk</gloss> and projected onto the **visual sphere** <term>driggola; the celestial sphere as it appears to an observer on Earth</term>.

When the **visual ray from the Earth's center** <gloss>garbhīya-dṛksūtra</gloss> is established, certain variations appear in the **visual disk** <gloss>dṛgbimba</gloss>; otherwise, there would be no distinction. Thus, one should not see a difference in their contact points. In this context, there is no loss of accuracy when using the visual rays, but all calculations are eternally based on the rays from the Earth's center. If the **minutes of arc** <term>kalā; a unit of angular measure equal to 1/60th of a degree</term> of the solar disk are equal, then they follow the solar theory. Even if this visual ray is not taken as equal to the calculated values, the minutes of arc situated in their respective spheres are nonetheless equal. Even by observation, they remain equal to one another. Such is the reasoning.

In this manner, even in the chapter on the **prime meridian** <gloss>rekhādhikāra</gloss>, one should bring the measure of the disk to mind. No one can truly perceive the solar disk as it exists at a single point on the sphere. Because of the **clear** <gloss>sphuṭa; corrected or true</gloss> movement of the sun in its own orbit, its position is not fixed in one way. The measurement in **miles** <term>yojana; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> is small, but if there is a difference in the numerical value, the number remains the same when measured in minutes of arc. Because of the scale of its own sphere, or because of its own distance, there is no other measurement. The difference in the **sine** <gloss>bhujā</gloss> is produced here; how wondrous it is that this occurs within its own sphere! Between the two, there is a difference between the sphere and the visual observation. In the measurement of the **radius** <gloss>koṭi</gloss>, the motion of the **bright ones** <gloss>planets/stars</gloss> is determined by some.

For those who calculate the value of the **hypotenuse of the sun's altitude** <gloss>nata-karṇa</gloss>, the proof comes only from the intersection of different types of central rays. If that is the case, it is accepted as equal. When brought to the center of the earth, it is equal there. The parts of the disk are divided by three-quarters of five and eight parts. In the **Śiromaṇi** <note>original: "shiramaṇau"; referring to the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*, the famous 12th-century treatise by Bhāskara II</note>, the difference between the two is calculated.

In the absence of the **sine and cosine** <gloss>doḥ-koṭi</gloss>, and because of the absence of the hypotenuse, the same holds true here. Indeed, the parts are divided by five and three-quarters of six. This is called the "family" in the stated formula. If there is no loss in the hypotenuse, it would occur only through the distance between the centers of the sun. Therefore, there is a mixture of different types of calculations. In the calculation of the sine and radius, there is no fault, just as in the case of the **conjunction of the planets** <gloss>kheṭa-yuti</gloss>. Thus it is said.

The second solar disk, as situated by the best of scholars in the **sphere of the zodiac signs** <term>rāśigola; the ecliptic sphere divided into the twelve signs</term>, possesses the same nature because of the measurement of the minutes of arc generated in that sphere. This is proven by the power of the visual ray. If the scholars observe the minutes of the solar disk in the **lunar sphere** <term>himaraśmi-gola; the orbital sphere of the moon</term>, and if they are situated in the same sphere, they move into the middle of the sun. By their own formula, the distance to the center of the sun is stated. By that distance, once freed, the instruction of the half-portion is revealed. If the ray of the **visual distance** <gloss>dṛg-antara-sūtra</gloss> is slightly more than the actual ray, it should be projected like the solar day. The difference between the moon and the sun is calculated using the **true hypotenuse of the sun** <gloss>ravi-karṇa</gloss> multiplied by the sine of their difference. If divided by the formula for the difference of the solar disk, the motion of the moon as it touches the lunar circle is understood.

<summary>Kamalākara discusses the geometry of the solar disk, arguing that while physical distances (yojanas) may vary, angular measurements (minutes of arc) remain consistent across different celestial spheres. He critiques previous methods from Bhāskara II’s Śiromaṇi regarding the projection of visual rays from the Earth's center versus the observer's eye.</summary>

</margin>Regarding the interval between the true semi-disk of the Sun <note>original: "sphuṭārdhabimbārkāntare"</note>, that same semi-disk is understood as it passes through the second quadrant in the northern direction, where the arc segments are clearly discerned.

Through the first and fourth divided parts, and also what is in the second and third, the calculation is performed repeatedly by applying the cord of the solar disk <term>arkabimba-sūtra; the mathematical line representing the diameter or ray of the sun</term> from the beginning of the quadrant.

In that context, regarding the path of motion, when the visual line is greater, then the arcs arising from that visual line are considered to be added to their own positions.

Thus, when the second proof is established, the disk situated at the tip of the "arrow" <gloss>the sine of the arc</gloss> is measured within the sphere by those who know the globe. The calculation of the solar diameter is achieved through the correction <term>saṃskāra; a mathematical adjustment applied to raw observations</term> of the differences between the north and south. Within the white circle <gloss>the lunar disk or the illuminated part</gloss>, the visual ray of the solar disk exists within the sphere. At the interval where the semi-disk of the Sun is subtracted, in the quadrant of the disk's northern ray—

In the verse, the visual rays are measured by the four Vedas <note>In Indian mathematics, "Veda" is a numerical symbol for the number 4</note>. From the corner of the Sun, the result is an arc. Without these visual rays, if one proceeds further, the ray reaches the very edge of the third quadrant.

If that is the case, then by the cord of the lunar disk, the visual rays would not belong to anyone. There would be no perception of the "shining" <gloss>the illuminated portion</gloss>, nor would there be a falling of the shadow. As the regions are obstructed, this is how it is conceived.

**Now for the derivation:**
From the center of the Earth <term>kugarbha; literally 'the womb of the earth', referring to the geocenter</term>, the solar ray should be placed within the sphere of the solar disk. Similarly, for the Moon as determined by mathematics, its position is established on the solar sphere. The cord arising from the Moon's longitude and the interval of minutes <term>kalā; 1/60th of a degree</term> between the Sun and Moon are considered equal.

When the center of the Sun is calculated to be at the point of contact, that is the solar position. There, at the center of the sphere of the disk, exists the circle of the Moon’s longitude at that time. Therefore, the center of the sphere of the solar disk and the center of the lunar sphere lie upon the same clear circle, which is measured by the circumference of the lunar sphere. This is how the proof is conceived. Thus, from the center of the Earth to the center of the sphere of the disk, a clear ray reaches the solar sphere, and from there to the lunar ray.

Now, that which is known as the "white" <gloss>the illuminated phase</gloss> is the true interval between the solar and lunar rays. That same interval exists between the solar sphere and the lunar sphere. The "arm" <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term> is produced from the distance in yojanas <term>yojana; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-13 km</term>. The combined northern rays of the lunar and solar disks form the "upright" <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term>. By applying these, the distance in yojanas at the center of the lunar sphere is found. Through this interval, the circumference of the lunar orb is determined. According to the scriptures, there are twenty "horns" <term>śṛṅga; the pointed ends of the lunar crescent</term> associated with that circumference. These are conceived of as the "digits" of the moon. In the lunar circle, the cord of the lunar ray and the cord of the disk's interval are one and the same; this is the "white" part of the lunar sphere.

That which is the "arm" of the illuminated part is the measure of the Moon in yojanas. Furthermore, if the arcs are calculated with the semi-sine, then the "white" portion is also calculated with the semi-sine. Here, in the clear expression of the calculation, the result is divided by the radius <term>trijyā; the radius of the sines, usually 3438 minutes</term>. Through this, the mean calculation is brought to the arc as previously stated.

Kamalākara employs right-angled trigonometry (Bhuja and Koti) to link the physical distances of the Sun and Moon to their perceived angular diameters.</summary>

<margin>54</margin>
In the variation of the division of the cords <note>original: "sūtra-bheda"; the different mathematical lines used to represent visual rays</note> where the solar and lunar disks are joined, and within the interval of the two cords at ten degrees of the circumference of the double sphere, when the Sun and Moon are situated transversely <note>obliquely or side-by-side rather than vertically aligned</note>: by this interval, the distance between the lunar cord and the solar disk cord is established. Where there is an absence of the "arrow" <gloss>śara; the celestial latitude or the sine of the arc</gloss>, the interval between the time-calculation cord and the hypotenuse-cord <term>karṇa-sūtra; the visual ray or straight line connecting the observer to the center of the celestial body</term> would not exist.

With the intention of measuring from the center of the root of the hypotenuse-cord, and wherever the surface of the lunar sphere <term>candra-bimba-gola-pṛṣṭha</term> is touched—specifically regarding the intention of measuring more than half the diameter—it is shown that from the "great arrow," water <note>This likely refers to the mathematical projection of the "liquid" or illuminated portion of the moon</note> is derived. Because the half of the lunar sphere is not visible at the area where the solar-ray cord and the hypotenuse-cord meet, the other half is observed through the hypotenuse-cord. Thus, in the chapter on visual rays, the half-sphere is not seen in that way. According to the rule that the Sun illuminates only half of the sphere, when the Sun is situated transversely, the interval between the semi-disk cord and the hypotenuse-cord is stated. By this, we grasp the interval of the lunar cord. 

This has been stated by the established masters <note>original: "siddhāntibhiḥ"; the traditional astronomers</note> regarding the moon. In the observation of the solar and lunar disks, by the interval of the cords, the connection of the illumination is shown. Therefore, in front of that, the interval is sixteen parts, or twelve gnomons <term>dvādaśa-śaṅku; the standard twelve-digit shadow-casting pole used in Indian astronomy</term>, and so on. This is stated collectively in the **Siddhanta Shiromani** <note>The "Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas," the influential 12th-century work by Bhāskara II</note> in the section on the **Elevation of the Horns** <term>śṛṅgonnamane; the mathematical calculation of the tilt and height of the moon's crescent tips</term>. 

In all calculations of the white portion <gloss>the illuminated crescent</gloss>, even on the "All-Supporting" <note>likely a reference to a specific astronomical instrument or a universal coordinate system</note>, this method does not occur in that specific measured place. Because of that, the elevation of the horns <term>śṛṅgonnati</term> is calculated from those measurements. Now, if the two celestial bodies are situated at a distance of less than six signs <note>180 degrees</note>, the transverse distance between the disks is calculated previously. In the circle of the moon's illumination, where the tips of the white horns are, I shall describe the different directions. When the moon is situated in the upper direction, its upper edge is illuminated. When it is in the lower direction, the lower edge is illuminated by the rays of the Sun. If the Sun and Moon are at the same altitude <note>original: "sama-kalā"</note>, the tips of the horns appear level. 

Where the two disks are clearly seen, and where the illumination on the lunar globe is measured by the local latitude and the signs of the zodiac, the difference in the appearance of the horns is determined. This occurs when the two bodies are on the same vertical circle <term>dṛg-maṇḍala; the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>. In that case, the levelness of the white horns is observed. For those observing from the earth, when the Sun and Moon are at equal degrees of altitude in the prime vertical <term>sama-maṇḍala; the east-west vertical circle</term>, the pair of horns will be seen as level. Even if the pair of horns is moving transversely, if they are in the prime vertical and the two bodies are at the same altitude, the horns will remain level.

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric derivation of the solar and lunar disks, focusing on the visual elevation of the moon's horns (the tips of the crescent) relative to the observer's horizon and the prime vertical.</meta>

<margin>
Principles
of Reality
58
<insert>58</insert>
</margin>

Even in the prime vertical <term>samamaṇḍala; the great circle passing through the east, west, and zenith</term>, also known as the transverse circle, when the disk is positioned at the intersection of the moon’s path and the observer's vertical circle, the two horns of the moon are situated below. Due to the position of the human eye <note>original: "nṛ-dṛṣṭeḥ"</note> relative to the center of those two lunar horns, they appear inclined. Just as they are seen there, in that same way, the line connecting the two horns is formed by human observation. 

Because there is no difference in the distance between the two tips of the horns, for the sake of calculation, where their positions coincide, the elevation of the horns <term>śṛṅgonnati; the angle of the moon's crescent</term> appears as a single horn. Although there is a slight difference between these two, because they are centered on the visual line of the previous time, they are easily understood. That directed line <note>original: "diṣṭa-sūtra"</note> is not seen by those looking through the vertical plane. The experts in this science do not consider it to be a single line of sight.

In sequence, it is considered that when there is an absence of latitude <gloss>śara; the celestial latitude of the moon</gloss>, the two horns should be level. This is the clear explanation of the weapon-like <note>The crescent is often compared to a bow or a weapon</note> shape of the moon. Those who truly understand this science know that if the two horns are on different vertical circles, how could they be level? The horns are level only when there is an absence of certain shadows or when the sun's rays strike evenly. This has been stated here because, without the logic of spherical geometry <term>gola-jñāna</term>, there is a failure in reasoning. Because of the observer's perspective, they always appear equal, except at specific moments. To say they are level at all times is false logic; they are only level in the prime vertical when the shadow is appropriate. 

As long as the two horns are on the transverse line, there is an absence of the vertical component <term>koṭi; the vertical side of the right-angled triangle used in projection</term> in the prime vertical. This is established in the final determination of the diagrams <note>original: "parilekhādi-nirṇayaḥ"</note>. Although this was mentioned in the *Brahmasphutasiddhanta* <note>original: "Brahma-srak-tau"; a fundamental 7th-century text by Brahmagupta</note>, it is explained differently here. In the observation, when the sun and moon are in different directions from the center, the zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term> is equal. When the sun is at a distance of five, six, or eight signs <note>original: "pādona-ṣaḍ-aṣṭa-dattāṃśakaiśca"</note>, and it is the time of the moon's horn elevation, if the vertical component is absent, the line of the horns is transverse. This occurs in the southern region as well, following the path from Capricorn and other signs. 

The manifestation of the moon's horns, because of its clarity, is represented in the diagrams. In the prime vertical, seen from the center with equal zenith distance, the sun and moon appear. When they are separated by the aforementioned degrees, and because of the clarity of the light, the vertical component is absent. The brilliance of the horns is not merely stated on the transverse line, but on another line entirely. In the northern hemisphere, this is seen to be the opposite. Thus, in the half-circle of the celestial sphere, the moon always appears with its horns at a specific zenith distance, separated by those portions of the arc.

It refines earlier theories from the Brahmasphutasiddhanta regarding how the sun's position relative to the moon determines the crescent's tilt.</summary>
