<meta>This page continues the technical geometric derivation of the moon's appearance, specifically investigating the conditions under which the "horns" (tips) of the lunar crescent appear level or inclined to an observer on Earth. It builds on the previous page's discussion of the prime vertical and the line of sight.</meta>

When the two horns are equal, there is a state of equality between their two elevations <term>śṛṅgonnati; the vertical height or "lift" of the crescent tips</term>. This true state of existence <note>original: "sphuṭa-sattva"</note> remains even in the six-fold division; there, the regulation of the two horns is established. 

This is also proven here: in the two diagrams, the cord <note>original: "sūtra"; likely referring to the chord or mathematical line connecting the tips</note> is the primary factor. Directly perceived, those two small horns are indeed situated on the same meridian circle <term>yāmyānyavṛtta; the north-south great circle</term>. Although equality of the horns is mentioned in the context of the chord and the "arm" <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of the spherical triangle</term>, it might not appear so in the diagram. Only in the state of a clear, true "arm" <note>original: "sphuṭa-bāhu-bhāva"</note> is this equality of the horns fixed. Therefore, it is not described as being contradictory to the diagram.

Furthermore, when the center of the moon is attached to a different point, emerging from the center of the orb, it might be on the ecliptic <term>apama-maṇḍala</term>. Just as the rays of the sun <note>original: "nigamakara"</note> emerge, the moon's crescent is sought after. According to the intersection with one's own vertical circle <term>drigmandala</term>, thousands of different positions are sought. Having seen the horns drawn there, the reflection is immediately produced. 

<margin>
<unclear>86</unclear>
<unclear>64</unclear>
<unclear>55</unclear>
</margin>

From which point does the division of the moon's horn come? If the center of the celestial sphere <term>gola</term> is the same as the center of the moon, then how could those two halves be unequal? There, because of that, there is an absence of the "perpendicular" <term>koṭi; the vertical side of the right-angled triangle</term> above the horizon. In such a case, when the center is always avoiding the observer's zenith, equality is seen. Because there is an absence of the perpendicular, the two horns of the moon are never seen to be uneven in the sphere by the observer. 

Indeed, wherever the rule of the "absence of the perpendicular" <gloss>koṭy-abhāva</gloss> occurs, the two halves of the horns are level. This is not seen otherwise. Just as in a place with a latitude of sixty-eight degrees, the zodiac circle is always slanted. The emergence of the moon’s horns always occurs from the vertical circle. During an eclipse, the elevations of the horns are clearly distinct. This half-elevation occurs because of the "increase in the womb" <note>This likely refers to the "garbhiya" or geocentric parallax/calculation</note> and the absence of the perpendicular. 

Everywhere, according to the specific location <note>original: "deśa-vaśāt"</note>, any errors in the true meaning should not be spoken of here, having fulfilled the purpose through one's own intellect. Having seen that the pair of horns is level in the slanted circle, one sees them in the visual circle; there, another existence of latitude <gloss>śara; celestial latitude</gloss> appears in the lunar orb. By that alone, one should calculate the entire circle on the level of the ground. By these three methods, the illumination of the solar-lunar orb is correctly understood. This equality of the horns exists only in the state of the "true arm," but when there is a difference between the illuminated part and the vertical circle, a contradiction to that perceived equality arises. Thus, when there is a "true arm-state," the horns are not necessarily equal. From the rule of observed equality, there is a lack of a clear "arm," and from the absence of the perpendicular, the visual elevation is established by this rule.

It concludes that perceived equality of the crescent tips depends on the observer's vertical circle and the absence of a perpendicular angle.</summary>

### The Absence of the Zenith Distance Cosine

The absence of the cosine of the zenith distance <term>nata-koṭi; the sine of the angle complementary to the moon's altitude</term> does not result in a fall [of accuracy], according to the famous *Siddhāntaśiromaṇi* <note>original: "Siddhāntaśiromaṇi"; the 12th-century masterpiece by Bhāskara II</note>. In the efforts to support the appearance of the moon's bright portion <note>original: "asita-prajātā-samarthane"</note>, the illustrious Viśvarūpa <note>An earlier astronomer/commentator whom the author is likely critiquing or comparing</note> stated that the very absence of that cosine is a state of "rest" or "lying down." Indeed, the line <term>sūtra; a mathematical chord or line of sight</term> follows the direction of the power of the tips of the crescent <term>śṛṅga; the "horns" of the moon</term>. Since this follows the method stated by Bhāskara, the rule for the absence of the cosine is not merely a matter of theoretical knowledge.

As it has been said: regarding the chord of the tips of the crescent, that which is observed as the zenith distance is only found in the union of sight, not in the [abstract] sphere <term>gola; the celestial sphere or armillary sphere</term>. For those who know the sphere, the distance between the two tips of the moon is situated on that very line. Thus, in the absence of the cosine here, the logic is understood. Even there, in the perception of the planet, the pair of tips is connected. Why then have the wise defined the "inclination of the east-west" <term>prāk-para-nata</term>? This is because, in the absence of such definition, one's own intellect might be confused by the transverse movement described in the *Śiromaṇi*.

If it were otherwise, it would lead to a contradiction with the Vedas. Indeed, in a specific region, if the appearance is crooked or curved, then at the beginning of the lunar month, the bright part of the moon would always be greater, and the two tips would not be seen as they truly are. This cosine of the zenith distance, even when it relates to the bright portion of the moon, is like the sun's reflection—it remains full even if moved by a slight deviation. This is Bhāskara’s statement <note>original: "bhāskaroktiḥ"</note>.

The absence of the cosine in the moon's tips is a truth established on the line of the highest degree. This explanation of Śrī Viśvarūpa's meaning is compared against Bhāskara’s words. This has been critiqued by the best of the experts of the Siddhāntas <term>siddhānta-vidvarya; master astronomers</term> and the assembly of scholars. In drawing the elevation of the lunar disk, one must consider the prime vertical <term>sama-maṇḍala</term>. By understanding this equality, the chord of the dark portion is produced. The movement of the two tips is transverse. There, in the half-spoken part, in the division of the sky, a distance of five or six parts was constructed by the noble teachers <term>āryaiḥ</term>.

When there is a difference in the bright spot, the position is fixed by the north-south circle <term>yāmyottara-maṇḍala; the meridian</term>. It remains full like the sun, not elongated, nor in its natural state. Without that north-south meridian, in a moon that is inclined, there is no difference between the tips. In the field of the north-south circle, the shape of the inclination is explained. According to the rule, this is not a fault in the knowledge of the bright portion. Even though these are established in the celestial sphere, for those who are not experts in spherical thinking, this consideration is provided for the sake of visual knowledge. I have explained these specifics of the sphere for the purpose of knowing the causes of auspicious and inauspicious effects at the time of observation.

The elevation of the moon's tips <term>śṛṅgonnati</term> is declared. By the sun, the calculation of the "bow" <term>cāpa</term> and the "arrows" <term>vāna; mathematical sines or offsets</term> are determined. The clear position of the moon is known by the sun-like brilliance of Narasiṃha <note>Likely the author or a revered teacher: Narasiṃhabhānu</note>. Having known this, the formula for the elevation of the tips is sung. This was correctly understood by Śrīpati, Bhāskara, and others, as clearly as the moon on one's own forehead <note>original: "lalāṭa candre"; a metaphor for something obvious or a reference to Shiva</note>. When the "arrow" is divided by the parts, and the sum of the sun's position is taken, the resulting arc is the "lord of the bow." This then determines the bright portion, refined by the "arrow" of the latitude <term>śara; the celestial latitude of a planet</term>.

### The Calculation of Deflection and the Geocentric Perspective

In the arc of the zenith distance, that which is called **deflection** <note>original: "vilam," likely a corruption of "valanam"; the angle between the ecliptic and the local horizon/equator</note> is derived from the addition or subtraction of the sun’s position and the directions. In the sky, this deflection arises <unclear>टा गवल नेदि</unclear> and is known by the wise. 

When the union of the directions is stated as such, and the Moon <note>original: "mṛga"; literally "deer-marked," a common poetic name for the moon</note> is in the upper half of the circle, and if the directions are the same, the first [calculation] is used. If the first star is half-diminished, then the observer <unclear>न रा य दि</unclear> perceives it accordingly. 

All of this follows the rule of the directions. Now, it is said that if the six [digits of the moon’s diameter] are diminished by the bright portion <note>original: "śuklena"; refers to the illuminated phase of the moon</note>, and if that result is divided, then in this sequence, the measurement of the light is determined. In the middle of the **true disc** <term>spashta-mandala; the calculated appearance of the celestial body's diameter</term>, one should place a second transverse line, which is famously known as the **northern arc** <term>saumya-chāpa</term>. 

Where the line of the disc <term>sūtra</term> is located, the observer stands upon the earth. This text explains the vision of the observer. As far as the sight of the sun in the west is concerned, it should be performed according to the knowledge of the experts <note>original: "śarajñe"; those who know the 'arrows' or sines/geometry</note>. There, at the center of the circle, the division occurs. 

<center>
**The Geocentric Theory**
</center>

The geometric logic here follows the **geocentric radius** <term>bhūmi-garbha-sūtra; literally "the thread from the womb of the earth," referring to the line from the earth's center to the planet</term>. In the celestial sphere, where one leads the sight of the sun and the moon, the distance between the centers of the two discs is known as the **bright distance** <note>original: "sitābhidhe"</note>. This is the true difference between the sun's sectors on the third day <note>original: "purodine 3"</note>. When the centers of the two discs are joined, they become "bright" in the **circle** <term>maṇḍala</term>. 

If the "arrow" <note>the versine or height of the arc</note> is less, then the difference between the two in the circle of the stars is not seen by men. For the moon <note>original: "himayune"</note>, which is newly bright and rising, that brightness is not produced merely by the measurement. In the circle called "the bright," when the sight is produced in the circle, then in the north and south, or in the two "horns" of the moon <note>original: "mṛga-kadwaye"</note>, the vision arises. 

This is the true sight of the moon, which is different from the theoretical calculation. In the manual, the true vision is explained by the "bright" circle. In the upper half, and in the transverse or inclined positions, the sight follows the **vertical circle** <term>drgvṛtta; the circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>. This is established through the **graphical representation of Bhāskara** <term>bhāskara-parilekhana; the method of drawing astronomical phenomena described by Bhāskara II</term>. Where the upper horns of the moon are calculated for the sake of the zenith distance, the position of the moon below is not simply the same. Even through the sight of the sun, the upper half of the moon's visibility does not happen in any other way. The realization of this comes from the solar sphere <unclear>भा न सि डि</unclear>.

...the father and the wind. In that same way, whatever is seen in the example, at some time, Rahu <note>The ascending node of the moon, often associated with eclipses</note> is slightly diminished. In the absence of this perpendicularity, the calculation is perfected. The errors arising from the absence of perpendicularity are produced by the opposition of these factors. Thus, on the lunar map,

the two lunar circles and the white portion <note>original: "sitā"; the illuminated part of the moon</note> are within the circle. Likewise, in the visible circle, the body of the Moon <note>original: "śarī"; literally "the one with a body," referring to the Moon</note> is joined with the Sun. When situated at the face of that, the center of the Moon <note>original: "himāṁśu"; "the cool-rayed one"</note> always resides in its own circle.

Now, from the center of the observer's height, the distinction is known. Standing in its own lower [region], first, the illuminated portion of the Moon <note>original: "mṛgāṅka"; "the deer-marked one"</note> becomes equal to that. In the middle of that circle, the dark portion <note>original: "asita"; the unilluminated part of the moon</note> of the Moon exists. Then, within the circle of vision, in the clear deflection <term>valana; the angle of the moon's orientation</term>, the lines are brought forth. In the movement of the Moon, the distance between the centers of the Sun and Moon is known. On the clear circle, the distance of the Sun is the hypotenuse <term>karṇa; the longest side of a right triangle</term>. Likewise, in the Sun's disk,

the portion [of the arc] is the limit of the Moon’s own movement. The distance between the Moon and Sun is the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right triangle</term>. The difference between them is the **tip of the horn** <note>original: "śikhā"</note>. This is the **base** <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of a right triangle</term> for the desired calculation. 
In this way, the visibility of the tip of the horn from the center of the disk is clear in its own circle. Therefore, the result is the arc produced from the perpendicular. In the visible [circle], from the tip and the center of the disk, the clear distance is the base. For the desired calculation, the arc is produced. Now, when these two are joined, the difference is very small. Because of the similarity in nature,

and because the Sun's disk is situated nearby, and because it is slightly diminished, the proof of their separation is not achieved. As has been said, for fear of the two-fold error, that which is slightly diminished is not a flaw. 
In the **Crest-Jewel of Systems** <note>original: "Śiromaṇi"; refers to the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* by Bhāskara II</note>, it was stated that in the absence of perpendicularity, the Moon's <note>original: "mṛge"</note> position is half. But in the previous formula, the modern method is now shown here with various proofs, because of the rule of the bright portion. 

Therefore, this is the union of the Moon. And thus, in the upper half of the position, by the previously mentioned perpendicular and base, the elevation of the horns does not appear as it was thought. O calculators, look closely! By the power of the observer’s eye, the elevation of the horns of the Moon's disk is described. Just as the eye sees it, it is not an expansion. If it were as stated, the rays of the Sun would emerge from the Sun’s disk. In the path of the Moon and other planets, it is said that their light is somewhat different. Within the disks, there is visibility. Because of the lack of error in the world, having abandoned the state of distance, the visibility is precisely as it should be. Thus, when the Sun and Moon are in their respective circles during the lunar and solar phases,

the visibility is according to their positions. **Thus ends the Chapter on the Elevation of the Moon's Horns in the Siddhānta Tattva Viveka (The Essence of the Truth of the Systems) composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.** For the wise, only the Moon's light is the proof of the calculations of the various thinkers. The multipliers are: Mars 80, Mercury 110, Jupiter 60, Venus 120, Saturn 150. Beyond these are the nocturnal ones. In the calculation made for the solar [system], four factors are used: the sign, degree, minute, and second. From the difference of the node <term>pāta; the point where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term>, the true <term>sphuṭa; the corrected or observed position</term> latitude is found by multiplying and dividing by the radius.

In the calculation of the body’s latitude, the result is produced by the rays of the cold-rayed one <note>original: "śīta ruve"; referring to the Moon</note>. (3) In the arc, the minutes of the latitude become true. From the difference of the node, the true position and the direction of the sphere <note>original: "gola dik"; whether it is in the northern or southern celestial hemisphere</note> are determined. Here, in the case of the true planets, the same method applies.

The wise should perform this process for calculating the latitude. (4) These results should be applied inversely to the "Quick Motion" correction <note>original: "śīghra phala"; a correction roughly equivalent to the planetary equation in modern astronomy</note>. Combined with the expanded true designation, one should perform the calculation as stated.

In the case of the true planets, when calculating the deflection, the movement is like that of the Moon. From the node and the orbit, the deflection of the latitude is true, starting from Mars. (6) There is no other method for this; in the absence of elevation, on the Meru mountain <note>the mythological center of the world, serving as the north pole in astronomical texts</note>, the calculation is made by the faces of the angles. (7) If the Moon stays in the celestial circle, that becomes the great circle. The perfection of these two methods is achieved through the power of the calculation here. (8)

Divided by the movement of the planet and the nodes, the degrees are found. Whether by the Sun or by the shadow of the gnomon, the minutes of the celestial circle are determined. From the deflection and the precession <note>original: "ayanena"; the slow shifting of the Earth's axis</note>, the calculation of the deflection is achieved. (9)

By the cosine of the deflection, multiplied by the radius and divided, the result in the precession of the circle is obtained. (11) The sines of the signs of the planets, multiplied by the radius and divided by the distance, yield the deflection of the stars. (12) In the sphere, the measurement of the cosine is the same; in its absence, the direction is called the "precessional direction." (13)

And in the arc of the deflection, the size of the planetary disk is understood. In the place of the node, the calculations for the signs are performed. (17) Mutually, the cosines should be calculated; the multiplier and divisor are established. When situated in the arc, the calculation is perfected. (15) Through the deflection of the latitude, the arc of the planetary latitude is produced as stated. Without the tip, there is no result. (16)

The latitude of Mars is brilliant. Combined with the movement of the spheres, the value is obtained. (17) Because the precessional calculation was not done by its own power, there is no error in the true position of the planet regarding its latitude. (18) In the orbit <term>vimaṇḍala; the secondary circle or the planet's own orbital path</term>, the planet is diminished or increased by seven. From the position of the node, the movement in the circle is different. (19)

As long as the circle exists—for both the northern and southern directions—the calculations for the latitude and precession are perfected by the same method. (20)

The multiplier of the planet, multiplied by the radius and divided by the cosine, produces the precessional arc according to its own direction. (21) The cosine of the planet multiplied by the radius, or the sine of the planet, gives the declination. (11) In the writing, the cause of the contact <note>original: "sparśa"; the beginning of an eclipse</note> and its direction should be corrected. The sine and cosine of the planet, divided by the radius, yield the result. (13) In the arc, the cosine is situated in the direction of the declination. The sine of the planet's declination is the true declination. (15)

From the sine of the signs, the true declination is found in the arc. In the calculation of the stars, the cosine is used here. (15) From the sine of 24 degrees <term>jinajyā; the sine of the maximum declination (obliquity) of the ecliptic, traditionally 24° in Indian astronomy</term>, multiplied and divided, the result is the latitude or the cosine of the deflection. Or, the sine of 24 degrees multiplied and divided by the cosine of the deflection. (15) In the arc of sixty-six, the sine is produced. Multiplied by the radius and divided, the result is the true declination in the arc. It is corrected.

The sequence for the magnitude of the planetary body is determined by the formula for the conjunction of the planetary orbits <term>yoga-sūtra; a mathematical rule for aligning celestial bodies</term>. When the planetary disc <term>vimb; the visible disk of a planet</term> is multiplied by the silver [constant] and divided, the resulting arc should be calculated here in degrees. In the calculation of the solstice <term>ayana; the sun's path between solstices</term>, one must apply the result of the true declination <term>sphuṭa-krānti; the corrected angular distance north or south of the celestial equator</term> produced by the arc. (29)

By dividing the values, one obtains the cosine <term>koṭijyā; the sine of the complementary angle</term>. Using the radius <term>trijyā; the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, usually 3438 minutes</term> and the cosine of the planetary disc, the arc is determined in degrees. These degrees represent the time-coordinates of the planet. When the planet is in the first or third quadrant, the correction is subtracted; in the second or fourth, it is added. (31) When the degrees are calculated at the end of the calculation, the result is the position of the planet within the zodiac circle.

Initially, for the two [the Sun and Moon], the displacement of the planetary disc is considered equal. (32) Multiplying by the desired parts, one finds the movement of the planetary body. This produces the maximum celestial latitude <term>parama-śara; the greatest angular distance a planet reaches from the ecliptic</term>. This is the displacement of the moving planet. (33) In the celestial sphere <term>gola</term>, when this value is adjusted by the declination, it becomes the true declination. In this treatise, by multiplying the planetary body, the arc of the result of the displacement is found. (34)

The corrected declination is adjusted by the direction—north or south—of the planetary body's latitude. Thus, for the planet, the sine of the true declination is obtained. (35) When joined with three signs [90 degrees], the result is the true "day-radius" <term>antyā; a technical term related to the planet's diurnal circle</term>. In the same way as mentioned before, the planet’s position in the night is observed through the calculation of time. (36) Thus, by using the coordinates of the celestial equator <term>nāḍī; the equatorial circle</term>, the planet's declination is made "true." Following the path of the planet, all calculations should be established. (37)

Through the coordinates of the equator, the latitude is corrected and the true declination is established. However, there may be some deviation <note>original: "vyabhicāro"; meaning a mathematical discrepancy or exception</note> in certain cases. (38) This occurs due to the nature of the celestial sphere and the solstices. In such a case, the sine of the true declination is nearly equal to the smaller sine. (39) Even if the position remains fixed, a deviation occurs in other places. Therefore, it is said that this should not be applied to all calculations. (40)

Here is the underlying logic <term>upapatti; mathematical proof or rationale</term>: The two poles <term>dhruva</term> and the pivot points must be fixed on the moving circle, not on the ecliptic <term>krānti-vṛtta</term>. Because of the difference in the planet’s longitude, the position on the celestial equator is more "true." Therefore, the true declination and longitudes should be taken from the polar circle <term>dhruva-vṛtta</term>, as these represent the planet's existence according to the celestial sphere. The point where the planet is marked on the ecliptic does not coincide with the intersection of the polar circle. Therefore, these two positions exist by their own nature. There, the intersection occurs neither at the position of the physical planet nor at the calculated symbolic position. The mark of the planet is not on the equatorial circle, but is separated by the displacement of the planetary body. Just as the polar circle is separated from the celestial equator, so too is the planetary body.

# Regarding the Calculation of True Declination

The celestial equator <term>nāḍīvṛtta; the great circle of the celestial sphere in the same plane as the Earth's equator</term> is indeed established as the celestial equator. By means of the chord of the arc, the degrees of the maximum declination <term>parakrānti; the greatest angular distance of the Sun or a planet from the celestial equator</term> are determined. Now, regarding the lords of declination, there is the true declination <term>spaṣṭakrānti; corrected for planetary latitude</term> and the scriptural declination <term>śāstrakrānti; the theoretical declination of the ecliptic point</term> which are to be understood as calculated through the quadrants.

Now, concerning the lords of declination, the degrees of true declination and the degrees of scriptural declination follow a sequence of two degrees. From the intersection of the celestial spheres <term>gola-sandhi; the points where the ecliptic and equator cross</term> and the planetary image, the calculation of the true declination and the deflection <term>ayana-valana; the arc between the pole of the equator and the pole of the ecliptic</term> is explained as a necessary procedure. Just as the declination is handled, so too is the planetary conjunction. Within the circle of the stars and the ecliptic <term>krāntivṛtta; the sun's apparent path</term>, the equinoctial deflection of the planetary image is calculated at the peak of the maximum declination. 

When the planetary image is at the zenith, the declination is simply the declination itself. Through the intelligence of the planetary members, the planetary sine <term>jyā</term> and the arc degrees are found in two degrees at the intersection of the spheres. With the planetary image and the intersection of the celestial equator, the equinoctial deflection is established. Once the true declination is known, the method for finding the declination is performed. Through the planetary cosine <term>koṭijyā</term>, whether the declination is even or odd, it remains the same. On one side, the maximum declination stays fixed where it is equal. When the planetary declination is elsewhere, the deflection is proven equal. (41)

->Therefore, the calculation of the oblique corner is not considered equal to the corner.<-

### The Rationale for True Declination

Now, the method for the rationale <term>vāsanā; the geometric or logical explanation of a rule</term> of true declination is presented, as well as the relationship between the two intersections of the spheres. Within the circle where the planetary disks are led, the interval between the three circles—the solstitial circle <term>ayana-maṇḍala</term>, the ecliptic, and the celestial equator—is the supreme limit. The interval between the ecliptic and the celestial equator is the traditional declination. In that place, in the moving circle, the ecliptic, the hypothetical ecliptic, and the celestial equator, where they are joined by the latitude <term>śara; the angular distance of a planet from the ecliptic</term>, the celestial equator is the only real one. 

In that moving circle and the ecliptic, the degrees of the disk and the knower of the disk are found. Likewise, in the orbit, the lords of declination and the true positions are established through the method of the sphere by those skilled in the expansion of the subject. When time is variable, the non-elevated part of the true circle is the sine <term>jīvā</term> of the disk's origin. Without that calculation, the success of the visibility calculation <term>dṛk-karma; the calculation to find a planet's rising/setting or its projection onto the ecliptic</term> cannot be achieved.

Having subtracted the local latitude from the corrected center <term>kendra</term>, one should project it. Similarly, by multiplying the sine by the cosine and dividing by the radius <term>trijyā; the standard radius of 3438 minutes</term>, the difference between the arc and the result is the sine of the latitude. That sine, being doubled and divided by the divisor, becomes the sine of the equinoctial shadow. In the three moving arcs, according to the direction, the arc is corrected by the degrees. (45)

The true Mars <term>kuja</term> and others, starting from the center of Capricorn <term>makarādi-kendra</term>, move in various directions. There, for the sake of Mars, the true degrees are made into the arc of declination. That is first multiplied by the planetary sine and divided by the radius. (55) The cosine of the true arc of declination is the base <term>bhuja</term>. The remainder is the cosine. Because of the true nature, the sum of the two declinations is the sine. (42) By the arc of the degrees of inclination, the spheres of the celestial globe are indicated.

# On the Measurement of Planetary Disks and Visibility Corrections

The disk <term>bimba; the apparent or physical area of a celestial body</term> appears as if setting or rising due to the power of its own position in the upper half of the celestial sphere. In the case of Mars <term>Kuja; the planet Mars</term>, when it is in the upper half **47** and positioned below the horizon, the planets located in their respective orbits move through the day and night. Just as in the calculation of the planetary motion, the names of the planets are associated with their clearer rays **48**. Through the logic of the celestial sphere <term>gola-yukti; the geometric reasoning applied to the three-dimensional celestial model</term>, the wise should understand the entry into the Earth's shadow. The measurement of the planetary circle’s disk, which arises from the center of the celestial sphere, is expressed in the formula for leagues <term>yojana-sūtra; a formula to calculate distance in yojanas, a traditional unit of distance approx. 8-9 miles</term> **49**.

The consideration of the sphere arises from the calculation of the two diameters. By means of these mathematical methods, for the two planets situated in the celestial sphere on a desired day, one should investigate the difference **50**. For those who know the Sun, the calculation of the difference is made through the Sine <term>siñjinī or jyā; the trigonometric sine function used in Indian astronomy</term>. By multiplying by the Cosine <term>koṭijyā; the sine of the complementary angle</term> and dividing by the Radius <term>trimaurvī; the radius of the sine-circle, typically 3438 arcminutes</term>, the resulting arc is determined for the planets **51**. In this assumed scenario, once the Sines are established and the results obtained, one divides by the time to find the arc of the Sun and the planet. When the difference of the two is in the same direction, it gives the assumed interval between the planet and the Sun. By calculating the power of the shadow, the half-interval of the planet is found. After multiplying by the Sun’s difference, the position of the planet is determined. Therefore, in the prescribed authority, this is how the work is to be done.

Then, for the wise who understand the names of the planets, the result arises from the arc of the entire sphere. Here, for fear of being too lengthy <note>original: "vistarabhayā"; a common phrase used by ancient authors to indicate they are summarizing complex proofs for brevity</note>, the detailed underlying rationale <term>vāsanā; the demonstrative proof or "realization" of a mathematical rule</term> is not fully stated for the learned **55**. The movement of a planet is greatest when it arises from the center of the Earth and reaches the horizon. At that time, the disk of the planet situated in the distance is not seen there because of its Earth-bound number **56**.

The time of visibility correction <term>dṛkkama; the calculation performed to find the point on the ecliptic that rises or sets with a planet having latitude</term> is described by the wise. It is of two kinds: the solstitial correction <term>āyana-dṛkkama</term> and the diurnal/latitudinal correction. Through the difference of the solstitial points, the success of the calculation is achieved. As long as the planet is on its own path towards the equator, it is not seen to rise. Because it faces the direction of the naming, it always faces the zenith. By lifting or lowering based on its own latitude, the result is found according to the north or south direction **58**.

In the northern path <term>saumyāyana; the passage of the sun from the winter to the summer solstice</term>, this is said to be in reverse. From the sign of Aries, at the setting on the horizon, the planet rises or sets according to its name. For the disk of the clear planet, where the thread of the calculation meets the horizon, that is where the zodiac sign <term>lagna; the rising point or ascendant</term> is found **60**. By subtracting the numbers from the equator's horizon, the solstitial visibility correction is found, and its interval is given in minutes of arc <term>liptikā; 1/60th of a degree</term> **61**. When the planets are in different directions in their paths, they are seen as "visible planets" <term>dṛggraha; the planet as it actually appears to the observer</term> after being corrected for the true Earth-shadow **62**. The true latitude <term>sphuṭa-śara</term> is processed with the planet’s declination. Alternatively, the deflection of the visible planet should be performed. In that case, the staff of the sine of the own-latitude is the Radius; by subtracting it from the uncorrected value, the true value is obtained **64**. At the top of the disk... <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page</note>.

# On True Declination and the Visibility Correction (Ayana-drkkarma)

From the formula of the diameter, the declination of the planet <term>kheta; celestial body</term> is determined. The difference between the calculated positions is found through the method of the celestial sphere. In the *Siddhanta Shiromani* <note>original: "shiramaner-ganite"; referring to the famous 12th-century astronomical treatise by Bhaskara II</note>, this was established for the use of wise men who possess the essence of astronomical principles <term>siddhanta-tattva</term>. Now, for the delight of calculators <note>astronomers/mathematicians</note>, I shall explain the correction known as **Ayana** <term>ayanavalambana; a correction to planetary longitude to account for the angle between the ecliptic and the equator</term> in a clear manner. 

Multiply the Radius <term>trijya; the standard radius used in sine tables, usually 3438'</term> by the planet’s [positional] qualities, and divide by the square of its own Cosine <term>kotijya</term>. Then, from that result, the declination is found. The maximum declination <note>the obliquity of the ecliptic, roughly 24 degrees in ancient Indian systems</note> is multiplied by the Radius and divided by the sine of the planet's position. This calculation, involving twenty-four parts, yields the arc **88**.

When the calculation differs from this, it is called the "unclear" declination. The true declination is derived from the difference of the squares of the sine and cosine, multiplied by the Radius. This provides the value for the true declination over three days **89**.

The arc equal to that, when multiplied by the sine of the planet's declination plus three signs <note>90 degrees</note> and divided by the sine of the corrected declination, results in the sine of the true corrected declination. The arc of that result is the final value **90**.

Alternatively, the cosine of the corrected declination, multiplied by the Radius and divided by the sine of the true declination, gives the count of the degrees in the arc. This is known by the name **Ayana** **10**.

### On the Calculation of Latitudinal Differences

Now, when the directions of the two declinations <term>kranti</term> are the same, one should perform a subtraction; if the directions are different, an addition is made. From the true degrees, the latitude <term>shara; the celestial latitude of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> is calculated in degrees. This is called the latitudinal declination.

Suppose a quarter of a part becomes the difference in leagues <term>yojana</term>. From the true daily motion, the arc is produced. If the first [value] is greater, it arises from the first or last quadrant; similarly, it may arise from the second or third quadrant **71**.

<unclear>paraspam rantyayata</unclear> In the end, when the directions of the two declinations are the same, the arc of the position is subtracted. In the second [case], it is subtracted from a half-circle <note>180 degrees</note>. In the third, it is added to the half-circle. Finally, it should be subtracted from the full circle <term>chakra; 360 degrees</term>. Having performed these calculations starting from the signs and degrees **75**, the planet becomes corrected through these formulas. Between the two planets, the middle position is found. <unclear>atvam ram vicabhyadhikam</unclear> Through this knowledge, the calculation should be performed **8**.

At the time of the calculation, for the rising and setting, the daily motion is used. For a planet situated on the equator <term>niraksha-desha; the "place without latitude," i.e., the terrestrial equator</term>, the rising of the signs is calculated. In the north or south latitude <term>shara</term>, the time is determined by this method. This is explained for northern declination; for southern, the calculation is reversed. Through effort, the planet of the direction is found. It is multiplied by six signs, then by seven, and then by the horses <note>symbolic number 7</note>. <unclear>vishvanna nane</unclear> It becomes the sign for those situated on the equator **75**.

What was composed in the *Shiromani* <note>Bhaskara's text</note> has been stated here through my own intellect as a sequence of planetary visibility corrections <term>drkkarma</term>. There, the sages have noted that the time of action is determined by the specific degrees of the planet's position **95**. When the Ayana and the latitude are in the same direction, the cycle is **151**. <unclear>ka nadi nasi pache kochan pru deshcha</unclear>

Because planets possess latitude, they appear to move along a circle passing through the poles of the ecliptic <term>kadamba-vṛtta; the circle on which celestial latitude is measured</term>. From the precessional parts, the declination <term>kranti</term> of the apparent planet is found **77**. The distance between the center of the planet's disk and the equator is the true declination. These equinoctial parts <note>original: "vishuvan-shaka"</note> are located at the junction of the celestial spheres. The visibility correction of the apparent planet is always equal to its longitude when it meets the horizon. The maximum true declination <term>sphuṭa-kranti</term> is multiplied by the sine of the true declination and divided by the radius of the day-circle <term>dinajya</term>. The result is the sine of the visibility correction; the arc of that result gives the equinoctial degrees **80**.

Multiply the sine of the degrees of the planet's position by sixty and by the radius <term>trijya</term>, then divide by the cosine of the day-circle. The arc of that result is the equinoctial degrees of the apparent planet corrected for latitude **81**. Multiply the sine of the planet's own cosine <term>kotijya</term> by the Radius, and the resulting arc of the "arm" <term>bhuja; the side of a right triangle</term> belongs to the planet corrected for precession **82**.

If the maximum true declination is greater than the given value, the calculation follows the previously stated rule for the quadrant. One should be known as the "base" and the other as the "complement." Wise men should know this from the specific direction of their own country, just as before, to find the apparent planet **83**. In this way, the difference between the equinoctial degrees of the two apparent planets is the latitude. When the signs are the same, they are added; when different, they are subtracted **84**.

In this calculation of the time of birth or observation, the time is given in units of <term>ghatika; a unit of 24 minutes</term> **85**. The sine of the day-circle, determined by the movement of the degrees and the obliquity of the ecliptic <note>original: "jina-laba"; referring to the 24 degrees of the ecliptic's tilt</note>, is the sine of the true declination. The planet's disk, which moves on the ecliptic, is also measured on the circle passing through the ecliptic poles <term>kadamba-vṛtta</term>. Because of the difference between these circles, this correction is preferred by the wise **87**.

The sine of the planet's latitude is multiplied by the sine of the visibility correction and divided by the sine of the true day-circle. The result obtained is the visibility correction in units of time <term>ghatika</term>. This is known even from the works of the *Siddhanta Shiromani* <note>original: "shiromani-krtadapi"; referring to the 12th-century masterpiece by Bhaskara II</note>. # The Calculation of Deflection and Visibility

The cosine of the arc <term>koti-guna; the R-cosine value</term> is multiplied by the radius <term>trijya; the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, typically 3438'</term> to produce the result of the two visibility corrections. Alternatively, the sine of the deflection <term>valana-jya; the sine of the angle of deflection</term> is produced from the planet's disk and its position relative to the solstices **1**. When this is multiplied by the uncorrected latitude <term>asphuta-ishu; the "rough" or mean celestial latitude</term> and divided by the radius, the visibility correction for the solstice <term>ayana-drk-karma</term> arises. This is due to the deflection of the ecliptic, through which the true latitude <term>sphuta-ishu</term> is determined by the method of progression. Subtracting the declination of the planet's disk, the sine is calculated from the planet's visibility <term>khaga-drg-jya</term> by the learned **2**.

The visibility correction in time <term>drk-karma-kala; the time difference between the rising of the ecliptic point and the actual planet</term> exists here at the interval of the nodes <term>pata</term> and is derived from the arc. In this way, the visibility correction related to the solstices <term>ayana-drk-karma</term> is described according to the traditional teachings. Now, the eighth step concerns the mean declination <term>madhya-apama</term> of the planet arising from the solstice **3**. From these two, the true declination of the disk is calculated. In the circle of the sun and planets, the sum <term>yoga</term> or difference <term>viyoga</term> of these two is taken based on whether their directions <term>disha</term> are the same or opposite. This method is well-known to the virtuous scholars **4**.

## Rising and Setting Corrections

Now, by this visibility correction, the planet’s longitude <term>graha-arka</term> is adjusted by addition or subtraction <term>dhana-rna; literally "plus-minus"</term> depending on the northern direction. This is called the "rising" correction <term>udaya-akhya</term>, and the "setting" correction <term>asta-sanjna</term> is derived inversely from what was stated **5**.

Similarly, from the sine of the hour angle <term>nata-kala-jya</term> multiplied by the sine of the latitude, and divided by the semi-diameter of the planet's own small circle <term>svopa-vrtta-dala-vishkambha</term>, the sine of the deflection known as the "latitude deflection" <term>aksha-valana</term> is produced **6**. 

When this is multiplied by the double-radius of the disk and divided by the radius, the "latitude-deflection-degrees" <term>aksha-lavajya</term> are found. At the time of rising and setting, this rule governs the arc of deflection **7**. 

By applying the northern or southern deflection correctly, the "true deflection" <term>spashta-valana</term> is found in the sky. Through the power of the rising and setting, the sine of the corrected position is found **8**. When the latitude <term>ishu</term> is multiplied by the cosine, and divided by the radius, the resulting arc <term>phala-capa</term> is used. By that sine, multiplied by the radius, the true latitude of the planet is determined **9**.

That true latitude <term>sphuta-shara</term>, when added or subtracted on the prime vertical <term>sama-sutra</term>, always results in the true visibility of the planet. Here, the true movement of the disk is established in the same way as the declination **10**. 

From the true deflection arising from the planet, the sine is derived. When divided by the radius and multiplied by the multiplier, the true latitude's direction is found. The true deflection of the disk, multiplied by the true latitude and divided by the radius, becomes the visibility correction on the celestial circle **11**.

## On the Diurnal Motion

In the transition between chapters, the true deflection is calculated. Through the true measure of visibility correction, in the order of the north and south breaths <term>prana; a unit of time equal to 4 seconds, also used here as a measure of arc</term>, the deflection is applied. If the deflection is southerly, the calculation is done inversely for the planet's longitude **14**. 

The rising of the planet's disk occurs at the horizon. The point on the ecliptic that rises with the planet is its "rising point" <term>udaya-lagna</term>. Due to the northern latitude, this is explained differently. For the sun and planets, the rising and setting occur daily due to the <term>pravaha-anila; the "pro-vaha" wind, believed to be the cosmic force that drives the celestial sphere from East to West</term> **15**. For those located on the equator, the rising occurs exactly when the longitude matches the horizon...

When the positions of the Sun <term>Arka</term> and the planet are nearly equal at the time of setting, the planetary disk <term>bimba-graha</term>—due to the calculation of its own visible position—either sets or rises. In the same way, at the moment when the rising point of the ecliptic <term>lagna</term> is greater or lesser, a specific calculation occurs **18**. The time interval, measured in units of twenty-four minutes <term>ghatika</term>, whether already passed or yet to come, is determined by the difference between the planet’s own rising point and the ascendant. These units of time, known as "time-minutes" <term>kalabhidha-ghatika</term>, are calculated through one's own methods. When the desired planet is near the time of rising or setting, it follows these rules **20**.

Wherever I have brought other methods from different places based on their class, they are surely from this essential truth <note>Kamalakara is likely referring to his source material or the "Tattva" in the title of his work, *Siddhanta Tattva Viveka*</note>. I shall now describe the visibility and the setting of the planetary disks and the stars at the time of their setting, without the need for the usual complex rising-point corrections <term>drkkarma-vilagna-siddhi</term>, for the benefit of the wise **21**. At the time of setting, first, the planet’s disk should not be calculated as previously described. From the longitude of the planet <term>sphuta-graha</term> and the intersection of the ecliptic and the celestial equator <term>kranti-pata</term>, and from one's own location, one calculates the time-units of the junction <term>sampata-nadika</term> within the planet's own day-measure **22**.

When the center of the planet is in the three signs of the quadrant, the remaining part of the visibility correction is considered. If the day or night measure is increased, the calculation for the planet and the Sun follows the known movement **23**. When this is established, the visibility is certain. I will now speak of the differences in what has passed or is to come in a single night **24**. At the time of the Sun's setting, if the Sun is increased by six signs <note>This identifies the point on the opposite horizon</note>, the remainder of the night is calculated. By the method of determining the rising sign <term>lagna-ayana</term>, one finds the rising of the planet’s disk <term>graha-udaya</term> at its own rising point **27**.

In this way, for those who seek to see, the planet is joined with the horizon at the time mentioned in the prescribed order. Whether at the rising of the Sun or the rising of the Moon, the ascendant is calculated for that time. One must understand this from the duration of the night. By understanding the sequence of what has passed and what is to come, the daily rising and setting are known. Thus, due to the proximity of the Sun <term>Arka-sannidhya</term>, the disk of the planet—which was previously visible continuously—suddenly disappears due to the intensity of the light. This is called "setting" <term>astamana; heliacal setting</term> **30**. Even if it remains invisible for some time, the great sages do not call that "setting" unless it is caused by this proximity. Generally, this invisibility to humans is born from the mass of the Sun’s rays <note>The text mentions "candramshu" which usually means lunar rays, but in the context of solar proximity/combustion, it refers to the overwhelming glare of the Sun's light</note> **31**.

These methods for calculating the disappearance are always applied to the moments of rising and setting. The wise should know this applies to the disk and to the Sun **32**. Following the rising and setting of the Sun, even before or after, the planetary disk remains visible in the sky. If the distance is small, the visibility is weak; if great, it is clear. Some say this is the only cause, but it is not. The proximity is a factor, but the true cause is the superior brilliance <term>tejas</term> of the Sun, which overcomes the lesser brilliance of the planet **34**. Whether they belong to different classes or the same, the disks are perceived by me as being obscured by this intensity of light.

Likewise, those positioned vertically and horizontally appear as if joined. **35** Because of the different types, such as coarse pearls and so on, it is appropriate to say that there is a distinct perception arising from the name of knowledge. For it is certain that the divisions of time are perceived as distinct through the application of mathematical logic. Therefore, in this context, the reasoning for such a conception is remembered—or rather, it is indeed the name itself. **37**

<margin>38</margin> Seven, the Vedas <note>original: "veda" = 4</note>, and arrows <note>original: "isu" = 5</note>, combined with parts... these are declared as the "time-parts" <term>kalansha</term> <note>These are the degrees of the equator that must separate a planet from the Sun for the planet to be visible to the naked eye.</note>. For the Moon and others, namely Venus <term>Bhrigu</term>, Mercury <term>Saumya</term>, Mars <term>Rakta</term>, and the rest, these values are decreased when they are in retrograde motion <term>vakra</term> by independent means. 

The diameter of the Sun's disk <term>arka-bimba</term> has been mentioned previously. Half of that is the "mark of the body" <note>likely referring to the radius or a specific lunar feature</note>. By its own time, the "time-degrees" <term>kalansha</term> are determined for the day. **39** When a 

planet is situated near the Sun within these time-parts, its disk <term>bimba</term>—due to the Sun's rays—is not visible even slightly. **40** Beyond that time when it exceeds its own time-degrees, it becomes 

<margin>41</margin> very clear before sunrise or after sunset. That disk of the Sun is thus declared. Therefore, in this matter, it is decided that if the difference between the planet and the Sun is within the stated time-degrees, it is obscured. 

When the degree-difference between the Sun and the planet is known, and that difference is less than the visibility limit, it cannot be seen on that day. If the difference is greater, it is visible; otherwise, it is not visible at all. Here, 

the calculated position of the planet following the observation is proven by the connection of the "latitude-arc" <term>shara</term>. The difference between the observed planet and the Sun is established through direct perception. As stated by the venerable Vishnu: "When there is a distance between the calculated planet and the Sun's disk, and that distance is equal to the stated time-degrees, it is considered the moment of visibility." This nearly 

equals the difference between the calculated Moon and the Sun. Indeed, for the sake of the people's understanding, the calculation of the disk at the end of the "time-degrees" is explained here by the Universal Scholar <note>original: "sarvabhauma" - a title for Kamalakara himself</note> as a matter of course. For the calculation of the diameter of the disks for those born of breath <note>human observers</note>, the distance in the form of the hypotenuse <term>karna</term> is used. 

When the distance is divided, the "time-degree" disk-minutes are produced. **45** These minutes are considered to be fifteen <note>original: "pañca-dasha"</note> when divided by the "time-degrees." In this way, the disks are calculated according to their own variations in size. This is described in reverse order. Therefore, the appearance of the disk, as previously described in the scriptures, is produced according to the "tithi-parts" <term>tithyamsha</term> and time-degrees. Such a famous method for the disks is not stated everywhere, because here the calculated diameter and the appearance of the disk are not always equal to the "time-degrees." **51**

Therefore, because the appearance is equal, the nature of that difference is explained. Furthermore, if the calculated time-parts are at the beginning of the Sign Aries and so on, according to the visual observation of one's own place, the mathematical methods previously mentioned may result in smaller values, but never more. For those with good vision, the calculated results are indeed observed in the sky.

<summary>This page defines the "time-degrees" (kalansha) required for planets to be visible relative to the Sun's position and explains that visibility is a result of the angular distance exceeding these specific limits. Kamalakara argues that mathematical theory must align with direct observation (pratyaksha) of the planetary disks.</summary>

...from the obstruction. The true Sun and the true planet should be calculated for the time of sunset in the west and sunrise in the east. If the daily motion <term>bhukti</term> <note>The distance a planet travels in one day.</note> is greater than the other, <gloss>addition</gloss> it is called the "fast-moving" <term>shighra</term>. Similarly, that which has a smaller daily motion is known as the "slow-moving" <term>manda</term>. **90**

Slow-moving planets like Saturn <note>original: "arkaja" — son of the Sun</note>, Jupiter <note>original: "jiva"</note>, and Mars <note>original: "kuja"</note> rise in the east. Mars rises in the east when emerging from the Sun's light. When the "fast-moving" planets <note>Mercury and Venus</note> have a motion greater than the Sun's, their rising occurs in the west. 

The calculation for the time of these events is now stated: The time-degrees <term>kalansha</term> are produced from the difference between the true Sun and the planet, multiplied by six. **91** 

According to these stated time-degrees, the positions of the planets are divided by the degrees of rising and setting. By the difference between the daily motions of the planet and the Sun, multiplied by the velocity of the retrograde motion, the time of the past or future rising/setting is found. That time, determined by the positions of the planet and the Sun, becomes suitable for practical astronomical use. A planet is considered "clear" or "visible" once it has moved outside the Sun's rays. **92**

When the Sun's position is less than that of a "slow" planet, the rising occurs in the east and the setting in the west. If the Sun's position is greater than a "fast" planet, the timing is like that of the Moon. If the Sun is greater than the planet, the rising is in the west. This is the convergence of time. Whether the planet is "fast" or "slow," I shall now explain the specific distinctions for the sake of clarity. From the stated time-degrees, one should calculate the elapsed or remaining days. **93**

<margin>68</margin> 

Furthermore, having established the group of planets, the wise observer should always treat a "fast" planet in retrograde <term>vakra</term> motion as if it were a "slow" planet. The gods did not describe this specifically, leaving the natural course of the planets and the Sun to be understood through calculation. In this matter, the special calculation mentioned by the Greeks <note>original: "yavana" — referring to Western or Islamic astronomical traditions</note> is considered useless and redundant. 

Venus and Mercury, when moving directly, rise and then reach retrograde motion in that same place. Previously, they would have set there; then, after becoming retrograde and then direct again, they reach their setting point. **94**

### The Derivation
The rising and setting certainly occur when one body is further ahead or behind the other; this is well-known. If the Sun is at a higher altitude at night, then it is dark. When the Sun's position is less than the slow planet, the rising of the Sun follows. In the case of a "fast" planet, the rising in the eastern direction occurs at night because the interval between them decreases. If it is greater, the setting occurs in the evening for the same reason. Thus, the conjunction occurs through the movement of the "fast" planet. If the Sun is greater than a "slow" planet, the future conjunction will occur after many months, provided the interval between the two continues to decrease.

<summary>Kamalakara explains the mathematical methods for determining the heliacal rising and setting of planets, distinguishing between the behaviors of "fast" (inferior) and "slow" (superior) planets while critiquing Greek (Yavana) astronomical methods as redundant.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition regarding the visibility of planets and stars relative to the Sun. It follows the previous discussion on the definition of "time-degrees" (kalansha) and how the speed of a planet (manda or shighra) determines its visibility.</meta>

<page-num>२२</page-num>

...the increase [of distance]. In that interval, if the distance is greater than the required **time-degrees** <term>kalansha</term> <note>The specific degrees of separation from the Sun required for a celestial body to be seen with the naked eye.</note>, the planets will rise or set. If the distance is less than the required amount, the planets are obscured by the Sun's glare. 

In this way, for the Sun's relation to the **slow-moving planets** <term>manda</term> <note>Such as Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, an increase in the interval leads to rising in the east, while a decrease leads to setting. When the calculated distance is greater than the required limit, the rising occurs; when less, the setting occurs. For the **fast-moving planets** <term>shighra</term> <note>Mercury and Venus</note>, the opposite logic applies regarding their movement relative to the Sun. 

When the Sun and a fast planet are at the same degree, that is the "greatest setting" <term>parama-asta</term>. From that point, as the distance increases beyond the time-degrees, the planet becomes visible again. This visibility is calculated based on the difference in daily motion between the Sun and the planet. <unclear>....</unclear> Thus, the time of rising or setting is determined by the days elapsed or remaining.

The time-degrees for the fixed stars <note>Nakshatras</note> are as follows:
For the stars **Agastya** <note>Canopus</note>, **Lubdhaka** <note>Sirius</note>, **Brahma** <note>Capella</note>, **Rudra** <note>Betelgeuse</note>, **Abhijit** <note>Vega</note>, **Indra** <note>Jyeshtha</note>, **Citra** <note>Spica</note>, **Aditya** <note>Punarvasu</note>, and **Anila** <note>Svati</note>, the time-degrees are specifically measured. **54**

For the stars **Bhaga** <note>Purva Phalguni</note>, **Aryama** <note>Uttara Phalguni</note>, **Ashvini**, **Isha** <note>Ardra</note>, **Yamya** <note>Bharani</note>, **Basu** <note>Dhanishta</note>, **Hasta**, and **Shravana**, the degrees are 14 <note>original: "shakra" — a numerical code representing the 14 Indras</note>. **55**

For **Agni** <note>Krittika</note>, **Rudra** <note>Ardra</note>, **Ahi** <note>Ashlesha</note>, **Mitra** <note>Anuradha</note>, **Ambu** <note>Purva Ashadha</note>, **Vishvadeva** <note>Uttara Ashadha</note>, and **Mula**, the degrees are measured as 15. The remaining stars are also assigned their specific time-degrees to determine their rising and setting relative to the Sun's position. **56**

The distance between the Sun and the desired planet or star is measured in minutes of arc <note>original: "lipta"</note>. Dividing this by the daily motion of the Sun <gloss>relative motion</gloss> gives the result in days, indicating when the object will rise or set. By comparing the position of the Sun with the rising sign <term>lagna</term>, one identifies whether the object is visible in the eastern or western horizon. **57**

It is not possible for stars located far to the north or south to be seen if their distance is less than the required degrees. This includes stars like **Abhijit**, **Hasta**, **Svati**, and **Vishvadeva**. Because of their high northern or southern latitude <term>shara</term>, their visibility is affected by the Sun's rays differently than planets on the ecliptic. **58**

Now, the calculation for the **new moon crescent** <term>shukla pratipada</term> is described. On the first day of the bright fortnight, to determine the appearance of the Moon, one must calculate the true positions of both the Sun and the Moon at sunset.

Whether in the north or south, if the distance from the Sun is very small, the planet is lost in its glare. If that distance <term>kendra</term> <note>Specifically referring to the orbital position relative to the Sun</note> is known, the rising and setting in the directions can be determined. If one performs the calculation according to the established method, the degrees of altitude and zenith distance <term>natonnatāṃśa</term> will not be wasted. Through the **three-fold inquiry** <term>tripraśna</term> <note>A standard Sanskrit astronomical method for solving problems involving time, place, and direction</note>, the visibility is established. If the time elapsed for the Moon is six times greater than the required **time-degrees** <term>kālāṃśa</term>, then the Moon, which destroys the pride of the wind, emerges as the Lord of the Night <note>original: "Indra", used here poetically for the visible Moon</note>. If the time-degrees are within the limit, some may still argue it is visible. 90 Those who look at the Moon and mock the calculations of others are themselves foolish. The sighting of the Moon should occur after the moment of setting <note>heliacal setting</note>, near the first day of the lunar month <term>pratipad</term>. 50 The Moon's appearance and disappearance should follow the logic of calculation; otherwise, whose face would not be lowered in shame? Therefore, the sighting is determined near the appropriate time. 51 Whether the visibility occurs after the setting or before, it must be determined from the time of the **lunar day** <term>tithi</term> closest to the rising. This decision is made based on the sequence of visibility and invisibility. These are my own reasonings, set down in these tables. Only a man of pure mind can truly understand the position of the Moon. 52

### Thus ends the Chapter on Rising and Setting in the "Discernment of the Truth of Siddhāntas" composed by the illustrious Kamalākara. ||52||

Now, I shall properly explain the two eclipses <term>grahaṇa</term> and their possibility, so that the mathematical effort of great men is not spent in vain. 1 If the degrees of the **Moon with its node** <term>sapātacandra</term> <note>The Moon's position relative to the points where its orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> are within the limit of twelve degrees <note>original: "śve-ndu-viśatyakāḥ" using the Katapayadi or Bhutasankhya system for numbers</note>, a solar eclipse is possible at the end of the lunar month. Similarly, a lunar eclipse occurs at the time of the full moon. 2 After knowing the specific time, one should calculate the positions of the planets for the divine observers. Through correct calculation and logic, one must determine whether to add or subtract the values. 3 If the corrected positions of the Moon and its node are within the required degrees, an eclipse will definitely occur; this should be understood by those who observe. 4 In those months, one should observe the motion of the node and the Moon... <note>The text specifies numerical values for months and degrees: 10, 40, 5</note> to see if an eclipse is possible. 5 The solar eclipse is always related to the observer's location on Earth. A lunar eclipse occurs at the full moon, and a solar eclipse at the end of the lunar month. 6 At the time of the Sun’s alignment with the node, the correction for **parallax in longitude** <term>lambana</term> must be applied. This is what defines the "contact" of the Sun and the Moon. 7 Because the true visibility occurs at the end of the conjunction, one must observe the Sun during the day. Otherwise, the calculation will not match the reality of the situation. 8 That which was spoken by the great astronomers regarding the solar and lunar eclipses is here clarified through the application of parallax...

...nor do I see the reasoning in that [the method mentioned] in the celestial sphere. 8

**Thus ends the Chapter on the Possibility of Eclipses in the *Inquiry into the Truth of Astronomical Principles* (Siddhāntatattvaviveka) composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.**

Just as here, the Sun and Moon become distinct in their positions through degrees and other measures by using instruments, I shall now duly explain the method for determining the possibility of an eclipse. By knowing this, the effort of calculation will not be wasted. 1

If, at the moment of the Full Moon <term>pūrṇimānta</term>, the Moon’s distance from the node <term>bhuja</term> <note>The angular distance from the points where the Moon's path crosses the Sun's path</note> is within 13 degrees <note>original: "rudra", a numerical code for 11 or sometimes 13 in this context</note>, there is a possibility of an eclipse. The same applies to the Sun at the time of the New Moon <term>darśānta</term>. 2

At that time, the calculation is not merely for the stars; the observed positions must be precise. In calculating the two-day period, logic must be applied, just as one performs the calculation for the zenith distance <term>natāṃśa</term>. 3

When these values are refined and corrected, they become clear. If the Moon's distance from the node is within 7 degrees <note>original: "naga", a code for the number 7</note>, the possibility of a solar eclipse is highly certain and should be understood as such. 4

At the end of the month, the Moon’s position added to its node... <note>Technical parameters: 180° 45' 5"</note>. One should observe the possibility of an eclipse as the node is added or subtracted over the months. 5

The Earth's shadow <term>kubha</term> and the Moon are situated on the line of sight <term>dṛksūtra</term> from the perspective of an observer on Earth. Thus, an eclipse occurs at the end of the full moon, or for the Sun at the end of the new moon. 6

For the Sun, at the precise time of the conjunction, the position is corrected using parallax <term>lambana</term> <note>The apparent displacement of a celestial object when viewed from different positions</note>. Because the bodies are on the same line of sight and the rays are blocked, the eclipse is manifest at the moment of conjunction. 7

The Moon's eclipse is seen at night, and the Sun's by day; otherwise, it is determined by the shadow of the Earth. Regarding the corrections for these eclipses, the great teacher <note>original: "Śrījiṣṇu", referring to the 7th-century astronomer Brahmagupta</note> and others used parallax <term>lambana</term>, but I do not see the physical reasoning for that specific logic in the celestial sphere. 8

**Thus ends the Chapter on the Possibility of Eclipses in the *Inquiry into the Truth of Astronomical Principles* (Siddhāntatattvaviveka) composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.**

When the Sun and Moon are distinct in their degrees, that marks the end of the Full Moon. To determine a lunar eclipse, the first effort must be made. 1

The positions of the Sun, Moon, and their nodes at that moment must be found, as well as the clear radii <note>original: "śravaṇau"</note> of the Moon and the Earth's shadow. Likewise, the line of sight and the parallax in minutes <term>kalā</term> for the Moon are determined. 2

The Moon's latitude <term>viśikha</term> <note>The distance north or south of the ecliptic</note> in minutes, just as before, and the calculation of the Earth's shadow should be performed. Having understood these, one should calculate the lunar eclipse that occurs at night. 3

Only through contact with the Sun’s rays does the "Nectar-Ball" <note>original: "pīyūṣapiṇḍa", a poetic name for the Moon</note> shine in the direction of the Sun. It does not shine in any other direction. The "Cold-Rayed One" <note>the Moon</note> is like a clay pot; it is always obscured by its own shadow on the side facing away from the light. 4

The part of the Moon's globe that faces the Earth is visible to those on Earth; no other part of the orb can be seen. At the end of the full moon, the entire visible half is illuminated. 5

If the Moon's orb enters the Earth's shadow <term>bhūmibhā</term> produced by the Sun, then an eclipse is said to occur. 6

If the conditions for an eclipse are not met, then further investigation is deemed unnecessary. 5

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the geometry of eclipses from the previous page, specifically focusing on the Earth's shadow and the physical causes of lunar and solar eclipses as described in Kamalākara’s 17th-century treatise. It transitions from general possibility to specific physical dimensions and the nature of light.</meta>

77
