When the Moon’s disk is known at the horizon and other locations, its precise dimension <term>sūkṣmamāna</term> is determined. 7 When the Sun is at a precise distance from that point, then, because of the Earth’s shadow <term>kūbhā</term>, the observer situated on the Earth sees the Moon’s disk—which was seen previously—enter into darkness. 8

Now, it is asked: how can the Moon be seen clearly there without the light of the Sun’s rays? The brightness of the Moon does not belong to it naturally <note>original: "na site svataḥ syāt"; the Moon is not inherently self-luminous</note>; it occurs due to the contact of the Sun’s rays. When the Sun’s rays are blocked by the celestial sphere <note>meaning the Earth's bulk</note> and do not fall on the Moon, the experts say that the Moon has entered the Earth's shadow. The shadow and the Moon meet each other in the sky. 9

The Sun’s own spherical body is vast, while the Earth’s sphere is always small; therefore, a conical shadow <term>sūcyāgra</term> <note>literally "needle-pointed," describing a cone</note> of the Earth exists, extending beyond the Moon’s orbit <term>candrakakṣā</term> and staying there. This is established through the method of calculation by those learned in the spheres. 17 

Being inside the Earth’s shadow is what we call "night." Why then is it feared as darkness? Because of its appearance, it is called "darkness" <term>tamas</term>. It is for this reason that the Moon’s node <term>pāta</term> is called the "seizer" or "eclipse-causer" <term>graha</term> <note>Kamalākara is explaining that 'Rāhu' is a mathematical point, while the shadow is the physical cause</note>. In a solar eclipse as well, the Moon acts as a coverer; one does not see the Sun's disk because it is hidden by the Moon's body. 19

The coverer is appropriate when it creates a precise obstruction. The node <term>pāta</term> is not like that; how can it be a cause when it lacks a physical form? 18 <note>The scribe has placed verse 18 after 19</note>. There is no clear obstruction of the rays by spheres that lack a physical body. The intersection of the orbits produced by those spheres is devoid of length and breadth; therefore, without a physical obstruction, how could the Sun—the one with a thousand rays—be hidden from afar? How can the node, which lacks a form, be seen? 18 

Even at the end of an eclipse, why does it not "swallow" the Sun? Since the node is just a position, it lacks a body. If one were to imagine it as a physical entity, it would contradict the observed mathematical results. 20 

The authors of the *Śiromaṇi* <note>Refers to Bhāskara II’s famous 12th-century work, *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi*</note> provide a solution to this, but it is not entirely correct, because an eclipse occurs even when the "seizing" disk is outside the node <margin>25</margin>. Some say the hiding happens only within the shadow. In the solar eclipse, the coverer is the Moon's body. The ancients saw this darkness as having the same appearance as the shadow. In both types of eclipses, it is called darkness <term>timira</term> because it is limitless. Thus, this "seizer" <term>graha</term> is famously marked. Or rather, in the eclipses of the Sun and Moon, it is situated according to the mathematical union of their forms. 25

The Moon, moving eastward <term>prāṅmukha</term>, enters into the Earth's shadow; at the end of the Full Moon, it becomes clear. 26 In a lunar eclipse, the contact <term>sparśa</term> <note>the beginning of the eclipse</note> is not on the west; for the Moon, the contact is on the east. In a solar eclipse, the contact is on the western side, and the release <term>vimukti</term> <note>the end of the eclipse</note> is on the east. The Sun always stays in its place while the Moon moves eastward in the celestial circle <term>vimaṇḍala</term>. 27 

Now, I shall explain the knowledge of the Earth's shadow <term>kūbhājñāna</term> according to the logic of the spheres <term>golayukti</term>, which is filled with reason. 28 The distance between the Sun and Earth, the dimensions of their disks, and the base of the shadow cone are all determined by the famous calculations of the Sun's motion. 29 The shadow's length is derived from the Earth's diameter...

<summary>This page discusses the physical and geometric causes of eclipses, arguing that the Moon is illuminated by the Sun and darkened by the Earth's conical shadow. Kamalākara critiques purely mythological explanations of the nodes (Rāhu/Ketu), insisting on a model of physical obstruction and relative motion.</summary>
<keywords>Earth's shadow (Kūbhā), lunar disk (Bimba), conical shadow (Sūcyāgra), Moon's node (Pāta), solar eclipse, lunar eclipse, Siddhānta Śiromaṇi, Kamalākara, celestial geometry</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of the Earth's shadow (Kūbhā) dimensions during an eclipse. The author, Kamalākara, is critiquing or refining the methods used by previous astronomers, specifically mentioning the school of Ranganātha (likely referring to his rival Munīśvara, Ranganātha's son) and the *Siddhānta Sundara*.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

Divided by the Earth’s diameter, that which is produced is the increased shadow. 30 The shadow's tip is calculated from the diameter of the Earth; the square root of the difference between the squares of the diameters <note>original: "vimbavargāntarapadaṃ"; a standard geometric step in calculating the frustum of the shadow cone</note> is known as the base of the shadow. This, when multiplied and divided by the distance <term>karṇa</term> <note>the hypotenuse or distance between celestial bodies</note>, results in the measure of the Earth’s shadow. 

When the square of the shadow's length is subtracted from the square of the distance, and the square root is taken, we find the primary result. That shadow-base, multiplied by the diameter and divided by the distance, gives the result in the form of a sine <term>jyā</term>. This is clearly identified by the arm of the shadow. When divided by the distance, the result is the precise measurement in leagues <term>yojana</term> <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</note>. That value is then multiplied by the radius. 34 

In the lunar sphere, the Earth’s shadow <term>kūbhā</term> is associated with the Moon's motion. It takes the form of an arc and its number is determined by the shadow's progression. 35 Within the celestial sphere, through the eyes of the wise, one always observes the shadow at an interval of six signs <note>180 degrees</note> from the Sun; it moves across the circle of the sky following the motion of the Sun. 36 

According to the previous methods regarding the shadow's circle, a more precise calculation is now stated, measuring from the center of the Earth to the distance of the Moon. 38 The precise shadow <term>sūkṣma-bhā</term> is determined by the form of the distance <term>karṇa</term>. When the distance is multiplied by the Earth’s shadow and the result is clearly defined, the arm of the Earth’s shadow is known. 39

By understanding the cause of this, the calculation becomes clear. Because of the contact of the rays between the Sun and the Earth’s spheres, the Moon is positioned nearby while the Sun <note>original: "sahasra-bhānu"; literally "the thousand-rayed one"</note> is far away. In whatever manner the Moon moves in its sphere, the Earth's shadow must be applied accordingly. 40 Conversely, the shadow is larger or smaller depending on whether the bodies are far or near; this variation arises from the distance between the Sun and the Moon and the Earth’s shadow. 41 

Having seen that the shadow was not accurately calculated by the previous methods, the son of Ranganātha <note>Munīśvara, the author of the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma* and Kamalākara's intellectual rival</note> has stated a more precise method according to his own intellect; I shall now explain that clearly. 42 

The square of the Earth’s radius is divided by the Sun’s disk <term>arka-vimba</term>. In the second step, the result is multiplied by the Earth’s diameter. When the Sun’s distance <term>arka-karṇa</term> is reduced by that, and the Sun’s disk is multiplied by the Moon’s distance <term>indu-karṇa</term> and divided by the Sun’s distance, the resulting value is subtracted. This provides the precise measurement of the Earth’s shadow disk <term>kūbhā-vimba</term> in the path of the Moon. 44 

Because there is a slight discrepancy in the measurement of the shadow in the Moon’s path, the tip of the shadow must be calculated for the Moon's orbit in the manner described. 45 Here, how can the shadow-knowledge of the Ranganātha school be considered complete? It is a tradition passed down through small segments, much like the nodes of the Moon. 48 

Those experts who are skilled in observing the celestial spheres should consider: what is the use of the confusion created by previous authors who calculated the Sun’s path with little care? On the day of an eclipse <term>grahaṇa</term> of the Sun or Moon, the calculation of the Earth’s shadow should be performed according to the primary method, and from the difference, the precise measurement <term>anayanam</term> is found. 49 From this slightly smaller value...

<summary>This page details the geometric formulas for calculating the diameter and length of the Earth's shadow cone during an eclipse. It features a notable scholarly critique where Kamalākara disputes the methods of Munīśvara (the son of Ranganātha) and other previous astronomers.</summary>
<keywords>Earth shadow (Kūbhā), lunar eclipse, planetary diameter (Vimba), distance (Karṇa), Munīśvara, Ranganātha, Siddhānta, geometry of spheres</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>The text is a continuation of Kamalākara’s *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* (17th century), a seminal work that integrated traditional Indian astronomy with elements of Islamic (Perso-Arabic) geometric optics. This page specifically focuses on the geometric construction of the Earth's shadow cone, essential for predicting lunar eclipses.</meta>

<margin>
S. T. V.
78
78
</margin>

The Sun <term>Ravi</term> is seen in such a way, and similarly, the disks of the Moon and the Sun are perceived in their respective orders. Here, within the distance between the center of the solar orb <term>ravi-bimba-gola-kendra</term> <note>original: "ravi viṃva gola kendrāddha"</note> and the center of the Earth-sphere <term>bhū-gola-kendra</term>, the solar rays are obstructed by the Earth. Because those rays do not reach the space behind the Earth, there is an absence of light, creating a region of darkness. This phenomenon is known as "Night," and is technically called the Earth's Shadow <term>bhū-chāyā</term> <note>literally "Earth-shadow"</note>. 

This shadow extends from the solar disk to the Earth’s globe. Because the solar rays emerge from all sides of the Sun's surface and touch the surface of the Earth-sphere, they converge beyond it. This convergence is what determines the tip of the Earth's shadow. Since the Earth is covered by solar rays, a circular shape is produced on its surface; this is how the form of the shadow is established in this science. The "mouth" or base of this shadow is the circle formed where the rays graze the Earth’s surface. 

From the center of that base, the distance to the tip of the Earth’s shadow is calculated by the penetration of the rays. In the region where there is no contact with the rays, there is no light or brilliance, only shadow. This shadow-cone is what the Moon enters. The extension or width of this shadow is calculated specifically for the purpose of determining the details of a lunar eclipse <term>chandra-grahaṇa</term>. 

To understand this, one must construct a central line <note>original: "madhya-sūtra"</note> extending from the center of the solar disk, through the center of the Earth, to the tip of the Earth’s shadow. In the middle of this, where the surfaces of the solar orb and the Earth-sphere are clearly aligned, one marks the centers of their respective surfaces. From those centers, an arc is drawn on the surface of the sphere equal to one-fourth of the circumference <note>a 90-degree quadrant</note>; this forms the "half-surface circle." 

Regarding the shadow’s proof, we consider the rays emerging from the half-surface of the solar disk that do not come into contact with the Earth. Now, consider the geometry of this half-surface: from the center of the solar disk to the edge of the half-surface circle, a line is drawn equal to the radius <term>vyāsārddha</term> <note>literally "half the diameter"</note>; this acts as the "arm" <term>bhuja</term> of a triangle. Then, a "upright" line <term>koṭi</term> is drawn parallel to the central line. 

Wherever this line touches the surface is not <unclear>certain</unclear> in the current context, as there is no contact with the central line due to them being parallel. Now, where the upright line exists, it represents the ray-line which touches the half-surface of the Sun and the half-surface of the Earth. If this line is drawn as an arc to represent an obstruction, as some ancient scholars <note>original: "prācīnaiḥ"</note> have done, it does not hold up when considering the actual curvature of the spheres. Therefore, their method is discarded <note>Kamalākara often corrected his predecessors, like Bhāskara II, using more complex geometry</note>, as the rays cannot behave in that manner. 

Thus, it is established by the leading astronomers that the Earth's shadow is known through the rays that fail to reach the Earth from the half-surface of the Sun. From the region below the half-surface of the solar and terrestrial spheres, an upright ray-line is situated at an interval from the central line. This line is fit to touch the central line [at the shadow's tip], because on a sphere, a line drawn from the center to the surface can extend to any point.

<vocab>
**Ravi-bimba**: The solar disk or orb.
**Bhū-gola**: The terrestrial sphere or Earth.
**Bhū-chāyā**: The Earth's shadow.
**Kendra**: The center point of a circle or sphere.
**Kirana**: Rays of light.
**Grahaṇa**: An eclipse.
**Vyāsārddha**: Radius (half-diameter).
</vocab>

The author critiques earlier astronomical methods, arguing for a more precise geometric model involving the "half-surface" of the celestial spheres.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical derivation of the Earth's shadow cone from the previous page. Having established the physical cause of the shadow, the author now moves into the specific geometry of the "shadow-triangle" using the concepts of right-angled trigonometry common in Indian astronomy.</meta>

Having imagined the form of the side <term>bhuja</term> <note>the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</note> as being measured by half [the sum of the diameters], and then a straight line as the upright <term>koṭi</term> <note>the vertical side</note>, where the two meet is the location—whether this is clearly understood or not, it is very well known. Therefore, because of the distance from its own place, one should see a line, a "sight-line," according to the rules, which acts as a tangent line <term>sparśa-sūtra</term> touching the surfaces of the solar orb <term>ravi-bimba-gola</term> and the Earth-sphere <term>bhū-gola</term>. From the position of those three lines, where the surfaces of the solar orb and Earth-sphere are clearly touched, the obstructing lines of the Sun and Earth should be drawn in order. Then, where that line is clear, a mark is made on the solar orb. The circle drawn with that distance on the surface of the orb is the solar circle. Similarly, on the Earth-sphere, there is an "Earth-circle" <term>bhū-vṛtta</term>. Thus, from these circles, the lines that make up the solar rays are united to establish the form [of the shadow]. 

Now, behold the production of the triangles <term>trai-sandarśana</term> <note>original: "tra saṃdarśanaṃ"</note> here:
From the center of the solar orb to the mark on the Sun, the radius <term>vyāsārdha</term> is the side <term>bhuja</term>. In the middle line, the distance from the center of the solar orb to the very tip of the shadow is the great hypotenuse <term>vṛhat-karṇa</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares is the surface-line from the mark on the Sun to the tip, which is the great upright <term>vṛhat-koṭi</term>. This constitutes the "Great Figure" <term>vṛhat-kṣetra</term>. 

Now, other triangles of the same class <term>sajātīya</term> <note>similar triangles</note> are shown:
From the center of the Earth to the mark on the Earth, the Earth's radius is the side <term>bhuja</term>. In the middle line, the distance between the center of the Earth and the tip of the shadow is the hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares is the segment of the Earth's surface-line, which is the upright <term>koṭi</term>. Similarly, from the Earth's center to the mark on the Earth, the Earth's radius is the hypotenuse, and the distance from the middle line is called the "Earth-path" <term>bhū-saraṇi</term>, which is the upright <term>koṭi</term>. The distance between the middle line and this is the side <term>bhuja</term>. Thus, the "Earth-path" is the side. 

From the Earth-circle to the tip of the shadow, the segment of the shadow's surface-line is the hypotenuse. In the middle line, the distance between these two is the upright. Similarly, from the tip of the shadow to the lunar sphere, the segment of the shadow's surface-line is called the "final" <term>antya</term> hypotenuse. The semi-diameter of the shadow's width located at the lunar sphere is the side <term>bhuja</term>. In the middle line, the distance between them is the upright. 

Thus, for the purpose of achieving all calculations here, a standard "Reference Triangle" <term>pramāṇa-kṣetra</term> of the same class is mentioned:
The difference between the radii of the Sun and Earth is the side <term>bhuja</term>. In the middle line, the distance of the Sun <note>the solar distance or hypotenuse</note> is the hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares is the upright <term>koṭi</term>, known as the "intermediate distance" <term>mānantarāla</term> <note>literally "the interval between the two measures"</note> from the center of the Earth to the surface-line. 

Then, from the center of the Earth, another triangle of that same class is:
The Earth's radius is the side <term>bhuja</term>. The Moon's distance <term>chandra-karṇa</term> is the hypotenuse. The square root of the difference of their squares is the "middle" upright <term>madhyama-koṭi</term>, which is the segment of the shadow's surface-line from the Earth's surface to the lunar sphere. This is called the "special tip" <term>viśeṣāgra</term> of the shadow's surface-line. 

Now, for the calculation of the shadow <term>kubhā-nayana</term>, a proportion <term>anupāta</term> is used:
If, for the hypotenuse of the shadow's surface-line, the Earth-path is the side, then for the "final" hypotenuse, what will it be? Thus, the semi-width of the shadow at the lunar sphere is found. Similarly, by the proportion of the Reference Triangle: if for the hypotenuse of the Reference Triangle, the side is obtained, then for the hypotenuse of the [actual] field, what is the side? 

Thus, having established the value in linear units <term>yojana</term>, and multiplying by the radius of the degrees <term>trijyā</term> and dividing by the Moon's distance, the result is the arc. That arc, in minutes of a degree <term>kalā</term>, is the width of the shadow <term>kubhā-vistāra</term> at the lunar sphere. Thus, the value of the Earth...

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical derivation of the Earth's shadow cone, moving from the general concept of the shadow-triangle to specific methods used by earlier astronomers like Bhāskarācārya and contemporaries like Muniśvara (the son of Raṅganātha).</meta>

By the proportion imagined where the Earth’s radius <term>bhū-vyāsārdha</term> <note>original: "bhūvāsakhaṇḍa"</note> is taken as the side <term>bhuja</term> <note>the base of a right-angled triangle</note> in the calculation of the Earth's shadow <term>ku-bhā</term>, the distance extending outward from the center of the Earth <term>ku-garbha</term> to the end of the shadow-cone is determined. In this context, it is said that the calculation of the sides, uprights, and hypotenuses of those respective geometric figures is possible by the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika</term> <note>a mathematical proportion: if A results in B, what does C result in?</note>. Regarding this, the teacher Bhāskarācārya <note>Bhāskara II, a 12th-century authority on Indian mathematics</note>, in his treatment of the lunar sphere, performs the calculation of the width of the shadow-cone using a "central string" <note>an axial line</note> as the perceived shadow. 

That calculation follows their own specific crude measurements. In this manner, on the Earth-sphere <term>bhū-gola</term>, the path of the solar rays <term>ravi-kiraṇa</term> is connected. Along that path, the measurement is held to be exact. Now, the distance between the center of the Earth and the Earth’s shadow is the "hand-hypotenuse" <term>hasta-karṇa</term> <note>a specific measured hypotenuse</note>. From the center of the Earth, three "ropes" <note>lines of calculation</note> like radii proceed. In the lunar sphere, which is much larger than the point where the shadow narrows, the distance between the center of the lunar sphere and the center of the shadow is the "small hypotenuse." 

The side <term>bhuja</term> of the crude shadow is small, and the upright <term>koṭi</term> is the string-segment of the shadow's extent. In this way, in the large and small triangles, the side <term>bhuja</term> is the distance between the centers. The "perceived hypotenuse" is the shadow-string. The upright <term>koṭi</term> is a line parallel to the perceived shadow-string. This geometric figure is of the same class as the proportion-triangle. The difference between the radii of the Sun and the Earth is always the side <term>bhuja</term>. If the distance to the Sun is the hypotenuse, then what is the side for the Moon’s distance? By this logic, the crude radius of the shadow is calculated. The upright <term>koṭi</term> representing the distance between the centers of the shadow is the actual shadow-radius. The crude shadow-radius is the upright <term>koṭi</term>, while this [newly derived line] acts as the hypotenuse. The upright of the complete shadow is the hypotenuse of the complete shadow. By this similarity of figures and the Rule of Three, the calculation of the actual shadow from the proportion-triangle is easily achieved. Thus, leaving aside the traditional geometric arrangement which is inconsistent with the logic of the globe, those who calculate the shadow otherwise are incorrect. Their method fails because it lacks the necessary geometric "Earth-nature" <term>kṣiti-bhāva</term> required to prove the expansion of the shadow's diameter on the lunar sphere through the intersection of solar rays.

On this matter, the sons of Raṅganātha <note>Reference to Muniśvara, author of the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma*, who was Kamalākara's great rival</note>, who rely on the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma*, say: Having defined the solar orb's diameter as equal to the Earth's radius, one observes the solar orb from the Earth-sphere. If the distance between the rays is equal to the Earth’s diameter, then what would the distance be for a solar orb diameter of one's choosing? By this Rule of Three, the distance between the rays is calculated. Taking half of that, and using the square root of the difference of the squares of the Earth's radius, the distance of the rays from the Earth's center is found. This distance, representing the interval between the two, added to the true distance <term>karṇa</term> of the Sun and Moon respectively, gives the two hypotenuses suitable for calculating the Earth's shadow. They claim that the calculation of the shadow is then performed using the known distance of the rays from the center of the Earth. 

At the previously mentioned time, the distance from the Sun to the center of the Earth's shadow would be unknown without this logic. Because of the Moon's disk, the celestial latitude <term>śara</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note> is at the tip, whether to the south or north. On that very circle, the distance between the center of the shadow and the center of the Moon is established. The distance between the two centers, whether southern or northern, is always determined at the time of the middle of the eclipse. By taking half the sum of the diameters of the shadow and the Moon, and considering the latitude, the beginning <note>contact</note> and end <note>separation</note> of the eclipse are calculated, just as the latitude was described before. This is the difference in distance. Regarding the lunar disk... <unclear>illegible</unclear>... they speak of the calculation of the half-sum of the diameters. This half-sum is always the union of the two. This is not yet proven. The contact of the shadow-orb on the lunar sphere occurs when... the orb...

He uses the "Rule of Three" and properties of similar triangles to refine the measurement of the Earth's shadow-cone during a lunar eclipse.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the geometry of the Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse. 19
</margin>

When the Earth's shadow <term>ku-bhā</term> <note>literally "earth-light-absence"</note> is situated in its own sphere, the contact with the lunar disk occurs according to logical proportion. Within the sphere where the lunar and shadow orbs meet, the surface of the lunar orb properly touches the surface of the shadow cone. 6

The size of the shadow in the Moon’s <term>śaśāṅka</term> <note>a poetic name for the Moon, meaning "the one with the hare-mark"</note> own sphere is always smaller than the base. Now, when the shadow is positioned as described, the contact of the two spherical orbs should be understood through the previous method. Therefore, the thickness of the shadow within the lunar sphere should be contemplated by one's own intelligence. 7

Furthermore, when the Earth’s shadow touches the lunar disk, the distance between the centers of the two spheres is exactly what exists in the Moon's own sphere. The contact of these two occurs at the end of the calculated distance. 8

Therefore, in its own sphere, for the purpose of demonstrating the conjunction of the two, the radius <term>vistāra-khaṇḍa</term> <note>literally "extension-part"</note> of the disk-sphere is combined with the shadow-disk; this sum of the radii <term>yuti</term> is established as the limit. 9

Having calculated the squares <term>varga</term> of the hypotenuse and the other sides, and having performed the division where possible, the depth of the shadow is determined. This value is divided by the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> <note>the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</note>. This is the method stated by the Sage <note>likely referring to Muniśvara, Kamalākara’s scholarly rival</note>. 10

When the shadow’s perpendicular is multiplied and then divided by the hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term>, and the Moon's sine <term>jyā</term> is likewise divided by its hypotenuse, the result is the distance between the centers of the two spheres at the moment of contact. 11

Now, by squaring the various parts of the Earth’s shadow and the Moon, and adding the squares of the distances, the Moon's hypotenuse is found. Through these two, the distance at that time is determined. 12

This distance between the shadow and the Moon at the moment of contact is established via the "surface-string" <term>pṛṣṭha-sūtra</term> method <note>a calculation based on the surface of the spheres rather than their centers</note>. This is how the difference in the shadow is understood on the surface of the spheres. 13

Because the "string" arising from the shadow’s surface is attached to the edge of the Moon's disk, the wise should observe the resulting connection. Though this requires great effort in calculation, the clear results are worth the labor. 14

Knowing the "string" that connects the sum of the half-diameters <term>mānekyārdha</term> <note>the sum of the radii of the Moon and the shadow</note>, the ancients performed all their calculations. What was written by the earlier masters was for the sake of practical application <term>vyavahāra</term> <note>simplified methods for everyday use</note>. 15

That which has been established by the "truth-knowers" <term>tattva-jña</term> <note>referring to those who seek exact mathematical reality</note> among the moderns, I have described here without end. Here is the demonstration <term>upapatti</term>: The "string" or line from the center of the Earth to the end of the shadow cone is attached to the lunar sphere. There, at the point of contact between the shadow and the Moon, the centers of the two orbs are separated within their spheres. The distance between those centers is held by the radius <term>tri-jyā</term>. In that place, a circle is formed by the duration of the eclipse... <note>The text transitions into a prose explanation of the geometry</note>.

The method for that calculation is as follows: When the Moon’s sphere is smaller than the Earth's shadow at the moment of contact, it is situated on the surface of the shadow. The point of contact forms a perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> above the surface of the shadow. By the distance of the Earth's radius, the center of the Moon’s disk is located in the lunar sphere. In this way, the "string" of the shadow’s surface is attached to the surface-sheet. By the distance of the Earth’s diameter, acting as the base <term>bhuja</term>, the Moon's center is stated. The Earth's center then acts as the perpendicular. On the surface of the Earth-sphere and the lunar-disk sphere, the clear section of the shadow-surface is found. With the half-diameter of the Earth acting as the base and the Moon's hypotenuse as the perpendicular, the calculation proceeds...

He focuses on the precise mathematical contact between the spheres of the Earth's shadow and the Moon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical proof regarding the geometry of the Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse. Following the introduction of the sum of radii on the previous page, the author now provides a detailed geometric derivation for the distance between the centers of the lunar disk and the shadow cone, specifically critiquing how these values are projected onto the lunar sphere.</meta>

<margin>
T. Tva.

80

In the second field of shadow calculation <note>original: "bhānayana-kṣetra"</note>, the central thread lies at the root of the perpendicular. The sum of that form's hypotenuse and the base is established, and by assuming eleven minutes <note>arc-minutes</note> for the Earth, the resulting perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> <note>the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</note> is the same as that situated in the lunar sphere.

The distance between the centers of the shadow and the disk-sphere is stated. In the calculation of the shadow, this second field of calculation is presented as a pair; thus, calculating it in this form is easy and well-reasoned. Everything is explained through another method: in the same way, for the Earth's shadow, the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> that reaches the Moon is the surface-string of the shadow within that lunar sphere. Here, by subtracting the Earth’s logic <note>referring to a specific mathematical principle or "nīti"</note> from the surface-string of the shadow,

that resulting form is projected as the perpendicular. The side <term>bhuja</term> <note>the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</note> is imagined within the lunar mass, and a triad <note>a right-angled triangle</note> should be formed there. In that context, the distance between the surface-string of the shadow and the center of the lunar disk-sphere is the shadow’s center-line <note>original: "madhya-sūtra"</note> within the lunar sphere. The sum of these two is the distance of the shadow within the lunar sphere; this is well-proven. In the second method described, if the shadow's surface-string here is hidden by the tangent string, that distance is called the shadow-distance. By the contact of the shadow’s surface with the lunar disk, the result is obtained. All of this is logically consistent.

Now, at the time of contact <note>the beginning of the eclipse</note> and liberation <note>the end of the eclipse</note>, the distance between the centers of the shadow and the lunar disk should be known by the wise within the lunar sphere. This is termed the "hypotenuse of duration" <term>sthiti-karṇi</term>. A circle of this duration-hypotenuse is formed. The square of the duration-hypotenuse is multiplied by three and the square of the sine <term>jyā</term> of the difference is divided by the radius; the square root of that result is the perpendicular side <term>koṭi</term>. Its arc is the line of minutes. It is written as follows: just as the sine of the latitude <term>śara-jyā</term> is multiplied by the radius and divided, that result is the sine of the duration-hypotenuse. Its arc is another name for the result. By dividing the perpendicular sine <term>koṭi-jyā</term> of that, or by multiplying it, the sine of the other perpendicular is well-known. Its arc-minutes are the half-duration <term>sthiti-khaṇḍa</term>.

Alternatively, the calculation is as follows: the perpendicular sine of the duration-hypotenuse is multiplied by the perpendicular sine of the latitude and divided by the radius; the arc of the result is the duration in minutes. These are multiplied by sixty and divided by the difference in the speeds of the Moon and Sun to find the minutes of duration. This is the half-duration <term>sthiti-khaṇḍa</term>. Here is the proof <note>original: "upapatti"</note>: On the lunar sphere, a radius-circle is touched by the centers of the shadow and the lunar disk. This is the circle of the duration-hypotenuse. There, imagine the ecliptic <term>krānti-vṛta</term>, the lunar orbit, the celestial equator, and the circle passing through the center of the lunar disk and the poles. Because of the movement of the poles, the latitude <term>śara</term> is the declination. The hypotenuse of duration is divided into field-minutes. In that context, the minutes of the arc-circle are the minutes of the half-duration. At the moment of contact and release, the two duration-hypotenuses are the hypotenuses <term>śruti</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares is the perpendicular. This is what was done by the learned to find the duration. It is established for practical use by those who know the movements. However, this was done by those who thought they were using arc-based right-angled triangles, but it does not somehow follow the linear right-angled triangle rule; therefore, there is no success in that method. According to that method, those arc-based triangles are used in the formula, but they are not for linear calculations...

<keywords>lunar eclipse, Earth's shadow (bhūbhā), sthiti-karṇi, sthiti-khaṇḍa, trigonometry, sine (jyā), perpendicular (koṭi), latitude (śara), ecliptic (krānti-vṛta)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric and astronomical discussion from page 90, transitioning from the abstract properties of triangles and sines (trigonometry) to the practical calculation of eclipse durations and the spiritual significance of observing these phenomena.</meta>

From the nature of the two triangles, the properties of the arcs and their sines are explained. As stated from the primary source, in a universal context, the sine <term>jīvā</term> <note>original: "jīvā"; the chord or sine of an arc</note> produced from the arc is present everywhere. The arc is the basis, and the half-sine is the horizontal side <term>bhuja</term> <note>the opposite side of a right-angled triangle</note>, while the perpendicular is the <term>koṭi</term> <note>the adjacent side</note>. Thus, a triangle is formed. In such a right-angled triangle, the radius <term>trijyā</term> <note>the standard radius of the calculation circle</note> is involved, where the arc may be slightly more or less.

<margin>1.</margin> <insert>Now, by multiplying and taking the square root of the difference, the full sine-string is determined. One should not imagine the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> to be unrelated to the hypotenuse and the base.</insert> 

From those values produced by the arc, the difference between the squares is taken; the square root of this is the horizontal side. The square root of the difference between that and the square of the other side gives the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> as the third part. This is always the case for an arc and its two sines <note>referring to sine and cosine</note>. When the arc is small, the sine is small, and when it is divided by the radius of the circle, the result is established. This is not just a matter of words; it is proven through the geometry of the radius-circle and the calculation of the arc. 

How then is the calculation of the Sun’s position <note>original: "sūrya-guṇa"</note> produced? This must be considered in its entirety as stated by the ancient sages. By the rotation of the Earth, the position of the horizontal side <term>bhuja</term> is established by the radius. By applying the calculation for the reduction of the shadow <note>original: "apavartana"</note>, the duration of the eclipse is determined. This duration <term>sthiti</term> is not merely derived from the minutes of arc <term>kalā</term>, but also from the difference in the Earth’s shadow. Because of this, the times for the first contact <term>sparśa</term> <note>the beginning of the eclipse</note> and the final release <term>mokṣa</term> <note>the end of the eclipse</note> are calculated. 

By knowing the two moments of the full moon accurately, the wise can determine the precise duration of the eclipse. Indeed, a lunar eclipse occurs at the time of the full moon. At such a time, when a person knowledgeable in the great sciences bathes with devotion, the person living on Earth is freed from all sins. This is the power of the light <note>original: "jyotiḥ prabhāvataḥ"</note> of the heavenly bodies. 

A human always sees the disk <note>original: "bimbam"</note> of the sun or moon as very small due to the great distance. The separation between them is five and a half minutes of arc <term>liptā</term> <note>1/60th of a degree</note>. This has been explained by me regarding the rising of the Moon and the Sun. Through the intelligence of the senses, the minutes of arc at the edge of the moon are understood. At the moment of rising, the calculated digits are added to find the time of the phenomenon. The distance between the two disks must be observed. When half the disk is eclipsed <term>grāsa</term>, it is seen for the sake of the resulting calculation. The duration of that half-contact, when added or subtracted from the time, gives the actual times of the first contact and the final release. 

Newer scholars say that because the celestial bodies are spherical in shape <term>golākṛtā</term> <note>original: "golākṛtā"; ball-shaped or spherical</note>, the bright and dark portions are seen according to the alignment of the disks. Due to the smallness of the circles, the eclipsed portion appears as a smaller or larger curve on the rim.

त• त• वि• ८१ <note>Abbreviation for Siddhāntatattvaviveka, page 81</note>

Regarding the flow of blood-red light and the falling of the sine <term>jyā</term> <note>the trigonometric sine or chord</note>, some say there is an interval here; that is incorrect. Why? Because of its infinity, and how could it be otherwise? For as they say, as the disk <term>vimba</term> <note>the celestial orb</note> proceeds from the rising mountain, so too its edge is seen. In their view, there should be a proportion of parts there, as is appropriate for the celestial sphere <term>gola</term>. Otherwise, how could the third part of the lunar sphere be constructed? It is accepted for the sake of establishing the difference in the rising of the disk. This is seen only to this extent by astronomers <term>jyotirvid</term>. The resulting visibility that emerges is quite extraordinary. 

Now, concerning the eclipse of the Moon, the "circular form" described by the established Siddhāntas is taken as the authority. It was accepted by the ancient teachers through imagination. Due to the distance between the Sun's rays and the half-number of the Earth's shadow <term>kubha</term>, a certain water-like reflection <note>original: "ambu-vimba"; refers to the reflective or optical properties of the celestial orbs</note> appears here. By the separation of those Sun-ending rays of light, the entire eclipse becomes reddish-brown <note>original: "piśaṅga-varṇā"</note> in color. Others say that due to the lack of difference from the surface of the Earth's shadow, this was not mentioned by the masters of mathematics because of its complexity. 

Yet, is there not something wondrous said here? Even when the Earth's shadow <term>bhūchāyā</term> causes an eclipse, people still observe other light-disks. It is a sequence established that the Sun and others always appear to go to the low point <note>the nadir or beneath the horizon</note> during the night. In the case of a solar eclipse, the method of grasping is the reverse. These things are known. If it were not so, the planets hidden by the different parts of the half-day would be like a mirror-image of the water-disk; they would not be seen by the light of the Sun. Without a connection to light, how could they be seen? Otherwise, according to the logicians <term>tārkika</term>, because of the lack of connection with the rays, there would be no actual knowledge. We see the obstruction of the eyes. Just as the Moon was previously covered by the Earth's shadow, which is not a solid substance, so it appears through these differences. The eyes of those delighted by the brilliant Sun's rays somehow see the Sun and Moon in the water, even when they are eclipsed, at a distance between the visible parts.

->**Thus ends the 18th chapter on Lunar Eclipses in the Siddhāntatattvaviveka composed by Śrī Kamalākara.**<-

Now, from the one situated part, the Sun has yellow rays. It is shown that from the Earth, there is an interval. The Sun's disk does not fall on the Earth's surface <note>as a solid object would</note>. It is seen by people as an obscured Sun-disk. Furthermore, the shadow of the Earth that falls during a disk-eclipse is seen as dark. Because of the sight situated at the opposite end, the wise say there are many types of eclipses of the Earth. Just as the cool-rayed Moon has an covering, so it is here. Thus, at the time of a small lunar eclipse, the connection with the Earth's divisions and the light of the solar eclipse is an obstruction. This should be considered by the wise. 

Now, here, the clear Sun-disk is equal to the first part at whatever time it exists. Here, there is a term called "the end" <term>anta</term>. In the <term>Yavana</term> <note>referring to Greco-Arabic or Western astronomy</note> system, there is first a clarity for the sake of the solar eclipse. That which is described as arising from the Sun's rays is called "that" <note>likely referring to the tangent or a specific shadow function</note>, because from the knowledge of that, this is shown. It is a constant companion. This is distinguished by the intelligent here. From those very clear solar rays, reddish-brown in color, the rising sign <term>lagna</term> is diminished by three signs <note>a standard calculation for the nonagesimal or the highest point of the ecliptic</note>. Following the "Three-Question" <term>tripraśna</term> <note>the chapter of astronomy dealing with time, place, and direction</note> method, the position is clear...

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse of the Siddhāntatattvaviveka by Kamalākara. Following the discussion of lunar eclipses and optical reflections on page 81, the author now turns his attention to solar eclipses, specifically focusing on the complex geometry of parallax (lambana). Kamalākara is known for his critical stance, here evaluating the methods of the great 12th-century astronomer Bhāskara II.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>

The motion known as *dattayattagati* is calculated by the distance of the sun and moon. The degrees of zenith distance <term>natonatāṃśa</term> <note>The angular distance of a celestial body from the zenith, used to calculate how much the observer's position on the Earth's surface shifts the body's apparent position.</note> are determined according to the rule. From this, the measure of the sight-line is observed on the Earth. 

So too, after first understanding all the knowledge regarding the rays of the Sun and the Moon's distance, one must calculate the divisor for parallax <term>lambana</term> <note>Parallax; specifically the difference in longitude between the Sun and Moon as seen from the Earth's surface versus the center.</note> during a solar eclipse. This calculation should be performed using the ray that originates from the center of the Earth <term>kugarbha</term> <note>The core or geometric center of the Earth, used as the standard reference point for astronomical calculations.</note> in relation to the Sun.

Regarding this, there are three distinct positions on the Earth's surface <term>kupṛṣṭha</term>. The difference between these three is called parallax. This was famously described by the most excellent of teachers, the venerable **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II (c. 1114–1185), the author of the Siddhānta Shiromaṇi and one of India's most celebrated mathematicians.</note>, in his work the **Shiromaṇi** <note>The Siddhānta Shiromaṇi.</note>. Salutations to him! 

However, his methods for determining parallax were simplified for the sake of brevity. Because they were intended to be concise, those methods of imagining parallax are not entirely capable of expressing the true, literal grasping of the eclipse. They are sufficient for the common practical use of the world <note>original: "loka-vyavahāra-siddhai"; meaning "good enough for general time-keeping or ritual purposes."</note>, but they are not suitable for those seeking a truly accurate experience of the phenomenon.

Therefore, I shall now explain the stable and fixed method established by the ancients to remove the confusion regarding the proof of parallax. I speak this for the sake of worldly practice, based on the difference in motion. The parallax in minutes <term>liptikā</term> <note>One-sixtieth of a degree; a minute of arc.</note> is determined by the difference in the speeds of the two luminaries. 

Since the measurement is taken from the Earth's surface, the minutes of parallax arise from the sine of the zenith distance <term>dṛg-jyā</term>. In that calculation, the sine of the altitude <term>nati</term> <note>Parallax in latitude.</note> is also slightly involved. The Sun's rays form the base <term>bhuja</term> and the Earth's radius forms the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term>. The hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term> is the sum of their squares. From the surface of the Earth, the geometry is thus established. 

<note>Kamalākara is describing a right-angled triangle where the observer's position on the surface creates a displacement from the calculation made at the Earth's center.</note>

Through the rule of three, the relationship between the base and the perpendicular is known. This allows us to calculate the minutes of arc for the "surface-view" chord as opposed to the "center-view" chord. This difference is what the wise call the true parallax. This is entirely consistent with what is observed. 

Because the Sun is seen from the surface of the Earth, the parallax of sight occurs. It is well-said that this is due to the difference in viewpoint. Some say that there is no difference in the observed displacement, but they are mistaken. The position of the Sun as seen from the Earth's center is one thing, and the position seen from the surface is another; the interval or subtraction between these two lines is parallax.

As the planet moves toward the horizon, this difference reaches its maximum. How can this be calculated accurately? I have described the resulting mathematics of the sphere <term>gola</term> <note>Spherical astronomy.</note> and the calculations <term>gaṇita</term> for the sake of the experts. Without this understanding of the sphere, the instruments of calculation remain incomplete.

<summary>Kamalākara discusses the geometry of solar parallax (lambana), arguing that while Bhāskara II's traditional methods are useful for general practice, a more rigorous geometric approach involving the Earth's center and surface is required for true accuracy.</summary>

While the previous section introduced the concept of parallax (lambana) and acknowledged the authority of Bhāskara II, Kamalākara here begins a more rigorous geometric demonstration of why standard calculations for zenith distance and sight-lines must be refined for precision.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

<margin>
N. Vi. <note>Abbreviation for the title *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*.</note>
82
</margin>

The sight-line is that very thread which connects the observer's eye on the horizon to the Sun. The distance between this visual line and the line originating from the Earth’s center <term>garbhasūtra</term> <note>Literally "womb-line"; the geometric radius from the center of the Earth to the celestial body.</note> in the lunar sphere is the cause of parallax. From that, the difference between the zenith distance of the Sun as calculated from the Earth’s center and the horizon is quite small. It was calculated by the ancients <note>original: "pūrvaiḥ"; referring to earlier astronomers like Bhāskara II.</note> based on those preceding factors. At the desired time, that calculated zenith distance is very small; therefore, in the visible disk of the planet, the calculation according to the Earth's surface makes it even smaller. When the visual observation is made, the result of that visual sight-line is established by experience. 

How can this be correctly understood by the wise? One should consider the maximum parallax at the horizon. From that, the result at the specific time of the zenith distance is obtained. In that context, the parallax is to be calculated through the visual sight-line and the center sight-line of the Sun. One should create... <unclear>illegible</unclear> ...the center-born and visual-born sine of zenith distance <note>original: "dṛṣṭatekaṇakoṭī"</note>. These form the base and the perpendicular of a triangle. The hypotenuse is the result of their union. Just as the degrees of the circle are divided by the Earth's radius, the union of the base and perpendicular occurs at the center. In a rectified calculation, what is visual is seen, and what is center-born is certainly distinct. 

Thus, it is clearly evident that the visual and center sight-lines are separated. The sine of the difference between them is the parallax. Here, the geometric method for the rule of parallax is not explained as simply a matter of the Earth's surface and the observer's distance. I have also considered the addition of the base and perpendicular here. By the difference between the visual hypotenuse and the center hypotenuse, the parallax is produced. That great "Earth-egg" <note>The globe of the Earth.</note> is divided by the sine of the angle and converted into minutes of arc <term>kalā</term>, or calculated through other methods. This parallax is established from the center-line. Therefore, it is not always said to be calculated from the horizon alone. Because of the power of the sight-line, the tiny planet is known by the people. Even so, it is said that on the horizon, in a specific place, the visual circle is diminished. From that, the logic is established. In this regard, the ancients did not make it clear that the result lies below the Earth’s radius <term>kujyā</term>.

### Or, the true nature and calculation of the parallax in latitude is explained.

One should measure a single line from the Earth's center <term>kugarbha</term> to the point of the planet situated in its celestial orbit <term>khakakṣā</term>. The arcual degrees of that planet's position are determined from that center-line. However, an observer situated on the Earth’s surface <term>bhūpṛṣṭha</term> does not see the planet at that same point of the celestial sphere. They see it via the visual sight-line, not elsewhere. Because of the distance between these two perspectives, the planet is known to be shifted. One should draw another line to the celestial sphere to the point where the planet is situated. At the part of the celestial sphere where that line touches, the observer sees the planet. By investigating the difference between its true position in the zodiac and this observed position, the parallax <term>lambana</term> is understood.

<summary>Kamalākara provides a geometric proof of parallax by comparing the sight-line from the Earth's center with the sight-line from the observer's position on the surface, arguing for a more precise calculation than that of his predecessors.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, sight-line, Earth's center, zenith distance, celestial sphere, Bhāskara II, Siddhāntatattvaviveka, spherical geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s technical exposition on parallax (lambana) from the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*. Having previously critiqued the simpler models of his predecessors, he now delves into the specific geometric relationship between the Earth's center (garbha) and the observer's position on the surface (dṛṣṭi) to explain the divergence in a planet's apparent position.</meta>

Here, the calculation of the visual sight-line and its motion is established through the knowledge of the interval between the <term>garbha-sūtra</term><note>The geocentric line, or the line connecting the center of the Earth to the center of a celestial body.</note> and the visual line situated on the sphere. When a degree of the zodiac is viewed from the center of the Earth, that is one line. Then, a second line is imagined from the eye of the observer on the surface of the globe. The gap between these two on the <term>dṛg-maṇḍala</term><note>The vertical circle or the circle of altitude passing through the zenith and the planet.</note> is what is known as <term>lambana</term><note>Parallax; the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different points.</note>. 

For a celestial body situated at the first point of its orbit, the first geocentric contact occurs. The parallax at that time is caused by the northern or southern deviation. The planets are situated in front of their own degrees of the ecliptic. Therefore, because of that bodily displacement, they are joined with the initial parallax. Those are the zenith distance degrees produced for that specific time. This is not the case for the second [geocentric contact]. To achieve the calculation of their union, some here say:

### On the Increase and Decrease of Parallax

The planet’s rising occurs when it is at the horizon; the sight-line is then furthest from the geocentric line. As the planet moves higher, the distance decreases, and the nature of the parallax changes accordingly. As a planet moves closer to the hand <note>original: "hasta"; likely a metaphor for the observer's reach or a specific measurement tool</note>, the parallax becomes small, but as it is situated nearer to the Earth’s center <note>meaning: closer to the Earth in terms of orbital distance</note>, the parallax indeed becomes greater. Such is the case for the Moon. Regarding the Sun and the Moon, the difference between their two parallaxes is what scholars call the "parallax in longitude" <note>original: "lambana-antara"</note> during a conjunction, similar to how it is calculated for two stars. 

In the conjunction of the geocentric lines of the Sun and Moon, the first [parallax] is not the same as the second. Those planets which are situated high above the Moon, and those which are positioned below, have an interval in their geocentric lines. Because of that interval in the lunar sphere, the first and other designations arise. The parallax in altitude is described according to the divisions of the vertical circle. Furthermore, from the <term>tribhonalagna</term><note>The nonagesimal; the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the ascendant, which is the highest point of the ecliptic.</note>, that very same value is known as <term>nati</term><note>Parallax in latitude.</note>. 

### Geometric Calculation

When the Sun and Moon are in the same geocentric line in the same portion of the sky, but are viewed through the visual sight-line, the Sun appears at a different elevation from the Moon. The Moon is delayed <note>original: "vilambita"</note> because of its lower orbit. Because there exists a distance between the visual line and the geocentric line, the hypotenuse is the measure of that delay. 

Thus, when the distance is greater, the parallax is greater. The ancient independent teachers have described parallax in a single form, but here the purpose is refined. Now, I shall describe the parallax at the time of rising and setting. The square of the Earth’s semi-diameter <note>original: "bhū-vyāsa-dala-varga"</note> is subtracted from the square of the distance of the Sun or Moon. The square root of that difference is multiplied by the Earth’s semi-diameter and divided by the Sun’s distance <term>karṇa</term>. This result, when handled with the <term>trijyā</term><note>The total sine, usually 3438 in Indian trigonometry.</note> and the Moon's distance, becomes the parallax. 

Thus, for those situated on the Earth's surface during the months of the Sun’s transit, the parallax in altitude is declared. The <term>trijyā</term> multiplied by the Earth's radius and divided by the distance in <term>yojanas</term><note>A traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-15 kilometers.</note> of the Sun and Moon, provides the measure of their interval. This is how parallax is established in the solar months. In this state, the planet's position as seen from the Earth’s center and the observer’s eye is explained. This very fact was articulated by the <term>Muniśvaras</term><note>Refers to Muniśvara, a rival astronomer of Kamalākara. Kamalākara is likely critiquing or refining Muniśvara's "vāsanas" or rationales.</note> and I have accepted the rationale they created. Because of the difference equal to the Earth's semi-diameter between the geocentric and visual sight-lines, a gap occurs in the celestial sphere. Therefore, for all celestial bodies, the gap in the celestial sphere becomes the tip of the minute-hand of the Earth's shadow...

<margin>Tattva-Viveka 83</margin>

->**83**<-

### The Geometry of the Sight-Line

The experts explain the motion of the wandering stars based on the intersection of the visual and geocentric horizons. There is a line for the <term>garbha</term><note>The center of the Earth.</note> and a line for the truth of the observer’s position. One must understand that the difference between these two lines—measured as an arc on the <term>bhagola</term><note>The celestial sphere or the sphere of the fixed stars.</note> and the <term>dṛggola</term><note>The observational or vertical sphere.</note>—constitutes the parallax. This is caused by the difference between the Earth's radius and the planet's distance. Because of this, the <term>dṛk-sūtra</term><note>The visual line of sight from the observer on the surface.</note> is considered to be "fallen" or displaced from the geocentric center. 

Therefore, through great expansion and with a focused mind, the learned should understand the calculation of the <term>lambana</term><note>Parallax; the apparent shift of a celestial body against the background.</note> at any desired time. This involves the difference in the heights of the observers and the specific spherical conditions of their respective locations. 

### Munishvara’s Defense of the Sārvabhauma Method

**By the son of Ranganatha** <note>original: "Ranganatha-tanayena"; referring to the author, Munishvara.</note>, it is maintained that there is often a contradiction in the treatment of the spheres in other texts. This famous system, which moves from "with-parallax" to "no-parallax," has been established by me. The solar parallax should be subtracted from the lunar parallax to find the true difference. Because the conjunction of the two occurs at a specific proximity of time, the method of other scholars regarding the logic of parallax is often incomplete. 

In my own work, I have shown that the visual line is always used for the Sun and Moon. This is not a mere illusion, but a calculated reality based on the <term>Sārvabhauma</term><note>Referring to Munishvara’s major work, the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma*.</note> method. When there is a difference between the parallax of the Sun and the Moon, that value is not simply a product of the lunar sphere; it is the interval between the geocentric line of the Sun and the visual line at that moment. 

Whether this is perceived as right or wrong depends on understanding the difference in the types of parallax. Those scholars who are not intent on the essence of the spheres may find themselves ridiculed by the wise. Thus, the calculation of parallax has been described by me to demonstrate its constant and varied nature. 

<column-break/>

### On the Earth's Surface and the Horizon

Without these logical proofs, one cannot account for the rising and setting of the stars. The parallax is greatest at the horizon and decreases as the body rises toward the <term>kha-svastika</term><note>The zenith; the point directly overhead.</note>. On the <term>ku-pṛṣṭha</term><note>The surface of the Earth.</note>, the sight-line is different from the line at the Earth's center. This difference is perceived by the eye as an angular displacement. 

By imagining the <term>bhū-mā-sāntara</term><note>The distance between the Earth (center) and the Moon (or Sun).</note>, we can calculate the horizontal parallax. The sharp-rayed Sun, when situated on the visual circle, always experiences a decrease in parallax as it rises from the horizon. One must calculate the <term>ravi-karṇa</term><note>The solar hypotenuse; the distance from the Earth's center to the Sun.</note> and use the sine of the altitude to find the exact displacement. 

If one looks from the Earth's surface rather than the center, the time of sunrise will appear different. This variation in the appearance of the solar disk is due to the observer's position on the globe.

<margin>19.</margin> <margin>Pu. 63</margin>

### The Horizon and the Intersection of Rays

In that specific region, the <term>himagarbha</term><note>Literally "snow-womb/interior"; here referring to a specific atmospheric or spherical boundary discussed in the Sārvabhauma system.</note> is designated as the <term>titija</term><note>original: "titija" (horizon-born); a synonym for the kṣitija or horizon.</note>. Where the seven rays <note>Likely referring to the solar rays or planetary influence.</note> are found, there is the investigation of the rise and set. At the moment of rising or setting, the <term>bhūjā</term><note>The terrestrial sine or the distance from the center.</note> is not illuminated by the same light. One should understand that what is considered "gross" or approximate calculation <note>original: "sthūlamāyediti"</note> is meant for general comprehension, whereas the true state of rise and set is calculated through the detailed expansion of the <term>titija</term> at the desired time.

### The Geometry of the Lunar Triangle

In the triangle formed by the <term>karṇa</term><note>The distance/hypotenuse of the planet from the Earth's center.</note> of the Moon, where the Earth serves as the base, the visual ray of the Moon (<term>shashi-dṛk-sūtra</term>) is the perpendicular (<term>lamba</term>). 

In this mathematical segment of the horizon:
1. First, take the square of the perpendicular and the square of the Moon’s distance.
2. The square root of their difference <note>Using the Pythagorean theorem.</note> becomes the first segment (<term>khaṇḍa</term>).
3. The difference between these established segments is then multiplied by the distance of the Sun and divided.
4. This result, when adjusted by the <term>trijyā</term><note>The radius of the standard circle, usually 3438 minutes.</note>, gives the <term>lambana</term><note>Parallax; the apparent shift in position.</note>.

### Comparison of Solar and Lunar Parallax

This calculation is first performed according to the initial <term>ayana</term><note>Precession or solar path.</note> at the desired time. In the triangle of the Sun's <term>karṇa</term> (distance), where the Earth is the base, the visual ray of the Sun (<term>ravi-dṛk-sūtra</term>) acts as the perpendicular. 

The second segment is derived similarly:
The square root of the difference between the squares of the perpendicular and the Moon’s distance becomes the second segment. The difference between these two segments, when multiplied by the Sun's distance and divided, yields the parallax. This difference in visual intersection is what distinguishes the two paths.

### Gross Practice vs. Subtle Reality

It is said that this difference between the geocentric and the topocentric rays is calculated through the <term>arka-gola</term> (Solar sphere) for the first, and the <term>candra-gola</term> (Lunar sphere) for the second. At the end of a conjunction (<term>grūmānte</term>), the distance between the visual ray and the geocentric ray is what defines the diverse parallax. 

To determine the true essence of this position, all factors must be calculated. This difference is impossible to grasp for those without <term>sūkṣma-dhiyā</term><note>A subtle or refined intellect.</note>. The learned say that there is no fault in the methods of the ancients if one understands they were intended for:
- **Worldly Practice:** To facilitate ease of calculation for the common astronomer.
- **Approximate Results:** Where "gross" (<term>sthūla</term>) methods are sufficient for general ritual timing.

However, for the true visual parallax required for eclipses, the relationship between the Moon and Sun must be calculated using these new, precise methods. Without this, even a great planet might not be seen where the calculations say it should be. The visual ray of the planetary house must be solved to find the first and second segments, subtracting the squares to find the true hypotenuse.

# On the Calculation of Visual Parallax

Because of that light, there is no arc <note>Referring to the mathematical arc of displacement</note>; that which depends on it is the <term>vilambana</term><note>Displacement or "delay" in position, usually referring to parallax in longitude</note>. One should discard the notion of any other cause. Having first projected the interior of the sphere <note>original: "pūrvā-garbhaṃ"</note> from the primary light, and having calculated it by means of the various visual rays <note>original: "dṛk-sūtraiḥ"</note>, one determines the mathematical conjunction. From that effort, the second displacement <note>parallax</note> arises. 

Thus, by the power of the difference between their own positions, the visual parallaxes for various latitudes <note>original: "viṣuvat-kāṇāṃ," likely referring to the equatorial or observers' positions</note> are certainly produced through the first and second methods. From the solar form on the circumference of the sphere, this visual path is progressively calculated. It is equal to the conjunction born from the center <note>geocentric conjunction</note>. 

### Parallax of the Sun and Moon

At the moment of the <term>darṣānta</term><note>The end of the new moon; the moment of true conjunction or eclipse</note>, the displacement of the Moon and the Sun is calculated only from their <term>madhyama-lambana</term><note>Mean or middle parallax</note>. This is due to the difference between their two middle parallaxes. In this way, the displacement can be calculated from two methods. 

At the end of the conjunction, the zenith distance <note>original: "natāṃśa"</note> of the Sun at that moment is used. First, the visual parallax is calculated, and then, by means of the clear zenith distance, the second parallax is determined. One should consider the second as equal to the first in some instances. 

In this way, the displacement of the second is known. Then, the zenith distance arising at the end of the conjunction should be calculated. By using the clear zenith distances, the second displacement at the end of the conjunction is produced. In some cases, it may not be exactly equal. Thus, at the end of the conjunction, there is a difference between the first and second parallax. 

### Resolving the Question of Mean Parallax

One might ask: "If there is a difference in parallax, how can the middle of the eclipse for the Sun be of only one kind?" 

Listen to the explanation: At the time when the Moon and Sun reach conjunction, they are at the same visual level. At that first point of no displacement, there is no inconsistency at all. This should be carefully noted. This is the reason. On the sphere, the mark of the visual ray is indeed higher.

# The Physical Basis of Visual Parallax

Now, the logical explanation <note>original: "upapatti"</note> of visual parallax is provided: 

An observer situated on the Earth’s surface <note>original: "bhūpṛṣṭha-sthito draṣṭā"</note> views the Sun from his own position as if it were touching the Earth at a certain distance. This is because there is no other way to explain the visual perception. The object seen is marked by the observer's line of sight. Therefore, because of the visual point on the Earth's surface, he sees the Sun as if it were attached to the point where the visual ray intersects the horizon, and not otherwise. 

Now, consider the ray that extends from the observer's visual point to the rising Sun; that ray is understood to touch the Earth-sphere. There, from the seen visual point, the Sun appears to be attached to the Earth-sphere as far as the visual ray extends, because the visual ray is a single line. From the point on the Earth’s surface, the interval between the two—the perceived and the actual—is the visual distance. 

From the visual point on the horizon at the time of rising or setting, and through the connection with the clear visual ray of that horizon, it is established on the sphere. Thus, for the calculation of parallax at sunrise or sunset, the geometry of the field is as follows: The ray extending from one's own visual point to where it is clear on the celestial sphere is like a staff <note>original: "daṇḍavat"</note> or a transverse line. It is attached there. On both sides, according to the distance, the positions on the Earth are separated by an interval.

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric derivation of parallax, moving from the general theory of displacement to the specific calculation of the observer's line of sight versus the geocentric line. It builds on the "first method" mentioned on the previous page, using the Earth's radius as a primary variable in right-angled triangles.</meta>

<warning>Minor bleed-through from the reverse side and some fading on the left margin.</warning>

In a region not on the horizon, because there is no relationship with other regions, there is no specified division in the rising and setting positions. From the Earth's center, taking the Earth's semi-diameter <note>the Earth's radius</note> as the **base** <note>original: "bhujā"</note> and the distance to the Sun as the **hypotenuse** <note>original: "ravi-karṇaḥ karṇaḥ"</note>, the square root of the difference of their squares <note>a standard application of the Pythagorean theorem: $\sqrt{Karna^2 - Bhuja^2}$</note> determines the **perpendicular side** <note>original: "koṭi"</note> in that region. 

In this same way, from the center of the Earth to the point fixed on the "true line" <note>original: "spaṣṭa-sūtra"; the geocentric vector</note> of the lunar sphere, the distance is the **hypotenuse**. Using the Earth's radius as the **base**, the square root of the difference of their squares is calculated. From that position, the distance to the lunar sphere—fixed along the "true line"—is the **perpendicular side** of the segment of the visual ray. 

In these dissimilar triangles, this **perpendicular side** should be subtracted from the previous **perpendicular side**. The remainder is the distance between the spheres of the Moon and the Sun along the visual line <note>the line of sight from the observer on the surface</note>. 

Here, along the visual line, that distance is the **hypotenuse**. The sine of the parallax <note>original: "lambana-jyā"</note>, in the form of the distance moved on the lunar sphere, is the **base**. The square root of the difference of their squares is the **perpendicular side** along the geocentric line <note>original: "garbha-sūtre"</note>. This explains the construction of the "great triangle." Because these triangles are similar, the hypotenuse of the "true line" and the perpendicular of the other correspond. One should not doubt how this is possible; even though they are situated in similar triangles, the result is achieved without contradiction. 

The distance to the Sun <note>original: "ravi-karṇo"</note>, having been brought from the Earth’s radius as the **base**, is used in the calculation of the parallax. Again, by proportion: if the distance to the Moon is the radius of the circle, then what is the resulting sine of parallax? From this calculation, the visual parallax <note>original: "dṛg-lambanaṃ"</note> at the rising and setting points is determined.

### The Second Method

Now, the second method is explained. The first line is the geocentric line, and the second is the visual line from the surface. The third is the solar line from the center of the Earth <note>original: "ku-garbhād"</note>. Within the solar sphere, the distance between the second and third lines is measured in minutes of an arc <note>original: "capa-kalā"</note>. That same distance exists for those on the lunar sphere. Therefore, by proportion with the distance on the lunar sphere, the result of the difference between the first and third lines is found. The difference between the "true" geocentric line and the visual line is thus established as the visual parallax <note>original: "dṛg-lambane"</note>. 

In the same manner, the theory of parallax is established for the entire circle. Here, for the diagram of the line of sight attached to the Earth's surface, the difference between the distances <note>to the Sun and Moon</note> is always used in this geometric method. Therefore, the logic of parallax is the same for the rising and setting forms. If the diagram were otherwise, the result would change. 

Now, following the first method, let us imagine the theory of parallax: the distance from the Earth's center to the point on the "true line" is the hypotenuse, and the Earth's radius is the base... <note>The text cuts off as it begins a new calculation</note>
