...from the center of the Earth to the point where the Moon’s sphere meets the "true line," the distance of the Moon is considered the **hypotenuse** <note>original: "karṇaḥ"</note>. That distance itself, as the **base** <note>original: "bhujā"</note>, has the square root of the difference of its square taken to find the **perpendicular side** <note>original: "koṭi"</note>. This segment, from the mark of the planet to the solar orbit's intersection with the lunar sphere, is the **perpendicular side**. By subtracting this perpendicular, the remainder represents the distance between the spheres of the Moon and Sun along the solar orbit.

In this context, where this is the **perpendicular side** and that is the **base**, the sine of the parallax is determined by a proportion: if the base is found at the measure of the solar orbit's marking, then what is the value at the distance brought about by the previously calculated hypotenuse? The resulting sine of the parallax <note>the apparent shift in position</note>, multiplied by the **total radius** <note>original: "tri-jyā"; the radius of the standard sine-circle, often 3438 arcminutes</note> and divided by the distance of the Moon, becomes the final sine. The arc of that value on the "circle of vision" <note>the observer's vertical circle</note> is the parallax of the planet or Sun.

Now, it is explained how the parallax of a planet or the Sun is established when the observer is situated anywhere, even using only a single eye <note>meaning a single point of observation on the surface</note>. From the Earth's center, the distance to the Sun <note>the Sun's "hypotenuse"</note> is imagined as the **base** at the measure of the planetary orbit. A calculation is performed where the line of the planet meets the Earth. Similarly, the distance from the Earth's center to the point where the Moon's "true line" ends is taken as the Moon's distance. In the "circle of vision" <note>dṛk-tulya-vṛtta</note>, the distance from the Moon's parallax to the Sun is the "resultant parallax." The square root of the difference of the Moon's distance squared is taken as the resultant. The difference between those two on the solar orbit is the distance between the lunar and solar spheres. 

From that, the parallax is established as previously stated: if the base is the resultant parallax of the Sun, then what is the value of the parallax sine in terms of yojanas <note>a traditional unit of distance</note> for the distance brought about by that difference? Again, multiplied by the **total radius** and divided by the Moon's distance, the sine of the parallax is produced. The arc of this is known as the **visual parallax** <note>dṛg-lambana</note>.

Next, regarding the parallax at the time of rising and setting: it is even greater from the Earth's surface than otherwise. Why is there a lack of this parallax in the middle <note>at the zenith</note> when it is at its maximum at the horizon? Listen: from the center of the Earth, one should draw a circle representing the solid Earth. Then, a second circle should be drawn from the Earth's center at the measure of the planet’s position. A third circle should be drawn from that center at the Moon’s distance, and a fourth should be drawn for the Sun. This should be known by the wise as the "arm-line" <note>karṇa-rekhā</note> extending from the center of the Earth. There, at the intersection of the first circle and the vertical line of the horizon as seen from the center, is the mark of the planet. From those two, the horizontal lines are drawn everywhere parallel. At the intersection of the Earth's surface and the horizon, there is the vertical line from the mark on the Earth's surface to the mark of the planet. A line drawn from there to the Sun touches the lunar sphere; this is the line for parallax at rising and setting. The interval on the lunar sphere between the "center-line" <note>the geocentric line</note> and that line is the parallax. Because this is seen at the horizon, it is known as the **parallax of rising and setting**. 

For the Sun, the line of rising/setting parallax is the solar line itself. On the lunar sphere, the distance from the center-line to that limit is the parallax. As the Sun rises higher, it moves further from the line of rising/setting parallax toward its own planetary line. On the lunar sphere, from the center-line to the rising/setting line is the maximum parallax, and it increases toward the planetary line based on the observer's position on the Earth's surface...

<summary>This page provides the geometric derivation for calculating the sine of parallax (lambana-jyā) using the Pythagorean theorem and proportional reasoning (the Rule of Three). It specifically explains how to calculate the distance between the lunar and solar spheres as seen from an observer on Earth's surface and why parallax is greatest at the horizon.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of parallax (lambana), moving from the basic geometric definitions of "base" and "perpendicular" to the actual physical observation from the Earth's surface compared to its center. It focuses on how the "line of sight" varies as a celestial body rises above the horizon.</meta>

For the purpose of making this known: to the extent that a celestial body is elevated <note>meaning its altitude above the horizon increases</note>, the **parallax** <term>lambana: the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different points, here the Earth's surface versus its center</term> of the **Earth’s surface** <note>original: *bhūpṛṣṭha*</note> is determined from the **line of sight** <note>original: *dṛk-sūtra*; literally "sight-thread"</note> as an **increase** <note>original: *upacaya*</note>. This is also because, just as on the line of the **Earth’s surface horizon** <note>original: *bhūpṛṣṭha-kṣitija*</note>, there are specific marks for the **Earth’s center** <note>original: *bhū-kendra*</note> and the Earth’s surface; from these, lines are to be drawn in reverse.

In that context, the line for the surface of the Earth is the line that calculates parallax. For both cases, the parallax is that which is raised from the **center-line** <note>original: *garbha-sūtra*</note> in the sphere; it is more than what is measured by the line of sight. The half-horizon of the Earth's surface is the limit of visibility. One line, reaching the direction of the Earth's surface, becomes the line for **rising and setting parallax** <note>original: *udyāsta-lambana*</note>. Then, at those degrees of the region, a line from the Earth's surface and Earth's center should be attached to the circle of the visible mark. The line which is reverse from that point of attachment is the line that calculates the parallax of the Earth's surface. In the sphere of the Moon, the parallax of the Earth's surface is the distance from the center-line; the "own-sight line" is separated by that distance, and thus the parallax would be even greater.

In this way, the observation of visibility itself is the parallax; that indeed would be the **maximum** <note>original: *parama*</note>. From there, as the Sun is elevated, a single line of the Sun should be joined to the circle of the visible mark. That line, which calculates the parallax of the Sun on the visibility circle, is the parallax of the visibility circle itself, starting from the center-line in the sphere. Because it is situated below that "own-sight line," that parallax is lower. Just as the Sun’s line is the place on the clear visibility circle, there is the maximum parallax; otherwise, it is clearly less. 

In the same way as mentioned, the line calculating the parallax situated on the Earth’s surface horizon becomes the line of sight. There, the parallax is equal to the parallax of the Earth’s surface. Thus, in the convention of "one's own visibility mark," in the line of rising and setting parallax, that very rising and setting parallax occurs due to it being one's own line of sight. Above that, however, it is below the rising and setting parallax line, according to one's own line of sight. Therefore, because there is an absence of parallax in the "own-sight line" when it takes the form of a **center-line** <note>a line originating from the Earth's center</note>, and due to the difference in terminology, the horizon of the Earth’s center is where rising and setting occurs. There, the "own-line" <note>the observer's local vertical</note> exists; from that observation, the parallax is equal to the rising and setting parallax, and not from any other visible mark. 

<insert>Commonly in the world, people say</insert> that the maximum parallax in the lunar sphere occurs when a line is brought from the Earth's surface, through the center, and extended to the Sun; <note>original: *tadasat*; literally "that is untrue" or "incorrect"</note> **that is incorrect**. In that case, the parallax is less than the rising and setting parallax of the great stars, and since it is significantly less than the parallax on the "own-visibility circle," its "maximum" status is not proven. Furthermore, the line of sight is not proven for someone not at the Earth's center. Thus, for whoever has a visibility mark attached to the Earth's surface, the Earth's surface horizon is their visibility mark. Their Earth’s surface horizon is the "rising and setting horizon" and the "visibility circle," and therefore the rising and setting parallax alone would be the maximum parallax. As that mark is on the Earth's surface...

The author refutes a common contemporary belief about the calculation of maximum parallax, insisting it must be based on the observer's specific horizon on the Earth's surface.</summary>

<margin>
Tantra Chapter
Commentary

86
</margin>

Knowing the rising and setting of celestial bodies from the visible horizon, one must understand the relationship of **parallax** <term>lambana: the difference in the apparent position of a celestial object as seen from the Earth's surface versus its center</term>. Here, a specific **correction** <note>original: *saṃskāra*</note> is applied by those skilled in the science of **calculation** <note>original: *gaṇita*</note> to ensure the math aligns with observation. While the calculation provides the result, the **geometric proof** <term>vāsana: the underlying rationale or intuitive geometric demonstration of a mathematical rule</term> is considered here through the "eye of the sphere" <note>referring to spherical astronomy</note>. This is understood by few others; therefore, we shall not dwell too long on the explanation. In this context, the second method for the geometry of the parallax field is stated, and the first method for the parallax field should also be understood by the wise.

Indeed, the difference between the **zenith distance** <note>original: *dṛg-aṃśa*</note> and the observer's own position on the ecliptic is what creates the need for this correction. Because of the rotation of the **celestial sphere** <note>original: *bha-maṇḍala*</note>, the difference arises; from that difference, for the sake of obtaining the **true parallax** <note>original: *sphuṭa-lambana*</note>, one must consider the motion. What is determined by the power of that motion is the difference in **declination** <note>original: *krānti*</note>. This is suitable for observation. It is well known in the equality of the celestial sphere that the difference between the two "threads" <note>meaning the lines of sight/calculation</note> represents the time that shows the true position.

As it is said, for the sake of bringing about the **true corrected parallax**: first, find the difference between the **nonagesimal** <term>tribhona-lagna: the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point (ascendant); the highest point of the ecliptic</term> and the Sun. Turn this into sines and divide by the radius. From that, using the **sine of the altitude** <note>original: *śaṅku*</note> of the nonagesimal and the **radius** <note>original: *trijyā*</note>, the parallax in units of time <note>original: *ghaṭikā*</note> is found. The square root of the difference of the squares of the sine of the zenith distance and the nonagesimal's sine gives the **perpendicular** <note>original: *koṭi*</note>. When multiplied by the Earth's radius and divided by the distance, these become the minutes of parallax. 

The **parallax in latitude** <term>nati: the north-south displacement of a celestial body due to parallax</term> is calculated by dividing the sine of the parallax in altitude by the radius; the arc of that result is called *nati*. Alternatively, the square root of the difference of the squares of the zenith distance of the nonagesimal and the Sun can be used. The sine of the distance between the nonagesimal and the Sun, multiplied by the **sine of the parallax in altitude** <note>original: *dṛg-lambana-jyā*</note> and divided by the radius, gives the **true parallax in longitude** <note>original: *sphuṭa-lambana*</note>. Thus, the calculation of the parallax in latitude and longitude should be performed by the observer.

<summary>This page details the mathematical formulas for calculating true parallax in longitude (lambana) and latitude (nati) using the nonagesimal (tribhona-lagna) and the sine of the zenith distance. It emphasizes the "vāsana" or geometric reasoning behind the numerical corrections applied to astronomical observations.</summary>

<keywords>parallax, lambana, nati, nonagesimal, tribhona-lagna, zenith distance, dṛgjyā, celestial sphere, astronomical calculation, vāsana</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical derivation of parallax, focusing on the specific geometric corrections required to calculate the "true" parallax in longitude and latitude. It builds on the previous page's definition of the observer's line of sight versus the Earth's center.</meta>

From the sine of the **zenith distance** <term>drgjya: the sine of the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term>, the **parallax in longitude** <term>lambana</term> and the **parallax in latitude** <term>nati</term> are determined. From this, the sine of the **true parallax** <note>original: sphuṭa-lambana-jyā</note> is divided; such is the method. Here, the result of the calculation for the true parallax in longitude is the root of the difference of the parts. From ninety degrees, the sine of the parallax in latitude <note>original: nati-jyā</note> and its corresponding arc—this parallax—is directed toward the observer's view.

When the Sun and Moon are positioned in the **nonagesimal** <term>vitribha-lagna: the point on the ecliptic exactly 90 degrees from the rising point; the highest point of the ecliptic</term>, the calculation for the Sun and Moon is considered. The distance between the Sun and the observer's line of sight, using the two **hypotenuses** <note>original: śruti; referring to the distances of the two bodies from the Earth</note>, results in the second parallax in longitude, which is also called the parallax in latitude. What I have stated here regarding the seven-fold parallax in longitude is based on the initial crude calculation. By the process of rough proportion at this time, the calculation of the position of the parallax is performed. Once that is done, the **true nature of the unified parallax** <note>original: yuktaika-vilambana-sphuṭatvaṃ</note> is sought through the stated paths.

Alternatively, for the parallax in the other line of sight, whatever is clear should be performed here. From that, by the process of rough proportion at this time, the calculation of the position of that parallax is made. This is the method for the **sine of the true parallax** <note>original: sphuṭa-lambana-siñjini</note> as told by the experts. 

Now, the reality of the arc of that true parallax is described. The sine of the true parallax mentioned initially is considered the **base-sine** <note>original: koṭi</note>. Some say there are five points of intersection, while the base-sine is the first. In the celestial sphere, the sine of what is called the true parallax by the ancient people is considered the base-sine. How can it be said that the celestial sphere and the sine of the base are the same? Because of the difference between the celestial sphere and the **parallax in latitude** <note>original: nati</note>, the true parallax is calculated. This is not appropriate, because the Moon's orbit resides within that circle. 

The sine of the true parallax, multiplied by the **radius** <term>trijyā: the sine of 90 degrees, often used as a constant in these calculations</term> and divided by the base-sine, results in the true parallax. This is called the arc of the true parallax. Otherwise, for the sake of those known as experts in the **geometry of the sphere** <term>gola-vitti</term>, the base-sine of the parallax in longitude is multiplied by the radius and divided by the base-sine. If this is subtracted from ninety degrees, the difference in the celestial sphere is the true parallax. 

Thus, the true parallax in minutes and seconds <note>original: kalādyam</note> is determined. When divided by the difference in the **motions of the Sun and Moon** <note>original: ravi-candra-gatyoḥ</note>, it becomes a value in **units of time** <term>ghaṭikā: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>. This must be understood through repeated calculation. When the line of sight is unified, the parallax is most clear. When there is no difference between them, the parallax is absent.

In the Sun's position, it is not clear from the **nonagesimal** <note>original: vitribha-lagna</note>. The middle region between the two arises from the **ascendant** <term>lagna: the rising point of the ecliptic</term>. The calculation of the Sun there is the goal. However, this is not just the truth of a solar eclipse. The parallax depends on the **point of the eclipse** <note>original: tattva-lagna</note>; it is not fixed. Those who know the geometry understand the solar method. 

Indeed, how is it stated in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>The authoritative ancient Indian astronomical text</note>? It is said regarding the middle of the two motions of the Sun... that by the words "the sines of the latitude and the base of the zenith distance," it is equal. Here, whatever is established by the **ascendant** <note>original: lagna</note> and the **altitude and zenith distance** <note>original: natonnate</note> is considered "middle." In the case of the nonagesimal, they are "true." Therefore, the meaning of the Sun’s words is that the two motions are true, and the sines of the zenith distance and altitude are for the nonagesimal.

It specifically critiques traditional methods and aligns the geometry with the principles found in the Surya Siddhanta.</summary>

When the division of the **vertical circle** <term>drgvṛtta: the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial object</term> is calculated, the degrees of altitude and zenith distance result in the determined movements of the planets. The **true position of the Sun** <note>original: sphuṭārkasya</note> is found by halving the lunar day. Thus, the parallax of the planets is established from the vertical circle at the **nonagesimal** <term>vitribha: the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point</term>. To find the cause of the calculation for the Sun's disk when it is small, one must consider the sphere of the sky. When the vertical circle is used for the true calculation of the degrees, there is no parallax there if the Sun is at the **nonagesimal** <note>original: vitribhalagna-arka-same; when the Sun is at the highest point of the ecliptic</note>. This is because the vertical circle and the ecliptic coincide at that point. Due to the lack of difference there, the true calculation of degrees is corrected. 

From that, various methods arise; the distinctions are explained through the specific types of the **true parallax in longitude** <note>original: sphuṭa-vilambana</note>. Because the parallax is small and there is no difference in the direction of the latitude, it becomes manifold. In this context, that which is accepted regarding the **geocentric horizon** <note>original: garbha-kujeṣu</note> is suitable for practical use due to the small difference in the zodiac signs. Where a difference arises between the two, it is called **true parallax** <note>original: sphuṭa-vilambana</note>. This difference between the vertical circle and the line of sight is spoken of as being toward the east or west. Thus, the movement is not corrected from the west because it is already completed. Furthermore, even from the vertical circle, the wise must consider the matter according to the **geocentric line** <term>garbha-sūtra: a line drawn from the center of the Earth to the celestial body</term>. 

The distance between the vertical circle and the geocentric line is what constitutes the celestial sphere. Therefore, the **parallax** <note>original: vilambana</note> of the geocentric and topocentric lines must be calculated by assumption. The **line of sight** <term>dṛṣṭi-sūtra: the line from the observer on the surface to the planet</term> is situated where the observer stands. At the end of the **New Moon** <note>original: amānte</note>, the geocentric lines and the lines of sight are considered; the Sun is seen ahead or behind, and thus the true conjunction is corrected. In the Great Age <note>original: yuga</note>, the conjunction that has passed or is to come is not simply the apparent one. Therefore, the parallax of the Sun is equal to the nonagesimal; otherwise, the parallax is different. By correcting it according to its own calculation, the **true time of observation** <note>original: sphuṭa-dṛṣṭi-kāla</note> is found. 

The difference found in the zenith distance is the distance of the Sun. This belongs to the two lines—the topocentric and the geocentric. Without doubt, after considering this, one should observe the **New Moon** <term>darśa</term>; otherwise, there will be an error in the conjunction of the two luminaries. Therefore, the **geocentric parallax** <note>original: dṛg-garbha-sūtra-lambana</note> is declared. By the two lines—the Earth’s center and the observer's eye—the Sun is reached. The geocentric line within the Sun's sphere is what defines the difference between the vertical circle and the geocentric line, because of the lack of distance between the observer's lines at that time. 

Here, the distance between the Earth and the observer is the line of sight. For the sake of establishing the conjunction, the difference in time must be calculated. From that, the geocentric line of sight is determined. As the wise have said, to establish the calculation of parallax, the interval between the Sun and Moon must be used. From that, the conjunction arises. When the circles of latitude and the vertical circle are one, the calculation is simplified. Even when they differ, the correction of the Sun and planets' half-diameter is established. Where these two circles do not coincide, the Sun and Moon are situated on different lines. The completion of the movement of the centers of their disks occurs even when there is a difference in their directions.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the conjunction of the Sun and Moon, specifically addressing how parallax affects the observed timing of an eclipse. It transitions from the general theory of parallax to the iterative mathematical method required to find the "true" moment of conjunction as seen from the Earth's surface.</meta>

The **conjunction** <note>original: dyutiḥ; refers to the meeting of the Sun and Moon</note> is explained as occurring on the **circle passing through the poles of the ecliptic** <term>kadamba-vṛtta: a secondary circle used to measure celestial latitude</term> when it coincides with the **line of sight** <term>dṛksūtra: the straight line from the observer's eye to the celestial body</term>. By marking the position of the Moon on the zodiacal circle and aligning it with the line of sight, the time of the conjunction is determined. Just as the latitude of the Moon’s disk is situated on the circle of the ecliptic poles, so too is the Moon's position on the line of sight established. The resulting correction is known as the **parallax in latitude** <note>original: nati</note>, which is distinct from the **true latitude** <note>original: sphuṭa-śara</note>. To determine the precise eclipse of the Sun and Moon, one must calculate the sum of the radii of the two disks. When the difference between them is minimal, it is called the **conjunction** <note>original: yoga</note>.

In the east-west direction, the circle of the ecliptic poles is positioned north or south based on the observer. When the centers of the disks of the Sun and Moon are both situated on this circle, there is no east-west distance between them; this is the **moment of conjunction** <note>original: yutikāla</note>. Wise men have taught that this occurs when the two bodies lack any longitudinal difference on the **zodiacal sphere** <term>bhamanḍala: the celestial longitudes of the stars and planets</term>. If there is a large discrepancy, the position of the Moon must be calculated for that specific moment using the degrees of the zodiac. By the method previously mentioned, one must calculate the **true parallax in longitude** <note>original: sphuṭa-vilambana</note>, which represents the distance between the Sun and Moon as seen by the observer.

How, then, is the calculation of this conjunction performed when the Moon's disk is affected by its latitude? If the parallax is calculated from the line of sight, the degrees of altitude of the Sun are corrected by the observer's parallax. This same method is used to calculate the altitude. The initial parallax is determined first, and it must remain consistent. Through this, the true position of the Moon's disk is found. 

Now, the **iterative method** <term>asakṛt: a repetitive calculation used to reach a precise value when variables change</term> is described for finding the difference at the **end of the New Moon** <note>original: darśa-virāma; the moment the lunar month ends</note>. The difference between the Sun and Moon is calculated for the moments before and after the conjunction. The **true parallax** <note>original: sphuṭa-vilambana</note> is equal to that difference at that specific time. The time of the New Moon is corrected by the parallax, and this corrected time is surely the **time of the true conjunction** <note>original: sphuṭa-darśa-kāla</note>. 

Through this, the calculation of parallax must be repeated. Once the parallax for the New Moon is known, the conjunction is found; then, from that conjunction, the parallax is calculated again. This **iterative process** <note>original: asakṛt-sādhya</note> must continue until the parallax values converge and the exact time of the conjunction is established. The first parallax found is the difference at the start, but the final parallax is what determines the true position.

<summary>This page describes the geometric and iterative methods for calculating the exact moment of solar-lunar conjunction (an eclipse) by accounting for parallax in both latitude and longitude. It emphasizes the "asakrt" or iterative technique to ensure the calculated time matches the observed reality.</summary>

</margin>

If it were so, then at that point, the sum of the **line of sight** <term>dṛksūtra: the line from the observer's eye to the planet</term> and the marked positions would reach a state of rest. Therefore, because the iterative results of **parallax** <term>lambana: the difference in the apparent position of a celestial object when viewed from different points</term> eventually become equal, the wise have declared this the "attainment of the result." This is what truly reveals the **true visible moment of conjunction** <note>original: sphuṭa-vāstava-darśī-kāle; the precise time the Sun and Moon appear to meet from a specific location on Earth</note>. 

This time is distinct from the moment of conjunction calculated for the Earth's center. Because of the variation in parallax calculated from the time of the flow <note>original: srāvakāla; likely referring to the progression of the eclipse</note>, the corrected end-time of the conjunction is determined. However, even this is considered "true" only in a relative sense here, as its proof arises only from the parallax generated at that specific moment of flow. In this way, because of the difference from the time of flow and the fact that the various calculated parallaxes are always very close to one another, their proximity to the truth increases sequentially. 

Why then would a person of impure intellect be confused when they are so nearly equal? Some have recorded <note>original: mudritam; could imply a printed text or a fixed tradition</note> views that contradict this, but because they do not account for the inequality of all the constituent parts, they cannot achieve a successful result through iterative parallax. Nevertheless, for the sake of worldly practice, everyone accepts a calculation based on a difference of a few **ghatikas** <term>ghaṭikā: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>; yet even that should be analyzed according to the scientific rules of calculating conjunctions.

Thus, the previous method has been explained. In this matter, one must truly observe with a knowledge of the **principles of the sphere** <term>gola-tattva: spherical astronomy</term>. The very time at which there is a specific interval in the parallax is the **moment of conjunction** <note>original: yutikāla</note>. Or rather, that time which is chosen as the moment of conjunction becomes smaller when parallax is added. Because of this small difference, it is impossible to make a final decision here regarding the state of equal parallax between the times of the preceding and following positions. This is because of the discrepancies that arise in the application of these rules. 

This difference occurs because of the variation in the two "spheres" <note>the observer's sphere vs. the Earth-center sphere</note> in the systems of Brahma and others. The success of the calculation of their union cannot be achieved without acknowledging this difference. Due to the arrangement of a single system, the "Earth-difference" <note>parallax</note> is not seen to be equal at all times. 

I shall now explain the theory behind this according to the rules of the sphere, specifically addressing the "True Parallax Instrument" mentioned by **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II, a 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, which he established to harmonize his own system with the circle of the stars without contradiction. Some priests/scholars, due to their ignorance of the **Geography of the Sphere** <term>bhūgola: the study of the Earth as a sphere</term>, have become confused regarding parallax.

It is well known that the distance on the Moon's sphere between the line from the Earth's center to the Sun and the line of sight is called **parallax**. On the Moon's sphere, the distance between the point of the line from the center and the line of sight within the **vertical circle** <term>dṛṅ-maṇḍala: the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term> is the **parallax in altitude**. 

From this, the method for bringing forth the **true parallax** is as follows: Imagine the ecliptic circle on the Moon's sphere itself. Then, having established the two poles of the ecliptic, create an east-west circle. Where that circle meets the center-line, and where the motion-circle meets the line of sight, and where the "Middle Circle" <note>equator or local meridian</note> meets the point of the **nonagesimal** <term>tribhonalagna: the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point</term>—these must necessarily be considered. Even so, the points do not always touch because of the variation in latitude from the rising and setting points. Thus, that motion-circle should be moved by the degrees of the interval between the line of sight and the center-line...

It focuses on the geometric derivation of parallax in longitude (lambana) and latitude (nati) by analyzing the intersection of the ecliptic and the observer's vertical circle.</meta>

</margin>

By the distance of the intersection, the point located on the **ecliptic** <term>krāntivṛtta: the sun's apparent path</term> marked by the **geocentric line** <term>garbhasūtra: the line from the Earth's center to the celestial body</term>, which is calculated as an arc of that circle, is multiplied by the radius. Similarly, with reference to the center of the **ecliptic pole** <term>kadamba: the point 90 degrees from the ecliptic</term>, the sine of the angle of the **parallax in latitude** <term>nati: the north-south displacement of the moon's position due to the observer's location</term> is determined via the circle situated at the mark of the **line of sight**. 

In the same way, on the circle of the **equator** <term>akṣavalaya: literally the "axis-circle"</term>, its rotation is understood as the sine of the arc on the circle known as the **sine of the zenith distance of the ecliptic point** <term>vinibhalagna-śanku: the sine of the altitude of the point on the ecliptic highest above the horizon</term>. 

Thus, with reference to the center of the ecliptic pole, another column <note>original: koṣṭha; likely referring to a cell in a table or a specific geometric quadrant</note> should be constructed on the eastern or western circles. Here, the ecliptic pole is situated very close to the ecliptic itself. In that context, the difference between the circles of the geocentric and visible marks on the ecliptic is the **true parallax** <note>original: sphuṭa-valana; here used in the sense of the true displacement or lambana</note>. 

Even on the geocentric mark-circle, the difference between circles similar to the ecliptic is the **parallax in latitude** (*nati*). Its **base** <term>bhuja: the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term> is measured by the sine of the visible motion. The square root of the difference of the squares <note>original: tadvargāntarapada; referring to the Pythagorean theorem calculation: $\sqrt{c^2 - a^2} = b$</note> is the sine of the distance between the geocentric mark and the ecliptic-like circle. This is not the converted value, but the **true parallax** <note>original: luvana; an OCR error for lambana</note>. 

The **perpendicular** <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> is situated on the ecliptic. The sine of the distance between the geocentric and visible mark-circles is the parallax. Here, this small geometric area is contained within a larger similar geometric area. Thus, on the geocentric circle, the base is the sine of the distance between the geocentric mark and the **altitude circle** <term>śanku-vṛtta</term>. The distance between the center and the geocentric mark-circle is measured by the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>dṛgjyā</term>, which serves as the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares gives the distance between the center and the geocentric mark on the altitude circle, which is the **visible altitude** <term>dṛg-dṛti</term>, serving as the perpendicular. 

From this, the proof of the parallax in latitude and the true parallax in longitude—represented as the base and perpendicular—is established. If the small hypotenuse, small base, and small perpendicular are known, then by using the sine of the visible parallax, the results for the observer on the Earth's surface are obtained. This larger geometric area, contained within a similar larger area, follows the same nature. 

Furthermore, the difference-sine is given by the intersection of the geocentric circle, the ecliptic, and the vertical circle. This proves the corrected displacement. The difference-sine of two areas separated by three signs <note>90 degrees</note> is the maximum. That itself is the sine of the perpendicular of the ecliptic on the geocentric circle. This known sine is the base, and the radius serves as the hypotenuse in the vertical circle. The square root of the difference of their squares is the sine of the perpendicular arc. 

Therefore, with the radius as the hypotenuse for the large base and perpendicular, and the sine of the vertical circle for the observer's position, the parallax in latitude (as the base) and the true parallax in longitude (as the perpendicular) are both established. In this way, the three-fold method proves that the parallax in longitude, in the form of a perpendicular, is converted to the scale of the radius via the sine of the angle of parallax in latitude. 

Again, there is a further proportion regarding the interior of the ecliptic: if this is the case in the circle of the perpendicular-sine of parallax in latitude, then what would it be in the circle of the radius? This calculation establishes the true parallax in longitude as the sine of the distance between the visible and geocentric marks on the ecliptic. Otherwise, one might say that at the non-intersection of the line-of-sight mark, the vertical circle, and the ecliptic-like circle, even while looking at the vertical circle, the extent of the visible parallax is merely the sine of the distance between the visible mark-circle and the other points on the ecliptic-like circle. That is not the distance between the two points on the ecliptic. However, when converted to the radius, it is measured as the sine of the true parallax perpendicular. This is proven through direct observation. Therefore, the proportion is: in the circle of the perpendicular-sine of the parallax in latitude, the value is the perpendicular-sine of the visible parallax...

It transitions from the general geometry of celestial circles to specific "direct" methods for calculating corrected parallax, comparing the geocentric and observer-centric views of the Sun and Moon.</meta>

<margin>
S. T.
Vi.
89
89
</margin>

By the logic of the maximum parallax of the stars, what is determined in the **radius circle** <term>trijyāvṛtta: a standard reference circle in Indian trigonometry with a radius of 3438 minutes</term> as the **cosine of the corrected parallax** <term>sphuṭa-lambana-koṭijyā</term> in the form of the **ecliptic parallax sine** <term>krāntivṛttīya-natajyā</term> is established as the **arc of the distance of the corrected parallax** <term>nātśruti-sphuṭa-lambana</term>. Here, the intersection of the **vertical circle** <term>dṛgmaṇḍala: the circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>, the **ecliptic** <term>krāntimaṇḍala</term>, and the **equator** <term>nārī-maṇḍala</term> is analyzed. On the circle where the ecliptic touches the sky, calculations are made using the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>dṛgjya</term>. For those who understand the celestial sphere, the **corrected parallax** <term>sphuṭa-lambana</term> is simply the rising value of the division in the path of the observer's line of sight, which is easily understood. Here, in the visible sphere, if a calculation for parallax arises, there is a triangle formed with the center of the Earth as a vertex; the same occurs within the sphere of the Sun. Therefore, let the wise understand the calculation of parallax and latitude parallax through the difference in zenith distance. The "Direct Method" <note>original: sakṛt-prakāra; a method for calculating a result directly in one step without needing multiple iterations</note> for parallax is established as follows:

The maximum parallax in longitude is determined from the degrees of the sine of the zenith distance. Then, multiply this by the **sine of the altitude of the nonagesimal** <term>tribhonalagna-nara</term> and divide by the radius; this is situated on the ecliptic. Multiply the **sine of the distance between the Sun and the nonagesimal** <term>tribhonalagnārka-viyoga-jīvā</term> by the maximum parallax and divide by the radius. This yields the parallax sine for that specific time and direction. Just as the sine of the maximum result <note>original: triprāntyaphala-jyā</note> is used for the Sun, so too is this corrected parallax calculated for a planet at its lowest point <insert>using the sine of the distance from the observer</insert>. Therefore, the logic of the "Direct Method" is joined here as well. Just as the **equation of the center** <term>śīghraphala</term> is calculated for a planet, so too for this parallax, which is always clearly manifested by the motion. From the **nonagesimal** <term>vinibhalagna</term>, the difference between the Sun and the nonagesimal is understood in the celestial sphere. Thus, using the centers of the **epicycles** <term>pratimaṇḍala</term>, one should calculate the sine and cosine of the zenith distance. By this, the direct parallax is found. By multiplying the sine of the distance between the Sun and the nonagesimal by the maximum parallax, and taking the square root of the results, the value for the desired time is found. The arc of that value is the corrected parallax. This is known by the wise as the "Direct Method."

Even if the result is obtained by multiplying the difference-sine of the nonagesimal and the Sun, the arc of that result will be the corrected parallax. This "Direct Method" is established as originating from the teachings of **Brahma** <note>original: brahmatodbhava; likely referring to the Brahmasphuta-siddhanta or the school of Brahmagupta</note>. That corrected time is for the conjunction/opposition. Without falling into error, the corrected parallax calculated from the time of zenith distance becomes the corrected time of the conjunction. Regarding these corrected values, even though the "Direct Method" is established, for the sake of clarity, I have explained it here properly. One should accept the precision of the parallax. Therefore, the virtuous say that the "Direct Method" is well-known and logically sound. I understand its meaning thus: The sine of the nonagesimal is equal for both the Sun and the Moon. From that, the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> is calculated. When the two are separated by their respective latitudes, the difference in their visual parallax <note>original: dṛṣṭi-lambanake</note> in the interval of the celestial circles is calculated in ghatis <term>ghaṭi: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>.

<summary>This page details the mathematical "Direct Method" (sakṛt-prakāra) for calculating corrected parallax (sphuṭa-lambana) and latitude parallax (nati), specifically focusing on the geometric relationship between the Sun, the Moon, and the nonagesimal point on the ecliptic.</summary>
